Life
By R. Father Francis Leonard Hansen of the Order of Preachers.
From the third edition, which was printed in Louvain in 1668 after the second Roman edition.

Rose, virgin, of the Third Order of St. Dominic, of Lima in Peru, Province of the Americas (S.)

A. Leonardo Hansen

Chapter I.
Holy homeland, parents, name, and pious childhood.

Limaa of South America, which is lapped by the Pacific Oceanb, a noble emporium and the metropolis of the Kingdom of Peru, gave birth to this new Rose in the New World in the year of the Virgin's birth, 1586, in the month of April, [This Saint is born in 1586 in Lima, Peru,] who, because she opens the earth with bursting flowersc, drew her name from her office. The twentieth day of this month is sacred to Blessed Agnes of Montepulcianod, the Dominican virgin, renowned for the heavenly dew and the various flowers that suddenly sprang up, and it was also the birthday of the Rose. Therefore, everything here is in springtime: for indeed the Virgin's ancestors were Gaspar de Floribus, Maria ab Oliva; he, a native of the island of the Spaniards, who is named from the Port of Divitee; she, born in Lima; both were of noble birth, but of very modest means, unless it was their great fortune to have such a little daughter blessed from heaven.

[2] [& at Baptism she receives the name Elizabeth] When Rose was born, the paternal house on St. Dominic Street fell to her lot, and indeed right next to the Church of the Holy Spiritf, so that even the place itself seems to have foretold both her future sanctity and her institute. The mother testified under oath (in the Remissorial Process) that this birth had been for her, as the most joyful, so the easiest, and least subject to those agonies with which she was wont to struggle most terribly in the childbirth of her other children. Nor was the midwife any less astonished when she saw this infant emerging from the umbilical wrappings heavy and as if encased in a little coat. Indeed, this is how roses are born, their innate coverings cloaking them on all sides like a mantle. Then on the most festive day of Pentecost itself (which to the Spaniardsg is called just as to the Italiansh the Feast of the Roses), Rose was baptized, and was made entirely wet by the first dew of heavenly grace. However, her name at that time was not Rose, but Isabella or Elizabeth. namely, from his maternal grandmother Isabella de Herrera, to whom that honor seemed to be owed in the human manner amid the lustral waters.

[3] [which his mother later changed because of a prodigy,] Furthermore, so that the name Rose, divinely destined for the child, might at last emerge after three months of twists and turns, a prodigy was awaited, which was as follows. The little three-month-old lay resting in her cradle when, summoned with the handmaid and the daughters, her mother came and saw her daughter's face covered all over with a rash, though the rose soon vanished. Everyone was stunned. But when the others were struck higher, the mother, suddenly distracted by the spectacle, soothed the little one with a embrace and tender kisses. "You shall be called Rosa from now on, and you shall be." "nor will I henceforth call you by that name": as if he were protesting with the mother of the Forerunner with these words of Ambrose: "He has his own name, which we recognized, not which we chose." However, the grandmother took the matter ill. He grieved that his name was thus erased from his tender granddaughter and almost despised. so that to settle this dispute not even a five-year period was sufficient, until at last the Sacramental Chrism put an end to the controversy, when the most holy Archbishop of Lima, Turibiusi, in the parish of Quivi, while anointing Rosa with the sign of confirmation, omitted her baptismal name, He preferred to pronounce Rosa, whose fragrance he had perhaps already then perceived in the spirit with which he was filled. Indeed, it seemed that the anointing had taught him everything, so that he could not err in the name of Rose, to whom from then on his grandmother also consented.

[4] [so that she would first be called Rosa,] However, after several years had passed, the Virgin herself, with a fearful conscience, brought another suit concerning the name Rosa, namely to provide an occasion and a place for a heavenly oracle by which the name Rosa was to be finally confirmed. This is how it happened. The girl had grown up, and now, of age, she learned that she had never been given this substitute name at baptism but in her cradle; she suspected that it had perhaps been bestowed on her for the mere lure of vanity, to commend the beauty and charm of her face. [4] [so that she would first be called Rosa,] However, after several years had passed, the Virgin herself, with a fearful conscience, brought another suit concerning the name Rosa, namely to provide an occasion and a place for a heavenly oracle by which the name Rosa was to be finally confirmed. There, in tears, he prostrated himself before the Mother of Mercy, laid bare his anguish, and asked for a remedy. Nor did the Virgin, the mystical Rose, delay to console the little maiden Rose; for from on high, yet by an inexplicable ray of inner illumination, she spoke to the fearful one and taught her that, remarkably, this very name of hers was being proven divine by the little Boy she carried. but he himself, of his own accord, wanted to add this further to his reward. by which Rose of St. Mary would be nicknamed in the future. Thus she, who trembled at her given name, was steadied by her surname, secure and joyful that she possessed both as gifts from heaven.

[5] The merits of the Saints do indeed have this (as Ambrose testifies), [to whom the surname from Saint Mary was then added.] but I think those who receive a surname from heaven in addition to their name are rare. The girl received the grace of her unexpected name, not without a lofty sense of inner sweetness, so much so that one day, returning home from the most holy Communion, she implored her mother with unusual ardor to repeat to her as often as possible the single name of her twin favorite, Rose, and to sing it more frequently. to be wonderfully delighted, pleased, and enraptured by it from then on. The parent stood amazed at the novelty. But the Virgin added that she had just now returned from the feet of the Great Mother of God, that this had been dear to her heart, and that with her blessing and maternal auspices, her soul had passed into the Rose sacred to the flourishing Jesus of Nazareth. Therefore, if the epithet Rosa had ever seemed suspect to him, it was now safe, being, as it were, divinely approved. Therefore, by frequently hearing this most pleasant address, he wishes to be reminded from time to time of both his name and his office. The humble Virgin modestly kept silent about the rest that had happened to her in that sacred Communion, and it was enough for her mother to know that the name Rose, which she had first given the infant, had pleased heaven.

[6] The infancy and childhood of Saint Catherine of Siena are said to have been so sweet and gentle to everyone [The still tender girl provides a clue.] that most people began to call her not Catherine, but Euphrosynek. The infant Rose had the same quiet, sweet, charming, and consistently serene disposition, so that she seemed to be the common joy of the entire household. The household observed that the little one never cried in her swaddling clothes, or with shrill cries had ever disturbed the silence of the house, had never cried, except that once, when her mother had taken Rosa outside while visiting a noble matron, she could not be restrained from crying until she was brought back from the other house to her own, as if even then she knew to shun and avoid public company. Henceforth, the parent resolved not to take the very placid infant with them when they had to go out.

[7] The infancy and childhood of Saint Catherine of Siena are said to have been so sweet and gentle to everyone, [A still-tender girl provides a sign] so that most people began to call her not Catherine, but Euphrosynek. [7] She was three years old when, in her most tender disposition, she began to display above her age robust examples of virtue. It happened that, as the very large chest was being closed carelessly and hastily, it accidentally caught Rosula's right thumb. The mother rushed in, alarmed, but the little daughter hid her pain, pretending to be calm and hiding her hand until, after a few days, the dead blood under her fingernail began to rot. A surgeon was summoned, and to draw out the pus that had accumulated in the injured joint, he used harsh remedies to remove almost the entire nail, and finally, with slow but forceful shears, he uprooted the remaining stub. You would have seen Rose unmoved under these torments, as if the thumb it was so often lashing with iron were not her own. John Perez de Zumeta, the surgeon, was more astonished than the others, unable to understand how so slight a maiden could have endured such steadfastness in those torments; for throughout that entire period of the most bitter treatment she had neither uttered a groan nor ever paled at the sight of the stylet, the blood, or the fresh wounds. Rosa was not yet four months old when a painful abscess developed in her ear. Thus, once again, by the surgeon's hand, and after a gentle plaster, it was finally necessary to use iron. Rosa, while the damaged part of her cartilage was being cut away, bore the wound with such strength of face and spirit that even those standing by could not look at the razor cleaving it. Blood flowed onto the shoulder, and Rosa did not recoil from her own stain.

[of future holiness and patience,] And first of all, she was patient and her spirit among her pains was fearless, strong, and calm, so that it was clear she was a rose that neither the thorns frighten, wear down, nor bring down. The mother, on the advice of an inexperienced person, dusted her lightly with a powder, but the result was a complete failure: for it, smelling of the Hermetic box, with its caustic acrimony gnawed so horribly at the affected parts that the household servants quietly noted the girl trembling all over at regular intervals. Yet this constant, most intense pain from her could not draw even the slightest groan or sign of distress, except that when her mother asked about the pain, since she could not deny it entirely, she replied that it was slight and easily borne. And so she spent the night with her mother in those torments, silent, motionless, and very still.

[9] But the anxious mother, the next day gently removing the poultice, was horrified to see her little daughter's head, from one side swollen with blisters, from the other deeply ulcerated with pits, & "O (she said) miserable one, did you spend the whole of this night in such a voracious ardor of this kind of torment?" Rosa fell silent, and when pressed she never replied to anything except that her sufferings had remained within moderation. The surgeon, when summoned, then spent forty-two consecutive days healing the wound and grafting on new skin, with a torment scarcely less severe than that with which the first night had lanced the Virgin. But even then, Rose did not lack the steadfastness to endure such a stubborn pain, a quality the surgeon doubted could be expected from her or indeed from anyone at her age. The same was true for Rosa, now six years old, whose strength was tested under the same surgeon's forceps when a bean had to be removed from deep within her nosem, finally extracted on the third attempt. You would say that this disposition was born of patience. But these were the rudiments and timely preludes of a mind about to treat its own little body as if it were another's. All that remains is to briefly touch on the rest of his childhood.

[10] [Only five years old,] to Rose, who was five and still standing on the border between childhood and infancy, the knowledge and fear of God insinuated itself in a truly marvelous way; because between games This is how it happened: her older brother was playing with her, and he (whether by accident or on purpose, it is uncertain) smeared the innocent girl's beautiful, golden hair with mud. It was a joke, and a childish cause for laughter. But this did not please the maiden, who was offended not by contempt, but by the filth. And so he interrupted the game. To whom his brother soon said, "Behold, my sister, if the offense of your disfigured hair afflicts you so seriously, know that the styled ringlets of maidens are the snares of hell, by which the unwary souls of young men are entangled and dragged down into eternal gehenna." "So suppose the hair you seem to love is hateful to God." The boy, jesting, having spoken this with a sort of oratorical gesture, repeated the game. But the words penetrated the girl's mind more deeply than was fitting for the speaker's impetuosity or her own age. There were no words, but thunder: for suddenly that most capacious mind conceived at once the immense horror of hell, grasped the vastness of eternity, and, more attentively revering the divine majesty, weighed within itself the foul misfortune of all sin.

[11] [Only five years old,] to Rose, who was five and still standing on the border between childhood and infancy, the knowledge and fear of God insinuated itself in a truly marvelous way; Finally, with an orderly procession, they then immigrated: a high estimation of God, filial fear of the Deity, concern for salvation, and the abhorrence of sin. Who would have expected so many serious things from play and mud? But under the same radio, as she progressed further, Rosa clearly recognized that God's help would be for her, necessary, and to be implored continually. From this, an immediate attempt to pray arose within him, which dictated the following short prayer: "Jesus be blessed." Jesus be with me, Amen. From that time on, this manner of praying before the Virgin became so sweet to Rose that she did not cease visiting her, even in her sleep, for the presence of God had by then completely filled her inmost senses.

[12] Finally, under this so early and sublime knowledge of God, five-year-old Rosa felt, [perpetual virginity] was more clearly drawn and prepared for the exalted disciplines of St. Catherine of Siena: for by her example she too immediately made a vow of virginity in the very dawn of her tender years, namely in the fifth year of her age; She even, later, by imitating that same Seraphic virgin, cut her hair down to the very skin, her mother unaware, so that no tuft would remain by which, contrary to her sworn vow of virginity, she might be either drawn or dragged to marriage: for she had already learned well enough from her playtime that the filth of mud could sometimes seriously threaten her tresses. Thus, this childish play of hers and the outcome of her toys were, as was marvelous, so exceedingly happy: for it is a rare stroke of grace that led Rosa, from innocent amusements, with a single, sudden step, into that maturity of judgment by which she might at once know how to appreciate God's majesty, the price of eternity, and the secret prerogative of the virgin's flower.

[13] One thing here must not be overlooked, that those who have since moderated the Virgin's conscience, [Vowed to God.] prudently conjectured that these heavenly allurements of grace had been granted to her at that first dawn of the use of reason, when, according to the more accurate theology, every person is bound to turn himself to God as his ultimate end, in proportion to his capacity. Be that as it may, for Rose it was not so much dawn as midday, while thus enlightened she blossomed and chose the path she ought to have taken. However, it was by far a greater thing that, from the path he had chosen, he never afterward deviated in the admission of any more serious fault throughout his entire life, which, as his own confessors unanimously affirmed, will become clear from what follows.

Notes.

Lima is the most famous city in the New World, which the Spaniards call in their native tongue "Ciudad de los Reyes," that is, "City of the Kings." John of Laet, book Chapter 10, West Indies 22 He describes that city in greater detail, and Francis de Echave, in his work on St. Turibius, Archbishop of Lima, which was printed in Spanish in Antwerp in 1688, presents a new ichnography of the same emporium.

b The Pacific Ocean washes New Spain and Peru. The same is true of the Southern and Australasian Sea. but extends more broadly, namely to the Southern lands, New Guinea & the Archipelago of St. Lazarus toward the Indian Ocean, as Baudrandus affirms.

c The Collector of Life alludes here to the following Ovidian poem: "They call April 'the open season.'"

Our ancestors, on April 20, illustrated the Acts of this holy Agnes, where in the second volume of that month, page 796, a miracle concerning flowers found from heaven at the site of her prayer is recounted. She was canonized by Pope Benedict.

e According to Baudrand, the rich port is a large island of North America, which is called by the Spaniards la isla de Puerto Rico, and by the inhabitants Boriquen. Indeed, the celebrated Baudrand assigns as the principal and eponymous city of this island the following words: "A North American episcopal city under the Archbishop of Saint Dominic, fortified with a double fortress and a capacious harbor on the northern part of the eponymous island in the northern region." It was captured and plundered by the English in 1595 and again by the United Provinces in 1615. It is, however, under the dominion of the Spaniards, and is also called the Church of Saint John of the Rich Port. The above-cited John Latius, book. 1 on the Indies of the West ch. 1 & 2 describe in detail the origin, location, and varied fortunes of this island and city.

f In the aforementioned plan of the city of Lima, the village of St. Dominic and the Hospital of the Holy Spirit, which was built for sick sailors, are assigned not far from each other.

g I have heard that Pentecost is commonly called Pascua del Espiritu Santo by the Spaniards. But Palm Sunday, which the French commonly call Paque-fleurie, is called by the Spaniards Pascua florida or Domingo de Ramos. We wish to learn from the natives, or from their copious dictionary not long since begun to be published, whether the festival of Pentecost is also sometimes called the Feast of Roses among them.

h Veneroni, in his Italian Lexicon, testifies that Pentecost is vernacularly called by Italians Pasqua rosata or Pasqua di Maggio. Hence the biographers' allusion to the name of St. Rose, and this epithet "rosata" is at least suitably apt in the Italian idiom.

i This venerable Archbishop of Lima died on March 23, 1606, whom Pope Innocent XI in 1679 first enrolled among the Blessed, and then Pope Benedict XIII among the Saints, as is found in the Most Illustrious Fontanini's Codex Canonizationum, page. Hence it is clear that our ancestors could not have illustrated the Acts of this holy prelate, since they had to await the judgment of the Apostolic See. This must therefore be done in the supplement to our work on April 27, the day on which he is now commemorated in the Roman Martyrology.

k The biographer here alludes to the Greek word εὐφροσύνα or, in Doric, αὐφροσύνα, which means joy. Hence, perhaps, some of the Saints who are venerated in the Catholic Church also received their names.

l Since Mercury is called Ἑρμῆς by the Greeks, the adjective Hermeticus, formed from that Greek name with a Latin inflection, appears to have been composed in this way, so that hermeticus is the same as mercurial. Since chemists, by way of metaphor, call mercury "Mercury," I suspect that here, by the odorous powders of the hermetic box, a remedy mixed with mercury is being indicated, one whose use among physicians and surgeons is frequent and already well known.

m Phaseolus, or bean, and also faselus, is a genus of legume which lexicographers understand to mean peas or beans. From the accompanying notes, I take it that this refers to a growth in the nose, which is perhaps metaphorically called a "bean" by surgeons, because it resembles a pea or a bean. Thus a cancerous growth in the nose is called a polyp because of its resemblance to the animal of the same name, as Sennertus states in volume II of his Medical Works, page 347 .

Chapter II
The holy maiden's great reverence for her parents, her wonderful eagerness to obey, and her diligent concern for them.

[While Mother was inviting St. Rose] In the dilemma between honoring one's father and mother and obeying God rather than men, it was often necessary for Rose, by her wit and prudence, to avoid offending either. The heavenly Bridegroom was drawing the Virgin he had chosen for himself along the secret paths of holiness and perfection; on the other hand, the rough and crude mother was drawing her obedient daughter to the pomps and vanities of the world, to vain adornments of the body, to the affairs of the world. At these crossroads, it was more than human skill to remain obedient on both sides, never to have deviated so much as a fingernail from the Groom's guidance, yet never to have strayed from her mother's precepts in following the Groom.

[15] It happened that in the presence of honorable matrons, Rosa, with her mother present, was more importunately asked to place the wreath of flowers on her head, which by chance, for the sake of its fragrance, had been set in the middle and pleased everyone. The modest virgin demurred shyly; the troublesome women pressed her, and the mother, wishing to please them, ordered her daughter to obey the decree. How do you suppose he struggled here with the obedience of vain, modest adornment—horror! Nevertheless, Rose's ingenuity prevailed: for she secretly fastened a long, pointed spike to the underside, and thus she did not so much place the thorn-studded wreath upon her head as press and drive it in, so that it was nothing but torture to her, though to others it seemed an ornament. He certainly obeyed his mother. but at the same time she ran toward the scent of the Spouse crowned with thorns. This noble stratagem would have remained hidden forever, had it not later been so laboriously extracted by a foreign hand—the needle that had deeply gnawed beneath a flowery blandishment. Uncertain, however, whether the needle or the circle's hated adornments pierced Rosa more deeply, she who was friendlier to thorns than to garlands.

[16] Another. The mother, with a womanly zeal, urged her daughter to polish her hands in vain and to imitate the vain customs of the world, for the sake of delicate whiteness and elegance. For this purpose he had purchased a special kind of fragrant gloves, so that with them, in a feminine manner, Rosa might at least compel her hands, during the night's wear, into the borrowed charm. You would have said that not a virgin's gloves, but a victim's frying pan and fire were being prepared; so terrified, she stiffened, groaned, and hesitated. Until, more fiercely compelled by her mother's command, she obeyed and accepted the hated task. She wished that night would never come, so that she wouldn't have to go to bed with such a bandage on her hands. However, the necessary hour for sleep arrived, the light was extinguished, and the fearful Virgin, barely tasting her first slumber, began to feel the pyrotechnica gloves she had donned. They seemed full of Mulciber; nor did they seem alone. But in reality, they burned, scorched, and roasted both hands. Rosa, inexperienced with this new frying pan, was glad to have the opportunity to free her hands from such a harmful tool. He drew it out, and saw flames shooting from the bright sheaths. At last the entire room seemed to glow, and an open fire loomed: but when these were cast far away, the heat, the flame, and the torment vanished, and Rosa, bathed from heaven in a wondrously sweet coolness, rested most sweetly. When morning came, he most placably returned the cruel gloves to his mother, laid out the whole affair in simple candor, showed his hands, which were burned in places, and earnestly begged that he might not be subjected to such punishments again. But the unbelieving mother, while suspecting her daughter's pious pretext, more curiously inspected both hands and, finding them truly blistered, scorched, and parched, as soon as she touched them she finally trembled and thereafter ceased to urge her most obedient daughter more harshly toward the same fires.

[17] [The daughter had subtly devised a plan.] Nevertheless, these were only a truce, not a sincere peace: for the mother, soon resorting to other arts, now tried to persuade her daughter to adopt a girlish style, to wear necklaces around her neck, bracelets on her arms, and ceruse on her face according to the custom of the region. now to press more strictly, so that she would at least set her beautiful hair (which was still then in its natural curl) with curling-irons, so that with linen, fringes, and with the rest of her adornment would embellish herself somewhat, graciously set off the rosy beauty of her lips with rouge and ointments, and with her bearing, her dress, and the charms of her natural grace would enhance her dignity; finally to rebuke, to terrify, to slaughter the innocent, because she was crude, uncouth, and unkempt, departing from the spirit of the age with a wanton and excessive neglect of feminine adornment; because she descended to the vilest singularity, below the lot of her peers; because she would of her own accord corrupt the hope of a wealthy and splendid marriage, to which nature and the excellence of her form were calling her, with the most abject hypocrisy, and sordidly stifled it. But the Virgin, mindful of her purpose, and more anxious to blunt her own beauty than to sharpen it, she received all this with fixed eyes on the ground in unconquerable silence, and grew accustomed to digest this daily drudgery with habitual use and deaf dissimulation, until the final violence of her mother's command was at hand, against which to rebel or to obey more slowly she deemed equally forbidden.

[18] [so that he would obey his mother,] She saw this as a convenient way to extort from him whatever she wanted. So when she had once forced upon her daughter a calantike to be worn, glittering with gold and silk netting, she added a precept to instill in her a duty of obedience to counter her resistance. This penniless virgin obtained only a brief postponement of the sudden decree while she went to her confessor, to whom she earnestly protested that unless the order were revoked, she could not but obey her mother's command to wear this adornment; she would rather bind her head with this most odious ornament than, by resisting, forfeit the duty by which she was bound to her mother's authority. A prudent confessor, perceiving the Virgin's pious devotion and the knot of her perplexity, at once came forward and earnestly dissuaded her mother, urging her to cease at last from needlessly testing the perplexed Daughter with the vain and costly burden of useless cares—the Daughter whom from infancy the divine Spirit had prepared by most secret instincts for the fitting sublimity of a higher sanctity. The mother was terrified. And so this wave, so that obedience would not waver, voluntarily subsided.

[19] The ingenious Virgin at last devised a suitable plan [so as not to offend God], by which she would henceforth both submit to her mother's authority and nevertheless free the rest of her person from vexation in its adornment. And so he besieged his mother with the most tender flatteries until he had elicited permission to take a plain, unadorned sack of coarse, undyed cloth for a cloak—a type of garment that is neither immodest nor unusual for women who, estranged from pomp and nuptials, strive under the simplest attire to pursue a solitary devotion. Thus, Rosa armed herself with this most chaste scheme, so that by its protection she might repel from herself all worldly vanity of female adornment. And the outcome did not disappoint: for under such a clay-like coating, unstained by any dye, Rosa lay hidden in the covering of her body until she reached the age of twenty, when at last, lifted up to the whiteness of the Dominican habit, she easily went on to evade the remaining snares of pompous luxury. Once, then, having thus abdicated and been excluded from the feminine world, it did not seem difficult to Rosa to obey her parents in all other matters, however arduous. I will give a single example of this paradigm, from which the rest can be inferred.

Cañab in the Peruvian kingdom is a place renowned for its metal mines, but in other respects it is extremely unhealthy because of the exhalations from its putrid soil and its bitter cold. Rosa, having been ordered here, withdrew with her parents, and not long after, unprepared for those noxious frosts, she began to suffer in her hands and feet from a dreadful strain of the nerves. The mother, thinking to drive off the force of the disease with the most immediate remedy she could find, tightly wrapped the patient daughter with the rough skins of certain animals that were common there. but with the added order that he should not, without permission, remove them from himself or in any way move them. Four days had passed when, remembering the care she had given, the mother asked her silent daughter how she was and what she had done with the bundles of skins. She replied in her illness that she was no better than before, and that the bandages, just as her mother had bound them, were still stuck to her without moving. The mother, amazed at the simplicity, while removing the films, sees the Virgin's flesh, eaten away, inflamed, and ulcerated on all sides by pustules burning with a biting heat. Then, truly astonished, she said, "Why did you endure so many days and nights under these torments, and not cast off the skins that were so dismembering you?" The obedient daughter replied, "And did you not, mother, wisely forbid me to put them down without your permission?" I obeyed.

[21] At one point, the mother, to test her daughter's readiness for such exacting obedience, [with remarkable simplicity and reverence] commanded her to shape the silk flowers she was then painting with a needle by running the threads in reverse, against all the rules of the art and the proper order. Rosa obeyed. He twisted the work, and completed the flowers. Then the mother, as if scolding, said, "Come on, not flowers, but monsters of flowers." What did you do? "Did you doze off, sleepyhead, while your unskilled laziness was producing such hideous nonsense?" To this she replied very meekly, and indeed to herself, that although untrained, she had found this way of painting utterly foreign and misguided to the town, yet she had not dared to guide the needle and thread any other way than her mother had prescribed, nor to attempt anything on her own in the opposite manner. She nevertheless declared herself most ready to sew or to undo whatever her mother commanded. Whenever she had to return during the day to the tasks of spinning, sewing, or weaving, she never took anything from the chest but always, with her hand outstretched, asked her parent for the needle, the threads, the shuttle, and all the other necessities of the craft, believing that such things were owed to a daughter's reverence for her mother. But deeming this importunity intolerable, she would sometimes, with bile and nausea, send her daughter back to the women's domestic workshop, shouting that there was no need for so many interruptions, since the tools of the household trade were freely available to women willing to work. To whom does Rosa belong? "I would rather," he said, "obey, add the profit of explicit obedience to my duty, and, together with my stipend, pay a humble tribute of filial respect." He certainly valued even the slightest opportunities to obey.

[22] [She obeyed her mother] About three years before the blessed death of Rose, it happened that she was invited by the illustrious matron D. Maria de Usategui, wife of the royal treasurer D. Gundisalvi de la Massa, from her father's home to a free lodging in her own house. The virgin complied, because her parents were ordering her—indeed, commanding her to be obedient in all things to Gundisalvo and his wife. Thus the occasion to obey was not changed but doubled: for humble Rosa, though she considered herself free from the paternal roof, did not deem herself exempt from submission; with the same promptness with which she obeyed in her own home, she obeyed in a foreign one, not only the commands of Gundisalvus or his wife, but also at the beck of their daughters and of the household staff, down to the lowest servants of the house. You have a prime example of this below the chapter. 5. He was testifying. Where you will see Rosa, at the very point of death, obedient to Gundisalvo.

[23] [and other preceding ones] But obedience beyond death is far more marvelous. After the Virgin's death, a little handmaid at the Lima monastery of St. Catherine had carelessly lost a silver spoon. The foundress, who was also the abbess at the time, had ordered every corner to be searched, the chests to be ransacked, and everything to be gone through, in case the lost pledge could be found. Seeking everywhere and for a long time, anxiety doubles with the search, everything is disturbed, nothing is found. He was now troubled not by money but by his reputation: for there was a danger that some innocent person might rashly be suspected of theft. And so, Prefect Lucia of the Holy Trinity, turning to the portrait of the late Rosa hanging there, broke out amid a great tumult of thoughts into these words: "Hear me, blessed Rosa, I command you, by the obedience owed to me in this convent by all, to have the silver we lost found." "I swear to you in holy truth, O Rosa, it will be your duty to see that a spoon is ready by the time I return from the evening service." She returned, searched, and looked around. And behold, the spoon was openly on the table in the cell, where it had been searched ten times. Indeed, the reputation of Rose, now deceased, for obedience had become so celebrated among all, the presumption so lofty, that it was believed she would even in death obey a superior to whom she had never been subject in life, and it seemed enough to command the very image that had once dwelt there.

[24] [and to her confessors.] From this anyone could easily see how precisely Rosa had obeyed her confessors up to this point: for ever since she had taken the habit of St. Dominic, she hung entirely on the words of her spiritual fathers, and whatever they even hinted at seemed not a command to the Virgin but a heavenly oracle. Once, after prolonged floods of tears, he languished from a headache not without danger, nor was he soothed by his usual vigils. The confessor, having been admonished, commanded him to devote at least four hours to sleep, beginning at midnight. The Virgin tried to obey to the letter. But being accustomed to a different routine, she could not meet the appointed hour, whether by sleeping or by staying awake, and that alone was enough to torment her with a severe pang of conscience, as if she were guilty of disobedience. They noted this spirit of domestic obedience in Rosa, and whenever she was asked to apply fomentations to her little body, or to take rest, or to allow herself in any other way to be treated more indulgently, they would say that her confessor had so commanded, and at once Rosa, without a word, obeyed in a thoroughly childlike manner.

But let us return to the mother. That Rosa was accustomed to postpone her drinking for three or four days at a time [nevertheless observing her mother's slightest wishes], was evident from the fact that she had imposed a law upon herself, while living with her parents, to never drink unless her mother, when asked for permission, had expressly consented. For if permission to test her strength was denied, she would usually stave off her thirst for another three days, not drinking until her mother gave her leave. Indeed, she complains that her mother does not deny her this permission very often. Furthermore, history cannot adequately capture the rest of his respect, love, and piety toward his parents: how diligently he always worked to provide for his parents' needs through the labor of his own hands. how officious she was, ministering to them from their sickbeds, staying by their side, and procuring medicines and other necessities; how attentive and diligent she was, in order to forestall their domestic quarrels, soothe their spirits, and mitigate their afflictions, is testified by nearly every page of the sworn deposition.

[26] Quaestor Gundisalvus (of whom mention is made above, and more frequently below) marveled in silence that Rosa, though drained of strength, [with tireless labor of her hands,] and utterly weary from her daily tasks, nevertheless persisted in manual work until the middle of the night. The Questor's wife, once she saw her frail form beginning to collapse and her entire side, weak and numb, scarcely able to draw breath, earnestly urged her to grant her failing little body at least a brief respite from those labors. The industrious virgin replied that she could not, without scruple, fail to attend to her parents' needs.

[27] More marvelous is the fact that Ludovica Urtado de Bustamante, [by her diligent industry] widow of Bartholomew Alphonsus de Lombrera, standard-bearer, affirms in the Process, that Rosa was so occupied with holy exercises day and night, so impeded by a multitude of illnesses, so often absorbed in heavenly raptures, yet accustomed to complete in a single day so many tasks that any other, even the swiftest and most skilled, would have scarcely finished in four days: and lest we suppose that, because of Rose's great haste, her works were (as is often the case) produced hollow, incomplete, and imperfect; The sixty-eighth witness, above the others, affirms that those particulars were so consummate in perfection, elegance, and care that they generally seemed to exceed the limits of human art and industry.

[28] Nor did the anxious love and concern for his daughter stop here; he was trying with all his ingenuity to relieve his parents' poverty. In the home garden, she had divided the beds for the violets she tended with her own hands, and when they were ripe she would gather them in bunches and give them to the maids to be sold. He brought whatever little he had to his mother. When once asked by a religious man (the twenty-first witness) how much benefit her parents received from this meager trade in flowers, she replied that it was indeed very small, unless the heavenly Spouse supplied it from elsewhere by wondrous, yet mysterious, means.

[29] [she helped the sick with care,]If at any time her father or mother was laid low by illness, nothing was more important, more sacred, or more solemn for the daughter than, setting all else aside, to serve the invalid, to seek out medicines, to prepare them with her own hands, to administer them, to bring food in small portions, to provide cooling remedies, and to apply soothing lotions. Then he would spend sleepless nights, sit on his bed, make the bed, do everything—omitting nothing that could bring comfort. Meanwhile, he knocked at the gates of heaven with prayers, groans, and sighs. Nor in vain. But this is more appropriately reserved for the history of miracles. That single act of the most singular piety toward his mother, and the most extreme example of compassion, should by no means be omitted here. In the very last article of her life, now about to flee, Rosa, seeing her suffering mother beside her, and not unaware how terribly the impending blow of her daughter's death would overwhelm her, begged her Spouse—and this was the dying woman's last breath—to spare her mother, who was not equal to such excessive sorrow, and easily succumbing, would strengthen her with robust consolation, and, with a sweet and mighty hand, harden the poor mother for this fatal blow.

[30] [& consolated the afflicted.]It happened, and more abundantly indeed, than the solicitous piety of the dying Daughter had demanded: for as soon as the Virgin yielded up her spirit, such a flood of consolations from on high inundated the mother, that she confessed under oath, her heart had long laboured to contain this sweetness, under a certain tempest of joys, and as if her chest were about to burst, she leapt about with gasping, triumphant leaps, so that in the very same chamber of the dying Rose she had to seek a more secluded corner, in which, under the most exquisite tearing-asunder of such great joy, she could breathe more freely. But when he was summoned into the adjoining room—whether to remove himself from the sad spectacle of the corpse or to go with others to see Aloysia, who at that moment was perhaps rigid in ecstasy—he delayed, so as not to desert his corner, aware of so great a jubilation, too soon. Never, indeed, did Rose's most dutiful piety care for her mother more solicitously or more sumptuously.

The biographer elsewhere frequently employs other such allusions and antitheses, which the learned reader will easily observe, although he is not expressly warned about them.

Chapter Three
Imitation of Blessed Catherine of Siena, and entry into the Third Order of Saint Dominic

[While St. Rose refused to enter into marriage,] Various and countless are the ways, the winding paths, the journeys by which the eternal Wisdom leads chosen souls to struggle up to the lofty summit of perfection. But it pleased her to lead the chosen Rose of St. Mary in a special way through the seraphic compendia of Catherine. when human prudence (often an inept judge of spirits) was most insistently trying to divert the Virgin to other paths of calling. At first, the mother, inflated with not unreasonable hope, had already destined her tender little daughter for a noble and most honorable marriage; nor did the outstanding beauty of her form, her keen judgment, the sweetness of her upright disposition, and her manners—both then and thereafter most polished in every grace of civility—deter her from her purpose.

On the contrary, the innocent Rosa believed that by the timely (as mentioned above) cutting of her hair, [which her mother had destined for her,] she had, like St. Catherine of Siena of old, cut off her mother's hope and the snares of marriage. Meanwhile, she was no less diligent in using fasts to wear away the rosy beauty of her face. to deform her exceptional figure with a shapeless sack, to shun public view, and, so as not to please people's gaze, to hide at home; so that during the entire four years she lived with her parents in Kent, she neither set foot beyond the threshold of her home nor deigned to see her neighbor's pleasure garden, except that on a single occasion she was ordered to accompany the matrons' daughters there to visit the mills of the Indian works. Wherever she arrived, she always sat motionless in a single corner, never carried away by the spectacle of the famed curiosity. Yet even so she could not escape, but would kindle admiration for herself in the minds of many, both by her then celebrated reputation for virtue and by the majesty of her modest countenance, and would soon, against her will, set aflame the circle of her honorable nuptials.

[33] In these circumstances a certain matron, distinguished by her birth and wealth, was earnestly urging her one and only son to take Rosam as his wife, for she believed that by this happy alliance she would acquire more goods than she would give up with her son the heir. Both sides were pleased with the generous terms, especially since Rosa's parents, though modest in means, were heavy-handed in the number of their children—for they numbered eleven. Only the Virgin's consent was lacking, whom any mention of marriage, mindful of her vow, terrified. Yet she did not dare to reveal to her austere mother the impediment that held her against her will. Hence, no one has more closely understood or appreciated the insults, the domestic quarrels, the slanders, and the abuse that the Daughter, meek but unarmed, endured from her parent, brothers, and kinsmen, than whoever has looked into the history of Saint Catherine of Siena, where Lapa, the mother, cruelly reproves, presses, and agitates the tenacious virgin of her purpose, were it not that against Rosa, beyond words, even beatings, kicks, and slaps have raged. Nevertheless, the idea of her Seraphic mistress (as she had grown accustomed to calling the Virgin of Siena) prevailed in the mind of the most steadfast maiden—one she had chosen from her earliest years to imitate in bearing, dress, and manners, as the sixty-eighth witness clearly affirmed in the Process.

[34] [and in the new convent of St. Clare] It seemed that everything conspired to prevent Rose from obtaining the habit of the Tertiaries, which she so earnestly desired, and for which she was entreating with so many vows, so many sighs, and at the cost of so many tears: for behold, another path to the religious life was offered, which indeed could in no way have displeased Rosa, had it not been divinely destined solely for the high footsteps of St. Catherine of Siena. In those days the new monastery of St. Clare was being erected in Lima, a sanctuary of all holiness, rigor, and observance, under the auspices of the most illustrious Lady Mary of Quinnones, whose uncle was that very renowned servant of God, the then surviving Archbishop of Lima, Don Turibius Alphonsus Mogrovejo, of whom mention has been made above. Nor are the admirable sanctity, pastoral zeal, and numerous miracles of this incomparable man unknown in Rome at the Sacred Congregation of Rites, where his apotheosis has long been vigorously debated.

[35] [place offered;] With these auspices, I say, the new monastery was being founded, and Rosa—already chosen by name, consecrated, and approved as one of its foremost foundresses—was invited of her own accord as if to the most worthy planting ground for such a flower. When she learned this for certain, she first and foremost sought to discern the divine will's good pleasure, and she commended the mystery of her vocation to the supreme Arbiter, not refusing, for the sake of the asylum of her virginity and her desire for a stricter life, to embrace the most strict enclosure of the offered convent and the arduous way of living. Above all, she had already learned from experience that she could scarcely escape the importunate annoyances of suitors, the advances of parents, brothers, and household servants, and the sight of the filthy world. But behold, the mother was an obstacle to the secret of divine providence. This was thought to be the first thing everyone would agree to, either out of reverence for such a great archbishop, or because, being burdened with children, she could most fittingly provide a dowryless daughter. However, she objected, citing the household's poverty, which Rosa supported by her labor. Also standing in the way was the grandmother, who, worn out by age and illness, had as her only support a bed and constant care. At last, other obstacles arose, by the providence of God, who was reserving this incomparable Rose for the flourishing garden of Saint Dominic through so many twists and turns.

[36] [but by divine will,] For this to become more widely known, two prodigies from heaven were required; First, this happened in this order. Those who had seen Rosa so eager for heavenly things, so devoted to spiritual exercises, solitude, and the mortification of the body, and so weary of the world, repeatedly urged her to enter a monastery, where she could more freely devote herself to God. The confessors themselves at last approved it; men of standing and distinguished in the spiritual life urged it on, and, with the grandmother aware of it and consenting, Rosa humbly submitted to their unanimous judgment and sought success through her brother, meanwhile entrusting herself to the divine will (as above). Now in the Holy Scriptures At the Monastery of the Incarnation (which follows the Rule of Saint Augustine), the nuns, eager for a rose, had by the brothers' diligence secured her admission and a place; and nothing remained but for her to leave her father's house and secretly, without pomp or noise, make her way to the convent, to be admitted through its open gates and planted among the white lilies there. Therefore, having chosen the next Sunday for this pious theft of herself, she sets out on the road, taking her only brother as her most joyful companion, and makes for St. Passing by the neighboring church of St. Dominic, she turned into it with her brother, so that there in the chapel of the Virgin (which is named and venerated as the Rosary) she might presume to receive the maternal blessing of the most august Mother of God as a fitting viaticum for her journey, and from there would more eagerly complete the rest of the way.

[37] However, she had scarcely knelt on the ground for the prayer, [which is said to have been declared], when she felt herself nailed immovably to the floor. Thus he interrupted the brother who was lingering and, beyond the appointed time, dawdling morosely, saying that it was time to go and that there would be more convenient space in the monastery for a longer prayer. But Rosa, since she could by no means move from her place, blushing and at the same time dissembling, would from time to time try to see if she could somehow explain herself by the most secret restraint of such a prodigy. Meanwhile, having turned back twice or three times from the church gate, the brother rebuked the hesitating man, exaggerated the danger of delay, and seized his arm as he tried to rise. They both struggled, but to no avail. A rock you would call firmly fixed to the ground, or a huge mass of lead, to be lifted by no power of pulleys. Rosa understood the mystery: that by the decree of the heavenly Spouse it was not that she should secretly hide herself in the most populous monastery's eagerly sought refuge, but that something else of her order's institution had been provided for in the secret counsel of eternity. In short, the essence of the matter was that only the footsteps of Catherine of Siena awaited Rosa to be enclosed among thorns. Therefore, being motionless, she turned to the most merciful Queen of the Holy Rosary; "I promise you," she said, "that I will return straight from here to my mother and, as long as you command, will regard the family home as my ascetic cell." How strange! Suddenly she who had been lead felt herself a feather; unassisted she rose, returned home, ingenuously revealed the matter to her mother just as it was, and in exchange for the monastic life chose another form of enclosure (which chapter… X is referred to) concerning the parent's consent.

[38] Another prodigy. The Limian plain produces butterflies as well as parrots, [by a double miracle] truly marvelous in the variety and elegance of their colors, so that they almost seem to rival one another in glory. Chance had Rosa, more attentively fixed on her Seraphic mother in her two-colored habit, undecidedly deliberating with herself whether to take the habit of the Dominican Order. when one of those butterflies, most beautifully striped in black and white, fluttered playfully around the Virgin with a gentle, yet by no means accidental, flight. She was immediately seized by ecstasy and clearly understood what she had long pondered in her mind: that it was a divine response concerning the third habit of the Order of Preachers, which was finally to be assumed.

[39] Nor did the outcome disappoint her now that she was secure: for a little later, [she resolved to imitate St. Catherine of Siena,] with all obstacles removed, she fulfilled her vow and, in the Rosary chapel of the Blessed Virgin, where she had once been held motionless, by solemn ceremony, from the hands of Father Master F. From the hands of Father Master F. Alphonsus Velasquez, her then confessor of the Order of Preachers (to whom the Provincial Father had entrusted the task), she was clothed in the two-colored habit which she had loved in her Sienese mistress. This happened in the year 1606, on the very day of the feast of St. Lawrence the Martyr, to which you might not inappropriately apply that passage from Tibullus, Book. two elegies 6: The laurel has given good omens; rejoice. …Indeed, Rosa rejoiced to see herself surrounded by a variety of things. and burned with new fires for Laurentio; She was dissolved in sweet joy, and the new Tertiary considered herself blessed, not in name only, but in reality.

[40] Indeed, this desire had grown with her from infancy: for from the age of five she had already consecrated herself to St. Catherine of Siena, [whose example,] as the second and twenty-second witnesses in the aforementioned Process repeatedly affirm, they learned from Rose's own mouth. But when she heard the Seraphic history of that same life read at home by chapters, she listened with the utmost attention, as if it were being read to her alone, and felt herself kindled to the marrow to follow in its footsteps, not to rest until she conformed herself, both outwardly in her dress and inwardly in her golden fringes, to the example of so great a heroine and teacher. But if it was exceedingly arduous for Rose, at the age of twenty, to struggle through so many obstacles to the sacred habit, it was scarcely any less a struggle for her to keep it until her death. Yet it held back, though sorely tempted to take another: for human prudence, ignorant of the divine counsel, did not cease to urge, offer, and thrust forward various things as if they were better and more worthy. Of these, I will relate two that most heavily weighed upon her steadfastness.

[After overcoming various difficulties,] Quaestor Gundisalvus, a most excellent man, who had taken refuge with the Virgin,[41] [After overcoming various difficulties,] Questor Gundisalvus, a most excellent man, who, with the Virgin entrusted to his care, had authority—nay, even domestic sway—whether of his own accord or induced by others, proposed a reformed rule for the sacred Carmel, which he believed more aptly suited to the genius of the Rose; It was imminent, and of course more fitting in the famous parthenon of the Barefoot Virgins than out in the open, where she was to become a Tertiary for God, devote herself to the spirit, and immerse herself in contemplation. But when he lacked the necessary endowment, the treasurer would reply that he was entrusting this entire matter to him and would do everything to secure his admission to the most devout monastery without difficulty. He was a mighty ram for smiting Rose's heart, especially since the mother herself, who was already content in her straitened circumstances at home, was also pleased, and the designs of many servants of God were conspiring. Rosa, though tenacious and certain of her calling, nevertheless refused to appear conceited or to openly scorn the favorable judgments of so many experts with an immodest air. And so, as she had done on other occasions, she entrusted the matter to the examination of four of the most eminent theologians selected from the Order of Preachers, promising to embrace whatever their majority vote might decide. being secure, she would not permit God to be drawn by the calculations of these men from the chosen path of a humbler institution, which the Seraphic Mother of Siena had most happily trodden.

[42] [And mature deliberation,] The outcome answered the vow: for, with the Deity at work, the quartet of the aforementioned theologians hung in equal balance on both sides, ever motionless, and marveled that neither side could find the weight by which they might at last break away from such a steadfast equilibrium. Thus the virgin's steadfastness prevailed, and she, now more confident, interrupted Gundisalvus and the other suitors with a modest and serious reply. that indeed the Carmelite deserts, offered to the reflowering, are wonderfully both to be taken and to be affected, yet that this work and office is proper to divine, not human, inspiration: that the Spirit blows where He wills, and that the apt choice and change of the regular state is not of the will of the one who wills, nor of the one who runs, but of the merciful God. that he had not, in fact, received any other divine impulses so far, apart from those he professed by his conduct; that they must live and die under the dear habit and teaching of St. Catherine of Siena, yet that a time will come when a new monastery of this name and institute will arise in Lima; but he does not yet know whether perhaps it is divinely reserved for him. Surely Carmel was worthy of this rose, and the rose worthy of Carmel, had not a loftier zephyr commended it to the gardener of the Order of the Rosary.

[43] Therefore, having rammed this one, another remained. but the most importunate of all, because the most subtle: for through the very narrow passages of humility, hellishly driven, it shook Rose with a single tremor of scruples, which opened the way to the most fearful thought, [the Third Order of St. Dominic] namely, that she was a filthy sinner, far too unworthy of that snowy veil of Catherine of Siena; that more was promised by the garment's whiteness outwardly than was hidden within; that she lacked everything that would correspond to its external splendor: Therefore, the habit, which for the Seraphic mistress had been a protestative symbol of virtue and religion, was in herself nothing but a mask and a public lie of assumed sanctity. It doubled the anxiety, that the white garment seemed, to human applause, to offer a handle and an entrance of its own accord. For the Virgin, averse to the growing fame of her name, was distressed that from her white habit she was recognized in public by other women, that from afar people would point their fingers at her as she approached, that she was noted by their eyes, praised by passersby, and that she herself was sometimes compared to St. Catherine of Siena by those she met. These were torments that could make her sacred habit hateful to her, and at first suggested it should be shyly hidden, then finally set aside, to the point that her familiar refuge (I mean the chapel of the Holy Rosary, in which Rosa had taken her habit) she ran to it, as if to be there once again rooted in an immovable firmness and, by the usual protection of the great Mother of God, to drive off the most troublesome temptation.

It happened, as he had hoped; For, with his knees fixed on the ground before the altar, he finally embraced it. While praying, he was gradually overcome by a most peaceful faintness of mind. The Sisters of the Third Order, who were standing by, noticed the supplicant's face and saw Rosa hanging for a long time before the sacred image with a devout gaze; at first she turned pale with a snowy pallor, then after a while she flushed with a lovely blush, and finally her whole face shone and sparkled with rays. They suddenly conjectured that something very great was being enacted, as if under the rainbow of the Rosary, whose mysteries are distinguished by this triple tint. And indeed it was so: for Rosa, her own color and the genuine hue of her face at last restored, broke into a cry of victory: "Come, sisters," she said, "we must praise God, who deigns to bind us Tertiary Sisters to Himself by the common, steadfast bond of charity, and to unite us forever." They grasped the obscure enigma with great ease, as many as had been aware of the previous conflicts. But for the Virgin Pæana, these were the vanquished temptations, which she so crushed that they never returned. It cost so much for the victor Rosa to obtain and most steadfastly retain the white seraphic habit of Catherine. Furthermore, how fully she had put on the virtues of her Master (though we shall reserve that for the following chapters), let this be the crowning point of this chapter: that once, when the confessor was silently observing and marveling, Rosa appeared to him, in form, countenance, and features, so transformed that in her living likeness she bore the very image of St. Catherine of Siena.
