Chapter IV
True humility of spirit, kindness, affability, and other virtues.

In her acknowledgment of her own nothingness, how profoundly and unceasingly the Rose remained occupied, the very action of her humblest life proclaims. Here we will briefly summarize a few of the many. It seemed to her that it was not enough that at home, having forgotten her daughter, she occupied herself with the vilest household tasks, unless she also humbled herself below a slave. A certain Mariana was serving in Rose's father's house; she was of Indian descent and of humble origin. Rosa often prayed, implored, and compelled her in the secret part of the house to lie down on the ground, now prone, now supine, and to trample her with her feet, and as if she were a contemptible rag, to diligently wear her away with her soles. He was even demanding that spittle, kicks, and beatings be added, and that nothing be omitted which smacked of the vilest contempt. But if Mariana tried to be sparing, or, only in passing, to carry out the orders, Rosa, as a supplicant, pressed her more sharply. He would not rise from the ground unless he had fulfilled his vow. Whenever she was assailed with insults by her brothers or mother on account of the singularity of her life, she believed herself to be so worthy of them, nay, and of ten times more grievous ones, that she would even of her own accord magnify the faults imputed to her, and increase her own guilt, lest besides, or be seen as suffering and being despised beyond her deserts, which she so eagerly sought to do everywhere.

[46] He attributed his illnesses to his own sins. He had grown accustomed to hiding the severity of his ailments (which he endured in his sick, emaciated little body, frequent and most severe) so that no one would come to his aid. But when, from too much or the tearing of convulsions, or when they could no longer be concealed by the dangerous violence of her symptoms, she would frankly confess how grievously she was tormented, so that from the immensity of her sufferings men might judge how enormously sinful and guilty Rosa was before God, who, it was said, had justly deserved to be continually scourged by the wrathful Deity for so many crimes. She believed this of herself, and she was also desperately eager for others to believe it. Hence it was that she often used such timid expressions in the presence of her intimates and family. He marvels that God has not yet plunged the wretched world, which bore so great a sinner, into a horrible abyss. Likewise, by his own demerits, he deserves in the underworld the place that is the lowest of all and most infamous: the vilest burden of the earth, an abominable cancer of the human race, a purulent sewer and dregs, unworthy of the light of heaven, of the breath of air, or of the earth's sustaining back: that by his contagion the elements are polluted, the soil burdened by his crimes, and the ocean made bitter. Whenever something unfortunate happened, whether at home or abroad, he did not hesitate to blame it on his own crimes. And though he felt and said these things so earnestly, he could not bear that those who knew his innocence would refuse to believe such things. He would have been more distressed if they had replied that such things were said out of mere humility. He was guided by this: that he should trust himself, who alone knew himself.

[47] But if they went so far as to oppose the praises and lauds of Rosa with refutations of such criticisms,[she abhorred her own praises,] the poor wretch was terrified, and as if struck by lightning she trembled, protesting most mournfully with pallor, silence, and groans that she was being tormented. It happened that Michael Garzes, the Canon, would familiarly visit Gundisalvus the Quaestor at his home. Here he, among his friends, as is customary, straying into various topics, began to recount perfunctorily those things which extolled to Rosa (then living in the same house) the marvelous sanctity of life, the discipline of the body, and the excellence of her deeds and manners. Rosa happened to overhear this conversation from the next room, but unable to bear the nausea of her own praises, she leapt up in pain and rushed into the inner chamber where the questor's little daughter, Michaela, was. There, with a most free outcry, and with tears bitterly blaming his own lot, he beat his innocent breast, despised the dregs of his own praise, and was blinded by his own shame. And so, to drive out and avenge the torment with torment, he struck his head more frequently with his fist, where the crown of thorns was denser with its spikes (of which, below, chapter). VIII) had secretly besieged the times. How was she wounded by the very praise she believed would be met with delight by her spirit—contempt, slanders, insults? But let's go on.

[48] Rosa had attempted a certain exceedingly arduous work of heroic virtue, in imitation of St. Catherine of Siena, [an innocent woman reproved, she begged for pardon,] and had accomplished it: which, believing it to be exceedingly harmful and fatal to her health, Lady Isabella Mexia, moved by pious horror, She admonished Father Master Friar Alphonsus Velasquez, the Virgin's confessor, to disapprove of the act with a harsh reprimand, as a rash excess, and to deter Rosa from attempting similar things beyond her strength. He reproached the Virgin, since he had not yet, from the circumstances at the time, understood what praise she might more fittingly have deserved. But the humble Rose, as if acknowledging a fault in the most sacred work, begged for forgiveness, promised to amend, and was glad to be rebuked there where she had most feared applause and vain glory.

Whenever he fell at the feet of the confessors to accuse himself, [he exaggerated his slightest failings before them,] she was so soon overwhelmed with tears, broken by sobs, and rent by deep sighs from the bottom of her heart, that you would have believed she was guilty of nothing less than enormous crimes, for which she wept so bitterly. She appeared like another sinner in the city, was ashamed, beat her breast, and trembled as if about to be swallowed by the gaping maw of hell. Yet even in such a display of the most humble contrition, there was not always sufficient matter in which the sentence of sacramental absolution could be safely pronounced. Certainly, the confessors later admitted in unison that they had had to work repeatedly so that, in the purity of such an innocent life, by scrutinizing they might find something that clearly pertained to the tribunal of penance. Meanwhile, Rosa was no less hard at work, magnifying every little flaw to an enormous degree and earnestly demanding a remedy with serious tears and groans. Not content with these, besides the frequent weekly confessions, she had also imposed upon herself a daily confession before her holy Father Dominic, in which, as if in a chapter of correction, she minutely laid open her faults to him and humbly asked for pardon and a remedy from his counsel.

Maria Usatigui, because Rosa had lived in her house for three years, considered herself so inferior to everyone that she submitted to the wills of not only the head of the household and her husband, but also to her tender daughters, indeed, subject to the will of servants, maids, and household slaves, she received everyone's commands as if they were orders, believing she had favor whenever anything was commanded of her as a mistress, as if she had been taken into the house on sufferance; From which, for a small drink of water, he would, with knees bent, beg Gundisalvo (if he happened to be there) for the opportunity to taste. He noted the great and lengthy things concerning the most gentle humility of Rose, and he ordered them to be read in Gundisalvi's house. Let this last one suffice. She lay near death, when in the final quarter-hour of her life a precious liquid was offered to her from a chalice to comfort her heart, to be sipped. The sick woman refused, saying she could not; but when she heard that her master himself had commanded, she remembered her duty and obeyed. She took it, drank it down, and in a faint voice added: "Go, tell my lord Gundisalvus that I, what I truly could not, was able to do at his command, and that even at the very gates of death I was not forgetful of what a subject owes her hero." With what humility she then turned to those present, asking that whatever she might have wronged in that household or family—whether by a bad example or by gloomy and ungracious conduct—be forgiven her, provided it did not require a longer period than was necessary to attend to the remaining matters.

[51][Besides her bodily charms,] For a long time Rosa was unaware that she had anything left in herself worthy of praise or that could be considered beautiful, until a certain woman, having casually caught sight of the Virgin's hands (for women's nature is keen and remarkably observant of such things), commended their whiteness, grace, and proportion. The Virgin recoiled at the unexpected vanity of the little herald, and, seizing a live coal, horribly disfigured both her hands, until the most biting heat split the skin on every side into wrinkles, and it swelled in scattered blisters, with such pain and damage that for thirty days and more Rosa could not manage to dress herself. Hence a Marian servant had to be summoned to her aid; she, however, after the Virgin's death, revealed the entire matter, as it had transpired, which Rose's humility (like many other greater things) had, by custom, concealed.

[52] [the austerity of fasts,] The humble Virgin had long sought by every means to erase the native beauty of her most lovely face, so that it might not please mortal eyes, and this not only by constant fasting and dire austerities, but also by frequently pouring cold water over her entire body; She seemed already to have regained consciousness, while on her bloodless face, apart from a dull pallor and a cavernous emaciation, nothing was visible. But when he discovered that, by these gradual clues, the austerity of his fasts was being detected, judged, and extolled by the curious—deeming he had more to fear from the moth of vain glory and praise than from the splendor of an exceptional form—he resorted to his customary refuge of prayer, and with anxious prayers he implored God to grant him a face that would at least not betray to mortal eyes the rigor of his abstinence and the many outward signs of his fasts. How strange! Soon a vivid color returned to her cheeks, the fuller roundness of her face was restored, a glow to her brow, vigor to her eyes, so that one could almost swear that Rosa was utterly ignorant of what fasting is.

[53] Hence a charming incident befell certain Argives who, out of idle curiosity, were chatting in the temple vestibule and saw Rosa returning home from church with her mother.</s4> who, while chatting in the temple vestibule on the Friday of Holy Week, had seen Rosa returning home from church with her mother. The Virgin had spent the entire Lenten season on nothing but bread and water, but especially in those last days of Holy Week she had barely allowed herself a morsel of food. Moreover, from early morning on the preceding Thursday (named from the Lord's Supper), it had remained motionless in the same St. until noon on this Friday. Dominic, in the temple of the same S. Dominic, had kept vigil before the most sacred Host, then, according to custom, solemnly placed in the little shrine of the Holy Sepulchre, and had taken neither a morsel nor a drop of water during that entire interval of some thirty hours. Therefore, when the above-mentioned comrades saw her returning home with her mother, her face rosy like the dawn, her appearance lively, charming, and by no means pale, they rashly suspected that the Virgin was coming from a feast and a sumptuous banquet. and loudly and freely, joking to a passerby, "Behold (they say) a nun richly dressed!" Behold, how splendidly she shows on her face that she has been sumptuously feasted on cakes by someone today! "Do the Blessed fast like this?" The mother was displeased by the rash, inept criticism; but humble Rosa was strangely pleased that her fasts should thus remain hidden, even to the point of such a ridiculous charge of gluttony and gorging. It was a thorn that would not harm Rose, but delight her.

[54] He had the same concern and care for concealing the remaining treasures of his charisms, [and other virtues], so that they would not fall prey to human praise. Hence it came about that most of her illustrious works, visions, and the secret delights of the Spirit were so concealed under the obscure veil of silent humility that the constant vigilance of her mother, companions, and confessors could scarcely ever penetrate to the knowledge of a few. A certain person, exceedingly celebrated for her spirit and piety, burned with a long-standing desire to explore more deeply the wondrous works of divine grace, which could no longer be hidden in the Rose's meadow. And so, having despaired of every other catoptroscope, he, by the authority of the Virgin's confessor, which he wielded, compelled her to herself, through the winding, oblique detours of the question, extract whatever she could, even from an unwilling source.

[55] He refused for a long time; the confessor hesitated for a long time, not unaware that he was hiding it [carefully], how arduous it would be for him. Finally, seizing the opportunity, he skirted around the Virgin with veiled questions. and she herself, having most swiftly anticipated their purpose, seized the inquiry, and with these most gentle words put an end to the disturbance: "Know, my Father," she said, "that from my earliest years I have ardently prayed to God not to allow any mortal to learn of it, should He perhaps begin to work within the innermost recesses of my little soul by His highest mercy." The Inspector of Hearts granted my wish. Therefore, stop scrutinizing, and do not weary yourself and me with a useless effort. "Let the minister not take away the benefit of this secret, because the Lord has granted it." Nevertheless, whenever, under the heavy burden of conscience, he did so—whether at the hands of confessors or of Doctor John de Castillo (as below in the chapter). XII) The spirit was subjected to an examination, discovered great and wonderful things, because it had to; However, when questioned further, he was cautious and sparing in his words, weighing his replies. Therefore, what she then modestly and reluctantly revealed, I would consider very few and almost commonplace, if compared to those things that Rosa kept sealed under the divine benefit of secrecy and the seal of humility.

[56] [& He had the Virgin Mary as an assistant in this humility,] The Virgin Mary wonderfully favored this virtue, the model of the humble; For she herself helped Rosa to humbly conceal the instruments of her mortification. This is how things were. She had gone to the Church of St. Dominic to pray as was her custom when a sudden recollection of one of the various devices she had adapted to subdue the little body—which she had inadvertently left at home in a conspicuous spot—struck her, where it could easily be seen by any of the household entering the absent one's chamber. The Virgin paled with confusion, fearing that this sign would reveal how devoted she was to penitential exercises. Immediately, therefore, she turned in supplication to the Virgin Mother and anxiously begged her to come to the rescue in the peril and to conceal the very instrument in another of the places she had in mind. Fear vanished at once, terror fled, and Rosa, returning home from the sermon, found her instrument not where she had left it, but where the Mother of Grace had insisted it be kept.

[57] [which he combined with gentle affability.][57] Such great humility was inseparably accompanied by the sweetest meekness, placid affability, and a gentle brow: all who knew her were amazed that not a single harsh, boisterous, or critical word ever escaped her lips; never a word of boasting, arrogance, or ostentation; nothing in his manners or movements appeared grim or gloomy, which would have a sour austerity. Entirely serene, courteous, and kind, she was unaware of her own high-mindedness, but only of her submission to every human being. You would have denied that the rose was, which was so devoid of thorns. But bear with me: for she was all thorns in herself, which the next four chapters will teach at length, through the varied discipline of her own body.

Notes.

These curious observers of St. Rose are tropically called Argus, alluding to Argus, the keeper of Inachus's daughter, whom poets fabled to have been endowed with many eyes, and who was therefore called by the Greeks panoptes, that is, all-seeing, is nicknamed. Hence that Ovidian poem: Argus had a head surrounded by a hundred eyes.

The Greek name κατάσκοποσ in Latin means speculator, or explorer. Hence the holy apostle Paul in the Epistle to the Hebrews, chapter. 31 mentioning Rahab the prostitute, who had received the spies, he calls them spies.

Chapter V.
The prodigious abstinence of the Saint, and long fasts.

[By the example of St. Catherine of Siena] A foundation so deep, broad, and solid was worthy and safe for supporting the immense, lofty, and ponderous superstructure. Indeed, they surpass the force of a narrative that has become rather dryly familiar through the bare accounts of Rose's fasts; for (as we have said) many things lie hidden. It is briefly evident that the Virgin, by following in the footsteps of St. Catherine of Siena, gradually led her to the practice of abstinence and fasting, almost to the very height of starvation. Let us consider each item in turn.

[59] The pupil had not yet passed the swaddling clothes and years of infancy, [having been accustomed to abstinence from a tender age,] when she forbade herself, which is unusual and burdensome for that age, to eat any fruit. The attentive mother was often astonished that her little daughter was not captivated by any such delicacies, sweetness, or varied beauty; for she would at once give away the fruits offered to her, never even bringing them to her lips. On Wednesdays, Fridays, and Saturdays (unless his mother or a doctor compelled him otherwise), he abstained entirely from all food, tasting nothing but bread and water. At the age of fifteen he made a vow to abstain from meat for the rest of his life, so far as those to whom he was bound to obey would permit. This was the prudent guidance of a wise dog for the tender girl, by which he wished to withdraw from her little body so that at the same time he would take nothing away from another's right or authority over her. Meanwhile, the cautious little daughter did not lack ingenious stratagems by which she could hide from her mother this strict regimen of abstinence for a long time, so that she would not be forced by her to eat more substantial food.

Sometimes noble matrons, out of devotion, would invite the mother and daughter into their homes, and after a sober meal was set out, they would ask both of them to recline with them. That was the hardest thing for frugal Rosa, who was even offended by the smell of meat. But the mother, a matron more eager to please her daughter, ordered her to eat nothing but meat, so that she would not appear to be profaning the honor of the table with an uncouth singularity of parsimony. The daughter appeared sorrowful, but suffering the greatest punishment of an unsettled stomach: for whatever meat she had forced down with reluctance, she would shortly afterward, having found a more secluded corner, have to bring it all back up, not without miserable torment. If ever the sick stomach could not by any force rid itself of the loathsome morsel, a paroxysm was at the door, and the most burning fever would seize the one who was nauseous. Nor did he obey the physicians with any better success when, at times, they tried to restore a languishing or slowly recovering patient to his former strength by having him eat meat: for it was so far from helping a weak person with this kind of nourishment that even a single dose, like a poison, would further weaken the patient, and with the disease renewed, it was not the patient but the illness that gained strength.

[61] Having once emerged from a rather serious illness, and so that he might recover more quickly, [and after that, by order, having tasted] by the doctor's prescription, they had forced a little meat down his stomach, which was still tender. when suddenly a total bodily weakness, soon followed by a tremor and fainting, struck the poor woman down, and a sudden asthma descended upon her chest and lungs, pressing down and cutting off her ability to breathe. In barely a few hours, this anguish had worn the Virgin out, and for several days she could neither walk a step nor stand on her feet, until, having regained the power of her accustomed abstinence, she too was gradually restored to herself. She had almost recovered, and once again they forced her, unwillingly, to eat a meat dish. The aforementioned symptoms returned again, and there was no remedy except the usual diet of bread and water.

[62] [He would repeatedly relapse into illness,] He suffered from frequent sciatica, and usually from such violent pains that they would upset the very depths of his stomach. Under this torture he would sometimes spend entire days, even with the nights, without food; but once the torment was somewhat eased, his family's piety soon compelled him to take a little broth with his meat. She herself, most urbanely declining this, would rather request that familiar fare be brought to her—bread and water. When these were brought, as if to indulge herself deliciously, she would dip the heart of the bread into the cold water, and having taken it, after a little she would rise from her bed, as though refreshed and amply restored. Questor Gundisalvus, at his home, saw Rosa recover from this medicine—not an anaretic one, but a thoroughly ascetic one—and he protested that he could in no way understand how this fast could have the power to nourish.

[63] As frequent experience has shown. There, on another occasion, observing in the virgin clear signs of manifest weakness, the same paterfamilias wished to take a little from the roasted bird's wing to somewhat lift her failing stomach. Rose, already so well-versed in evil, nevertheless tried to comply with the urgent request. She had scarcely swallowed the morsel when, with a premonition of danger, she politely left the table, rushed into the adjoining oratory, and drew the bolts. And when, as was her custom, she came out a little pale late in the evening, she told her quæstor and husband in a trembling voice that she had come very near dying; for from the small amount she had tasted of the bird, she had suffered a dreadful obstruction of the chest that had nearly suffocated her.

[64][Wherefore it ought to have been left to his discretion] These cruel experiments moved the compassionate inquisitor to beseech the Virgin's confessors, physicians, and parents, one by one and almost under oath, not to wish to bring Rose to the brink of death by ordering her to eat meat. to suffice as so many tragic examples of events in testimony to ready obedience beyond one's powers; It would be cruel and manifestly ungrateful to God to attempt anything further to assail the fast of Rose, from which his life so clearly depended. Audebat & Rosa, under such an advocate, felt freer to urge the doctors not to slaughter them for the sake of eating their flesh, as healthy as they wished. Indeed, whatever other food of more vigorous sap she would not admit, so it seemed to the Deity, to whose will the very laws of nature themselves obey. He was perhaps alluding to the heavenly vision (which he recalled), in which Christ, standing between the scolding and the flattering, had taught Rosa that he wished to be constantly honored by her with such strict exercises of abstinence, that his life and health would be a matter of care and concern for her heart. nor was it to be doubted but that He, who for her had poured out His life on the cross, who with the last drop of His most precious blood had so dearly purchased her, who had heaped into her soul so many treasures of grace, and such an abundance of heavenly gifts, would at last, for her frail little body, grant—and had already granted—the power to abstain from the nourishment of flesh. Therefore, let Aesculapius give way, rest, O doctors; For what you see is a divine mystery.

It was later persuaded to the mother that she should equably indulge her daughter in such a strict regimen and form of the most meager diet. Whether he noted an excessive emaciation in a pale face, [one should take cheap & moderate food,] or noted the strength depressed below the mediocre level, he would call this one rigid diet of the most severe frugality, as if it were a heinous crime, to account, crying out impatiently, and almost at those very people who once thundered with words at Mother Lapa of St. Catherine of Siena; proclaiming a singular, speechless, and self-wounding one. And when even that failed to help, she imperiously ordered that from then on she be seated at the family table with them every day, so that she could finally see what and how much she ate. In turn, the daughter implored her parent to grant her at least those dishes that she could not only eat but also digest. With these things thus established by mutual agreement, the prudent daughter made the kitchen maid Mariana one of her own. He skillfully extorted from her, under an oath of silence, that she prepare for herself at each meal a single morsel of bread crusts and a handful of herbs, but without salt or seasoning, boiled in plain water, with only a few raisins sometimes sprinkled on it, so that the mother, attentive to every morsel, seem to be cheerfully and sweetly providing for her, where a rare grape enhanced each mouthful. But what was actually happening was quite different: for the herbs the Virgin had selected with special care for her own nourishment were exceedingly bitter, and their rustic, malignant flavor tormented the palate. He would season these delicacies with ashes, so that no similar condiment would be lacking.

[66] He had heard that in a nearby forest a notably bitter herb grew: [which, among the most bitter herbs] was secretly brought to him; from then on he had a tasteless porridge made for himself from it, and so that this most exquisite provision would not run out, he planted the same herb in his own little garden, carefully tended it with his own hands, persuading his mother that it was useful for medicinal purposes. Once a curious mother found a pot full of goat's bile in the garden fence. Immediately suspecting what it was, she asked her daughter why she had hidden it there, or to what purpose. Seeing that she had been discovered, Rosa simply replied that she was using it to sprinkle her doll. Truly an excellent appetizer to whet the appetite. It later became known (as revealed by the handmaid Mary) that Rosa, at almost every dawn when she was not required to renew the sacred Synaxis, used to bathe her entire mouth with this austere gall-based ointment in memory of the Passion of the Lord. Especially on Fridays, however, she would prepare for herself an electuary from the same gall and crusts of bread, as if to dine with Christ on the cross on a sponge soaked in vinegar, sprinkling it drop by drop with biting vinegar and copious tears. In its own dialect, it was called GASPACHOS, because it was eaten cold. When the supply of gall failed, certain leaves of a most bitter shrub took its place; by sucking and chewing them, the thirsty for Christ in death recalled, with wounded thought, the ill-tasting hyssop.

[67] In that fertile tract of the western Indies grows a marvelous plant, [secretly prepared.] It is called GRANADILLA by the natives; I believe that the Latins still lack a proper name for a thing so late discovered, except that faithful Europe, by way of a circumlocution, calls it the Flower of Passion. Here, in this marvelous play of nature, most of the instruments of the Lord's death and our redemption unfold themselves conspicuously, as if in a miniature stage-set: for among the little leaves, charmingly dotted with bloody spots, the little column of the God of the scourge rises up. Below, a crown of thorns surrounds it; above, three nails are fixed and protrude. The surrounding leaves are interspersed with thread-like, pendulous tendrils. The center of the sinus is filled in heaps with granules of exquisite flavor. And so the roses were pleasing to eat, not the grains of this blessed flower, because they were sweet. but the shoots, because they were most bitter: boiled, they were eaten more willingly, since both the bitterness of their juice and their kinship to their flower made them a more fitting representation of the Passion than of a mere meal.

[68] His fasts were so frequent. We must now turn to the fasts, although one might not unreasonably doubt whether the hunger itself or such food could have been a more severe torment. Those who solemnly testified to Rose's virtues after her death reported that she observed two kinds of fasts: one common, in which she once a day took a very small refection of bread and water, but only at night; the other, which she called her own, in which she tasted nothing at all, neither by day nor by night. First, although it was almost a daily occurrence, especially in the last years of his life, which he spent in the house of the treasurer Gundisalvus; However, he observed annually, according to the rule of the Dominican institute, those seven consecutive months which lie between the Feast of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross and Easter of the Resurrection, consecrated to the Order's fast. During those months, therefore, he more strictly mortified himself by also reducing his intake of herbs and bread, until, at the beginning of Lent, he deprived himself of all bread entirely, subsisting only on the seeds of golden apples, most sparingly. For he would take no more than five of them on the six days of fasting, adding only a drink of kid's wool, so that by this austerity he might more keenly contemplate the bitterness of the five wounds of Christ.

[69] For the rest of the year he had prescribed such a scant measure of bread that what was believed to be enough for a single day would scarcely last him eight days in all. While Rosa was still at home, living in her own little cloister (as mentioned below), the wife of the treasurer Gundisalvus used to send her every week eight small loaves of coarser bread, so that one less daily meal would be necessary for her upkeep. But at the end of the week, with an equal number of fresh loaves having arrived, it was found that Rosa had eaten only one and a half of the eight very small loaves over the entire week, and had returned the remaining six and a half that had been left in the pantry.

[70] [And the food so meager,] I don't know whether I should call what follows a fast or outright starvation. It was observed that the Virgin, with a single loaf of bread, and that a very small one, and a small vessel of water, spent the entire fifty days without any other food or drink, namely from the Feast of the Resurrection until Pentecost. Also, in another year, he ran the entire length of that same fifty-mile stretch completely without drinking, not even sipping a drop of water. When, however, the quæstor's house received her as a housemate, it was nothing new if she passed several consecutive days without any food at all: she also usually used to shut herself in her private oratory on Thursdays, and would not leave until Saturday: So that during this three-day period, completely sleepless, she not only lacked all bodily food and drink, but also (which most amazed the quæstor Gundisalvus) remained for the entire time fixed to one corner, so that she did not leave it for a moment for any need. Indeed, the spirit's fatness sustained the body, which was emptied by fasting, and the meditation of heavenly things was its nourishment.

[71] At one time, the mere sacramental species, which she received, imparted such strength that, rightly, it could be called a fast, for in the fortitude of that food she accepted no other nourishment for the entire eight days. Once, forced to take a small amount outside her usual mealtime, she could not keep it down, so she returned it and, as if guilty of intemperance, condemned herself to a strict ten-day fast.

Finally, he even found a way to ruin the taste of plain water: whether he drank it or abstained, he would cheat the palate; for, in order to deprive his throat of relief and refreshment, he would go for entire weeks without a drink. But she drank not a cold, but a warm draught, fearing to find any pleasure in the ice-cold water. When asked the cause, he gave another, namely that he was treating his drowsiness in this way, which was brought on by drinking water, especially cold. Let other minor matters be passed over here, since we believe these suffice to show that nothing vulgar can be inferred about the incomparable frugality and abstemiousness of this Virgin.

Far more marvelous was what her household noticed, That Rosa always drew more strength from fasting and prolonged hunger than from bodily food: yet (as we shall relate below) she shared with her teacher, St. Catherine of Siena, the fact that she applied her hungry mouth to the wounded side of Christ: for apart from this favor and honey, neither disciple nor teacher knew of anything else by which they could be satisfied.

A paroxysm, also called an accession or an invasion, according to Castelo in the Medical Lexicon, is the worst part of the entire cycle, which is from the first onset of the disease until the time of its cessation, whether it occurs on the third, the fourth, or every day.

b Sciatica is arthritis of the hip. It takes its name from the injured part: for the mouth, which receives the head of the femur, is called the ischion, that is, the hip. Thus Gorræus, who explains this disease more fully in the Medical Definitions, page 198.

c This medicine is called not anaretic, that is, not selective, which epithet is from the Greek verb ἀναιρέω, that is, to choose or to select is formed, if I am not mistaken. Here again, among the adjectives anareticum and anachoreticum, there is an analogy such as the biographer often affects, as we have already noted above. However, this antithesis or opposition appears to consist in the fact that the anchorites use not selected food, but rather whatever is readily available.

d. Asclepius was, among the ancients, a god of health or medicine, about whom the poets tell many fables, which are not relevant here.

From Gaspacho, or rather gazpacho, which the Spaniards call a broth or porridge made from oil, vinegar, salt, toasted bread, and other ingredients, as Francisco Sobrino explains in his Spanish-French Dictionary under this word. From this passage I gather that this embamma is eaten cold, and I found elsewhere that the common people and country folk in Spain eat it.

John de Laet, already cited several times, book. Chapter 10, West Indies 2. Concerning that fruit of Peru, he writes the following after other things: "Its pulp is white and insipid." The plant is like ivy, creeping and climbing in the same way wherever it is placed. Elegant, laden with its fruit because of its size: it bears a flower like that of the white rose, on whose petals certain marks resembling the Passion of Christ (as Monardes says) are discernible, which you would think were painted there with great care, &c.

Chapter VI
Cilices, scourges & chains, with which the holy Virgin afflicted her tender body

[The holy one, having placated the divine wrath] It is marvelous that in a body so dry and emaciated by so many fasts there was still room to receive the scourges. blood, which would follow the beatings. Nevertheless, Rose was so zealous and eager to punish her body that a limit had to be set for her by her confessors. At first, when she had begun to wear the habit of St. Catherine of Siena, not content with ordinary cords, she had fastened two iron chains to her back as a whip, and with these, in imitation of her holy Father Dominic, she scourged her back and shoulders so harshly and cruelly each night that streams of blood were left on the walls, on the floor, and on her undergarments. The innocent virgin believed that she deserved these punishments for her own sins. Moreover, moved by pity for the public calamities, she strove by these means, following the example of her mistress, to appease the wrath of the Deity and to soften His justice. Hence, now for the sufferings of the whole Holy Mother Church, now for the straits of the imperiled republic, she most mercilessly slaughtered herself as a bloody victim, that she might win clemency and turn away the scourges.

[73][for the public good of the Kingdom of Peru] If any divine scourges threatened either the Kingdom of Peru or the city of Lima, his native land, he strove to divert them by his own: and not oblivious of the souls seething in the flames of purgatory, he doubled the strokes of the chains in new torrents of blood, which would extinguish part of the pyre. At great expense, he strove to secure for the agonizing the means for that supreme moment of such momentous importance, from which eternity depends. However, for the sins of others, to atone for the insults to God's majesty, and for their conversion to a better path, it cannot be said how many furrows of scourges, how many bruises of rods she has plowed into herself daily: The daily labor and pain of being whipped did not know respite, for it was distributed so over the various parts of the body that, while one set of lashes was given time to swell and heal, it passed to another, and the next day it returned from that to the first, which had scarcely healed: Moreover, this alternating but cruel succession of blows tormented all the more terribly, the keener the sensation in the parts wounded the day before.

[74] [or by the necessity of the city of Lima] The household servants (though the Virgin was unaware) had grown accustomed, from the most secret part of the house, to hearing these blows of discipline every night; But once, they shuddered beyond the usual, while Rosa, with no distinction now between her wounded and uninjured parts, raged more fiercely and for longer against her entire self with the loud clatter of her blows. A popular disturbance had broken out in the city of Lima, arising from trivial (as is usual) beginnings. The apostolic man, and an outstanding luminary of the Seraphic Order, the venerable Father F. Francis Solanus, in life and after death, renowned for his constant reputation of holiness and the glory of his miracles, had perhaps delivered some things more obscurely in his sermons, which the common people of Lima, having misunderstood them, twisted into a meaning entirely foreign to them. Hence a sudden panic seized the entire city, as if, by the public prophecy of P. Solanus, it were about to be swallowed up by an opening in the earth. which, however, he had neither said nor dreamed of. Rose, warned of the danger that threatened both the renowned name of the holy Man and the tranquility of a withering Lima, mourned sorrowfully and imprinted that night's horrid scourges upon her own flesh, so that by the sacrifice of her own blood she might soothe the common turmoil of her people, which to the thoughtful seemed more perilous than any earthquake.

John of Lorenzana, the Virgin's confessor, [with an iron scourge and other methods,] When John of Lorenzana, the Virgin's confessor, [with an iron scourge and other means,] judged that this severity of discipline should be tempered somewhat more justly to her bodily frailty, Rose extorted from him by the most earnest prayers that she might at least be permitted, within a certain space of days, to inflict upon herself five thousand lashes, which is piously believed to be the number of blows that Christ received in his own cruel scourging. Having obtained this permission, Rosa was just as anxious not to exceed that number as she was to fill it. Indeed, so that he would not overstep, he would ask his confessor for a new permission as often as either some need of his relatives or a public cause seemed to demand special assistance from him. However, to make up for it, she paid the closest attention to how many days, from time to time being laid up by illness, she had been prevented from her daily discipline, and after carefully calculating, once she had recovered, she would divide the portions of the missed strokes into the next period of days in such a way that, with the compensation made, the permitted number of five thousand would be exactly met, and no less diligently would she complete the number corresponding to the current period of days. With equal obedience he acquiesced to his confessor, when he ordered the excessive cruelty of the iron chain to be exchanged for another, twisted from strands into a knotted and rough scourge, so that he might conform himself more humbly to the common practice of his Order. Therefore, I leave it undecided whether Rosa placed this iron plow of scapulae more sacredly or took it up.

[76] Moreover, how eagerly Rosa's genius inclined toward the exercises of penance, [to which she had been accustomed from her very childhood,] she had already abundantly shown by serious preludes, almost from infancy. He was four years old when he began to torment his tender shoulders—shoulders that then still lacked the rough girls' whips—now with the weight of raw sides, now with the burden of a heavy trunk, as if from that moment he had set out to carry the cross after Christ, laboring under the infamous wood's vexation. Mariana, the household maid, was the only one aware of these secrets for some time; she alone was the Virgin's confidante. And so she, now plucked to a more remote corner of the garden, now to the house's solarium, would kneel on the ground and pray that (which she herself with her tender hands could not yet do) she might load her little shoulders with the force of the stones scattered there: under that weight and crushing pressure, the little one endured in a long and laborious prayer. She finally motioned to the maidservant to remove the weight without a sound, lest the mother, arriving unexpectedly, disturb the secret devotion and block any chance to repeat it.

At other times, under the same silence, she would ask for an inform bar to be placed on her shoulder, afflicting her tender body, and, gathering her small remaining strength, she would try to bear the most difficult burden with all her might, while she unwound her very long braids. It groaned under the unequal burden, it struggled, it resisted, until at times, overcome, it was pressed down to the ground. These were the rudiments of a more severe discipline, which threatened the body as much as the more mature age threatened the mind. She had not yet turned fourteen when she began to be noted, among other penitential works, for walking barefoot at night through the garden, a long cross laid upon her, which, as she carried it on her livid shoulders, she brought herself to the ground with frequent genuflections, measuring the most bitter Way of Calvary with equal steps and sighs. Neither the malice of the air, nor the dreadful darkness, nor the cold, nor the whirlwinds of the winds were unpleasant to the Virgin, who from every side drew upon her little body whatever discomfort she could.

[78] [and forbidden to flog himself with an iron chain,] He had been forbidden (as we noted above) to use the iron chain as a whip any longer. She interpreted the matter thus: not that the chain would spare her altogether, but only that it would not kill her. And so she fastened it most securely around her waist with a triple wrap, inserted the key into the little loop at the seam's end, and hid the key where even she herself could not retrieve it. This was the most secret kind of torture, which, whether for a long time or perhaps always, had remained hidden from everyone: for Rosa, or even her own secretary Mariana, wished her to be unaware of it. but God did not will it. Thus it was finally revealed. During the night, Rosam was seized by the sharpest pain in her hip while lying in bed. She felt that the entire chain was afflicting her, like one that, through worn leather, pressed deeply here and there into the flesh and nerves. From which the cold of iron had drawn the kidneys and flanks into the consensus of the most atrocious pain. Rosa was tormented because she alone could neither break the harmful chain nor unfasten the lock without a key.

[79] [It had so tightly girdled the little body that it pierced the flesh,] Meanwhile, under the unbreakable bond, the dreadful torment of sciatica grew, which at last, after long groans, wrung even pitiful sobs from a reluctant Rose. Waking, Mariana sprang up, approached to lift up the sick woman, offered whatever assistance was needed, and at the same time asked perplexedly about the illness and its cause. This poor virgin had to reveal her secret in order to ask the maid for advice and a way to free herself, before her mother, upon waking, would rush to help them both. Both tried their utmost; alternately they wrestled and conferred, but neither could overcome the stubborn, late-clinging obstinacy. The maid remembered that such strong locks could sometimes be opened by a powerful blow; so, while she went to fetch a stone, anxious Rosa, fearing that her mother would either be summoned or would soon appear of her own accord, fled to prayer, which, like a key, pierced both heaven and the lock. What a wonder! Returning with the rock, Mariana hears the gate with a loud creak spring open of its own accord; the chain slowly slackens, she is drawn out, carried off whole. but everywhere avulsion was necessary, which was followed by skin and blood. When this task was done, Rosa rested, so that the same maid could take her turn, and at dawn, as if she had suffered nothing serious, she hurried back to her usual labors.

[80] [And so the confessor even ordered her to be taken away.] The wounds of her torn flesh had barely begun to heal when Rosa returned to her chain and again fastened herself to the iron torment. Until at last the confessor, by some uncertain means, learned of it, and immediately ordered the virgin to send him the chain, as it was, without delay. She complied, tore the chain, which had once again grown into the flesh, from her loins, and carefully wrapped it, together with the key, in a cloth for the sacrist of B. He handed Blasio Martinez over to be taken straight to the confessor. The sacristan, suspicious from the weight that it contained gold torcs or precious jewels, stepped away from the Virgin, unfolded the wrapping, and discovered a treasure of miscellaneous gold—a chain freshly stained with blood, the flesh and membrane clinging to it in places, quite clearly interspersed. Some of these little vials or circles, after Rose's death, were kept for a while at Our Lady of Usategui and gave off a marvelous yet unfamiliar fragrance to everyone, a testament to the painful sweat they had absorbed from Rose's little body.

[81] Nor were the paps of the arms immune from such bonds: [Then he would bind the arms with cords,] for Rosa, for the purpose of torture, had fastened certain tight ligatures around them, which perpetually twisted the coiled muscles. But then their muscles ached the most whenever they had to lift something from the ground or stretch their arms upward. Meditating on these secretly painful bonds, she accompanied the bound Spouse from the Garden of Gethsemane to the houses of Caiaphas, Annas, and Pilate. Nor had the skin, though it covered these ligatures so tightly, closed up so completely that Mariana could not see them when she was treating Rose's torn shoulders: for this kind of service was often required, because the cracks on Rose's back were constantly open, into which, at intervals, Mariana would sprinkle unknown powders by order, so that they would not fester, and cover them with blotting paper, to be renewed by repeated rubbings the next day. Moreover, as ropes and thongs bound their arms and loins, they tormented the chest, armpits, and sides with handfuls of nettles and tiny thorns in a most frequent bite, so that no part was spared from the torture, while, by chance, the cilicium was still lacking on account of the scarcity. Thus indeed, Rosa, in the thickets, fashioned herself into a lily among thorns.

[82] But when she saw herself enriched with a huge gift—a short tunic of coarse cloth, as though she had received purple, linen, or a robe—she congratulated herself and her trembling flesh on the new belt of penance, the cloak of austerity. At first, although rough, it seemed less broad and winding than the Virgin had wished, since it scarcely exceeded a span of two palms. But shortly afterward, another thing happened thanks to the generous gift of a religious person, which made Rosa content in every way. This was a tunic, and it hung from the neck and shoulders down below the knees. The coarse, plaited from the coarser horsehair, scarcely equaled the weight of a cuirass. This ponderous harshness, almost unwieldy by its own coarseness and unyielding in its rigidity, could have sufficed for the most robust penitent to attain the utmost austerity; yet it was not enough for Rosa, for she armed herself more diligently with a shaggy undergarment, fixing on every side the shortest spikes. You would have thought it was a hedgehog, not a rose. To the virgin, however, this most rudimentary hairshirt seemed less a hairshirt than a scourge, and she wore it for several years, until, because of frequent vomiting of blood, she was forced by another's command to cast off this harmful armor.

[83] She herself, however, devised another method of punishing her little body, [and each of her body's members], which she would secretly substitute for the previous one, perhaps with less harm to her health, but not with less inconvenience. He had fashioned a sack of the thickest wool into a tunic, and so that its hairy coarseness could not be detected, he had covered the cuffs of the sleeves with carbasin cloth. Under this weight, she was burning, stiffening, and weakening from her exhausted strength; she could advance her foot with the greatest difficulty, and whether she had sat down or fixed her knees on the ground, she could scarcely raise herself to her feet. Any movement was a punishment. The limbs languished, as if under a burden; And she seemed to herself to be swathed in a leaden rather than a hemp mattress. Only the soles of their feet remained untouched, by neither cilice nor the scourge of discipline. Nevertheless, the mistress of her own flesh found a way to dispense to them their own share of torments. So whenever the oven was fired up at home to bake bread, Rosa would seize the opportunity, and barefoot she would step onto the still-warm top of the oven opening, where the fiercest heat burst forth. He was there brooding with the keenest sense of the dreadful fires of hell, which he believed were due to him more than seriously. Thus, from the sole of his foot to the crown of his head, there was nothing left in him to endure voluntary torments.

[84] [tortured with a peculiar torment.] With these caresses Rosa soothed that frail little body, which otherwise had already suffered enough from its frequent infirmities, as will be seen below in its proper place. Nor was he lacking in the spirit to attempt even greater things, had not prudent confessors forbidden it, who repeatedly, with the precept of moderation interposed, cried out that a discreet use of one's strength should be made. Indeed, if Rosa was not a friend to her own body, let this be the ultimate example: that the most cautious virgin did not allow the delights of the spirit to overflow into the solace of the body. It is recorded that very few of God's friends cared about it; Rosa had read of the one Mexican, Gregorio Lopes, and immediately resolved to imitate him. Thus, down to this most subtle division of flesh and spirit, he had chosen to conform himself to the crucified Spouse, in that he permitted neither the glory of the soul to flow into his still mortal body, nor the joys of the higher part to descend into the lower, lest he impede the Passion. She felt that from the marvelous, heavenly flavor and savor, her spirit was sweetened, her mind intoxicated, her soul enriched. But in the meantime she was on her guard and, as far as she could, kept herself from being admitted to any partaking or sympathy in such feasting—her wretched body, which she had devoted solely to suffering and torment. And if the King brought her into his cellars, she herself ordered her body to remain outside. I think this divorce can be counted among Rose's more sublime works.
