Chapter VII
A thorny crown, with which he crowned his head.

[He placed a crown on her head] It was fitting for the beloved B. A disciple of Catherine of Siena, in the school of patience, is crowned with the prototype of her teacher. This is the remarkable ambition of the saints, by which they prefer to carry Christ's thorny crown rather than a golden one in the exile of this life. But for roses in particular, it is neither safe nor proper to be without a thorny hedge. But to the point. At the very threshold of her adolescence and childhood, a devout and most tender compassion had pierced Rose's little heart most acutely, from the avid gaze of a pious image that proclaimed with a silent cry, "Behold the Man." It clung to that thorny crown which had crimsonly sat upon Christ's head. He was ashamed to appear a delicate member under the thorny Head. And so he then fashioned his first crown (the second will be discussed a little later) from malleable tin, which, whether of cords or of rods twisted together, formed a spiral. He had set certain small, inwardly sharpened pins at intervals in it, and with that diadem he secretly bound his head, not without wounding it, the victim crowned beneath the bands.

It lasted for several years, and the pious Virgin endured under this burdensome crown of thorns. [armed with ninety-nine spikes,] but that was nothing more than a childish trial of a more powerful torment, which loomed from the second crown, armed with ninety-nine spikes. She imposed this latter upon herself about ten years before her death, that is, shortly after she had taken the habit of the Dominican Order, and she did not lay it aside until her life ended: For, having begun to contemplate more attentively the crown of thorns of his Redeemer, miraculously transferred to the head of St. Catherine of Siena, she judged it by no means fitting to rest until she, too, had brought that same crown to her own head with as pious an emulation as she could. It did not seem to her that she had fully assumed the habit of the seraphic mistress, unless she had also taken up her crown of thorns.

[87] With a new zeal, then, and a desire to suffer, he bent a silver lamella into a circle [whose shape is described], and into this he fixed from the inside, of the same metal, three rows of spikes, so that each row contained thirty-three spikes, namely, for the number of years of Christ, which a mortal fulfilled on earth for mortals. Certainly nothing could have been devised either more pious than a loving mother toward her children (as physicians call them), or more severe than a harsh mother, unless this very severity had been suggested by piety itself. Moreover, so that the hair, which had grown back shorter after the cutting, would not hinder a deeper impression of the spikes, she had shaved every strand right down to the living skin with a razor, leaving only a few unkempt curls on her forehead to cover her crown by day and hide it from her mother. Although the very small remainder of this hair on the crown had been used for something else, it was also used for new intensifications of pain. But of this in the next chapter, where a concise account will pass to Rose's vigils.

[88] [& which the Virgin diligently used,] How terribly this crowned headgear gnawed, how painfully it pricked the veiled head of the sacred maiden from every side—for twisting it, a single needle would have been enough—can be inferred from the fact that the spikes did not all come at once, but one after another, in pairs, threes, or even more, in the most troublesome succession, invaded the exposed skin, and, according to the occasion and variety of the movements, each one was twisted in the individual narrowness of the wounds. It is known that, under this densest thicket of piercing metal, the Virgin's very speech was painful: what if her chest was shaken by a cough? Was his wretched head relieved by a sneeze, or was some other, more urgent need to cough violently distorting his face and the expression of his mouth?

But these things seemed like child's play to Rosa, and worthy of the aid of art, by which they might rage more fiercely. Therefore, where the ends of the crowns or plates met, he fastened bands crosswise, by the tension of which he not only held this pointed circle in place, so that it would neither slip from its position, or loosen of its own accord, but also, whenever she wished, she would intensify the pain by drawing the ends of the bands tighter, thus applying force with her own torture; and on the sixth day, in particular, the Virgin delighted in this epimetron of punishments, in memory of the Spouse, crowned with thorns. He would have preferred, for a closer resemblance, to wear a crown of wild thorns, and had seriously discussed the matter with his confessor. But he dissuaded her, for he feared that a serious, pus-filled infection could easily develop on Rose's head under a rotten thorn. She too, upon reflection, realized that such a crown could not be hidden adequately even under a veil, with its points protruding at random and only adding to her torment unless all its spikes were bent inward. Therefore a blade had to be used, and a silver one at that, so that it would lie more solidly against the wounded skin and hold the embedded little spines more firmly. And the outcome did not disappoint: for after the Virgin's death the goldsmith, who had been instructed to remove several of the little plates from the spikes (perhaps so that they might be given to devout persons who requested them for home or devotional use), using forceps found each one so firmly set that none could be pulled out, although they bent here and there under the pressure of the iron. So that you may understand that the rose's thorns yielded no more to its own patient than they did to her.

[90] [torturing more and more;] Yet not content with this daily torment, she found a way to open fresh wounds each day: for when she put the crown on her head in the morning, she never fastened it in the same spot where it had stood the day before, but by shifting its position slightly she moved the headband to one side and then the other, so that by alternating its placement it would pierce more deeply and renew the pain in the uninjured spot. Moreover, every Friday he would add to the heap so that he would lower the crown down to the cartilages of the ears and the lower part of the occiput, by which the temples (where the flesh is softer and the sensation more delicate) were besieged by the pointed stimuli and more painfully girdled, and wounded more deeply. She repeated it again on Saturday, in commemoration of the Virgin Mother, whose soul had been pierced by the sword of sorrow at the death of her Son.

[91] This most bloody briar-patch long eluded her familiars, her household, and even her own mother: [until this torment, which had long eluded her household,] for who, under the band and veil of the most cautious Virgin, would have either noticed or sought out such a fateful punishment? The confessor himself, who had permitted Rose this secret form of penance (for without his nod the obedient Virgin would undertake nothing, not even the slightest thing), merely aware in passing of the thorny garment, had neither seen the crown nor even in the faintest suspicion touched the foreign contrivances by which this Rose had trained herself to provoke it more atrociously within herself. But at last, by the providence of the Deity, the matter could no longer be hidden, and it brought about that such an example of virtue would come to the attention of many. It happened that the father was seeking out Rose's brother for a boyish prank, to make him a eunuch. Rosa interjected herself rather sweetly, to appease her father's anger. But as he forcefully pushed aside his daughter who stood in his way, he accidentally, by a slip of the hand, struck the Virgin's head lightly, on the part where the hidden crown was raging. Immediately, three streams of blood followed the unexpected blow, running down the innocent Daughter's forehead and staining it purple, a clear enough sign of what kind of porcupine was secretly nesting under its white covering.

[92] Here Rosa, feeling more betrayed than struck, blushed doubly; [and at last it was discovered by chance,] However, attempting to conceal what she was, she cautiously withdrew to a more secluded chamber, hastily set aside her crown, wiped away the blood as if in jest, and, placing cloths to soak up the trickle from her wound, she immediately covered her head with her usual veils. But with this clever escape, the anxious mother was neither deceived nor eluded. When Mariana, the maid, followed the Daughter from the track, she asked about the wound. He asks the reason why blood has burst forth from a slight touch. Finally, with Rosa excusing herself and pleading in vain, the more suspicious one ordered her to loosen her veil and bare her head. When he saw it, gaping in a monstrous circle on every side, he easily guessed whence these marks so bloody were in the zodiac. Nevertheless, she remained silent, well aware that if her daughter were forced to betray or cast aside her own thorn, she would soon invent other—and indeed far more cruel—means to continue tormenting her. And so then, as if unaware of the mystery, she gently washed her daughter's head with hot wine, believing—or pretending to believe—that it would be enough to heal the wounded parts, while she devised another remedy against the crown itself.

Without delay, the series of events was brought to the one who would more surely forbid the Virgin's use of the crown. [Our John of Villalobos somewhat mitigated it.] namely, to one of the spiritual Fathers, whose guidance Rosa obeyed. He was R. Father John de Villalobos, Rector of the Society of Jesus's college in Lima, a man celebrated for his holiness and piety, and a most skilled guide for leading souls to perfection by safer means. As soon as he learned what had been done, he immediately ordered Rosa to bring him whatever it was with which she had so far been spikily tormenting his head. She brought her crown, not even granting herself the briefest moment to wipe the drops of blood from between the points of the spikes. The prudent and pious father, having viewed the offering with horror and compassion, judged it to be more rigid than was fitting for that sex and the tenderness of that age. Therefore, he appealed to the Virgin on the grounds that she should entirely discourage the use of the most severe and cruel instrument. She, perceiving that reason, not force, was being used with her, humbly set forth her own reasons in return; finally, it was agreed on both sides that she would be restored to her crown, but with its powers diminished. Thus the prudent and discreet Father, seizing a file, smoothed down the more prominent thorns of the crown, dulling, blunting, and scraping them; if not enough, he nonetheless considered himself to have achieved no small measure with this treatment.

[94] [This thorny crown] But Rose, valuing the crown more than the kingdom, rejoiced to have recovered it with the full number of its thorns intact, and at least only slightly weakened by that chastisement. Thus he once again looked back to his former remedies for pain, and to the painful pulling of the bands he now added this: that he would sometimes strike the crown with his knuckles so that it would penetrate more deeply into his head. For the diligent file had not worn down the barbs so much that they could not perform their bloody work. It happened once when Rosa, by chance falling to the ground, lightly bumped her head on a nearby shelf, where, because the crown had been struck more forcefully, a copious flow of blood ran down onto her shoulder. Of Christ's thorny crown the Church sings: "Blessed thorn, whose spikes break the power of the Tartarean king." Our Rose herself deserved to experience it: for whenever the Tartar foe drew near to the Virgin, about to utter foul and filthy words, no other catapult was needed to drive him away than the Rose's vigil, who thrice touched her crown with her finger in reverence to the Holy Trinity. It was a drum, whose light touch struck the demon, who, in the most shameful flight, shunned the contest. More delightful, and perhaps more marvelous, is what was discovered about the virtue of this crown after the Virgin's death.

[95] [He filled the one who touched it with spiritual joy] A certain most devout servant of God, shortly after the funeral of the exalted Rose, had gone to the house of Gundisalvus the treasurer, with the sole intention of devoutly handling the instruments by which Rose, while still living, had mortified her virgin flesh. A crown of horrid thorns was offered to him along with the ring, and he had scarcely touched it with his hands when suddenly he began to blaze with an unusual fire of divine love, to melt in the inmost depths of his spirit, to grow sweet with the wondrous taste of heavenly delights, as if he had gathered grapes from the very thorns of paradise and figs from thistles for the palate of his soul. A more convenient treatment of this prodigy will be given below in chapter. XI, when it comes to the betrothal ring of the Rose: it suffices here to have hinted at how seriously and festively heaven itself applauded this crown, when in honor of such rigid compunction it distilled an anointing so sweet and precious.

[96] [After the death of the holy Virgin,] Let us not be reluctant to turn our eyes once more here to the thorny prototypal figure of St. Catherine of Siena, for our Rose, crowned with thorns, was seen to imitate it in such a way that she did not so much fully express it as she did transfer it into herself. Here is a notable argument for this matter, observed at Rose's funeral. While the corpse of the deceased lay in the coffin, that heavenly providence (to which nothing is accidental) had arranged that the florid wreath, which by custom encircled the virgin's head, was missing. The hour of the funeral was at hand; the body was to be carried out for burial. But the earthly flowers seemed to have fled, as if unworthy to crown the now heavenly Rose. Therefore, in that confused and anxious perplexity of those pressing on from one side and those hesitating from the other, recourse was finally had (not without a divine prompting) to the crowned image of St. Catherine of Siena, which Rosa had been accustomed to adorn during her lifetime: and so her own proper crown, publicly known to all, is transferred for the time being to the Disciple. Under this rose, the body of Gundisalvi is carried from the house to the tomb, so that she who, while alive, had vividly portrayed the seraphic teacher in her thorny bearing might, even in death, represent her more clearly under the common crown. All who had recognized the mystery attested to the fact that this singular testimony of virtue had been granted to the Virgin not by human design, but rather by the necessity of fortune—more by chance than by deliberate plan.

[97] This, too, seems to pertain to what has been said: that while the funeral procession thus crowned Rose on earth, the court of the Blessed, by a vision not unlike a miracle, crowned her in heaven; so that it is evident from both that she succeeded to the very crown of St. Catherine of Siena herself. The matter became known in this way: soon after the blessed Rose's passing, a certain person of exceptional holiness and renown saw in an imaginary vision among the myriads of angels a glorious host of holy Virgins, walking straight toward the throne of the Saints. of the Trinity: in the midst of the procession shone Rose, holding a palm in her hand, resplendent in a snow-white robe, but not yet crowned like the others: for at the foot of that divine throne stood the virgin Mother of God, holding a radiant crown in her right hand, with which she was to crown Rose with her own hands, awaiting this solemn procession of virgins. Indeed, in a very similar manner, St. Catherine of Siena was once received into heaven when she left the earth, so that as both teacher and disciple there was no flaw from the thorn to the golden crown. Blessed are the spines, for such a hand has bestowed upon them a diadem in the heavens!

Notes.

Here the biographer alludes to the medical or surgical appellation of a pious and strong-willed mother, about which Castellus in his Medical Lexicon, at our page. 322 thus discusses: It is the dura, an inviolate, thick, cuticular membrane (for they signify the same thing), which first appears, the bones of the skull having been removed or at least opened, covering the brain in a sphere (which is commonly called the DURA MATER), to which another, thin and soft, is attached, which is called the PIA MATER.

An eulogy of this man can be read in John Nadasynski's *Annus Dierum Memorabilium Societatis Iesu* on March 29, page 174.

Chapter VIII
The hard bed of St. Rose, and the night vigils.

[He devised ingenious contrivances for the virgarians,] He was so intent on emaciating his body that he did not even allow the few hours of nightly rest to be free from torment: hence he had his bed made so hard that it would drive away sleep rather than invite it. Indeed, art and industry have advanced to such a degree that the little bed for sleeping is also a pillory. Thus, of course, nothing was permitted to the afflicted body, and sleep was taken away so that it might be devoted to prayer. The attentive mother promptly sensed where her daughter was headed; for she had noticed in the girl, almost from infancy, both a penchant for sleeping on hard beds and a habit of hiding her austerity. So he ordered her to lie with him in the same little bed for a while at night, so that by art he might defeat the art. Rosa obeyed, but with that ingenuity by which she nonetheless most skillfully combined the merits of obedience and penance on that very motherly bed: For scarcely had slumber overtaken her mother when the Virgin slowly withdrew the part of the mattress on which she had been lying, until the planks beneath received her little body, and the wood of the headboard, or one hidden nearby, took the place of the removed side rail. The same contest between St. Catherine of Siena and her mother Lapa, and the same stratagem.

[99] [on which she herself would sleep most harshly,] Pleased with her success, Rosa went on more securely, not only laying bare for herself the planks on which she would sleep, but also roughly hewing a stone to place under her neck: But after long detours, the mother, awakened at last, finally interrupted her daughter's cunning and, as if she had discovered some monstrous crime, rebuked the girl, adding a taunt about her clever stubbornness: If it was her intention to deceive her mother in this way, she might go on and from then on board up whatever bed she pleased of her own accord, so long as she at least provided her neck with a more humane pillow and covered the boards themselves with a single simple blanket. At this word, Rose's heart leapt for joy; she seized her mother's permission—though tinged with reproach and bitter bile—as a sweet caress, eagerly accepted the condition, and offered her mother the most tender thanks for such generous indulgence. He soon threw a rough mat of planks, which he had just gathered, into the corner of his spacious room. He also placed two cushions on the seat. And with little effort, he finished the bed, as his mother had permitted. But whenever night fell, the pillows were removed and in their place a roughly hewn log was set, with small stones hidden under the bed for this purpose during the day; these were arranged beneath the blanket so that the body would be jolted by the uneven surface, for it was feared that the hard flatness of the planks would prove too luxurious. It is easy to guess how many of the pebbles from the scree beneath the blanket pierced the lying Rose; indeed, on the solid planks themselves the traces of the stones were clearly visible, and the boards themselves, worn away here and there in little hollows, testified amply how gently the gaunt Rose had lain upon them.

[100] [and the mother observing these things] Moreover, the little stones, scattered and arranged so as to be movable, gave way easily to the force whenever the little body had to be turned to the other side. Therefore, additions seemed to be both tougher and more stable for the stirrup supplement. He placed three winding, unseasoned, and knotty logs on the planks and, between the joints, secured them with thinner pegs under a blanket so they wouldn't give way. However, during the day he would hide these stolen logs separately under the mattress, so that his mother would neither discover them from the uneven spread of the bedding nor, if he left them out in the open, use them as grounds for legitimate suspicion. Finally, only Mariana's handmaid was privy to the secret, whom Rosa had compelled by strong entreaties not to reveal it. And if a room were to be torn apart, it would either leave these beams in place, or promptly replace them. For a long time, this backbending rack of cruel Rose's pleased the fervent one, and would have pleased him longer, had it been more atrocious—that is, had it been sharper and more savage in every respect. For between the empty spaces of the wood, there was nothing that could sufficiently torment the incubus.

[101] [She would deceive piously;] Then a new and final thought occurred to him for making the little bed most dreadfully harsh. He laid seven planks entirely on the tightly joined boards at even intervals, which he properly secured with ox-hide thongs so they wouldn't loosen. Furthermore, he filled each of the gaps between the distant timbers with sharp fragments of tiles, shards of clay pots, and broken triangular pottery, so that the pointed end of each piece was turned inward toward the body, and with such a dense packing that none could waver or give way. since the subjects below were firmly holding the beams, lest anything fall out; and they would wedge the sides with logs, so that the phalanx would not be loosened. Who would believe that it was finally possible to see a meadow worthy of this kind, in which Rosa could rest? Nor was a pillow worthy of such a noble bed lacking. but more on this below. Mattresses were fastened to the same frame, but they were made of Cilician wool, bristly and like the bottom of a sieve, so that they would prick at the same time and not hinder the punctures of the shells, but would instead conceal them. Moreover, she had secretly hung a vial filled with gall at the head of this little bed, and she would not settle down to sleep until she had moistened her throat with a drop of it, in memory of the Bridegroom who had drunk gall and myrrh.

Rosa once confessed that, on her bed's doorstep, this bitter draught was no less troublesome to her when she took it than the hardness of the bed itself, if she had woken up sooner. For then she felt her palate had completely dried up to her tonsils and epiglottis, her tongue was almost motionless from dryness, and the windpipes of her throat were inflamed on all sides, so that breathing itself was torture for her parched throat. Therefore, it is no wonder that (as later became known) horror and dread often pervaded the very marrow of the otherwise intrepid Rosa at the mere thought of her own so cruel little bed: the poor woman trembled, was on fire, and sweated before she even touched the pillow, remembering what feathers awaited her there, how many menacing quills would be pricked up there for her slaughter, what torments she would have to endure, how many times she would have to turn her weary little body to the other side; for she knew that she would by no means rise from it without a frozen stupor in all her limbs, and as if from a fracture of the hips, shoulders, legs, and arms, miserably torn apart. When a certain matron, after several years, familiarly asked how she could have endured such a horrible and arduous bed even for a single night, she replied with her usual wit and grace that she derived from that couch a pain sufficient, in itself, to atone for any very dear friend offered to God.

It happened that Rosam, as if scarcely equal to the just terror, hesitated longer on the horns of a dilemma, whether she should mount the bed so fertile in so many torments. But Christ came to the aid of the doubting and nearly fainting Virgin, appearing to her in a visible and gentle form, and most peacefully addressed her with these words: "Remember, Daughter, that the bed of Calvary was far harsher, more constricting, more dreadful, on which I took the sleep of death for you." You know what torments I endured there for the sake of your love; you know what iron spikes, not wooden, pierced my hands and feet until they had torn my beloved soul from me. Remember these things, weigh them against your penitential cloak on the scales, and you will see our bed, under the dictate of charity, appear in bloom. It is remarkable how deeply these words penetrated the Virgin's soul, and how much strength and constancy they gave her, so that she would not falter from that dreadful exercise of patience. Indeed, for sixteen years she remained unconquered in the most bitter torment of this ordeal, and (what will surprise you even more) only at the end did she become aware of it and unwilling, though her mother looked the other way. She indeed tried again and again to turn her daughter away from such an excess of austerity and to tear the enormous bed to pieces, but, divinely stirred by a promptings of conscience, she dared neither to lay a hand on it nor to command it. He therefore preferred to try to succeed through the confessors. But they too seemed to be flustered as if in a matter of great perplexity, and they did not resolve on anything certain. And this was a clear enough sign that it was God's will for Rosa, chosen for her tolerance of the harshest bed, to be crucified with her beloved Spouse.

[104] [Hence the pillow that was ordered to be placed under the head,] We must now speak of the cervical. Rosa was trying, by gradually discarding softer things, following the example of St. Catherine of Siena, to get to the point where, without her mother's knowledge, she would finally place an unformed stone under her head to sleep on. Thus, at first he applied a coarse cloth to the neck, then gradually a raw side, and finally a polygonal stone, rough on all sides. But this mother, as she noticed, lifted it. and stuffed the daughter's pillow with wool, but at her command, to stuff it. Rosa fell silent, took up a pillow, stuffed it, but not with wool, as her mother believed: she gathered the force of the shavings that are cut from the carpenter's adze. He filled this pillow, and fell asleep on it. But the mother's shrewdness did not remain hidden for long; she snatched the stuffing, threw away the wooden bits, had wool substituted, had wool stuffed in, and gave the orders clearly and loudly. Rosa obeyed, but not beyond the letter. He stuffed the wool into the neck, but not by itself. because between the wool and the outer cloth, where the face of the recliner was to be supported, he secretly hid some rolled rushes, which he had taken from an old basket for this purpose. The face of the sleeping Rose was excessively smudged, so much so that her mother often noticed these impromptu stains on her daughter's cheeks and forehead, yet could not divine their origin, being already certain of the wool that had filled the pillow. Until, by chance, carelessly placing his hand on the neck, he seized the enemy in ambush, discovered the rushes, and pulled them out. You would have seen here again Lapa, the mother of Catherine's daughter, heaping up insults, abuse, and accusations; Barely restrained from striking. Thus Rosa, who sought hard things for herself, had to endure the hardest.

[105] [new industry made it repeatedly harder,] From then on the mother, in the virtue of holy obedience, commanded her daughter to empty her pillowcase again and to put back only the wool. Rosa obeys again, but beyond the letter; Indeed, she doubled the wool, and, seizing a rod, she pressed so much of it onto the collar that this cork eventually matched its hardness. It seemed to him that he had returned to that wooden pillow which his mother had long ago taken away from him. It was a trunk from a tree, which the natives call pacaya, hollowed out in the middle to receive the head and neck of the Virgin. In place of the bundle of knotted sticks, which had been banished by a mother's decree, a bundle bound at the ends with ropes had taken its place. But this one, too, had been driven away by his mother. Finally, when this last purely woolen shawl came to hand, with no further counsel at hand, she said to her daughter, "You have indeed prepared, my Rose, you have indeed prepared; for here I find nothing but wool." It is enough, indeed more than enough: for you have known how to turn wool into a slaughterhouse. What more can I command? So that you would not flout your mother's command, you condensed these flakes into the hardness of solid rock. Do as you wish, and even if you bring death upon yourself, I will no longer interfere with you.

[106] [so that the mother threw down her entire little bed,] At last the mother's most eagerly awaited moment arrived, when, with the permission of the confessing Fathers, she was allowed to tear apart with her most avaricious hands the penitent little bed of Rosa. Those who were in charge of the Virgin's conscience had, about three years before her death, observed that she was being unduly weakened by her own exertions: her little body, exhausted by so many scourges, fasts, and illnesses, was no longer fit for the ordeal of such a harsh bed, and that, if she could not be persuaded to accept the remedies, the torments should at least be eased or removed, or their severity reduced. And so they hand over Rose's little bed, heavy with the hardships of three lustrums, to the greedy mother for plunder and spoil. I could hardly say what spirit the Daughter of Penance, more eager for life, received it in. But the eager mother, as if a signal had been given for an assault, invaded that most savage pile as if it were a hostile fortress, threw down logs, beams, and timbers, loosened the planks, and not without a great risk of stumbling, picked up the broken pottery fragments, scattered them, and finally threw them into a nearby river, so that they could not be used again. However, he first wanted to count them, and found that the sharp fragments were just short of three hundred.

Therefore, after these things, Rose had to lie down and sleep differently. [and forced her to sleep less uncomfortably.] Yet scarcely more gently or comfortably: for he repeated his tablets and planks; He laid a simple and worn-out mat, without a cushion, for them. And so, sleep calling, he laid his pale limbs down to rest. Yet even this poor bed seemed to him to savor of luxury. Thus, in the last years of her life, which she spent in the house of the treasurer Gundisalvi, she passed her nights in a humble chair, her small frame gathered in, her head for a moment resting against the column of the bed in which Gundisalvi's minor daughters lay. Thus Rosa took her briefest sleep more in measure than in depth, especially in winter, when she was so chilled, stiffened, and stunned by the night's frosts that upon rising she could scarcely stand on her feet. Sometimes, roused by the bitter cold, she would kindle sprigs of sea spray at her brazier, and in doing so would try to catch a little fugitive warmth from the smoke to warm herself. Hating the very body he loathed, he would not sit on a cushion anywhere, but either carried out his labors standing as long as he could, or, when he had to sit, used a hard staff instead of a stool.

[108] Meanwhile, remembering and longing for her former bed, she constantly pounded the ears of the confessors, [But she, recalling the usual hardness of her bed,] complained that she was idly wasting her life, since she was forced to take a break from the former exercises of penance, and that she remained, deprived of all merit of patience, from such great indulgence of the Fathers, She would daily sing to her holy Father Dominic the title Rose of Patience, and that she herself, patience being taken from her, was neither a Rose nor a Daughter of so great a Father; Finally, they would allow her, who did no good, to at least suffer evil and afflict her body. All he achieved was that one of the confessors granted the Virgin permission to spend the entire Lent of that year and the next (which was Rose's last) in a bed he had rebuilt from the old form of austerity, packed with wood, stones, and tiles. Rosa immediately did as she had been permitted and added the utmost secrecy to the utmost care, so that not even after her death (for it was only then that the matter became known) could this second bed of punishment be found anywhere. It is credible that she herself, as soon as the permission to use it expired with the Lenten fast, would have willingly paid the tax, scattered the fragments of shells, thrown away the pegs and the wood, so that nothing would be discovered. Although a diligent search, both by the mother and by the quaestor, has not been lacking in the house.

But neither had he abandoned Rosa, now near death, because of this almost contemporary, most severe desire to sleep. Semianimus lay on his own deathbed of extreme pain, [and in it, desiring to persevere until death,] deprived of that other deathbed of extreme pain. But who would have devised such a cruel little horse for the innocent little one who was already dying? At least he wished to be laid on the more humane layer of the couch he was leaning against, and to die naked on the floor. But when she realized that she would not be able to get this from her household servants, she addressed her brother, who was standing by, with her last words and asked him to remove her necklace. When these were set aside, the dying woman rejoiced that she felt herself leaning on the planks of the bier against her head and shoulders, and was wonderfully content that she would dwell with her crucified Spouse in the hard wood. A little later, she peacefully breathed out her happy spirit into the hands of the Creator. Nor were there lacking those who recognized in the dying man's pale face the very image of Christ expiring on the cross, which had been similarly observed in St. Catherine of Siena when she was also bedridden.

[110] [She would stay up late,] Moreover, anyone accustomed to the usual hardness of a bed will not find it difficult to understand how much time Rosa devoted to her vigils, since the bed itself robbed her of sleep. Saint Catherine once confessed to her blessed confessor Raymond that she had had to fight more earnestly against no enemy than against sleep. which he eventually reduced to the point that he was content with only two hours altogether. He put the same test on his pupil Rosa Catharina and honored her with the same victory: for she too confined her sleep within the narrow limits of two hours, and at times even granted the importunate exactor less time. Thus he divided his time between day and night so that he would set aside twelve hours in all for prayer from both, devote another ten to manual labor, by which he supported his parents, and allocate the rest to the body's necessities and brief rest. This facility for vigilance is aided by prolonged fasting, by wearing sackcloth and the hair-shirt, by abstaining for a long time from all food and drink, by deep meditation, and by the steadfast fixing of the mind on heavenly things.

[111] [and to the demon who instills drowsiness] Yet the cunning foe did not abandon his arts or his schemes, but most persistently strove to draw the Virgin back to sleep, especially when she had risen for her night prayer. These contests were a great trial for the roses: for whenever she began her prayers with her knees fixed to the ground, it seemed as if lead had been placed in her heavy eyelids. Whether standing erect, a most ponderous dizziness held sway over her head, a languid lullaby of drowsiness. Whether he had fallen to the ground with arms outstretched, he clung to the sweet caress of the soporific body. Rosam was ashamed to succumb to such a feeble adversary; she fought even as she fell, striking her neck against the wall, beating her sides to drive out the creeping lethargy. She was victorious in the end, but not without a struggle. He triumphed, but on the cross: for in the Virgin's chamber lay a wooden cross of human stature, from whose arms on either side protruded strong, solid nails, sufficient to support a body. So she applied both hands to the struggle against sleep, her little body swinging in a pendulum from their tenacious clasp, while she finished the Office of the Virgin Mother, broke the onslaught of slumber with these little jabs, roused herself for her watch, crucified the enemy, and only as a victor did she draw back the rigid tendons of her arms from the dear handles of victory: Thus she won the palm, but with calloused palms.

[112] [He resisted fiercely.] He also found another way to fight. He had fixed a slightly taller nail oblong on the cell wall, which rose almost to the palm's tip. Against the snares of sleep, he would most tightly bind that little tuft of hair which had remained on his forehead to cover his crown, and with that torment he would summon wakefulness, recite prayers, and strangle drowsiness. Yet so that she would not entrust the entire weight of her little body to a few strands of hair, she stood on the tips of her toes, touching the ground—but barely—and, with each successive sway, her slippery support wobbled. You, reader, can gather from such a dreadful remedy how severe the young woman's unrelenting sleep disturbance must have been: in bed she was beset by mice and thorns, and outside she had to contend with keys; whether Rose wished to sleep or to stay awake, she had to endure torments.

Notes.

The tonsils are the extreme parts of the throat around the root of the tongue, touching the gullet on both sides. Physicians call inflammations of the throat with swelling of the tonsils "tonsillitis," which the Greeks call paristimiae, on account of a similarity—see Castelli's Medical Lexicon, p. 373.

b Greek ἐπιγλωττὶς (as if you were to say in Latin super-tongue) It is a cartilaginous membrane of an oblong shape, located in the inner part of the throat, and it arises from the root of the tongue, which in Greek is called glōtta. According to Castalio, it is also called the lingula, ligula, that is, the tongue of the larynx: for it is a certain body resembling the tongue of some kind of pipe, where the artery and the larynx are connected with each other, and like a lid, always standing open whenever the animal breathes, through which it swallows, lying against the larynx, etc.

Bronchus (Greek βρόγχος) is, according to Galen, a certain cartilaginous body on the trachea, so named because it consists for the most part of cartilages, which the Greeks call bronchia. Hence a bronchocele is called a large, round swelling of the throat that arises in the trachea.

from Polygonus, that is, having many angles from the Greek adjective polygonous, which is composed of the adverb πολὺ, that is, much, and the word γωνία, which means angle. Hence the triangle and similar figures have their origin.
