Chapter IX.
Love of solitude, avoidance of company, and a very small room.

[Hating girlish games] Thus the love of solitude once carried St. Catherine of Siena, still a little girl, to the point of seeking out a cave. Thus, from early infancy, she taught Rosa to seek solitary corners at home and to avoid worldly commerce. Little girls from the neighboring houses would gather at her home to play, for that is the first passion of that age; each would bring her own dolls, to whom dressing, feeding, and adorning are the common occupations of girlhood. They displayed these with innocent rivalry to the silent Rosa, urging her to produce or fashion her own. She denied that she wanted to have or even touch a doll. that he had heard an evil spirit at one time utter a cackle through a puppet (perhaps it was an idol). Soon, having left her playmates to their dolls, she withdrew to a secret corner, as if to devote herself more quietly to her secret thoughts. There, sometimes discovered by her brother and asked what she would rather do, she would reply that she preferred to play with the boys in their festive nonsense than to sit in that dusty, flea-infested corner. She replied to the words, wise beyond her years: "Let me here withdraw alone with God; for among dolls, who knows whether God is present there as well?"

[114] [From tender years he loved solitude,] As he grew older, his desire to hide grew; Spotting some shady plane trees in the garden close to the wall, she chose a spot where, with the help of her brother Ferdinand, she could build a small oratory, hidden from all view of the household. There, by weaving a lattice of branches, palm fronds, and twigs, he enclosed a small space and skillfully arched it above with leafy curves. Inside, he affixed a humble little altar to the wall. He placed a cross of thicker paper, with little flowers and multicolored feathers, and diligently gathered whatever sacred images he could find and brought them into his basilica. It seemed that this little angel, Rosula, had packed all her delights into him, so completely did the solitary girl dwell in him all day long: she would hurry there from lunch, from her bed, from supper, as if to a retreat for prayer and meditation. there he withdrew from the household's turmoil, games, and stories, devoted to himself and to the heavens. Nowhere outside this paradise could he be happy, so much so that at home the proverb went: "Go to the garden if you seek a rose." But since she was only allowed to enjoy this comfort by day, when she had grown a little older she persuaded her mother to provide another room inside the house, in which she could sleep alone at night, apart from the other children. These were timely preludes to her tender disposition, so that from these forerunners one could infer what she would undertake later.

Gradually, by her stature and age, Rosa had come to be fit to accompany her mother on her outings, especially when more distinguished matrons were to be visited. But for his only daughter, who loved the wilderness, it was most troublesome to see or be seen in public. So she now besought her mother with prayer and with tears, not to take her away with her. She was astonished, not yet understanding why that very thing, which the other young women are accustomed to praise excessively, was so distasteful to Rosa. And so he compelled her, unwilling as she was, several times by his authority, certain that she would obey the order without reply. Once, when her mother was urging her to accompany her on the customary rounds of urban greetings, the virgin, passing by the domestic furnace, eagerly drew a stone from the furnace's opening onto her foot, and from that wound and contusion she was allowed to remain at home, limping not feignedly. And our Peruvian Rose blushed more truly and more charmingly with this wound to its foot than the legend tells of the Cyprian Roses, namely that they did not blush until the chance blood from the wounded heel of the goddess of Cyprus had sprinkled them.

[116] Moreover, the Virgin devised another stratagem so that, as a lover of solitude, she might more often use a cunning stratagem to redeem herself from the vexation of going out in public. Whenever she foresaw that her mother would invite her to a visitation, she would secretly smear her eyelashes and eyelids with Indian pepper, which is the most pungent, so that by this very rubbing her eyes would soon burn, redden, and stream, as if a bitter discharge from her head had suffused them. Thus, as the mother was about to leave and called her daughter to accompany her, it was enough to show eyes fluid and swollen with a noxious itch, as if they were then in no way fit for the open light to bear. Therefore, out of pity, the mother, so that the air outside might not harm her daughter's affected eyes, granted her the most eagerly desired freedom of solitude and concealment. A hard escape indeed. But to Rosa, her own seclusion was dearer than the pupil of her eye, and this cunning served her well for a long time, until at last the frequent comings and goings betrayed the matter to her mother, which is how it happened.

[117] By a mutual agreement, Lady Ludovica Vargas Carvajal and Maria de Oliva had arranged, [which the mother eventually discovered,] each taking her own daughters, to travel to the famous shrine of the Virgin, which is named Monte Serratob; The appointed day arrived, and the carriage with Lady Ludovica and her daughter Isabella Alexia stopped before the doors. The prepared mother calls Rosa; But this is revealed by his eyes swelling again and his eyelids inflamed with a saddened flush, so that it was clear his vision, inflamed by that discharge and blinking from excessive pain, could in no way endure the dangers of the breeze or the sun. The mother, now at last reflecting on herself, after she had politely excused herself to Louisa, began to ponder more attentively what she herself wanted, why that troublesome discharge was besieging Rose's eyes just then, whenever a visit from respectable matrons was to be received at home or paid abroad. Hence, looking more closely at her daughter's face, she saw that what appeared to be the case was not a sham. For both the blush of shame and the acrimony of pain were frequently affirmed by silent trickles. Finally, with her mother's permission, she brought her mouth and nostrils closer, caught the scent, and with her tongue protruding, detected the sharp bite of the most savage collyrium.

Who can recount the scoffs, the shouts, the taunts that the mother then hurled at her daughter? [whose anger he soothed with a gentle reply.] "What are these deceptions for?" he said. What good is such a foolish trick, if it pleased you to deceive your mother's eyes? But to play with your own eyes—what a crime, what a danger? Have you so soon forgotten that only recently the slave of Ferdinand Perez, by a very similar abuse of garlic and pepper, lost the sight and use of his eyes altogether? The Virgin replied briefly and modestly, "It would be better, O mother, for me to be blind than to see vain and worldly things in abundance." Stunned by the reply, the mother allowed her daughter to stay at home as long as she wished, only on the condition that she would no longer tear her eyes with this dangerous rubbing. Rose's long-awaited leave of absence proved as costly as very few would have been willing to buy (and most would have been unwilling to grant) at that price.

[119] [Since she wished to live alone at home in a cramped hovel,] So much did Rosæ prefer solitude and dislike public life that she not only scrupulously avoided private palaces and the city's public spectacles, but also shunned the general circuits of celebratory processions and the crowded supplications. She admitted that it was exceedingly troublesome and burdensome to her to see, amid women's conversations, pompous garments, cloaks, and precious headbands, to be drawn into idle tales, to be distracted by so many reciprocal, vain urbanities and rituals. Above all, she lamented that she was neither safe from visits nor free in her own private domestic retreat. She had already stopped accompanying her mother to such duties outside, but she wanted to escape being visited at home herself, and she could not. The Virgin's meekness, courtesy, and modesty drew distinguished women, so that, under the guise of honor as they approached the mother, they would ask to see the Daughter as well—whom the sweet fragrance of virtue, the nobility of her character, and the admiration of her sanctity had, even against her will, recommended to many distinguished persons. Nor could access to these be easily denied, however much Rosa hid herself. Lamenting the loss of precious time. And although the conversation was only about God, he maintained that it would always be more useful and more pleasant for him to speak with God than about God.

[120] [with her mother refusing,] Therefore, with the Divine favor inclining her, she discerned a nobler path by which she might bravely free herself from those impediments: she humbly besought her parents, asking that she be permitted in the farthest corner of the garden a solitary spot for a cell, which she desired to be of a size suitable only to her single stature, with a small window to admit light, the key to the little door to be held by her mother alone (if she wished): there she would most usefully devote herself to manual labor, to the study of prayer and meditation, and to the collection of her spirit without the slightest waste of time. He added solid and weighty reasons, dictated by heaven and the zeal for divine obedience. The mother denied that she would allow her daughter to hide herself alive in such a tomb. After fruitless prayers and stubborn refusals, the Virgin, as was her custom, fled to the help of the heavenly powers, constantly invoking God first and the Mother of God, so that they might by their powerful intercession win for her the maternal consent: and she did not doubt that she would be heard. However, moved to a higher level into the experiment of certainty, it awaited and obtained from heaven a kind of pledge-like down payment, which was as follows. She had in her poor treasure a precious crown of coral. He presented this so that in the chapel of the Holy Rosary, this splendid torque might adorn the image of the Blessed Virgin. Therefore he approached the known religious man, offered him the crown, and hastily placed it around the image's neck. He neglected that he lacked a ladder by which he could reach the lofty image on the altar.

[121] [She fled to the intercession of the Mother of God,] The next day the Virgin returned to the chapel to hear Mass, and when she noticed that her image was missing a crown, she earnestly implored the senior sacristan to finally place the crown around the image's neck, insisting that it was of the utmost importance. She wanted to sweetly captivate the Mother and Virgin with this precious bond, so that her Son would thus interpose himself as a surety. These last words were an enigma, which the sacristan did not quite understand at the time. He nevertheless did what Rosa asked, and, placing the ladder, he, not without some effort, placed the crown around the image's neck and removed the ladder. At last the appointed day arrived, when Rosa, about to see the surety of her granted grace, hurried to the Church of St. Dominic; and behold, upon entering the chapel of the Blessed Rosary, she saw her crown, loosed from the neck of the Virgin Queen, plainly hanging from the hand of the little Jesus. All who were present in the chapel saw the same thing. But they were unaware of the mystery they were implying: that the crown had been transferred by the sacristan himself from the neck of the Mother of God to the fingers of the little Son. The sacristan, warned of the miracle, came, saw, and was amazed, certain that neither he nor any of his own had touched the crown since the day it had been placed around the neck of the holy Virgin. Rosa alone, aware of the miracle and the silent interpreter of the sign, exulted in a secret joy, fully understanding what it all meant. For the Queen of the Holy Rosary had promised the supplicant Rose the grace (which she desired). The son finally stood as surety for his mother. Thus that coral pledge-bond had been transferred to it from the discharged Mother.

[122] Rosa, now secure in her vow by this marvelous sign [and, through friends sent to her mother, fulfills her vow], and almost fully recovered, appointed a solemn and honorable embassy to her mother: it was headed by the master and chancellor, Father Francis John de Lorenzana, Gundisalvus de la Massa, royal treasurer, and the treasurer's wife, Maria de Usatigui. It was the feast of the Blessed Virgin's purification in the temple when the three nuns, among them Mary of Oliva (more in congratulation than in reproach), laid before Rose her steadfast desire to build for herself a narrow, solitary cell in which, without her confessor's permission, she would admit no one's presence or conversation. The mother, who until that moment had been more impervious to such appeals than the stony slopes of the Apennines, as soon as she melted like wax gave way, demanding neither a moment's time for deliberation or delay in so arduous a matter. Surely, thus the God who stood as surety fulfilled His promises, holding in His hand not only a parent's heart but also His Daughter's heart.

[123] And having obtained this permission, how do you suppose Rosa spent that day in festive, sweet joy? [and there, engaged in pious exercises,] It seemed to him that what was being sung about the righteous old man was coming true in him: "Simeon received his answer." She would joyfully sing along with him this pleasant hymn: "Now You Dismiss," as if she had almost reached the harbor of her desires, to enjoy thereafter the secret embraces of her little Spouse, who leads into a solitary place to speak to the heart. At last, Rosa felt compelled to the first Responsory of that day: "Adorn your chamber, O Zion." The cell that Rosa had set aside for herself promised nothing more than a bed, and she thought it would be long before the next day's dawn, when she would begin her work. Without delay, the next day he adorned the bridal chamber, carried boards into the garden, and within a few days a perfect, brief, narrow, humble shrine of solitary devotion stood complete. The little hut was five feet long, four feet wide; You would have called it a chest rather than a box, or a rather large chest, if a small window on the side hadn't proclaimed it to be a little house. One of the Virgin's confessors casually remarked on the narrowness of her dwelling, to which Rose wittily replied that there was just enough space for herself and her heavenly Spouse.

Indeed, as I was saying, it was the bridal chamber that the Virgin, daughter of Zion, had adorned. [She received a twofold privilege from heaven;] Having acquired this desert, the fortunate Rose saw to it that not a single moment of time there should slip by without fruit. He would stay here all day, adding the greater part of the night. He most fittingly spent his hours on various exercises of piety. She arranged her ascensions in her heart with the freest exercise of contemplation, almost unaware whether she was in the body or outside it. After she had withdrawn here, she appeared in a rapture to a matron of proven sanctity in the form and likeness of a most brilliant star, whose rays and all-around sparkling brightness the narrow walls could in no way contain. Lima, otherwise called the City of Kings, ought to have had its own star, silently inviting to the Savior's narrow cradle. By the way, I should mention here that, as many who knew Rosa during her life observed, she was wonderfully delighted by the sight of a clear sky, so much so that the girl, utterly ignorant of all astrology, would sometimes spend a great part of the night motionless, sweetly absorbed in the spectacle of the fixed stars: for the new Star of the Magi loved her sphere. Her soul yearned for it, her starry nature longed for it alone, and the mind, born of the heavens, could not easily be torn away from the nightly gaze of celestial flames: she confessed that she was renewed and restored by the long contemplation of the heavens and the stars. Indeed, he also urged others that the clear sight of the serene firmament could be counted among the chief things that uplift the spirit. The devout matron did not stray so far as to fail to recognize Rose, a friend of the stars, in the shape of a star, whose only shelter then was a tiny cell.

[125] [so that she might attend Masses celebrated at a distance,] Those who knew of her ardent devotion to the Virgin in temples and sacred things were amazed that she so constantly clung to her little reclusory, that on weekdays she scarcely ever went to church; accordingly, she was asked by some one how her most devout heart could bear it, that she did not at least go to assist at the Sacrifice of the Mass, leave her little cell every morning only to return so soon? Hear the response of the most candid simplicity, and be amazed. Rosa added that this was by no means to be attributed to her own convent, but because her mother (with whom, in her early years, the confessor had ordered her to attend church alone) was hindered by domestic cares from going to Mass every day, divine goodness had nonetheless provided for her so that, while remaining in her cell, she might see and hear not one but several Masses each day. It was inquired into how this was, and it was discovered that it had been granted to the Virgin from above, that by spirit (as if she had been physically present) she was present daily at each Mass, however many were said in the neighboring hospice of the Holy Spirit. not only those, but also those that were celebrated far off in the distant church of St. Augustine, which was no less than four or five blocks away by winding streets from Rose's home. No one will deny that it was a great and marvelous consolation for the cloistered Virgin's most strict solitude.

[126] [& mosquitoes,] Another privilege of the now solitary Rose was the mosquitoes, indeed their deference. Where the Virgin's hermitage had been established, the damp soil and the abundance of saplings either bred an endless multitude of gnats or drew them into the shade of her dwelling. A troublesome animal to people, if any other: for with its single proboscis, it both plays the pipe and serves as a soldier, since it strikes with its tube no less than it torments the drowsy with its pipe and drum. These swarms and troops would migrate to Rose's hut, especially when either the sun's drying rays by day, or the chills of a clear evening under a serene night, threatened their tiny bodies. Yet not even among so many legions of mosquitoes was there a single one that ever touched Rosa in her cell. The walls were dripping with them from every side, the doorway echoed, the window was filled by the constant backwash of passersby. But each one was careful not to step on the roses, and as if by arrangement, they spared their hostess.

It happened that either the mother herself or, with the confessor's permission, some more devout persons [though hostile to other visitors] would visit Rosa in her narrow dwelling to converse about divine matters; but these, as soon as they sat down at the door or the little window, were hostilely attacked by an army of gnats. An importunate horde rushed to besiege his face and hands: one was repelled, four would take his place, and they would often draw blood from the unwary from ambush, leaving behind a trace of itching and pustules. They were amazed that, under this Egyptian plague, Rosa stood motionless for entire days. But they were even more astonished when they noticed that not the slightest sign of this bloody ordeal appeared on his face or in his hands. The maiden smiled and replied to her mother and the others: "When I entered here, I made friends with the mosquitoes and struck a bargain with them that they would never disturb or harm me, and in return I would never harm them." We have stood by our agreements on both sides, and we enjoy this common shelter without hostility. but moreover, the same people energetically help me, to the best of their ability, in praising God.

That was indeed the case. For whenever Rosa opened the cell door at first light, [they would not sting her,] and loosened the window shutters, she would call to the mosquitoes (whichever had spent the night clustered against the walls): "Come, friends, to the praises of Almighty God." Immediately they burst forth in the softest harmony into booms, and scattered in circles they wove various witty murmurs, the phalanx so orderly and twisted into itself with the most graceful curves that you would have thought it was either a chorus or a dance led by reason. Having completed this duty, they flew off to feed; similarly, when at sunset they returned to the hospitable little house, Rosa would again insist that, before they gave themselves to rest, they share with her a few evening praises to the Creator. Soon cheerful whispers filled the corners in eager competition, and the organ's pneumatic pipes boasted a flying harmony, until, at Rosa's command, they all fell silent at once, as if under a single law of nocturnal silence. Only over the vilest little creatures was the power of innocence reserved, which Rosa approached so closely that she wandered in the solitude of her cell as if in paradise.

[129] Sister Catherine of St. Mary of the Third Order of St. Dominic, [as experience has shown,] and a long-time companion of Lady Eleanor of Castile, visited Rose in her hermitage; But, unable to bear the mosquito's insolence, he killed one that was already swollen with blood with his hand. Like a rose in wonder, she said, "What are you doing, dearest sister?" "Have my guests been slaughtered for me?" But Catherine said, "Call them enemies rather than guests, for behold how this mosquito is full of my blood." She replied, "What great matter is it that we have fed this tiny creature with our blood, when its Creator, who sucks us so often, feeds on our very gore?" Therefore, do not go on killing my mosquitoes, and I in turn promise you that they will treat you as they treat me and keep the peace. It is done. For from then on, no mosquito there ever bit or sucked Catherine. It is likewise established that the same thing happened to the mother, to the quaestor Gundisalvus, to his wife, and to others, so that there could be no doubt about the innocent Rose's admirable authority over the household flies. Sister Francisca de Montoya experienced it somewhat differently; she too was a tertiary alumna of Saint Dominic the Father. These sacred conversations with Rosa, seeing the hungry assault of so many flies, were somewhat frightened. When Rosa turned and saw it, she said, "Do not be afraid, sister;" In honor of the Holy Trinity, only three mosquitoes will bite you today. From then on, you will be immune (like me). It happened, just as the Virgin had foretold: a mosquito stung Francisca three times; from that day forward, in the presence of Rosa, she was always free from them. That this marvelous privilege of immunity was possessed by that solitary retreat from heaven, is well; That she was able to share it with others there, Rosa, was a unique thing.

[130] [while she persevered to the end of her life in a similar way of life.] In the last three years of her life, in the house of the treasurer Gundisalvus, the Virgin had appointed for herself a desert of almost equal solitude, and for the entire day and night, she would either hide herself in the domestic oratory, or keep herself constantly confined within the innermost chamber of the house behind barred doors, arranging everything and interposing every obstacle to avoid useless visits. Sometimes, with the permission of her confessors and of Gundisalvus himself, she would go to her mother's house to enjoy the sweet solitude of her former cell for a few days. and she has often complained most grievously in the presence of her intimates that she is afflicted by a constant pain, because her sex and her station in life would not permit her to retreat far away into the most rugged mountains, and there seek out a hollow rock, where, removed from all human contact, she might live. He constantly praised the most blessed times of the ancient hermits, sighed at every mention of Nitria and Thebaid, and, to sum up in a word, his desires had migrated to the burning Rose of St. Catherine of Siena, for whom he had once earnestly longed for the caves of the desert.

Notes.

The rose is a sacred flower to Venus, who, while rushing to aid Adonis wounded by a boar, ran on bare feet and was pricked in the foot by a thorn, staining the rose with her own blood, from which the roses, which had previously been white, then took on a red color, as the fabled history of the poets relates. The author alludes to this myth of the Cypriot goddess or Venus.

Not only in Spain is there a famous image of the Virgin of that name, but also in the city of Lima, as is read in the above-cited Francisco de Echave in his Spanish work on St. Turibius, page 227.

There are the famous deserts of Egypt, which were once inhabited by holy anchorites.

Chapter Ten
A wonderful betrothal to Christ, following the example of St. Catherine of Siena.

[This Virgin imitated St. Catherine of Siena] From the History of the Life of the Blessed. Rosa of Siena had learned that the seraphic virgin, still mortal, had been wondrously betrothed to Christ as her Spouse. She too ran along the bride's trail, following the scent of the Bridegroom, but among the young women, as it seemed to her; For his deep self-deprecation would not allow him to dare, even to the singular dignity of a bride and the title of such exalted rank, to aspire of his own accord. And yet in the treasures of the Rose there shone virgin purity and abyssal humility, which prepare the proper disposition for these nuptials and constitute the dowry: whereby it comes to pass that the augustest Empress of the angels presides by a special right over such rare weddings, since the singular Virgin is privileged with the supreme purity and humility. And each, as in the others, so more famously in these two, seemed neither the first of its kind nor to have a successor.

[132] Already the humility of the Rose. We have treated V in any case; [By humility and chastity, God was thus pleased,] purity must be dealt with here before the heavenly nuptials. But for this, the unanimous testimony of the confessors, as many as have received the general exomologesis of the Virgin, shall suffice for us in short. These were eleven. namely, six from the Order of Preachers, five from the Society of Jesus, and each one separately (as with one voice, and under oath) testified that the Virgin of the Rose had always shone with such purity that she was never stained by the venial spot of any lasciviousness. Nor in his entire life did a single foul thought ever creep into his mind. Indeed, the scarab does not touch the Hyblean rose. Thus such a rare gift of chastity, such humble and pure virginity, consecrated to Christ by a vow from the first five years of her life, was both a flower and a dowry for the future marriage of the Nazarene Spouse: as the rose loved the lily among thorns, so she felt herself to be despicable. Hence, prodigies were necessary, by which he would be prepared from afar for the mystery and raised to consent.

[133] The first (of those that became known) was this: [that after certain obscure preludes,] when that two-colored butterfly (of which above) chapter IV) Flying down from above, it settled on Rose. It was noted that for a while it wandered, crawling along the left side of the Virgin, until it came to a stop directly opposite the heart. There she seemed to be occupied more slowly and lovingly: for, like a bee intent on building a honeycomb, she turned in short circles, as if sketching something with a brush. When the work was finished, she vanished, and behold, the image of a heart, fully and most skillfully formed, was seen on Rose's garment, which, left there by the butterfly depicted on it, was clearly observed by the girls who were working in the same room. but these were ignorant of the secret symbol of the heart, inscribed on the virgin's heart. Only Rosa felt (but even more obscurely) the Spouse crying out from afar: Give me your heart. He conjectured that he would be distinguished not only by the multicolored butterfly but also by the same emblem of the heart that the Eternal Spouse had once exchanged with his Sienese bride. Nevertheless, these things were too remote to give any certain hope of such a marriage, and yet (as later became clear) they were, from afar, a prelude to that happy betrothal.

[134] Let us be a little more explicit about the other, which follows. After donning the white garment of the Third Order, another night in a dream the most beautiful and most graceful form of a man was shown to Rosa: it almost suggested a heavenly being, indeed the very one Fairer in form than the sons of men, were it not that his attire suggested a noble craftsman of marble or of cutting stone. And indeed, it was he. but then he had come, hastening and in love, as though to seek the Virgin there as his bride. Rosa, to whom the very thought of marriage had never once been pleasing, even in her dreams, felt deep down that nothing more fortunate could come into her entire life than that one marriage. This was the sympathetic grace that taught Rosa to love the secrets she did not know. So the most chaste turtle dove assented to the proposed marriage, and with the marital vow exchanged on both sides, that gracious Spouse departed, as though about to undertake a necessary journey, but not before he entrusted his new Consort with the task of carving, shaping, and polishing a certain number of marbles there, until his return from abroad. And Neonymphe, admonishing his wife Rosa that it is proper for a married couple to leave father and mother and become two in one flesh, told her to henceforth lay aside the care of supporting her parents, saying that he would otherwise provide for them so that they would lack for nothing.

Then the bridegroom was seen to have returned from his journey, marvelous. But Rosa, who had not yet completely finished her stonecutting work, blushing with shame, tried in many ways to excuse herself, saying she had been hindered by her parents' needs and was still unskilled in an art so unusual for a woman's hands, accustomed only to linen and wool. The Groom, smiling, said, "Do not think, my beloved, that you are the only woman I have occupied with these hard labors": and immediately, with the door of the adjoining room thrown wide open, he revealed to Rose a vast and splendid stone-cutting workshop, filled by the virgins alone, who were diligently intent on the arduous work. In place of a needle, there was a hammer and a chisel; instead of wool, there were the hardest stones and rocks. They were occupied with pounding, leveling, and skillfully squaring these to a standard; they cut, hollowed, smoothed, and scraped, and, so that the tool would take a harder stone more quickly, they thoroughly softened it with the frequent trickle of their tears. It seemed all the more marvelous that, amid these rough heaps of stones and dusty works, each virgin wore a garment not of plebeian, artisan-made cloth, but a precious, gleaming, and solemn one, more fitting for a wedding feast or a public theatrical pageant than for a sweat-drenched workshop.

[136] [and the mystical vision,] The Virgin marveled at the virgins engaged in such an unusual task, but it signified virtue occupied with arduous deeds. He surveyed the already finished, completed, and set-aside marbles of the individual artisans, in none of which anything was lacking to the most exacting perfection. At last, Rosa, by a chance glance cast upon herself, saw that she too, like the other virgins there, had been made glorious by the same most splendid robe. And she, who until then had shone only in her simple Dominican habit, suddenly found herself arrayed in a girdle adorned with gems and gold, so that she recognized herself as having been commissioned to join in those same labors there. How many and what mysteries Rosa learned from this single vision will appear in detail in what follows; here the story hastens to the outcome of the betrothal arranged solely with the Mason.

[137] [which is in the Rosary chapel] And now, after such preambles, the final one remained, by which this Spouse would at last manifestly and openly make himself known to the watcher of the Rose, and, having laid aside the guise of a mason, would more openly invite the Virgin to the nuptial chamber. Thus it happened, and in this order: on Palm Sunday, after the annual blessing had been performed, the sacristans distributed the branches throughout the choir and the church while the solemn procession was being prepared. Meanwhile, she was waiting there with the other tertiary sisters and Rose for her palm; Kneel in the chapel of the Virgin Mary's Rosary. But whether through negligence, and the error of a hasty sacristan, or (what I am more inclined to believe) by a singular design of Providence, it happened that on this occasion the Rose was without a stem, altogether contrary to custom; For in the previous years, in the distribution of palm branches (as a Daughter of the Order), she had never been omitted.

[138] Confused by this novelty, the Virgin—as is the way with the more tender of conscience—dreaded, before the image of the Mother of God, lest by some perhaps oblique movement of the mind she might have rendered herself unworthy of the company of that branch-bearing procession. He nevertheless went around with the rest of the faithful people. but sad and shy And having completed the circuit, she returned straight to the Rosary chapel (her haven): there at the feet of the Virgin Mother she poured out her heart through streaming tears, reproaching herself that she had either too ambitiously coveted the blessed palm, or through inertia had not sought it. Then, gazing at the face of the most august Mother of God, when she saw it serene and smiling on her more sweetly than usual, she recovered her spirits, and was now pleased with that very thing which had before saddened her. "Far be it," he said, "that I should receive the palm from mortal hands." O Lady, you exalted palm in Cadiz, you will bless me with an unfading branch.

At these words, she soon saw the Queen of Heaven, with a beaming and cheerful countenance, transformed into the little Son she was carrying in her arms, and from him, as from a favorable oracle, she cast a more gracious look upon Rosa. Struck with joy by this unknown thing, she turned her eyes to the little boy and saw him smiling at her in the same way. Between these two honeyed faces, in mutual ardor, fluttered Rosa, hurrying from one to the other, delighting in the alternating spectacle, and the more often she returned to either, the sweeter she found the face. On several occasions in the same holy image (as will be related below), this sweet gesture had moved the praying Rose. but never with that excess of sweetness, that aura and grace of familiarity, that most singular flash of hidden favor. so that he already felt deep in his soul that something great was being accomplished within him, and that his Lapicida was not far from him. They were emotions for which the crude ignorance of mortals has not yet found names.

What more is there to say? The little boy Jesus finally burst into this song of the drama. [Christ may have betrothed her to himself,] "Rose of my heart!" Be my bride. These words penetrated to the marrow of Rose, &, as if struck by a sudden blow, she fell lifeless within herself, and in a brief struggle of confused emotions, she was at once torn apart by a trembling plunge into the abyss of her own nothingness, and she swam, exalted by a swelling of joy; yet her spirit, not yielding to the miracle of such exaltation, could find no answer, save for the words of the purest humility: "Behold the handmaid of the Lord. Behold (she said) a handmaid, behold your handmaid, O King of eternal majesty; I am yours, I profess myself yours, I will be yours. He was preparing to say more. but the sobriety of the melting little soul was continually turned into the same proverb: drunkenness. And the infant love, which could not speak, longed to stammer. Surely he did not err, whoever he was, the boy who painted love.

[141] But the Virgin Mother, who at the wedding in Cana of Galilee once spoke up [and had received for herself great treasures of grace], here by no means remained silent, especially since she herself alone was present as the bridesmaid of this betrothal, had alone presented the Rose, and had alone implored the Son as sponsor. And so, she herself addressed the new bride; "See (he said) O Rose, the extraordinary favor with which my Son has honored you." It was a nuptial song that the Bridegroom's Mother sang, not with David's harp, but with her own mouth. Rosa was laboring under a burden of joy, and could not contain herself, for she truly knew it to be said: "Though love is a fire, it bears no light weight." It was scorched and withered; the Virgin, long since forgotten, for whom the lily of the valleys—so beautiful, so precious—had taken its place. Finally, it was most painful to him that on the same day and in the same place he had lost both the palm and the rose, since after this marriage they were no longer his. Then followed the most opulent bridegroom's nuptial gifts: for (as in the examination, of which below in the chapter… XIV, compelled to confess) rare fires of seraphic love descended upon Rose from the innermost treasures of grace, and with them drew in long train the inestimable riches of various charisms, by which the soul of the Bride was enriched, illuminated, and adorned, through the lavish magnificence of so noble a Spouse: they were pledges and jewels, and precious arrabones of the given faith, inexpressible in human words: hence, when the Virgin was pressed by her examiner on these matters, and dared not deny, she excused herself most simply by the mere poverty of words.

[142] [A Rose in Memory of a Benefit] Furthermore, so that a constant memorial of so great a benefit might be ever before her eyes, when she returned home, she at once began to think of having a wedding ring cast, which she would wear on her ring finger, as a pleasant memory of her nuptials, forever and openly. And with his brother, the only one of his kind, as his advocate, he briefly explained his desire and concealed the mystery. He, seizing a compass, drew a perfect ring shape on the paper, and to the proper proportion added the figure of a circle, on which the Virgin had wished the little Jesus to be engraved. All that remained was for them to agree on the motto to be inscribed on the ring's convex surface around its band. Here Rosa, looking at her brother, waited to see what he would decide. He, without delay, as if he had been the arbiter at Rose's secret wedding, inscribed these very words in a ring: "Rose of my heart, be my bride." Rose was dumbfounded and stunned by a sudden joy when she saw that her brother had, not by deliberation but by impulse, rashly plunged into the symbol, which was the very crux of the entire affair. in the lemma, which Christ himself had dictated and graciously expressed with his own mouth, he knew that it could not have been known to his brother, or to any mortal, by human means, but that it was the dictate of a higher instinct. Thus, leaping with a new joy, she silently adored the hand of the Bridegroom playing in the sweetest wonder.

[143] [He ordered a wedding ring to be made.] He then brought the ring, now more precious for its origin, on the Thursday of the same week to the church, and by great entreaty obtained from the sacristan a place for it in the little reliquary that serves as the repository for the Blessed Eucharist during those three days, and as a monument to the buried Redeemer. There she wished to bury her pledge of love with her dead Spouse for a time, so that when he rose again she might return to the light along with him: thus she declared to her beloved Spouse that she was not freed from the law of the husband even by his death, since she longed to share with him a common marriage bed even in death. Finally, on the solemn day of Easter, this ring, now made more sacred and dear by its three-day companionship with the Lord's sepulchre, returned to Rosa. And before the image of the Virgin of the Rosary, where the sacred bond of matrimony had been contracted, he slipped on the Rosary ring, not without a new miracle: for the ceremony was so secret that even the mother, kneeling close to the Virgin, did not notice the slightest thing, though she was most attentive to everything concerning her Daughter.

After the Virgin's death, a ring remained with her very modest personal effects, in the possession of the wife of Gundisalvus the treasurer, [whose touch after the Virgin's death] and this remarkable event occurred in the year 1618, in the month of February, in the house of the said treasurer. That man, conspicuous for his reputation of holiness (of whom, above, at the end of chapter 8), while he was handling the crown of thorns of the deceased Rose out of devotion, it stuck fast in the ring for a long time; which, as it had been handed to him by D. Maria de Usategui, he clutched in his fist, wrapped in a small piece of paper. And behold, whatever he had begun to feel from the touch of the crown, he felt far more strongly from the ring. The most powerful, sweet, and delicate touches above; Unusual, lofty, precious lights; The ardors of the Spirit, effective up to the melting of the soul, are gentle, subtle. They seemed to be excerpts from the wedding feast with which Rosa was enjoying herself in heaven with her Spouse.

Shortly afterward, this servant of God, as if spurred on by inner promptings, broke out in words: "Blessed be God, who is so marvelous in His Rose." Praised be the Holy Trinity, before whom Rose stands among the chief citizens of paradise on the highest summit of glory. Beloved Rose! Rose is chosen! Rose, bride of the most pure Lamb! You are great, you are sublime, glorious, resplendent, incomparable in the triumphal procession of the Saints." He repeatedly bellowed many similar things from a heart full of the sweetest feeling, yet his body remained completely motionless: for that innermost jubilation, having loosened the tongue and mouth of the seated man, had bound the rest of his limbs, so that he could neither rise from his seat nor move his arms or legs, which were stiffened by stupor. The right hand, which had entwined the ring, rested on the side of the seat's rib, but it lay fixed, as if nailed in place. He had placed his left hand over his heart, against the excessive fluttering of overflowing joy. but she too seemed fastened in an insoluble way: the lucky man tried again and again to wrench himself from the seat, to pull his thighs apart, or at least to draw in his arms, but he could do none of it. Meanwhile the tongue, the only part that could move, went on, now praising God, now praising Rose, while the whole person struggled with the excessive vehemence of her inner joy.

The quæstor's wife, long entertained by the devoted spectacle, [unable to contain herself with pre-spiritual joy,] calls her husband, as if to the perfunctory duty of a greeting: for the guest, who had taken his seat, was a very well-known and beloved figure in that household. As the quaestor Gundisalvus approached and greeted the man courteously, he saw that he barely rose and soon realized he could not. He tried in vain to utter the customary greetings and excuses; for he kept slipping back into the same praises of God and Rose. Then the querulous one, with the sweetness of sighs, accused the violence of the joys that the happy Rose's ring was bringing: "O, loves!" Oh, what little flames! What fervor! How sweetly the torrent flows, how it rushes through the very marrow! Oh, fires! Oh, what a delight! "O bonds of a burning soul, most tenacious, most delightful ties, inescapable embraces!" Gundersalvus, caught between pity and congratulation, asked whether it would please him to release the ring (from which these torments of joy arose), so that he might free himself from its prison of repose and delight. The man was silent, then finally nodded. But he could by no means straighten his hand and stretch out his fingers. The questor offered help: he accepted.

[147] Strange to say! He found the man Gundisalvus's arm stiff and inflexible, [and frozen in place.] His right hand was drenched in a cold sweat, as is common in the dead, its fingers twisted, entwined, and knotted around a ring so tightly that it took both force and time to straighten them out. From there, turning to the left, it was necessary to struggle with equal breath so that it might be torn from the chest. But once the ring was removed, the festivities ceased, the bonds were broken, and the freed man rose from his seat, who had just a little while before been held captive in the joyful bonds of marriage. He seemed to have shed his sleeves and stockings at once when the ring was restored, though he admitted it had been far better for him in that delightful shackle than in his compassionate friend's hasty release. Finally, he swore the oath of silence and left. If in another's hand the bridal ring of Rose could do so much, what could not the marriage itself do in the soul of the Bride?

Notes.

Hybla is a mountain of Sicily abundant in thyme. Hence the Hylæan bees are celebrated among the ancients, as is clear from this Virgilian verse: "The Hylæan bees have stripped the willow of its blossom."

I do not fully understand why Hansen calls the Hyblæan roses by that name, unless perhaps he meant to designate with this epithet those that are more beautiful or more fragrant: for scarabs or cantharides are driven away by the scent of any rose, as Ulysses Aldrovandus notes in his Work on Insects, book. 4 pages 464 is referred to by these words: "Blindflies and scarabs and many insects of this kind are so delighted by the stench and filth from which they originated and by which they subsist, that they are utterly stupefied and perish at the sweetness of odors; therefore, scarabs or blindflies that settle on roses are said to become torpid or eventually die." Then Aldrovandus, on the same page (469), confirms this by the authority of the ancients in the following manner: It is remarkable that scarabs have such a power of life that they even survive being cut in pieces, yet they die from the scent of roses, as Aristotle and Pliny have recorded.

How spiritual joy this man must have been filled with while handling the sharp crown of St. Rose, as we noted above. 95 was briefly reported.

Chapter Eleven
Constant study of prayer, and intimate union with God.

[Saint Rose was vacant] If, who adheres to the Lord, is one spirit with him, 1 Cor. 6, it will be permissible to infer from this how sublime a degree of divine union Rose had attained, who by the familiarity of prayer was continually clinging to God alone. Even as an infant, the inward anointing of the Spirit had taught her to pray and had instilled in her a fervor that, for the most part, not even her nightly dreams could divert to other objects. Hence, she was very often heard at night, while asleep, counting and repeating in equal number the little prayers with which she had kept vigil more at length during the day. As he grew in years, so did his religion grow, and with his stature, the elevation of his mind to God: for even in his boyhood he practiced a serious and frequent meditation. Through sudden and precocious growths, her lofty expertise in contemplation matured and reached such a point that, by about the twelfth year of her age, it was perceived to have already ascended to that degree of prayer which mystical theology calls the unitive. Thus it was established in the examination, about which see below, chapter. Fourteen.

[149] Moreover, she had grown accustomed to two modes of prayer: the first, and chief, was when, her body and mind both occupied, she would most quietly gather herself together to speak with God; the other, when, amid manual labors and external occupations, he fixed his mind immovably on God. He had set aside no fewer than twelve hours of any natural day (as we noted above). His posterior was continuous, and, as far as the course of our mortality allows, subject to no interruption, except when he endured visions at the appointed hour, of which in the next chapter.

[150] This was a rare and singularly admirable gift of the omnipotent right hand in the Virgin, [one aspect of which was its constancy] that whether the Rose slept or was awake, the presence of the heavenly Spouse never and nowhere slipped from the eye of her mind; while she was spinning, weaving, painting flowers on silk with a needle, talking with others, eating, reading, walking, in the temple, in the garden, at home, outside, in the street, in a cell, everywhere she turned, that lovely Majesty, whom angels serve and whose beauty the sun and moon admire, was close to her inward gaze (as if in a mirror). And, what surpasses all reason and admiration, this divine presence occupied Rose's interior faculties without any notable abstraction from the senses, so that, while she was speaking to God inwardly, she would simultaneously, and without impediment, attend to other necessary matters with her household outside. He answered questions in sequence, appropriately and in order; he conducted himself expeditiously, arranged his agenda, and carried out his plans with facility, attentiveness, and promptness—qualities typical of those who devote themselves exclusively to external affairs.

[151] Indeed, in St. Catherine of Siena a most similar paradigm of grace had already appeared, [by which she was thus united with God,] when, condemned to the noisy duties of the kitchen, she built in the innermost recess of her soul that most blessed chamber of unperturbed peace, into which no importunity of external tumults could penetrate. Thus Rose, wholly outward and wholly inward, while outwardly she busied herself with frequent ministry, was at the same time delighted with the Spouse in the secretest chamber of her heart. Hence they often observed the quæstor's little daughter herself doing this: when, while sewing, she drew the thread and needle upward, her arm and spirit seemed to be lifted into ecstasy; and, after a brief moment, her hand suspended in midair, she returned to her work, and the needle never strayed from the point it had left.

Another equally marvelous gift was added to this endowment, so that external objects would not distract her: when she prayed, she was not easily moved by things that had little to do with her, as if Rose's external senses had grown numb to everything except these. Hence, devout women often noted that whenever she had taken a corner in the most crowded temple, opposite the high altar, to pray more attentively, she would sit for hours motionless, her eyes fixed on the altar; she neither saw those who passed by nor turned her gaze to the sudden noises around her. in fact, if anything seemed to be heading straight for her pupils, she wouldn't bat her eyelashes, close her eyelids, or turn her face away; You would have thought it blind, when it was at its most eagle-eyed, gazing in wonder at its own sun.

[153] Hence the rock-like immobility of her own little body, [and she would long seem to cling to it as if motionless.] while she was occupied with prayer in the temple, in the chapel of the Rosary, in the domestic oratory: for whatever place and posture she had assumed at the beginning of her prayer, she was found in exactly the same position after hours, after a full day, after a solid day and night. Thus Gundisalvi's wife, at around the fourth hour of the afternoon, found Rosa in the same kneeling corner she had occupied at noon for the exercise of meditation. Thus in the churches, where either the Forty Hours' devotion or otherwise the Sacrament of the Holy Eucharist was exposed for adoration, Rosa (who had been seated there from early morning) would remain in her place as if petrified until evening, without refreshment or movement. Thus, in the domestic oratory of the steward, when Rosa shut herself in from the morning of the Friday holiday until Saturday (sometimes even until Sunday), she first asked Our Lady of Usategui not to be summoned during that entire interval, whatever might happen, even if Rosa's own mother came to speak with her daughter: When asked the reason, she frankly replied that she could not rise from the floor during that entire period, not even to open the door of the oratory if someone should happen to knock.

Contemplating the divine attributes, he had set aside three hours each day, which he devoted separately to giving thanks to God in the morning, at noon, and in the evening. In that most tender affection she recalled each of God's benefits toward her, marveled at His condescension toward her, and adored the munificent, prodigious generosity of so many gifts, graces, and charisms; and indeed she had new ones every day, which she would recite from the little book of her most attentive gratitude. He also established for himself a certain highest, luminous, and fiery mode of prayer, by which, mentally reviewing the glorious attributes of God one by one, he offered to each its proper worship of latria. However, since in these matters he trusted rather to another's than to his own ingenuity, he consulted a pious and learned theologian of the Society of Jesus, demanding that a sincere compendium of the divine epithets from the sacred Scriptures be compiled for him. He complied, and gathered a few things. But to a virgin, the magnitude of the affection seemed small: she had desired a bundle, not a sheaf. Thus, from Father Master Francis John of Lorenzana, a larger supplement was obtained, until the total number reached one hundred and fifty. He arranged these in the most perfect order into fifteen decades, and placed the verse under each decade: Glory be to the Father, etc. As it was &c. Poro affirmed that this formula of prayer was a terror to demons. She would certainly never have affirmed it, unless she had proven it by her own experiment. Finally, her heart's very systole and diastole were driven almost entirely by these attributes of God, and she had advanced so far in them that she once modestly confessed that even while sewing, at each puncture of the needle, she compelled herself to utter a new encomium of God.

[155] [& always speaking with or about God,] Furthermore, it was noted that the Virgin was so devoted to prayer that her familiar conversations with others usually also contained the power of prayer. She would answer everyone who asked, and at the same time her speech was a sign of a mind lifted up to God: for example, if, upon entering the garden with other women, the beauty of the green space was more lavishly praised, she would politely reply, "The garden is lovely and beautiful; may God increase its flowers." But she herself especially pointed to the garden of her soul, for which she prayed to God for the flourishing growth of virtues; and thus, ingeniously enclosing a double meaning in a single proverb, she satisfied the speaker with one sense and addressed God with the other. This was often observed, and in the most diverse matters, so that scarcely any of them escaped this spiritual and holy skill of equivocation, so that nothing might escape the Rose's mouth that was not a prayer.

[156] It happened that one day, returning home from church, [rapt in ecstasy by a little bird's song,] he felt unwell and had decided to prepare for himself a porridge of water and crushed bread. And so that she could light the fire, she had borrowed a burning brand from elsewhere. As he returns, he hears a songbird on the pergola. Rosa paused, not deigning to listen to the bird for a while, since it was, as she imagined, singing praises of God in its own way. The little bird continued to sing, varying the witty tricks of its charming voice, doubling its gurgles. But Rosa, suddenly abashed, rebuked herself: "What," she said, "is this rude little creature praising my Creator and its own, forgetting its own pasture?" And will I be busy preparing food for myself? How little the Author of nature has given to this little creature, and yet with its whole heart and all its strength, it repays a tribute of praise to Him. As for me, I am thinking about my food, and I am not concerned about what I shall repay the Lord for all that He has repaid me! Then, turning to the torch she had been carrying, she saw it completely extinguished, and was amazed that she had stood there for so long while it could gradually be put out by the tinder. She believed she had not been attentive to the little bird's music for even half an hour, and perhaps a full hour had already passed. Thus, drawing herself inward once more, she too began to praise God with such fervor that it gradually carried Rosa into ecstasy, and she did not come out of it until evening, having begun to listen to the little bird's melody in the morning, about three hours before noon.

It would be paradoxical if Rosa, who urged others on with such great effort, had not persuaded herself just as earnestly. Whatever he urged on his neighbors, whatever he impressed upon them, his sole aim in speaking was one. To attract others to this exercise and school, he relied on his brother Ferdinand, promising that the effort would be slight and the reward exceptional and invaluable. He, not doubting the fruit, believed the labor and difficulty to be greater than the Sister either preached or would have it believed. Whatever the Virgin boasted about the ease of the most salutary exercise, he himself interpreted as mere hyperboles and fables of feminine simplicity; yet Rosa did not cease to teach her brother by what method and with what reasoning he should keep his mind fixed in prayer amid the occupations of the world. With equal diligence she read the devout books she knew how to handle most skillfully on the subject of prayer, and she also advised others to read them with eloquent effectiveness. Among these stood out the learned Works of Louis of Granada on prayer and meditation, which Rosa had divided into sections for constant development and review, and had marked with various colored notations to show what she was to read each day.

[158] Nor with less care did he admonish, beg, and implore the confessors, as many as he could reach [then through his own confessors], to induce their penitents by every counsel and art to undertake the exercises of mental prayer. if they would turn their attention, this is a great pharmacopoeia, most fully equipped with every antidote against sins, by which the spirit is purified through the use of prayer, the wounds of the soul are bound up, the Spouse's perfumed chamber is opened, and the entire apothecary of ointments. Nor was Rosa content with this; she also most frequently urged the preachers of the divine word to inflame their hearers with exercises in prayer, to popularize the practice of pious meditations, and to recommend, both privately and publicly, the usefulness, sweetness, and excellence of this discipline with all the force of their arguments and the full exertion of their eloquence.

[159] It particularly moved Rosa, a devotee of the Virgin Mary's Rosary, to recite it. [He encourages the study of prayer;] but in the same rite and rule by which St. Dominic had originally handed it down to the world, by instruction of the Mother of God, that is, with the inseparable, decade-by-decade consideration of the mysteries of our redemption. He loved this form of prayer because it combined mental and vocal prayer, and each of its parts and affections—petition, praise, thanksgiving, and so on. Thus Rosa had fastened a bracelet of the finest little beads around her arm, whose beads she secretly drew at each Angelic Salutation, even when she found herself absorbed in conversation. This example certainly benefited many, and Rose's ardent exhortation that they become more familiar with frequenting the praises of God.

[160] [For indeed he planted and set up trees,] Indeed, this fervor for prayer in Rosa was of such great energy that it miraculously inclined even the most insensitive plants, the dullest wood, and the mute trunks to offer praise, reverence, and adoration to God. I bring an unheard-of thing. At early dawn, Rosa, as was her custom, was returning to her hermit's cell. As she opened the garden gate, she invited the trees, the shrubs, and all the herbs she saw in the distance to bless the Creator with her: "Bless the Lord, all you things that germinate on the earth." And behold, the twigs suddenly stirred into harmony and concert, leaves collided with leaves, the rustling of leaves resounded from every side, the humble little plants stirred their heads; The very legumes, flowers, shrubs, and clusters, through varied agitation, are somehow enlivened into a placid harmony of hymeneal murmuring. Nor was that enough: the shrubs themselves bent their branches to the ground, as if to kiss the soil in reverence to their Creator.

[161] [not without a prodigious miracle,] It happened that one morning a person of proven faith had joined Rose as a companion on her way to the garden; They had scarcely entered when, at Rose's usual invitation, she saw the trees bend in a rush, so that their very tops touched the ground and slowly swept the earth, as in the most solemn rite of profound adoration. The companion was astonished, especially because she saw that what Rosæ was seeing was neither strange nor new. For the conspicuous frequency of this prodigy became known from other sources as well, so that the Virgin neither tried to conceal it nor could deny it. He nevertheless added: "See whether that admirable artisan of the world becomes worthy of being loved." See if that eternal Majesty deserves to be praised, honored, and adored by us, to whom this earthly seed, the rudimentary trunks, and all plants, in whatever way they can, whisper a song of praise and pay homage with bowed heads.

[162] [stirs up to divine praises,] One more thing, and I'm done. In the year that was Rose's last, throughout all Lent a small bird would fly in at the setting sun near Rose's bedroom, but wonderfully vocal and sonorous; And there, the neighbor's tree stood at the front, as if about to burst into song at a given signal. Rose, upon seeing this evening choir, herself prepared to sing praises to God. Then, as if a signal had been given, he invited the little bird with a meter he had composed for that purpose, in almost these words.

Loose the threads, Philomela
      Bring forth a sweet song:
Let a breathless voice sing a hymn,
      Let us praise the Lord.
Praise your Creator.
      My Savior, I:
God of us both
      I will praise. Open your beak,
Open your throat by singing.
      Resounding in alternation,
Let us give melody to voices.

[163] Immediately the little bird would begin a witty little ditty with the softest drawl, [which she had long had with the bird] and gradually, growing more vocal, would rise into an epiphany. Completing an ode winding through many bends, she yielded the turn to the Virgin. Following the silent little bird was Rosa, and with the voice in which she excelled, she continued the divine praises with a most sweet and tinkling sound, her talent miraculously fruitful and prompt. As soon as Rosa fell silent, the little bird would abruptly resume its song, double the minutiae of its interruptions, confuse the accents with trembling, whirling arterioles, depress, elevate, and twist the most delicate witticisms of its slippery little voice, and suddenly, as if on a pause sign, it would fall silent. Rose's rival voice would continually enter, renewing the jubilation and magnifying the ineffable perfections of the Most High. It was lifted up by the urgent spirit, languished with eloquent sighs, and finally, with its abrupt silence, would bring its antiphon back to song.

Thus Rose and the little bird took turns in praising God, each singing in turn, and not perfunctorily, but for the entire span of the passing hour, with such discipline and order that when the bird was singing, Rose would not even move, and when Rose was singing, the bird, most attentive, would not chirp. Finally, at exactly six o'clock in the afternoon, as if her duty were done, she would fly away, to return to the same place the next day. But the Virgin, fertile in the moment with pious rhythms, closed the Odean stage with this nearly epilogue:

Yet, O King, all rights bind you to me.
Since you are the Creator, and I am a creature.

Then, turning gracefully to the women standing by, she added:

The little bird deserts me, suddenly my Succentor appears.
May God be blessed, who always remains with me.

In Latin, these cannot be rendered adequately, since in the Spanish dialect of Rosæ they were more charmingly bound by their own numbers. Let it suffice that for Rose the power of that prayer was such that she drew to herself the birds of the air and the earth's seedlings into a reciprocal fellowship of worship.

Hence the gargle from the Greek word γαργαρεὼν, by which physicians indicate that part of the throat adjacent to the pharynx, and which some call the plectrum or vocal refractor, because it contributes to the modulation of the voice, as Castellus affirms in his Medical Lexicon, p. 231. From this, one can gather enough about what is indicated by the gurgles of this singing little bird.
