Chapter XII.
Spiritual dryness and various mental afflictions, with which Saint Rose is exercised.

Like gold in the furnace, the trial that refines gold is the tribulation of the elect righteous—especially when the danger must be faced lest the greatness of the revelations exalt them. This oven was divinely granted to Paul, this one to Rosa, but by a different fire. He subjected that fleshly stimulus to slaps. They cooked these desolating coals in the punitive frying pan of internal abandonment. Both, in their anguish, cried out to heaven, and both received a heavenly answer: "My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness." To the point. She had ascended to the degree of perpetual union with God, and at the same time she began to suffer each day the most atrocious intervals of mental obscuration, which, not merely occasionally but for solid hours, so oppressed the poor soul that she often did not know whether she was in hell, in the dreadful prison of purgatory, or in what dungeons she was shut up and blinded. In the blink of an eye he found himself there, where there was no memory of the most sweetest Deity, no taste of divine presence, no trace, shadow, or mention of any consolation. It was a desert of darkness, languor, and dullness; a region of death, a night of swoon, a cavern of the ultimate calamity. The farther she is from the Virgin God, the farther she is from herself.

Thus the Holy One is tested by the fire of inner tribulation. The Rose lay groaning under the immense weight of darkness, unable to lift herself to supernatural things, nor even to natural ones. He tried to grasp, even in the smallest degree, the strictures of the Deity, but all light had fled. The will to love desired, but it had grown numb and frozen. His memory struggled to bring to mind even a single image of past favors, and it could not. He remembered, as if in a fog, that he had once known and loved God. But he felt that he no longer knew or loved the same man, whom he now barely glanced at from a distance, hastily and faintly, as if he were a stranger, absent, alien, a foreigner, and barely known by reputation. This, however, was the most troublesome of all: to be exiled so far from the knowledge of the Beloved. He then tried, at least by the traces, to seek him in the creatures. But neither those nor these appeared under the outline by which the usual analogy represented the Creator. Meanwhile, terror and anguish were vigorously playing their parts. The afflicted heart cried out, "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" But in the void of the emptied soul, no one answered, not even an echo. He returned to the struggle of efforts. But all warmth of feeling was extinguished, all reason dulled; a profound lethargy had seized every sense of piety. What would the Virgin do under these torments, so torn within herself as to be wrenched away from her Beloved?

[167] [and was tormented by such terrors,] But the chief part of her sorrows was that these seemed destined to last forever, and the vast misery promised no end; nowhere was there an exit from this labyrinth, and the escape appeared as if blocked by an adamantine wall. Hence perplexed Rosa could find no way to separate this her own misfortune from the infernal punishment of loss. She tried from this to draw the saddest consolation, thinking that under these torments she would slowly perish: for they did not seem to be of a kind that could long endure without the total destruction of the frail creature. But then the immortality of his soul importunately occurred to him, which no power of punishments could kill, nor any hell could extinguish. Sometimes she was close to uttering incoherent cries to implore for help. but he restrained his futile impulses, mindful that no one could come to the aid of such an inexplicable calamity, and that he himself lacked the fitting words to even faintly sketch it for those close to him; nor could a doctor be found anywhere in the whole world so eloquent, profound, and learned that he could either grasp the gravity of this punishment through speculation or encompass it with his mind.

For fifteen consecutive years, every day at least once—though otherwise most ready to suffer—Rose was plunged into this darkness of desolation, trembling and afraid, and for a full hour, sometimes longer, she panted under this ordeal. So far was it from any habit softening these terrors that it almost increased them: for when the next day the Virgin relapsed into the same chaos, the hideous terrors would again rush in under the same lurid mask of eternity, and the bound memory offered the dying woman no comfort that she had emerged from it the day before. Whereupon her soul withered again in evil, again fled from every comfort of escape, again the abysses covered her lifeless, and Rosa, now all thorny, nay, a thorn herself, lay rigid in the deepest vortex of tribulations. Yet on certain days a faint twilight of hope would creep in like a sliver, which, however she might look at it, she sensed would not be the end of this torment of the soul. Thus it seemed to endure the torments of purgatory rather than of hell. Nevertheless, even then he was most fiercely consumed by the bridegroom's morose absence, afflicted by the harsh exile of a cruel banishment, most bitterly saddened by the long divorce of widowhood, and what surpassed all the rest of his trouble was that even then he strove to know and to love, but could do neither. The mind, blinded, was confused, wandering, groping, and bumping about, deprived of light. he cried out, he knocked, he groaned with a broken spirit, and was not heard; The depths of his heart had grown parched, his vigor was gone, and with no vital motion, he was touched by divine affections, tasting them with no other emotion.

[169] For the horror of these figures was so great, [that she would beg for this cup of bitterness to be removed from her.] so intense was the acerbity of the horror, that Rosa, though from her tenderest years impassive to anything, earnestly begged that this single cup of bitterness might be removed from her, as though it were altogether beyond her strength and simply intolerable. Therefore she begged the Bridegroom to deign rather to lead her along the ordinary paths of his elect, and not to permit the one so in love to fall into the depths of the abyss, where there is no substance. And truly, as hard as it was for the soul to be separated from the body, harder still to be torn away from itself, but hardest of all to be torn away from the Beloved after the experience of union and the sweetest embraces. For, as Augustine testifies, as the soul is to the body, so is God to the soul that loves Him; and for that soul, it is a lesser punishment to cease to live than to cease to love or be repelled from love. Nevertheless, Rosa, recognizing that it was God's will, embraced the torment with a truly manly and heroic spirit, saying, "Not my will be done, but Yours." And so she steadfastly prepared herself for a similar rack the next day, never certain at what hour it would await her in fear.

[170] [Such was this affliction of the mind,] So abstruse, monstrous, and unknown was the form and nature of these desolations, that scarcely have any perceptive and learned theologians been found who would presume to define anything certain about their crasib. At first, Rosa went to various confessors, if perhaps they might know of some remedy, relief, or at least advice. but they did not fully understand what the Virgin was trying to say. Nor did the foreign wrappers of so many riddles match a mind sufficiently capable of thought. One answered that it was either delirium or insomnia. Another ignorantly dismissed the matter as ghosts and spectres. There was no shortage of those who suspected nonsense, or indeed the prestidigitations of demons, and vain terrors. They interpreted paroxysms as more moderate attacks of smoky melancholy, or black bile, and held that the brain, too greatly weakened by fasting and sleeplessness, lay open to the horrid incursions of lurid phantoms, arising from a scorched spleen or the corruption of foul blood. But Rosa, knowing for certain that this calamity was not bodily, was all the more distressed, finding no one who could either understand the illness or point out any anchor in so great a whirlpool of perplexity. He then groaned over his own rudeness, because, in the face of what he believed to be a barren lack of wit and an ignorance of how to speak, he could not express his distress in fitting words. Thus, he blamed himself entirely for the fault of his incurability.

[171] [as some might think, it was a bodily illness,] One meager consolation remained: that the mother, just as she could not help, was also completely unaware of her daughter's terrible struggles. to be stirred up otherwise, and a commotion, if he had found out. But this, too, was a fresh torment for the Virgin: for, as the parent had often noticed on his daughter's face, at certain hours of the day she would grow pale, be seized with anguish, tremble, and be almost frozen with a deathly sweat. She importunately asked what kind of sudden illness it was and why she was hiding it; and the less her daughter could tell what she was suffering, the more vehemently the mother urged the poor girl, pressing her, insisting, and swearing, as if she would probe one inner torment with another. The girl excused herself, saying she did not fully understand what was going on in her own soul. But the mother, suspecting a contagious disease (especially since it was the same crisis or uninformed credulity of some confessors), calls for a doctor and entrusts her own daughter, Rose—already beset on all sides by troubles—to his care. The innocent Virgin protested that such expenses, labors, and time were wasted in vain, that her evil did not rage and dominate in her body at all, but within, in her spirit. but it was not heard. Therefore, seeing that he was fighting in vain and was not believed, he fell silent. and submitted himself to a medicine which he knew would be of no benefit. Indeed, this was the one thing still lacking at the height of her great calamity: that she was furthermore compelled, like a sickly patient, to endure, to the point of nausea, purges, medicines, and bloodlettings.

[172] [which she herself could not explain,] Rosa would then strive, whenever it was necessary to speak more confidently of this furnace of her desolation, to find similitudes by which she might in some way shadow it forth. But he soon admitted that he was putting on an act. He said that the comparison of this elemental fire of ours was laughable, because it is only capable of inflicting what they call the punishment of sensation. It seems that Augustine sensed something of this when he found himself far from God in a region of dissimilarity; and likewise that the king-harpist perceived something in himself (Ps.). 54, which he called the cowardice of the spirit and the tempest, or what Paul emphatically calls anathema from Christ. But Rosa felt more within herself than those words could express. He said that those terrors and horrid images inflicted upon him a pain sufficient a thousand times over to snatch away his heart, his life, his soul. nor was he ever equal to sustaining himself, except insofar as he was miraculously preserved alive by the hand of the Almighty.

[173] There was one who thought the Virgin was led by God through the scarped ways of St. Anthony the Hermit, [and others, like St. Anthony the Hermit,] whom the monsters of hell agitated with terrifying forms. But it was a conflict, not an affliction, nor a flight of strength. It is believed that St. Catherine of Siena once suffered the same thing as Rosa, concerning the Blessed. History relates this as a matter of fact, according to Henry Suso. It seems that some likeness or proportion of this pain arises in the delicate mind that suffers from the most biting scruples, when the soul, left to itself for a little while, suspects heaven barred against it by its beams, God turned away in hostility, hell gaping open with a monstrous yawn. Finally, it depicted with greater vividness the agonizing horror of this Rose, the face of Christ in the Last Judgment, hardened and wrinkled, and terrible even to the demons themselves, as He is imagined on the point of uttering that dreadful word: "Depart, you cursed." The most sorrowful Rosa seemed to experience these wrinkles, this thunder, this terror, as she labored under the hour of her fainting. After various, but never sufficiently fair comparisons, he could say nothing other than: "The pains of hell have surrounded me; the snares of death have overtaken me."

[174] The Virgin was twice compelled to utter the other part of this scene, [that they might be visibly terrified by demons.] namely, what sort of Phoebus was wont to shine upon her after such clouds: for it could not be otherwise, but that that most sweet Spouse, after so dire and gloomy an eclipse, would notably refresh and restore the soul, so humbled by so many sorrows. At these words, Rosa would have preferred either to remain silent or to deflect the conversation elsewhere with skill and dissimulation. But when she saw that she could not escape, she ingenuously and candidly confessed (as best she could, for lack of fitting words) the great works of God that had befallen her after her most wretched suffering, seeing herself in an instant restored to the union from which she had fallen, or had thought she had fallen. He suddenly felt his entire soul shining and aflame all at once. But more about these at a more opportune time in the description of the examination in the following chapter. Let us meanwhile marvel at the play of eternal Wisdom, which, to bring forth a more abundant fruit, thus mortifies and vivifies the chosen souls, leads them to the underworld, and brings them back.

Notes.

Strictly speaking, sparks are called those that flash from glowing iron when a red-hot mass of iron is struck with a hammer. Hence it is quite clear that this word is used here for sparks.

Mixture among the Greek physicians is the same as temperament, or temperance, that is, a certain proportion of the primary qualities existing in the elements, from which the body is mixed and composed. Among grammarians, however, crasis is taken to be the contraction of two syllables coalescing into one, and the word has its origin in the verb kerañnuimi, which means to mix or to temper. It therefore appears that the word "crasis" is used here to mean a mixture of desolations.

Daniel Sennert, vol. 2. Works of the Physicians, page 219, chapter. 31. On epilepsy, he nicknames this disease sacred, great, childish, Herculean, falling, comitial, lunatic, and finally sontic, all of which he asserts are synonymous, and of which he incidentally gives an account. It is sufficient for us, from these epithets, to know that disease more distinctly, the causes and remedies of which can be read in the original.

Catapotia are properly pills from the Greek name κατάποσις, that is, deglutition: For, as Gorræus states in his Medical Definitions, page 215, it is a medicine that is not dissolved, but is swallowed or devoured as it is. It is formed into almost pill-like shapes so that it can be more easily swallowed, dissolved in the stomach, and then pass into the veins.

Phlebotomy is the incision of a vein, or the drawing of blood from an incised vein. For the praises and necessity of this remedy, Gorræus and Castellus can be consulted on the Greek word φλεβοτομία.

Chapter XIII
A severe examination by learned men concerning the spirit and visions of this Virgin.

[Besides other masters of the spiritual life] the Arcanum of grace, a light that had guided Rose along the right paths since her infancy, had at the same time, by a marvelous turn, instilled in her the certainty of a truly divine and never-deceiving attraction, so that she could not doubt the straightness of the path she was on, even though it was little-traveled, and obscure traces, scarcely noticeable. Yet he did not want to appear to be what he was, however certain it was. Hence, the most humble Rose did not presume to refuse the grave, serious, and repeated examination of her calling and spirit, at intervals. Among other prominent figures whom fortune appointed to this most subtle investigation were Doctor John de Castillo and Father Master Friar John de Lorenzana, to whom the entire Rosary, down to its very last part, was entrusted. There were others as well. but successors, and each one, only on occasion of the Sacrament of Penance, have at times more diligently searched the innermost recesses of his soul. Regarding the first two, it is necessary here to say a few things so that, from their holiness and expertise, the force, weight, and credibility of the most mature examination may be more aptly recognized.

[176] [two of the principal ones] Doctor John de Castillo, a physician by profession, and indeed a secular; At prayer, in conversation, and in spirit, he was a thoroughly devout man, and in those days at Lima he was regarded as an outstanding member of God's chosen servants. He had been trained to the highest degree in the study of philosophy and medicine, and had moreover penetrated with the keenest intellect into all the inner recesses of metaphysics; celebrated for this in schools, in lecture halls, and in the gatherings of the most learned men. especially because, with remarkable clarity of conception, he could explain sublime and most lofty ideas in his own terms. His life itself was a pure mirror of piety, so that he was thought to possess each virtue in a heroic degree. Mystical theology not by bare speculation; but he was alive, and had learned it exactly through the most profound experiments, and was an expert in it, having digested it methodically according to his own principles, rules, and headings. so much so that the famous mystagogue of our century, Diego Alvarez de Paz, of the Society of Jesus, then Provincial of the Peruvian province, while preparing his most excellent treatises on prayer, meditation, and contemplation, considered this man the foremost authority to consult in many matters, and even cites him in Book 3 on Contemplation. At around the third point, he argues that the will can be carried into the unknown, suppressing the name (if indeed he was still alive), presenting him as a master of oratory and solid perfection, and merely points with an outstretched finger. At the behest of Diego Alvarez, this Castille wrote a noble treatise on those things that occurred to him amid the rays and lights of contemplation. The Archbishop of Myrenae, who was most learned in such matters, saw the book and was astonished, and had it transcribed to be presented to the Roman Pontiff in person. These things about Doctor Castillo, then still living, were legally attested by Peter of Ortega Sotomayor, bishop of Trujillob, later of Arequipac, a former first professor at the University of Lima, a master canon, metropolitan archdeacon, and consultant to the Holy Office. and finally of Cuzcod. He added that he would reveal more, and greater things, about this man to the public, provided he should survive him. Now (but more briefly) to the other.

Father Master Fr. John of Lorenzana of the Order of Preachers (as attested by the same most illustrious prelate) held him to be on a par with Don Castillo, [examined the actions of St. Rose,] or at least not inferior in the degree of sanctity and public opinion. They shone in him, and contended for the primacy: the exalted perfection of life and the profundity of scholastic theology. A burning zeal for contemplation and an active skill in governing came together. Sharpness of insight and deep maturity of judgment. Flight from the world & the practice of business. Thus, one after another, the prefectures of so many schools, monasteries, and provinces had converged on him: for he was the morning prefect of the Royal University of Lima. a professor of theology, whom he was the first to introduce to the kingdom; and in the tribunal of the Holy Inquisition in Peru, a censor, whom they call a qualificator; also the Prior of the Convent of Lima; then Vicar General of the province of St. John the Baptist, and finally provincial and visitor; equally well-versed in the affairs of both the Church and the state. Beloved and admired by all classes of people: the highest, the middle, and the lowest. to whom, as to the city's common oracle, the most arduous affairs were eagerly brought from the councils of the high priests, from the royal senate (as they call it), from the consistories, courts, and dicasteries, from the internal and external forum; For everyone could see that the gift of counsel shone wonderfully in such a man. Furthermore, how great Lorenzana was in it, however you may rightly call the knowledge of the Saints a mystical theosophy, how great he was in the savor and experience of heavenly things, how practiced in the discipline of the highest contemplation, how keen-eyed and lynx-like in the prudent discernment of spirits—this one thing alone, if all the rest were lacking, I judge sufficient: that divine providence singularly entrusted to him the spirit of the Rose to govern. One here deserves to be mentioned, with no great disparagement of the others: Father Diego Martinez of the Society of Jesus, and the Fathers-masters Francis Alphonsus Velasquez, Francis Louis de Bilbao, and Francis John Perez. But the examination of the first two encompasses so much and so many things that nothing can be added from the rest.

[178] Now, to the matter more closely. To sit for the first exam, for the sake of honor, were Rosa's mother and Ms. Maria de Usategui. In the presence of these, Don Castille, over the course of a full three hours, questioned the Virgin in the domestic garden of the cell—one of them being John Castille—as if she were surrounded by an investigation, and after most diligently probing the veins of her soul, found everything sound and safe. When first asked how long she had conceived heavenly aspirations and a tranquil spirit of prayer, she answered most simply that she did not remember the time, for she had found herself from her earliest childhood most devoted to pouring out little prayers and with her whole mind raised to serious acts of meditation, so that nothing could have seemed sweeter to her than to speak with God. To think of God, to long for heavenly things. He was not ashamed to confess those things in front of his mother, who was all too aware of how true they were. When asked about her successive progress in this study, whether she had always prayed with equal application of mind, facility, concentration, and an untroubled serenity of mind, she replied that until about the age of twelve, she had perceived various fluctuations in her prayerful temper, though brief and moderate. that he had generally advanced toward God with a free and very quiet mind, but that at times he had also to struggle with the frailty of his little body, with drowsiness, and with distractions; yet from that time forward he prayed more expeditiously, because in the beginning he had felt himself wonderfully drawn inwardly by God, with his whole soul and all its powers, and with an unspeakable taste, he perceived that his intellect, will, and memory were so affixed to the divine beauty within that he could neither of his own accord tear himself away through the interposition of phantasms, nor be distracted from this union by the occupations of the external senses, but remained in the admiration of the most sweetest Deity present in the soul.

[179] [He questioned her about the manner of contemplation,] The doctor pressed her, whether she was not applying some force of her own imagination while her other interior faculties thus clung to the savor of God, and whether, and by what, and with what effort, labor, or support, she sustained herself in this most sweet suspension? Rosa denied that labor, force, or effort was on her part, but that she willingly followed the powers as if by a magnetic attraction, and turned toward her center with a sweetness whose single drop would correct and sweeten the bitterness of an infinite ocean. from which, remarkably, descend into his heart most delightful heats and heats, which he cannot explain; from this, to continually radiate into the depths of his soul the presence of a serene, loving, and propitious Divinity, and to perceive it there for certain. and could find no other delight than that, by this certainty born of experience, he felt God within himself. The doctor asked whether he had perhaps been reading books on mystical theology that either taught the method and art of this introversion or at least expounded its nature, signs, properties, and effects through dissertations. The poor Virgin denied having a supply or use of such books, saying that for her, experience and practice alone had served as her code. Therefore, he could not aptly express the innermost feelings of his soul; his ignorance was not even aware of a name, if any existed, for this intuitive mode of speech.

[180] [On the Prayer of Union,] Then Castille, being most versed in this school, undertook to explain to the Virgin the secret of this exalted hieratic illumination, namely that among the masters of ascetic discipline this mode of contemplation is called the Prayer of Union: here the intellect is formed by a kind of vision, not acquired, but poured down from above. nor be turned to corporeal phantasms, but be imbued with the luminous purity of spiritual form by images: here to fill the sanctuary of the faithful soul with God by an immediate and intimate influx; Here the affective part of uncreated Love is kindled and made bright by a fleeting flame, and on the palate of the will a happy foretaste of blessed fruition is begun. He added more from the aphorisms of mystical theology: on the simplification of the heart, on the guiding star of purified intention, on the nakedness of the affections, on resigned indifference, on the secret introductions, on the abyss of light, on the savor of all discourse through the intuition of heavenly things, on the fountain of life, and on other matters, which the Rose, capable of such things, found exceedingly pleasing. and thereafter, to her confessors, she would know herself more clearly and explain herself more meaningfully from this vocabulary.

The doctor went on, and, eagerly reverting to the purgative path, he inquired [and by the steps by which he had ascended to it] how long, by what labors, with what sweat he had been compelled to fight against the perverse inclinations of the mind, against the armed ambushes of vices, against the diseases of untamed and unbridled passions? He replied that he could barely remember such struggles, that from infancy he had felt himself to be the object of God's great favor, and that his inclinations toward virtue were supremely natural, without any turmoil of the passions. that from the very first awareness of God she had been filled with fear and horror of sin, and if any involuntary movement against reason sought to arise, it would suddenly vanish, beyond any conflict, at the apprehension of the divine presence. When asked what comfort she would draw from the creatures, should it happen that her mind, weary from the labor of contemplation, needed to be somewhat eased, or to be relaxed for a while through good humor? He replied that he could be refreshed by nothing created; that his sole and only delight consisted in feeling the God who is so certain in his soul; and that if he were to lose Him from his sight, even for a moment, it would seem to him more bitter than any loss, more dreadful than any hell. The doctor asked, since that rank can only be reached through tribulations and thorns, whether he had at least suffered persecutions and hardships elsewhere? The Virgin nodded. But out of respect for her mother, who was present, she did not go into specifics, content to say in general that she had endured various annoyances, insults, and vexations from her household staff because of the singularity of her life.

[182] But from them he opportunely digressed into those horrifying visions, [Then, having lapsed back to the aforementioned torments of the mind,] figures, and terrors, which were dealt with in the preceding chapter, and with great entreaties he implored the doctor to eruditely elucidate for him the nature, origin, critique, and proper interpretation of that desolation and punishment. Castille did not hesitate to declare what he felt: "When," he said, "in that darkness you thought you could hope for an outcome and an end, know that you have tasted the most grievous delays of the souls wailing in the prison of purgatory." But when no hope of escape shone for you, and a certain smoke of eternity permeated the darkness of horror, it was the very type of hell. By these punishments the soul is taught to know itself, as in the alternation of light and darkness it learns by experience of its own nothingness what it has from itself, what from God, and what the difference is between the two. The spirit is held in its balance by the most troublesome weight of this antibaryg, lest it be lifted up in the gifts of the Most High. This eclipse teaches us to appreciate the excellence of divine familiarity and the purely gratuitous dignity, and it bestows the gift of fear. In this furnace gold is tested, charity shines, and love, armored in robust masculine virtue, grows accustomed to loving not so much the delights of God as God Himself. I remember (he says) having read the same things about some of the chief and most holy friends of God (whose names are inscribed on the public tables of the Church) that you mention about you. Nor were there wanting among those who had begged the Lord to grant them immunity from this atrocious terror, who readily declared themselves willing to undergo any other kind of punishment. Finally, David looked back here as he groaned: "I am told every day, 'Where is God?'" yours? My soul is like a lone sparrow on the roof. I was brought to nothing, and I knew not, etc.

[183] [He commanded her to reveal the divine illuminations,] Having briefly explained these, the doctor in turn asked the Virgin how she had fared after those infernal darkness and horrors. Rosa, startled as if she had trodden on a snake, recoiled, turned pale, and fell silent, well aware that, if she were to reply, she would have to contend with both the abundance of the subject matter and her own poverty of words. Meanwhile the doctor urged him on, pressing him twice, three times. but nothing came of it. Finally, with a more severe frown; "I tell you," he said, "O Rosa, there is no room here for equivocation and silence." It is your own affair: if you have concealed anything, if you have hidden anything in the examination, you have denied God's benefit. If you do not say everything that I seek, neither you will understand me, nor I you, as much as is sufficient, and you will not fully grasp the riddles and meaning of the figures (which I have partly explained, and partly shall explain). The humble Rosa, whose face fear and shame had all aflame with bashful blush, and having first asked pardon for any rude or careless paradox that might slip from her, thus began: While I think and lament myself absorbed in the deepest vortex of gloomy desolation, behold, suddenly, at the very noon of our former union, I find myself as it were in the arms of the Bridegroom. I am restored as if I had never fallen away. I feel the ravenous impulses of the freest love, as when swift rivers, their banks broken, not by flowing but by holding back, rush through the cataract. The gentle South Wind soon breathes its grace, and spices flow. The soul is immersed in the immense ocean of divine goodness, and by an ineffable metamorphosis, detaching from itself, is transformed into the Beloved, and becomes one with him.

[184] [and heavenly visions,] When ordered to say more, she was seized with a new modesty, blushed, and faltered. However, she added that amid these favors of the most perfect union she seemed to herself to be immovably rooted in God, secure in His friendship, confirmed in grace, and to feel a certain inexplicable, fundamental, and solid gift by which she considered herself impeccable, like the one who confidently said, "Who will separate us from the love of Christ?" "I am certain, for neither death, etc." Nevertheless, here the Virgin protested that she had never dared to say this to any mortal, nor would she have said it even now, had not the rigor of the present examination compelled her; and if she had perhaps erred in the phrasing of her words, which necessity had forced her to utter, she humbly begged for the censure of correction. The doctor praised the simple purity of the obedient Rose and instructed her not to be alarmed, saying that nothing had been done wrong so far and that she should proceed with the rest. Rosa stepped forward and said in a low, broken voice that, for the most part, after those horrible darknesses, the humanity of Christ was clearly revealed to her, now with a manly face, now with an infantile or boyish one, gentle, beautiful, and affable. often also the Virgin Mother, beautiful, lovely, and sweetly familiar. When asked about the manner and quality of the vision—whether imaginary or intellectual, lasting or momentary, face to face or indirectly through transparencies? He replied that the proper names of those different visions were indeed still unknown to him, but that the glorious humanity of Christ was displayed to him in a certain near and clear passage, just as when some bright star, with a sluggish course, runs across the sky. not his entire stature could be seen, but only his face and up to his chest. However, the majesty of the Mother of God is accustomed to indulge her gaze for a little longer.

[185] Hence Castille concluded that the visions were imaginary. [by which she had been restored,] she sought further, and so, by what form could she apprehend the presence of the Divine? Rosa here labored to find suitable words, but at last, by the terms of remoteness, eminence, and causality alone, however she expressed herself, she insinuated that the light is without figure, measure, or end: from which it became clear on that score that the vision is purely intellectual, incomprehensible, and all-comprehending, subtle, stable, most pure, supremely manifold and supremely one, most distant and most near, intimate and surrounding, noble, exalted, and in no way at all comparable to any creature, even faintly, which can be apprehended by the mind more in the marvelous and immediate effects of certain vital effluxes than in its own substance. But what are these effects? "Tenderness," he says, "of a robust joy beyond all imaginable delight, kinship with divine sonship, an inner renewal of antiquity in the very essence of the soul, the fulfillment of the desires of the will, life and alacrity in all affections, firm, holy, graceful, and, in short, ineffable in every respect."

[186] The doctor saw that he had been satisfied in what he was most earnestly investigating, [and judges that all of these came from God,] not unaware that, in the matter of union, the more that is said, the less is said. Therefore, prudently turning to easier matters, he inquired about exercises in mortification and penance. Rosa, thinking that in this regard she was doing little and very ordinary things, on the side issued some edicts about her fasts, hairshirts, disciplines, and other austerities in her mother's absence, and that in these she had established the measure and limit not by her own judgment, but by that of her confessors. Finally, after much back-and-forth about self-doubt, the ardor of faith, and the security of hope, after many things had been tossed back and forth about self-doubt, the ardor of faith, the security of hope, and the stirrings of divine love, which were most shrewdly examined, the doctor concluded that the path the Virgin was treading was sincere and safe, and that no crafty imposture of the devil lay beneath it, and that such affections, effects, and illuminations could in no way be from illusion or the prince of darkness. He then repeatedly approached the Virgin, not so much to examine her as to converse with her, with confidence. always finding new things that she would marvel at and approve of.

[187] He held the same view of Father Lorenzana's master, [whose judgment John of Lorenzana agreed with], after he had most diligently probed the Virgin's spirit through a more frequent and piecemeal examination. It would be too lengthy to go through each item in detail. Let it suffice to add this one thing to the foregoing: that when he began to test Rosa on the illuminative way, he was astonished by the replies of the simple and uneducated girl, when speaking of the mystery of the Holy Sacrament. When she was questioned about the mystery of the Trinity, the hypostatic union of the Word, the sacrament of the Altar, the glory of the Blessed, the book of life and predestination, the nature of grace, and other secrets of faith and theology, she produced axioms so profound and solid, concepts so acute and sublime, sentences so clear, brief, fecund and weighty, words so proper, apt, clear, and concise, that another examiner did not hesitate to frankly confess that he had never encountered such a luminous and perspicacious mind; and praised the Father of lights, who revealed such great things, hidden from the wise and prudent, to the humble, little ones, and the uneducated. He was likewise impressed thereafter by the method of confession used by Rosa, by the clarity, propriety, and circumspection of her speech, so that it seemed to him he was not hearing a woman, but an elderly professor of both theology. There was such a proportion, gravity, and measure to the words, without digression, without circumlocution, without confusion and superfluity. Hence, when he was called to hear the Virgin's confession, he rebuked the subdeacon for saying that Rosula, or Rosella, was waiting for him in the church, as if he had spoken irreverently. "Do you see Rosula," he said, "who before God is a Rose, and indeed a great one?" A day will come when all will understand how exceedingly high, how great, and how magnificent this Rose is.

[188] [& Louis de Bilbao, a Dominican,] the master of the studium at Loreto, and the first chair, Father Louis de Bilbao, noted the same in the Rose while he was taking its confessions; Indeed, he too was scrutinizing the Virgin's spirit with a varied examination. He later confessed that, many times during the secret colloquies of that forum, he had been silenced by the depth and erudition of the solid discourses, by which Rosa promptly, most easily, and in order satisfied the most arduous and abstruse questions, and suddenly recognized a higher spirit speaking in the Virgin. Indeed, among all who then lived in Lima—celebrated by many for their sanctity and perfection—a most steadfast opinion had gradually prevailed: that Rosa was acted upon and carried along by the Spirit of God, abounded in the gift of wisdom, and was guided by knowledge poured down from heaven. Hence, the most holy Ludovica of Melgareyo held Rosam in such high esteem that, whenever she met her, she would greet her with nothing less than a humble bow on the ground, honoring her even when she resisted. If she had observed a passerby, she would have been eager to press her mouth and eyes there, where Rosa had left her trace. And if he needed to form little letters for himself in his absence, he would write them on the bare ground on his knees.

[189] [who always held the Virgin's sanctity in high esteem.] Especially Castillon and Lorenzana, having on several occasions, through mutual consultation, established the most perfect ways of the Rose, marveled in unison at these two most singular things: first, that by the briefest compendium, and as it were by a single leap, she had possessed the illuminative, and soon the unitive, degree, without having scarcely touched the purgative degree in passing, because, indeed, from infancy having been preempted by the blessings of sweetness, she had always been accompanied by the purest affections, in which nothing earthly had ever soiled itself as to require more diligent purging. The other, that in that horrible torment of obscurations, which was terrifying only to the inexperienced, that was Rose's strength, her spirit, her constancy, so that the frail virgin was not only to bear it, but, having first offered a voluntary resignation, as if she were contending with God, whether He would more grievously afflict, or she more willingly suffer, provided that in all things victory and triumph might remain with the divine will. This self-denial is most complete and, indeed, extreme. but at the same time it is exceedingly difficult for one who loves so deeply, for whom it seems happier not to exist than not to love. One must declare the Rose imperishable, for thorns of that sort do not suffocate it.

Notes.

In the Library of our Society, on page 355 and following, the virtues and writings of this pious man are recounted in his lengthy eulogy.

Trujillo is a city in northern Peru, an episcopal see under the Archbishop of Lima, which is distinguished from other towns of the same name, as we once noted in the Annotations to the Life of St. Francis Solano, volume 5 of July, page 870.

Arequipa is a southern city of America in the kingdom of Peru, a bishopric under the metropolitan see of Lima, which is 12 leagues from the sea. From Lima, however, it is 120 miles to the south and 75 from Cusco, about which latter city something must now be noted.

Cusco was the primary city of the Americas before the arrival of the Spaniards, and the seat of the kings. It is now a diocese under the Archbishop of Lima, from whose metropolis it lies 120 Spanish leagues to the east. John the Latter, book. Chapter 10, West Indies 30. Describes the state of this city in antiquity and modern times in several ways.

from Hieratic Greek hieratike, as attested by Pliny, book. 13, chapter 11. It is a finer kind of paper, used for writing sacred scrolls by hieros, that is, sacred. Hence also the adjective ἱερατικὸς is explained in Latin as sacerdotalis, that is, pertaining to or destined for sacred things. I think that here the term "hieratic" indicates the sacred art of contemplation.

A Greek word, *eutrapelia*, means almost the same to the Greeks as *urbanitas, lepore,* and *festivitas* to the Latins. Aristotle takes the same word for learned abuse. Saint Thomas 2. two. question Article 72 2. Acting on eutrapelia, he appears to have followed this Aristotelian interpretation. From the accompanying circumstances, it is abundantly clear that this term signifies an honorable recreation or relaxation of the mind.

This name was borrowed for the Latin inflection, and is composed of the Greek prefix ἀντὶ, meaning "against," and the word βάρος, meaning "weight," which the French call contre-poids. as if counter-weight, which the Latins properly call libramen or libramentum.

Chapter XIV
The Virgin's wonderful familiarity with Christ, the Mother of God, and St. Catherine of Siena.

To this virgin who reads books, the earthly grows bitter, the heavenly grows sweet, and they insinuate themselves of their own accord. This happened to Rose, whose entire conversation was in the heavens. He would sometimes read spiritual pamphlets during the day, and would diligently select chapters more suited to his present mood and the occasion. He lingered for a while over the little lines wherever the sweetest name of Jesus appeared; for from the tips of each of those characters he felt as if tiny sparks and amorous thorns were piercing his heart. Nor was this enough: the little Jesus himself, scarcely taller than a finger, often appeared to the beloved Rose on the same plane of the page: a little naked Love stood, then would walk with the lightest step, and in the meantime, with a serene, tender, and sweet gaze, he would caress the Virgin, pressing himself upon her, for he was the Word, most worthy of Rose's attentive reading, in whom are all the treasures of the knowledge and wisdom of God.

[191] [working with the hands,] What follows is more familiar. While Rosa was busy spinning at the loom, behold, she again saw the beloved Jesus sitting like a child on the spinning-wheel, spinning most quietly; there he spoke with silent nods to the beloved's heart. He smiled at him. He stretched out his forearms toward him, as if offering a embrace. He constantly burned her with his ardent eyes, and with every gesture, movement, and turn, he proclaimed his love. Let those who have the leisure judge what sense of joy the midwife might have had amid these consolations: I am more amazed that Rosa's eyes were still intact, able to see the thread, the needle, the web. And yet he saw that these favors soothed him in such a way that they neither wholly diverted his attention from the external work nor even parted it. That she had often enjoyed these delights with Jesus, her Spouse, indeed perhaps daily, is evidenced by the fact that if he were ever delayed and did not come as usual, Rosa would lament in querulous yet subdued tones, as if perplexedly speaking to herself, saying: "It is time, and he has not yet come?" It's twelve, and he's not here yet? Wretched that I am, for now I am without him! O happy soul, who now holds him with her! And since love teaches poetry, Rosa likewise had woven these complaints into a familiar meter, with this approximate meaning:

Woe is me! Who detains the draft? Behold, he delays:
      It is twelve o'clock, and yet he has not come.
I languish, my eyes fail, my vitals grow cold,
      He drags out delays elsewhere while she weaves intricate patterns.

[192] [& often ailing] It happened that Rosa fell ill from a sore throat, and behold that entire Desirable One, whose throat is proclaimed the sweetest in Cant. 5. It appears to the Virgin, invites to the game, and leaves the fruits of victory on both sides at the victor's discretion. Immediately the die is cast, fortune favors the Virgin, she gains, she wins. And without delay, it demands its fruit, perhaps recalling from the Songs what the Bride says: "His fruit is sweet to my throat." What's the matter? The sick woman asks for her throat pain to be eased, and she gets it. But the divine Collutor (as if not bearing the loss) returned, restored the game, and with a more fortunate throw emerged victorious. The reward for Victoria was Rose's patience, as her pains suddenly increased, keeping her awake all night. But it pleased Virginia to inquire with herself, whether she had made more profit in the first or second game, since she was far more ardently partisan to the Groom than to herself. For the one who marvels at the eternal Wisdom, it was as great a joy to have been conquered as to have conquered, for she, playing on the face of the earth, proclaims that her delight is with the children of men. The mother, noticing that her daughter who was lying down had her former pains return, and indeed with an increase, suspected danger and was terrified. But the prudent daughter, to dispel the fear, gently and modestly revealed the secret, namely that this was merely a game of the suitor's. And as she recounted the success in the humblest of words, she seemed (like Stephen of old) to glow for a while with a wholly angelic countenance.

[193] [Jesus in various forms] On another occasion, when Rosa had been standing for a long time—almost until midnight—in the solitary cell of the garden, a sudden languor and exhaustion seized her. He hoped it would be a temporary weakness, but it persisted beyond the usual period. She felt the certain growths of an uncertain evil. But it was late at night, when he could neither call for a doctor nor was he inclined to importunately summon any of his household for help: he knew he needed an electuary or a little drink, by which he could somehow soothe his stomach and restore his strength. But it was the Sabbath, and she was to approach the sacred Synaxis the next morning; she was fasting, in case the midnight hour had already passed. Whichever way he turned, he foresaw that either a lack of strength or the taking of any nourishment would prevent him from partaking of Holy Communion the next day. What remedy is here between rock and sacred? Rose, with familiar trust, fled to the heavenly Spouse for help in times of need. He commends himself to this apothecary, asks for a strengthening medicine, and obtains it, not from anywhere else but from that precious myrrh-box of the wounded side, from which St. Catherine of Siena once eagerly sucked. This nectarous breast of blood and water she did not apply to her mouth, but kindly to the Virgin's heart: here she found and drew the medicine for her weakness, here she drew the vital sap. Furthermore, the effect proved that the vision had been neither empty nor vain, nor purely imaginary: for Rosa soon recovered, her strength, agility, and vigor returned, and the next day she went to the temple, ate the Bread of Angels, and returned home full, refreshed, and healthy. Indeed, she has experienced how the bridegroom's breasts are better than wine, and more fragrant than the finest ointments. It was truly a great honor for Rosa to have been a mother and teacher of the Sena, not only her daughter and pupil, but also her foster-sister. But to other matters.

In the house of a certain illustrious lady, Rosa, as was her custom after holy conversations, asked permission to withdraw for a while to pray. [familiarly and wonderfully] The matron, as a mark of honor, appointed as companion a seven-year-old girl, born into her household. She, having left Rosa absorbed in meditation, slipped quietly to her mother, who was working in the adjoining room; after an hour she crept back to see if Rosa had risen from her prayer. And behold, she saw beside the Virgin a little boy, Jesus, most decently dressed in a multicolored tunic of blue and purple, whom, as soon as she saw him, she perceived to be surrounded by a shining brightness, and shining with rays from every side, she, not daring to interrupt the conversation, stood back from a distance, content with the permission of the most sweet sight, whose mysteries her age could not yet grasp, nor did she discover the matter before Rosa had passed from this life. The same thing happened in the house of Ms. Isabella of Mexico, which her daughter, who saw it, reported. Indeed, Rosa was walking far off among the more secluded porticoes of the buildings, and with her was Jesus, in stature like an eight-year-old boy, but already his radiance shone like clothing. They strolled together, hand in hand, with the gentle gait and bearing of lovers. They exchanged the most secret conversations with one another, and stared into each other's faces, as if oblivious or indifferent to all other things. But the Parvuli's gait was more stately than is usual for children; there was a majesty to it, and wherever he placed his foot, the floor would suddenly gleam with light.

Zeal, as the herald and scout of intense love, is said to have appeared, and so it even troubles the heavenly Bridegroom himself with its cares, allowing no rival, not even a tiny blossom. She excelled both in name and in deed: for she made sure that flowers would never be lacking on the altars at any time of the year, carefully providing them by means of small beds, one of which she had planted with basil (which many call ozimum). He was perhaps cultivating this one more diligently because, by its fragrance and name, it was more fittingly suited as an offering to the immortal and invisible King of ages. The plant responded to the industrious gardener's care, and the more it grew, the more it delighted him. But, as the joy of flowers is short-lived, one morning Rosa found her basil not only dry and withered, but uprooted and dead. Rosa kissed the corpse and left. but Christ appeared to the one who was going, and gently spoke to the Virgin. What do you ache? he said Am I not better for you, Flower of the Field, than all basil and the entire flower of paradise? And so you know that I am yours, basil, I uprooted that other one with these hands, broke it, and threw it away. Are you a flower, and do you love a flower? Behold me. Rosa learned that she was loved by her Spouse even to jealousy, and for the more frequent experience of such grace she would have readily laid out as a prey whatever flowers remained to her. They certainly give full credence to the ecstatic vision of a most devout woman, to whom Christ, in a rapture, declared that He bore the Rose in the innermost sanctuary of His divine heart. Knowing that, in turn, he alone dwelt most peacefully in her maidenly heart.

[196] [Even with the Mother of God,] Furthermore, from the very fact that the most august Empress of heaven was so gracious, serene, and frequent in her appearances to her Rose, it is permissible to infer that from the age of eleven until her final breath she never failed to grant the Virgin this precious comfort of constant familiarity. In the chapel of the Most Blessed Virgin, the Rose had become thoroughly domestic, and there she lingered all day long. Having there received the habit of her Order, and there espoused to Jesus, she could not be moved from the place. There she had devoutly taken upon herself the care of adorning the altar, the management of all its splendor; there she maintained a daily communion with the Mother of God through diligent service and mutual conversation. But in turn the Queen of the Angels honored her handmaid with such distinction that she almost performed the duties of a chambermaid for her, waking the Virgin from sleep each morning. This was the opportunity for such a favor: for a long time, necessary sleep had so deprived Rosa of uninterrupted, full nights that her health would have been seriously endangered had she refused treatment. Hence, it was necessary to use the authority of the confessors, who prescribed to the Virgin the evening use of lettuce and soporific seeds. and then a space of time in which to sleep; They prescribed the hour at which he should rise in the morning.

[197] [which roused her from sleep] The frail one tried to obey, but, overcome by her habit of late-night vigils, sleep would not obey, whether she came or went. Especially in the morning, as the time to rise drew near, the soporifics would begin to take effect and would force the slowest sleep upon the brain. Rosa was tormented by a great pang of conscience because she could not wake up at the exact prescribed hour, as she had so dearly wished. Therefore, she appealed to the Virgin Mary, as the true Morning Star, for help in her struggle and affliction. She came to her aid, nay, she came: for from that moment, when the time to rise had grown short, behold, in an instant the Mother of God appeared in sight, and with a honeyed voice she called to the slumbering Rose: "Arise to prayer, Daughter;" Arise: for the hour is at hand. Thus, gently awakened, she saw it up close and, as she confessed, face to face—that gracious star of glory, that brow most full of majestic beauty, that paradise of glory and delight—she recognized the voice, she revered the presence, silently questioning herself: "Where did this come from for me, that the Mother of my Lord should come to me?" Oh, welcome alarm clock!

[198] [She was rousing her for the oration,] Once, when the Lady arrived, Rosa was sleeping more deeply: it was her first sleep, and she had only just begun it a little while before. The excited virgin answered; I rise, my lady, I rise now: and she sat up in her bed, but weighed down by the burden of sleep and her eyes growing dim, the poor woman fell back onto her pillow. The most august Awakener came again, and (which she had not been accustomed to do before) stretching out her snow-white hand, she tapped the sleeper's side, shook the supine little body with her most familiar touch, and said again: "Arise, my daughter, be not slothful;" You asked me, and behold I call you to the usual prayers: arise, my little daughter, arise, for the hour has already sounded. It was far sweeter for Rosa to be called "little daughter" by such a mother than "Rose." Sleep yielded to the sweetness of this compulsion. But at once the most delightful Exciter departed: for as soon as Rosa lifted her heavy eyelids and looked back, she recognized the Mother of God, though only from behind, for she had already begun to go away with suspended footsteps, as if having fulfilled a friendly duty. Alas, how little indulgence in sleep, and what a loss it brings! How Rosa regretted that, on that occasion, the maternal face had been snatched from her while she hesitated! The favors of the heavens are delicate; If you don't squeeze them promptly, they will slip away.

[199] From the time that Catherine of Siena was once sent by Christ from heaven to Rosa (as was later learned), and was made her teacher, she frequently met this dear Disciple visibly on earth, especially while she was occupied with reading the rule of the seraphic teacher, which had been sent to her from Cusco (by the procurator Fr. Joanne Miguel, Order He had finally obtained the transmission of the religious preacher to the layman. The mutual conversation of these Virgins was so friendly and frequent, that, just as with Moses, after a long conversation with God on the mountain, traces of light remained on his face. they would thus appear from B.'s face. Catherine's eyes had crossed over into Rose's face, which by certain features of her mouth had almost made her resemble her. Hence, among her fellow Limans, Rosa was commonly called the other Catherine of Siena. especially after her death, when in her coffin Rose's face was more freely exposed to all. As for the wondrous delights, favors, and offices by which this seraphic teacher soothed her Rose, a more frequent and convenient discussion will return below in its proper places.

Notes.

These and similar amateur games may not please those whose judgment or censure of such games is expressed in the preliminary commentary, no. We have recounted Echard's words in 45 words.

Furthermore, we consider it more probable that these and similar things were rather represented to the imagination of the holy Virgin than actually happened. For apparitions of this kind, consult our Papebroch in the sixth volume of May, page 246 and following, where he includes a Parergon on the natural movements of the ecstatics during their rapture, sometimes made by words and deeds, which are not to be transferred to the decisions of historical questions.
