Chapter XV
Familiarity with guardian angels, and various contests against demons.

[200] [The Holy One made such familiar use of the service of the angels,] the Holy angels are the very heart of God, Bernard, sermon. twelve Psalm Who lives: Where are these words? "Lay your heart on him." Job. 7, adapts the mission of angels to the custody of man. Rosa had her angel not only as a guardian, but also as a most friendly companion. rather, the paramourb, the sympositec, the dyadd, so that even from this she might notice how sweetly Christ had set His heart upon her. Hence the Virgin had with her angel such a domestic confidence and familiarity that she would often use him as a messenger whenever the Bridegroom failed to appear at the usual hour. In the metrical rhythm of his own idiom, he then addressed the angel more familiarly, in this sense:

Fly, heavenly youth; say to our Creator:
To live without you is to die of longing.
Why does he tarry while the rose is in bloom?
      Quickly investigate:
Ask Let him hurry, let him incline the heavens,
Let him come down to me, let him show his face,
      Because I am sick with love.

[201] [As if she had suffered a fainting spell,] One night, with the garden gates locked, Rosa, as was her custom, was awake in the narrow cell of her hermitage, and she felt herself suddenly deprived of her strength, so much so that she feared a serious fainting spell. Therefore, she decided to return to her mother's house, after first consulting her guardian angel. The mother (who always kept the key to the garden with her) seeing the signs of an impending fainting fit on her daughter's face, without delay took two denarii from her purse and ordered a maid to go at once to the nearest apothecary's and buy sugar, charcoal, and an Indian cake, which the natives call CHOCOLATE, already well known to Europeans both in use and by name: for when dissolved in hot water with a third part sugar, it powerfully helps both the stomach and the heart.

[202] [through them to get herself a drink] The daughter earnestly begged her mother not to incur the expense for her own sake, assuring her that she would not run out of chocolate. The mother replied, "Do you think there's a single chocolate crumb to be found in this whole house?" Rosa denied it. but he added that this would soon come from the house of the quaestor. The parent frowned and said, "Either you're joking or you're dreaming: for who at this ungodly hour would send you hot chocolate?" Or from where, in the questor's house so far away from here, could it have become known that you, stricken by a sudden weakness, needed that medicine? You, shut up in the garden, of course had no one you could send there, nor did anyone from this house go who could inform the tax collector of your need. "Therefore, go, servant girl, and carry out the command." Rosa again pleaded earnestly, firmly asserting that the questioner's slave would soon arrive with the prepared medicine. It was so: for while the mother was still arguing with her daughter, there was a knock at the door. He was the quaestor's servant; he asked to be admitted, saying he had come from the quaestor's associate to deliver something to Rosa. The vessel was silver and filled with hot chocolate. The parent was stunned. But Rosa, immediately freeing the slave, ordered him to tell his mistress that the favor had come at the right time.

Here the mother, bewildered by the manifold prodigy, inquired, [deeming it opportune,] what had moved Gundisalvus's wife to send a springtime gift to the household at such a late hour of the night. Who could have told Rosa of either the lady's fainting spell or the certain arrival of the freed slave? Finally, by the supreme command of obedience, he ordered his daughter to find out, from wherever she could, at what hour chocolate was to be brought to him from the treasurer's house. The daughter replied with a smile, "Do not be surprised, dearest mother: for my guardian angel often honors me with such duties." Soon, when I began to fail in my cell, I sent an angel to inform Mrs. Mary of Usategui that I needed the help you had seen. He did what I had commanded. And I was certain he would not fail in his duty. With that confidence I left the cell, came here to you, sat down here to wait for the questor's servant, and to share the benefit with my parent. The mother was amazed at the prompt and swift obedience of Angel. but rather, seeing that his daughter was not surprised, since she had long been accustomed to such things by frequent use.

Another time she waited in that same garden cell beyond midnight, having ordered the closed door to be opened, while she was being summoned into the house as was the custom for nighttime rest: for her mother used to come down about the hour before midnight to open the garden gate, so that her daughter, once led out, could return to her own chamber. But on that occasion the mother, whether because she believed she had already brought her daughter back from the garden, or because she was distracted by other matters and had paid less attention, had forgotten to open the garden at the usual time for the Virgin to go out. So, seeing that he was waiting in vain and hesitating over what to do, he looked through the little window and saw close by a white shadow, graceful in movement and not unpleasant to behold, which beckoned the Virgin to follow it home. Rosa easily recognized, by the internal criteria she had learned from Saint Catherine of Siena, the guardian angel hidden in the shade. And so, he confidently set out on the road with him. They both reached the garden gate, which, at the touch of the shade, soon opened of its own accord without a key. Finally, that shadowy guide stopped the following Rosa before her mother's bedroom and vanished. Perhaps he sought this shadow, which surrounded the Lord, to be kept as the pupil of the eye, and to be protected under the shadow of His wings. Let us not be surprised that the angels of light assumed the appearance of shadows while carrying out their duty.

[205] The occasion of angelic guardianship requires that the following little story be appended here, [and how he procured an angelic guard for a certain traveler.] by which it will perhaps be made clear that he had not only his own, but also the guardian angels of others, obedient to his will. A certain religious man, about to accompany a church prelate on arduous and lengthy journeys, revealed his need to the Virgin and begged for prayers to avert dangers. Rosa promised, as she was wonderfully liberal and ready to help her neighbors, and she earnestly commended the man setting out to God and his guardian angel. He went on, secure in the promise, for he was not unaware of how much Rosa was worth to God and the angels. Nor did the outcome disappoint: for from Lima to Potosí, through various incidents and a thousand perils, he made the journey in the safest possible way. But from then on he felt, along with the Virgin's intercession, that he had suddenly been deprived of angelic protection: for, on his way to Truxillo, he endured the gravest hardships in its valleys and barely escaped unharmed. Upon his return to Lima, he bitterly complained about the Virgin, and at last even before her, that she had abandoned him in the middle of the road without the protection of his prayers. Rosa did not deny that it was true. He only asked how he knew this. He replied that he had, with the protection of an angelic guard, either successfully braved or successfully avoided every danger all the way from Lima to Potosí. Indeed, everything was against Potosi as he went to Trujillo, so that he could not help but suspect that Rosa had gone back on her promise and had in the meantime abandoned her angel entrusted to his care. Rosa said: You did not guess in vain; But know that those misfortunes happened to you because you were no longer the person you used to be. Then, descending more confidently to particulars, he warned the man of the secrets known only to himself, which from such a distance could have been made known to the Virgin only by divine means or through an angelic messenger.

[206] [The envious demons, by various means and under different forms] As much as the holy angels loved, surrounded, and caressed this angelic girl, so much the infernal furies sought to harm her, secretly and openly, by force and by fraud. That garden cell tormented the demon, so full of heavenly delights. He was angry and proud that she, a timid young woman, did not fear him, but rather, in that solitude, was voluntarily challenging him to single combat. And so the dark one attacked the Virgin in the darkness. The form was that of a huge molosser, shaggy, dark, and pitch-black; the only light by which it could be seen came from the red flames it was spewing from its nostrils, eyes, and ears in abundance. In this manner it prowled around the praying Rose, barking with a horrid bark, a monstrous, fiercely-toothed mouth, its foul tongue lolling, with a sulfurous stench, while simultaneously pricking its hanging ears, tail, and bristles, as if about to pounce. He had come not only to frighten, but also to harm, if he could: for (seeing that she looked down on him from her immobility) he gnawed at that virgin body with a rabid bite. He raged with a rush, with his teeth and nails, as best he could. At last, with a furious boldness, it snatched what it could not tear apart, dragging it across the ground, pulling it, turning it, as if it were some tattered rag. Until the fearless, but disgusted with rashness, Virgin exclaimed to the Spouse, "Do not hand over the souls that confess to you to the beasts." Nor was there any need for another club to drive off this Cerberus; for at once, his loins struck, defeated and broken, he recoiled, fled, and vanished, and Rosa, admiring herself completely unhurt and unharmed, returned to her prayer with a tranquil heart.

[207] [they attacked this Virgin;] Once, as the Virgin was retiring from the oratory of Gundisalvus the quaestor to her inner chamber, a demon from the underworld, lying in ambush along the way, struck her with a sudden slap as hard as he could, doing more harm with the sound than with the blow. But the intrepid maiden smiled, ready even to offer her other cheek, had she had a visible enemy before her. Elsewhere and on another occasion, in the house of Ms. Isabella Mexico, a cowardly enemy, afraid to attack Rosa in the front and at close quarters, from behind and at a distance hurled a crude stone at her with such violent and powerful force that it knocked her flat to the ground. Rose, neither hurt nor frightened, rose more agile than she had fallen. and reproached the vilest devil with shameful weakness. Confused by Victrix's spirit, he poured out his rage, which could not harm the girl, in the most disgraceful manner upon her spiritual writings. Among his roses, the codex of Louis of Granada was especially dear to him, which he used more diligently for arranging the articles of his meditations. The wretch seized him, tore him apart, and cast him far away into a sewer of filth, believing he had done a great thing by having at least stolen some weapons for such an Amazon in a base theft. Nevertheless, Virgo recovered her book and shamed the backstabbing thief, whom she dignified with no other name than that of a wicked cat or a SCABIOTIC.

Another time, after she had finished her prayers in the same oratory, she went, as evening fell, to a more secluded chamber in the highest part of the building, to savor in solitary meditation the sweetness she had tasted there during her prayers. But there was a most populous orchestra of gnomes, nay, a hippodrome. they ran about in squads, leaped, fought, up and down over the walls, cupboards, and tiles; The confused hunt disrupted everything, and nothing quieter could be hoped for, since the foul, light-fearing beasts were favored by night itself and the nature of the place. So Rosa, having changed her mind, descended to the lowest part of the house, seeking a hiding place of deeper silence. It was a storeroom, where at that time the tools of the vintage, baskets, cauldrons, and such utensils were stored. As soon as Rosa entered here, her hair suddenly standing on end with horror, she recognized that she was being awaited there by the Scabby One for a fight. Not yielding to fear, she commanded the little maid passing by to bring her a candle. She let the maid go, ordering her not to tell any of the household where Rosa was hiding. nor would he summon her to dinner, perhaps staying there longer than usual, until she herself came out of her own accord.

The maid had gone away, and Rosa had barely closed the door when she suddenly heard—so that it would never terrify her—and at the same time, with the bolt drawn from the outside, that she was being locked up like a captive by a demon. Soon, shut inside the pantry, she noticed her slimy enemy stirring more licentiously in a huge basket hidden beneath the lip of the floor. It was shameful for the noble Virgin to use the light present there as a comfort, or as any kind of protection. She therefore promptly extinguished the candle herself, and immediately challenging the hidden enemy from the basket into the arena, said, "Hey you, scabby pig, come on out, I'm waiting for you here: come out, if you dare." Try it, if you're any good. Act in this vile body, whatever God has permitted you; you can certainly do nothing to the soul. From the heavenly Spouse I have this most certain confidence. Come on, you horned beast, come on. Without delay The fiercest enemy leaped forth with a gigantic bulk, which made the cell tremble, but Rosa trembled not. He seized the virgin by the shoulders, squeezed her, and twisted her, as if he were going to tear her limb from limb into the smallest pieces while she struggled. And to double the terrors, he made her feel by touch how hairy, rough, and bristly the lizards were. He was miserably shaking her, throwing her, thrusting her, and pressing her, so that each of the Virgin's bones seemed crushed, her shoulder blades dislocated, her nerves torn; But beneath fragile flesh, an unbroken spirit thrived, and the little heart fixed in God laughed, scorned, and loosed the raging madness of the cyclops. The darkest struggle lasted for many hours, to the great shame of the adversary, who, despairing of victory, tried to wring out at least a single groan or a sign of fear, but could not.

Meanwhile, the questor's wife, while asking the household about Rosa, learned from a little maid where she had left her with a candle, and that she had expressly forbidden being called to dinner. She approached, and seeing that no light shone from any part, she drew back the bolt and went away in silence, waiting to see the outcome of her hesitation. At last, the middle of the night broke the long contest; The Virgin appeared, breathless with fatigue, yet her face was cheerful and she was exceedingly joyful, more so than is customary. So that no one would doubt that something great had taken place amid those darknesses. The next day, Gundisalvi's wife did not stop until, with Rosa narrating, she had elicited the entire sequence of events, where, among other things, it was noted that such conflicts were neither unfamiliar nor infrequent for the most steadfast Virgin. They had never expressed a sense of fear.

[211] [or might have been able to arouse to impudicity,] She had once stepped out into the garden to speak, when from the shady grove of the neighboring trees she saw an adversary coming toward her, no longer terrifying. but gentle, fair, and almost transfigured into an angel of light: for his appearance was human. but beautiful, proper, and urbane. The virgin was distressed that a man appeared there out of the ordinary, where before there had been no witness, no companion. Meanwhile, from a distance he began to woo her with gestures, soon acting boldly and impudently. Here Rosa, detesting the lying mask with sonorous voices, turned her back on the lemur. And she who had stood bravely in other conflicts, in this one alone (because it was a matter of chastity) begged for victory through a noble flight. When she had reached the garden gate more by flying than by running, she paused in a nearby portico, and seizing an iron chain, she lacerated her bare shoulders with the most bitter whips: amid the flowing streams of both blood and tears, she complained to her Spouse that He had forsaken her for a time while she was plunged into such straits: for she had never imagined that, in his very presence, she would encounter such a dangerous image of impure disguise. But at once the glorious Spouse appeared to the Virgin, and said, "Hear, Rose, if I had not been with you, then victory would have been won." It was a great consolation to the victor, a great and most instructive lesson. Similarly, these are, and are plainly genuine, like those of B. To Catherine of Siena, in a contest by no means unequal, it happened when Christ answered her: "While you were fighting, while you were winning, I was within, in your heart I was a spectator and a faithful helper."

[212] [since she immediately distinguished diabolical inspirations from divine ones.] In Rosa there shone forth (as her confessors attested) a marvelous gift for discerning true visions from Satan's illusions and for distinguishing holy inspirations from the cunning suggestions of the ancient serpent, which the Apostle calls the discernment of spirits. What is even more admirable is that from early childhood she was endowed with this remarkable gift, by which she benefited herself and others so wonderfully that she never encountered a Virgin who faced a more deceitful temptation from an adversary, nor a more fraudulent cunning from a seducer, than she did not at once and clearly perceive, expose, and disarm at the very threshold. It is amazing how many and what great victories he has won with this single weapon against the Stygian deceptions. Still a young woman, she learnedly reviewed for the examining magistrate the rules for distinguishing spirits from their effects. He said that the illustrations are truly divine, for they leave in the soul humility, a sense of reverence, an awareness of one's own worthlessness, order and unity of the heart, and joy in God. On the contrary, these impressions—pride, confusion of a divided heart, self-complacency, and restlessness toward God—smell of nothing but the pitch of an impious author. That, moreover, was what Christ did to the blessed Catherine of Siena. She had taught Rosa without another teacher.

Notes.

He is called a companion with whom we associate familiarly and converse wittily, who is called by the Greeks synomilos or synomilites. Plautus uses that Latin name in such a base sense that it is unworthy of angels.

b A proxenetas is one who intervenes in making stipulations or contracts, and whom the poet Martial calls by the Greek name προξενήτης, that is, a negotiator, mediator, conciliator, or interpreter for two who are stipulating with each other, or pledging, or entering into another contract. The root is in the Greek word προξενέω, which means to act as a conciliator of contracts.

Symmysta properly in Latin is a fellow priest. But since that meaning does not apply here, we must turn to the Hierolexicon of both Macros, which under the word Symmysta, Greek συμμύστης, affirms that this name is sometimes taken for secretary, or for one who is another's confidant.

I searched in vain for the meaning of this appellation in various dictionaries. But I finally realized that the name dinomilus is composed of the Greek adverb δὴν that is long, & the verb ὁμιλέω, that is, I converse or conversational. The biographer, therefore, by that composite name wished to indicate that the angel had long been accustomed to converse or talk with St. Rose, if I am not mistaken.

The ancients called veredos the very fastest racehorses, which they used for hunting, or the chariots themselves that such horses drew, as others prefer. Hence a courier is taken by some for a charioteer. The ancients also used relays of horses for their princes' couriers, who, changing horses at the stations, completed the journey very swiftly, and from this they were called riders. Whatever the precise meaning of these names may be, the author of the Acts could have omitted most of these synonyms, which do not seem to be sufficiently appropriate to be attributed to angels.

While Hansen here asserts that the Holy Virgin spoke to the angel in the metrical rhythm of her own idiom, he sufficiently indicates that this speech was composed in Spanish verses: for St. Rose used the Spanish language, being of Spanish origin, although born in Lima.

g John of Laet, book. Chapter 7 of the West Indies 2. Concerning the fruit of the Indian tree, which is commonly called cacao, and representing its figure engraved on copper, he adds the following about its use:But its principal use is in a drink which they make from them (which they call CHOCOLATE), insanely beloved by the inhabitants of these regions, and held in the highest esteem, since for those who are not accustomed to it, the foam that rises to the top and the sediment, like a boil, rightly provoke nausea. With this drink, the natives also entertain their guests of high rank. The Spaniards, but especially the Spanish women, are so infatuated with him that once they have grown accustomed to him, they deny that they can live without him. Then, after various methods of preparing it, she adds the following: They also make a certain paste from these, which they claim cures disorders of the chest and stomach as well as catarrhs, although it finds little credence among those who do not share this view.

I have no doubt that this drink of our holy Virgin, weakened by fasts and penances, has been beneficial. But from that one cannot draw a conclusion for everyone, since Latius, in the chapter soon to be cited, warns as follows: "Excessive use of a potion made from cacao is the cause of many infirmities and diseases, because it produces obstructions, corrupts the complexion, and induces cacochymia and similar perverse affections."

Potosi is a city in the Kingdom of Peru in America, at the foot of the mountain of the same name, and renowned for its silver mines; its location, barren soil, wealth, and mines are described in the aforementioned book, Book XI, of the Western Indies, chapter. 8 & sequente describes each one in detail.

Cerberus, the three-headed dog and guardian of the underworld, as the poets fabulize, is here taken for the very demon, as the sense makes clear.

Chapter XVI
Persecutions, illnesses, and other adversities patiently endured, and foreseen in a certain marvelous revelation.

[A slender rod is the rod of persecution] Roses and thorns do not come together late, but are born together. The same thing happened to this virgin. She had not yet left the cradle of her earliest infancy when she took on the appearance, the name, and the thorns of a rose. He had reached the ninth month of his life when his nursing mother's breasts failed, her milk ran out, and, since there was no room at home to hire a wet nurse, the infant's first hardships began. They tried to make up for the lack of milk with thinner porridge. But the extremely difficult feeding tormented the little one's mouth with dislocation. Yet Rosula did not weep, but by the idle sucking of her thumb she more deceived her hunger in silence than she soothed it. He learned to endure from a very early age, before he could even cry or speak. Age has grown, but with it, affliction; For she had scarcely begun to walk and speak when, every day, because of a dispute between her grandmother and her mother over the name Rose, the innocent girl found herself most wretchedly entangled in the persecution of both. For whether, when called by the name of Rose, she had rushed to the household, or to her mother, the grumbling grandmother would be raging in the little one with her switches. Whether she answered to the name of Isabella when called, the angry mother would unleash her virginity, slaughtering the undeserving. Thus, as the women's wrath alternated, the suffering Virgin, wherever she turned, fell into punishment and, for that very reason, because she had done no wrong, suffered.

[214] [from the austere household;] The disposition of the little daughter was as sweet as her mother's was harsh, so that it seemed as if only the chariot of patience had joined that most dissimilar pair. The virgin's modesty, sobriety, devotion, flight from the world, silence, and vigils were, to an impatient mother, mere offenses, which she punished with shouts, scoffs, insults, and finally fists and kicks; a gnarled quince-wood rod was often at hand. He used to frequently tickle the now-older girl, especially when it was discovered that, following the example of St. Catherine of Siena, she had cut a red lock of hair for herself. Nor were her household any gentler toward her, once it became known how extraordinary her life was—the frequency and intercourse of heavenly visions, and especially that abstinence, clearly beyond human strength, by which it was easily shown that a young woman could not naturally be sustained on so meager, almost nonexistent, a diet. The brothers feared that their sister might soon be dragged before the tribunal of the Holy Inquisition, to the great disgrace of their family, as a suspect in impostures and frauds, or openly guilty of sacrilegious falsehood and feigned sanctity, or to be convicted of it. Thus they would daily instill these fears with their scolding, tormenting her with these threats, leaving her abandoned on all sides. Nor did the thoughtless mother hesitate to publicly shame her daughter before friends and strangers, proclaiming her a hypocrite, a deceiver, a pretender, and utterly devoid of any genuine virtue.

[215] Added to the sum of the persecution was the unbelief of certain confessors, whether through negligence or ignorance. [And to the unbelieving confessors,] at first they strove to persuade the Virgin of what they had convinced themselves, namely, that she was not walking on a straight path, or at least not safely, that she was suffering from dizziness of the brain and other harmful disorders, and that the frivolous illusions she believed to be heavenly illuminations were nothing of the sort. These and similar things would have stunned any other, but not Rosa, who had steel for patience within her, and moreover, because she perceived more clearly from such harsh trials of affliction that she was being led by the very hand of the Blessed. May Catherine of Siena's ways lead to the union of the Spouse. Hence, on one occasion, when asked by a distinguished woman why she did not ask her seraphic mistress to be delivered from these torments by her powerful intercession, she heroically replied; What do you think, would my seraphic mother have me return to those things? I will surely seek another path for myself, apart from that one which the glorious one herself trod? Far from me be such lazy idleness. Both of them knew whatever Rosa had from B. Catherine would ask, and would immediately receive it, and this will be shown below. but the noble Disciple valued the painful stigmata of her teacher more than delicate consolations. The Spouse of the Crucified was ashamed to be found, even for a moment, apart from the cross and passion.

And indeed, either never or rarely was there a lack of ample material for his great patience [which was succeeded by illnesses], for when he was free from external affliction, a more severe one occupied him internally. And as they ceased from persecution abroad, one sickness after another fell upon the body. In Canta, she lay in bed for three years, afflicted by a dreadful nerve-traction, enduring immense torment without a single groan—except that she was wrung out more by compassion for their plight than by her own suffering, since she could not do without their care. Hence, in this and her other infirmities, she was accustomed to say: "Oh, what a healthy, pleasant, and happy fate it would be to be beset by more frequent illnesses, so long as it were free from the inconvenience of those closest to her!" Often the Virgin's throat and windpipe, from inflammation of the lower muscles, was attacked by the most acute angina, to which at times a more dangerous cynanche would sometimes grow: more often the whole lungs and bronchi had to contend with asthma; Very often, a severely troublesome sciatica pressed heavily on both hips, not less so at the same time a pleurisy was twitching the ribs. Stomach, pericardial, and intestinal pains, without order or number, brought on frequent convulsions; And these diseases, which were each strong enough to prostrate anyone very robust, would often strike the invalid in pairs, threes, or fours at once, from various and opposing causes, to the astonishment of the physicians, who wondered where in that dry, parched, and exhausted little body such diverse fires could find fuel and kindling. The virgin rightly perceived that these agonizing festivals did not arise so much from harm or the temperaments of the humors, but from the Spouse's benevolent hand.

[217] She had turned fevers—both the fleeting ones and the more stubborn ones, of almost every kind—into a mere matter of habit, almost a joke, but not so the joint pains of the hands and feet, to which the well-known (yet dreadful) names of gout, podagra, and arthritis have clung. This disease, because it is considered less life-threatening, finds no remedy or relief anywhere for the amount of misery, nor for the magnitude of the pain. Nor is there perhaps another who either more quickly drives away a long-standing patient's endurance or more voraciously consumes it. Nevertheless, Rosa received him with frequent hospitality and equanimity, and with the invincible virtue of tolerance, peacefully made him lie down with her on the same bed. It was a prodigious sight to see the most tender Virgin, with burning joints, swollen knots, and distended limbs, her muscles and ligaments tearing apart in unison, lying amid the extremes of pain, yet calm, serene, and placid. nor accusing his torments with a groan and a faint sigh. If he was asked how he was, he would answer that he was fine. or, in comparison to his own crimes, what he was suffering was very slight. To others, when they inquired about the severity of the illness, she would say that whatever came from the hand of such a sweet Spouse was sweet and honeyed. that others should think about the punishment of the underworld. With this single meditation, to dull the sharpest pains of this time. Often turning to the Spouse: "Lord," she said, "increase, increase the pain, so long as you increase love as well."

[218] [which she called heavenly favors,] Speaking of these more familiarly at one time to a certain devout person, she recounted this entire series of illnesses among the precious and more illustrious favors bestowed upon her by God, and added that unless she herself had experienced them, she could never have been persuaded that a single miserable body could be capable of so many and such varied pains. nor to think that there is any suffering of any kind or part that she herself has not felt. nevertheless, he always had a heavenly protection and strength to sustain all things unperturbed. that he had never allowed a movement of impatience to creep in, by which even a hair's breadth would have departed from the resigned conformity and submission to the divine will. Therefore she was ashamed of herself, that she had not yet responded to so many heavenly benefits as she ought; and from this, she rightly considered herself the most ungrateful of all creatures living throughout the world, since she had seen that the Lord treated her in the same way that he is accustomed to treat only his dearest friends, and yet she did not love so loving and amiable a God in a manner worthy of him; and perhaps this was her feeling when she prayed that, together with her pains, her love might be increased.

[219] [which he had foreseen from a certain marvelous vision,] That famous vision, which the most learned professors of mystical theology have held in such high esteem among the diviner excesses of the Rose, seems plainly to belong here. I will present it here, in the same way the Virgin herself, a few days before her death, faithfully recounted it to Doctor Castillo, with a bare and simple thread. "Suspended (he said) in the unitive light of the most tranquil contemplation, I saw the radiance of admirable excellence, beauty, and majesty, which the ever-present and subtle immensity had diffused without limit in every direction." The center of this delightful radiance was occupied by a multicolored, splendid arch, gracefully variegated with a manifold painting of lights, and above that another arch extended, of equal beauty and majesty with the lower one. And in the center of this convexion, the glorious cross of our Savior was conspicuously adorned: it was purple, blood-stained, pierced by the nails, and inscribed with the triumphal title of the Crucified One. Furthermore, the inner arc was filled by the radiant, clear, and pre-lucid humanity of my Lord Jesus Christ, surrounded by so great a mantle of glory as I had never before beheld in it. and it pleased His sweet goodness to grant me extraordinary and wonderfully vivid powers, by which, with an unturned gaze, I might most freely and at length behold my most magnificent King in all His splendor: for not, as at other times, from the side, or only from the head and chest down, but I saw him whole, and entire from the top of his head to the soles of his feet, & face to face."

The doctor here interrupted the Virgin by asking what color those arches had been. [that they would have to endure them later,] He replied: manifold and exotic, to the point that no comparison to any color in this visible world could be made, not even the faintest, except that the beauty of the rainbow seemed to have gathered in itself the variety and grace of all possible colors with a certain infinite excellence and dignity. Then Rosa continued: "I perceived from Christ's neighboring humanity sweetly flowing into the depths of my soul inexplicable flames and strains of glory, so that I believed myself already wholly released from this corruptible world and translated to the blessed joys of perpetual fruition." After these things, he, fair in form above the sons of men, set up a balance and, I know not what, weights on the open platform; soon the populous phalanxes of angels, adorned with festive attire and shining with splendor, drew near, bowing with graceful reverence to the Lord of majesty. A great number of happy souls also approached, and, with a similar rite of adoration, reverently honored the Savior, standing apart.

[221] The angels, having seized the balance and weights, began to load the scales, [and knew the forerunners of a more abundant grace,] piling affliction upon affliction into a heap, as if to probe their annoyance to the very limit. But Christ interposed for those who were striving, and, assuming the office as though by a loftier, angelic decree, lifted the scales into balance with his own hands, and from the heaps laid upon them distributed afflictions to the souls present, among which he set aside for me the portion of the gravest adversity. Then, with the weights once again imposed, graces were piled upon graces from the opposite side. and as the angels drew near to lift the balance, Christ interceded again, as though to more accurately perform the worthy function with his own omnipotent arm. And in proportion to the afflictions distributed, he most attentively divided those precious heaps of grace among the present souls. Nor did he pass me by: for to me, in proportion to the weight of the adversity I was granted, he has measured out an inestimable supply of abundant grace. I noticed the souls of those present so distended by the same treasure of grace, and so filled to the brim, that their mouths and eyes overflowed with its restrained fullness.

[222] When these things were accomplished, Salvator most fittingly raised a sonorous voice: [as she herself did a few days before her death] "Let all know," she said, "that grace follows tribulation;" Let them know that without the weight of afflictions one does not attain the fullness of grace; let them acknowledge that, as the hardships increase, the measure of charisms increases accordingly. They do not want to be wrong. This alone is the true ladder of paradise, and there is no other way to ascend to heaven except through the cross. Upon hearing this, (Rosa continued) I was seized by a most powerful impulse, which I cannot express in words, to leap into the middle of the forum, and there to cry out with the loudest and most powerful shouts to all men of every age, sex, and station: Hear, O people, hear, O all you nations, I declare to you from the mouth of Christ, that grace is not acquired without prior affliction; that it is necessary to heap up tribulation upon tribulation, in order to attain to the innermost participation of the divine nature, to the glory of the sons of God, to the perfect beauty of the soul. The same stimulus urged me most impetuously to preach the beauty of divine grace. I was in anguish, I was sweating, I was gasping for breath, and it seemed the soul could no longer be held in its bodily prison, but, leaving it behind, would burst forth alone and more agile, crying out through the world: Oh, if mortals knew what grace is, how beautiful, noble, and precious it is, and how many riches, what treasures, what joys, and what delights it embraces, surely everyone would desire to be afflicted and to suffer, and all would willingly ask for hardships, persecutions, illnesses, and torments for the sake of obtaining the single, most opulent gain of grace. This is the merchandise of holy patience, and a useful interest: no one would complain of the cross, if they recognized this as the legitimate scale for such great treasures.

[223] [He explained it to Doctor Castillo.] The doctor interrupted, wishing to probe further as to under what form or figure the Virgin had appeared to him. Rosa promptly replied that she had nothing in common with corporeal forms, but far less with color, or any created beauty. that its nature is altogether divine, yet he took good care to observe that grace is something other than God, even though it wonderfully contains the image of God and constitutes the soul deiform. Finally, the doctor asked by what kind of utterance—intellectual or vocal—Christ had spoken the words quoted above. The Virgin replied that she did not know by what name she would be called among those skilled in such matters, but that this was the manner of speech: that from the mouth of Christ proceeded a clear and most pure conception of wisdom, which, penetrating to the depths of the soul, signified within whatever it wished to be understood. This illustrious vision timely prepared Rosa for the final pains of her extreme paralysis, which brought about her untimely death. Therefore, when the happy passage of the Virgin is treated of below, the discussion of these matters will be resumed; for the whole patience of the Rose could not be explained in a single chapter.

Notes.

The Cydonian apple, which others call the cotoneaster, is a tree commonly known, about which Pliny writes in his book. Chapter 15 11 thus writes: Of these, the next in evil size is that which we call COTTON, and the Greeks Cydonia; Brought from the island of Crete. Now that tree is found in various regions, & by the Spaniards membrillo, the French coignier, by the Italians cotogno, Germans quittenbaum, and finally the Belgians queenboom is called. Furthermore, I changed the masculine adjective, which had been mistakenly added to this word by the author or the typographer, to the feminine form.

The Inquisition is a tribunal used in America, Portugal, Spain, and certain other regions, probably so called from inquiring, because the administrators of this sacred office inquire into heretics, hypocrites, and other sacrileges.

Angina, so called because it constricts the throat and blocks the passage of breath, is a very acute form of disease in which the throat is constricted, so that one can barely breathe.

d Cynanche, (written by the Greeks κυνάγχη & sometimes συνάγχη) differs from angina in certain circumstances, which the curious reader of such matters will find in Gorræus, in his Medical Definitions, page 253 and following.

Asthma, from the Greeks ἄσθμα, is an obstruction of the lungs, causing frequent and difficult breathing without fever, such as that experienced by those who have run quickly, as Gorræus teaches in the work cited above, page 58.

Pleuritis, which Pliny calls a compunction of the side, is an inflammation of the membrane surrounding the ribs, which the Greeks call pleura, with acute fever, a stabbing pain, difficulty breathing, and a cough. See the various species of this disease on page 378 of the recently cited Gorræus, if you wish.

The pericardium is a thick, tough sac arising from the base of the heart and surrounding it on all sides; for this reason, the common people call it the heart's capsule. The Greek wordpericardion comes from the preposition peri "around" and the noun kardia, which means "heart."

By intermittent fevers, diurnal, or rather ephemeral, are understood diurnal, or rather ephemeral, are understood diurnal, or rather ephemeral, are understood diurnal, or daily, from the root ἐπὶ through & ἡμέρα day, as if lasting through the day.

Paralysis (from the Greek word paralýō, to loosen or weaken) is the relaxation of the nerves, when the nerves and parts of the body are completely devoid of sensation and movement. On its various species, physicians who have already been frequently praised on this subject may be consulted.

Chapter XVII
Her ardent love for Jesus, her bridegroom.

[The Saint burned with such a fire of divine love,] How great an Aetna of divine love burned in the heart of the Rose, God was pleased at times to make manifest by visible signs, while from the face of the one praying He commanded splendors and little flames to sparkle. One night, Rose had to sleep in a room with a virgin companion. Waking around the cock's crow, she saw in the darkness a multitude of eyes sparkling in the room. Soon, looking around in fear, he noticed that it came from no other place than the face of the one praying in the corner of the Rose. For she had secretly let herself down from her bed onto the floor to pour out her prayers, and where she thought she was hiding most securely in the darkness, she was betrayed by bright sparks, shaken from her mouth and eyes as she prayed, a prodigy that testified to how fierce a fire raged within. It has been discovered that this very thing happened to an unsuspecting Rose on multiple occasions in front of different people. Nor were the sparks of that same ardor any less numerous: those fiery sighs by which, like fans, that kindled and burning heart both blew away its heat and at the same time fanned it more fiercely. He often had this in his mouth: "Lord, who would not love you?" But I, O good Jesus, when will I begin to love you worthily? Alas, how far I am from a perfect, intimate, and most robust love for you! I don't yet know how to love you properly. What a shame! What good is that heart to me, if it has not yet completely burned to ashes for your love? Indeed, this is a proper thirst for the love above, so that it thirsts only for that which it already has in abundance.

[225] [so that he might break forth here] Warmed by this affection, Rosa, in order to obtain divine love, gathered from the very fiery quiver of divine love several dart-like phrases into the form of a little prayer, which I am pleased to transcribe here verbatim, because it pleased everyone, benefited many in its use, and is teeming with both signs and stimuli for the beloved. This is his tenor: "My Lord Jesus Christ, God and man, truly our founder and Redeemer, for whatever I have ever offended you, I am truly tormented in mind;" Because you are who you are, and because I love you above all things. My true God and Spouse of my soul, the entire joy of my heart: I, I desire to love you, most benign Jesus, with that perfect, most efficacious, most sincere, ineffable, most intense, incomparable, incomprehensible, irrefutable, unconquerable love with which all the citizens of heaven love you at once. Moreover, I long to love you, O God of my heart and my life. God, my delight, I would love you as much as your most holy Mother, my Lady, the purest Virgin, loves you. Rather, I desire you, O health, O joy of my soul; you, I say, I desire to love as much as you yourself, my God, love yourself. I am consumed, I shall fail, I shall be consumed by the fire of your divine love, O my most benign Jesus. That rhetoric is crude in the ears of the world. But in the school of divine love, one recognizes oneself here and finds one's own expression.

[226] [in all public conversation;] The ingenuous virgin knew that she could conceal all other virtues within herself; only charity, because it is a flame, could in no way be restrained or hidden. Henceforth, Rose's conversations, greetings, and responses were continually seasoned with mention of divine love. When speaking with relatives, matrons, and virgins, Rose's first words were: "Let us love our God, let us love." If an encomium was to be offered for any absent person, Rosa would sum up the whole matter with this concise summary: "She loves God with great strength and seriousness." Whenever she had fallen down to the confessor's feet to make her sacramental confession, fortified by the sign of the cross, she would burst out with these words: "The Lord be with you, my Father." May God be our love. Oh, who will grant that we may love him perfectly! Alas! Those who do not love him, do not know goodness. In every conversation, Rose's only delight and comfort was to hear or speak of divine love. to inculcate the supreme obligation to love God, who so greatly loved us and loves us; to distinguish the various titles, modes, and incentives of this love; finally, she would skillfully bend every other argument of the dissertation, however diverse, to this point, so that the love and loveliness of God might become the theme of the remaining discourses. Once this was achieved, it was marvelous to hear the Virgin, otherwise taciturn, reasoning with the most fitting yet fervently ignited words about the debt of charity, about the affable goodness of God worthy of being loved, of the beauty of a majesty so worthy of being loved: it was only that emphatic words did not fail Rose here; Here his fertile eloquence flowed abundantly; his spirit burned within him, his voice sharpened, his eyes flashed, his heart was kindled, and no one present could doubt that his mouth spoke from the abundance of his heart.

[227] [while she believed herself to be alone,] But when she was truly alone in the recess of her little hut, or when she thought no one was watching her, she would more tenderly and freely, in prose, verse, speech, and song, utter the most solemn axioms of seraphic love. Questor Gundisalvus, together with his wife and children, would sometimes secretly listen at home to Rosa, in a secluded spot, more fervently celebrating divine loves with pious cries. The gist of the outcry was this: it invited, in turn, each order of creatures to the love of the sweetest Creator, urging the elements, the heavens, the angels, the plants, the animals, repeatedly repeating: "Let us love God, let us love God, love is God, God is love." Nor did he say these things perfunctorily. For two and sometimes three hours, she would remain fixed in one spot, gazing at the sky, and linger over these words with such warmth and impetus that she moved the hearts of her listeners to a love and contrition they had never known.

[228] Soon, finding no way to either temper or satisfy himself amid these passions, he gave himself more freely to his feelings. She would seize the harp (which she had never learned to play) hanging by chance on a nearby wall, and with the touch of its strings she would moderate the impulse of her voice, sweetly singing the wounded laments of love, so that with every gesture and rite she might display herself as a lover to her Beloved. It happened once that all the harps were out of strings; nevertheless, Rosa played the harp, her attention more intent on the inner sound of the ten-string psaltery with its song. It seemed that, beneath these most innocent attempts at harmony, Rosa, with her boy Love, was being reborn, and, forgetting all her sorrows, she indulged in the simple tenderness of her innermost affections: her heart bubbled with joy, and, believing no judges to be looming near her, she soothed her fervent spirit with that slight evaporation. However, it happened that, carried away by sweetness, she did not notice the household servants coming and going. Hence, these verses, bound to Spanish numbers, have often been heard from the singer's mouth, which I have rendered into Latin however I could:

Behold, among the flowers you grow more beautiful & among the olives
Jesus, little one Nor do you spurn being soft to Rose.

They were charms by which the Virgin more freely courted the favor of her Beloved, who, having been born of a poor mother, Olive, and a father, de Floribus, had in no way disdained the Rose, since he had allowed himself to be so tenderly loved by her. Many things would remain to be said about the similar vapors of a loving Rose, about the pre-nuptial aspirations to the bridegroom, and about the eclogues; perhaps not unworthy of relation. But we must hurry to more solid matters.

Zeal is the companion and witness of the most vehement love: [She also grieved all the more because of it,] nor could the Virgin conceal its ardors within herself. He was so zealous that he would not tolerate anything being done, either privately or publicly, that would in the slightest violate the honor of God. She certainly did not conceal even the slightest thing. First of all, she detested idle tales in church (the rustic scourge of nobility) so much that, though timid and very sparing of words, she could not help admonishing the talkative, out of respect for the place and for proper urbanity, yet with such moderation, humility, and prudence that she seemed more to beseech than to rebuke. At home, all the family members were careful not to utter any foolishness in the Virgin's presence. They feared Rosa's censor still rested on her little eyebrow. They knew the girl, who was so patient with her own atrocious and frequent injuries, could by no means endure alone those by which she would have thought God was even slightly offended. This sense had been with him since early childhood. for where were the other offspring, for the garrulous genius of a rude age, or they spoke of filth they did not yet understand, or innocently repeated amorous ditties perhaps overheard elsewhere, Rosa would weep, flee, report the childish disgrace to her mother, complain that God was offended, and whatever she could not otherwise punish sufficiently, she avenged on herself with the bitterest tears.

[230] [That God is offended by sins,] He detested lies, which are seldom absent from the constant jesting and talk of boys, so terribly that it became his own apophthegm: "One should not lie for heaven's sake, nor for the sake of the earth, because God is truth." Hence, if anything false were told to others, even in good faith, out of a zeal for accurate truth, he would most calmly correct the person reporting it, interjecting politely that it had not happened that way, but in this manner. that it was not this, but that which was done or said. About an hour before the blessed Virgin's happy passing to better things, a certain religious priest, whom she had desired to see before her death, came there. As he entered, one of the women present said to him in all sincerity: "You have come at the right time, O Father;" For Rosa had already ordered you to be called. Hearing she was dying, and more scrupulously concerned about the truth, she gathered her weakened spirit into her voice: "Let us not err," she said. I did indeed desire to see you before my death, and I said only that, nothing more. Indeed, he had such great concern for the truth, because God is truth. Tears, because he had learned that they belonged exclusively to God's treasury, he would not allow anyone to shed them in vain, desiring that they be reserved by all as a tribute to the divine majesty alone. So, on a certain occasion, seeing a weeping mother, as if seized by zeal for a divine quest, she said, "Hey there, mother, what are you doing?" Are you going to lavish the treasure of the one and only God on Gaza? Rather, you should remember that this precious liquid is owed to God alone for the washing away of our sins. The Virgin was so zealous that nothing might detract from the honor or tribute of the heavenly Spouse.

[231] [& was especially delighted by the conversion of a certain sinner,] A genuine offspring of that most friendly zeal was the immense joy with which Rosa rejoiced whenever she saw the honor and worship of God promoted by some notable success. A rumor had spread throughout the market of Lima that a certain woman, solemnly dedicated to God, having broken the bonds of her religion, had fled from Cantabria to the Indies, and, under the guise of a man, had long wandered about the city of Guamanga. Finally, by a marvelous benefit of God, having returned to her heart, she now lives in the aforementioned city, having resumed her sacred habit and dwelling in religious enclosure among the nuns. When these things became known to Rosa, you would have seen her zeal blazing with as many affections as the various objects of sense carried her off in different directions. He was first pained by the sacrilegious insult, by which divine honor had been violated by such a foul and public outrage; but at the same time his soul rejoiced in the victory of divine grace: he pitied the fallen sinner, and at the same time he overflowed with joy that in her the Lord's wondrous goodness and mercy were publicly celebrated by all. Rosa was celebrating this festive day, not that she would have been any happier if she had been granted the scepter and empire of all America by lot or as a gift. Finally, she feared as much as she hoped for the constancy and stability of the penitent's conversion; but to the abundance of her consolation came a heavenly revelation, by which Christ himself assured Rosa of the sincerity of her monastic repentance, adding that she would not only persevere but also attain an eminent degree of holiness.

Nothing could satisfy Rosa, who longed to testify to her love for her Bridegroom. Hence she constantly lamented to the confessors that she was unworthy of the sweetness of the Divine goodness in her, and did not know how to properly cultivate such an exalted friendship. And while he was doing everything, [with the fever of a public speaker upon him,] he complained that he had done nothing. It happened that one of his confessors became gravely ill, and at a very inopportune time; For the day was at hand on which he was to deliver his formal address to the people. He therefore sent messengers to tell Rose that he was overcome by a sudden illness and would not have the strength to preach. to lament a notable defect that was to have partially obscured the festival; especially since a great multitude of distinguished persons had been invited to the assembly (from which exceptional results were hoped). Rose, startled by the sad news, after a brief deliberation replied: "It is a matter of honor and obedience to God." Go, tell the sick man that he will most certainly preach on the appointed day. Help will arrive in time, so that he can, though not without a condition burdensome to another. The condition, however, was that Rosa should in the meantime contract her confessor's fevers, just as once happened to Saint Catherine of Siena. Nor did the Virgin hesitate to make a pact with God under such a burden: for she preferred to be scorched to the very marrow by others' fevers, rather than have the glory of God suffer detriment in a mutilated celebration because of a lack of participants. It happened, as the Virgin had foretold: a few hours before the sermon the preacher recovered and performed his duty splendidly. Meanwhile, a fever not her own was tormenting Rosa. Yet he did not wish to be absent from the conversation, for he easily overcame the feverish heat, inflamed by zeal for the glory of God.

The most restless force of love devised nothing that might not serve its one and only beloved. [She was preparing spiritual garments for Christ about to be born.] She had learned, as history relates, that the holy Catherine of Siena had once given Christ, who was then hidden under the guise of a poor man, a garment. So then the loving Rose devised a method and a way by which she might most devoutly clothe Christ with a garment, laboriously woven from spiritual threads. Nowhere was he poorer than when he was born in Bethlehem as a little child, naked, with his destitute mother driven out of the inn, freezing in a stable, wrapped in swaddling cloths in a humble manger. Thus, Rosa devoted all her skill and effort to preparing for little Love garments worthy of a singular and precious stitching of pious deeds. It seems fitting here to quote more simply from Rose's own pamphlets the formula by which the Virgin defined this work for herself. So that nothing might be overlooked through a failing memory, he had noted each item in the following order on a small slip of paper: "Jesus." In the year 1616, with the favor and help of Jesus Christ and His blessed Mother, I begin to prepare a garment for my sweetest Jesus, about to be born, trembling, naked, and poor in Bethlehem. Let there be 50 Litanys, 900 Rosaries, and 5 days of fasting in reverence of the Holy Incarnation. The devotion consists of 9 stations for the Holy Eucharist, nine decades of the rosary, and 9 days of fasting, corresponding to the number of months the Most Pure Mother carried Him in her womb. They spend five days of fasting, five stations, and as many entire rosaries in honor of His Nativity. I offer the Lord's cincture: five crowns, five days of fasting, and as many stations in reverence of His circumcision. Let 33 sacred Communions, 33 Masses, and 33 hours of mental prayer border the fringes and linings of both the shroud and the bandages. 33 Our Fathers and Hail Marys, the Apostles' Creed recited the same number of times, with the Glory be to the Father and the antiphon Salve Regina; Also, 33 Hail Marys, 33 days of fasting, and three thousand lashes, in veneration of the age she lived on earth. Finally, in place of a cradle, I will offer my boy my tears, sighs, and acts of love, along with my soul and heart, so that nothing remains for me, for it is fitting that I possess nothing. It is clear that from the devout workshop of the Mystical Rose this kind of garment emerged on multiple occasions: for she would from time to time present the same as a generous gift to her dearest friends, to be applied and wrapped around the naked Jesus, her beloved. And what did he not do to be loved by all? Salvator was pleased by Rose's ardor here, and God sealed that great zeal for spreading her love with an immense miracle, which I set aside to relate separately in the next chapter.

Chapter XVIII
The manifest miracle of the sweating image was obtained by his prayers, so that people might be stirred to the love of Christ.

[Rose in the oratory of her lodging] In the oratory of Gundisalvus the treasurer, among other sacred images celebrated for both their beauty and their reverence, stood out that one which depicted the manly face of Christ on canvas with the most skillful colors and lines. Rosa was devoted to this particular image with the utmost devotion; she would gaze at this effigy (whenever the veil was drawn back on the altar) with fixed eyes, drinking it in with the most avid eyes, and almost absorbing it in her most intense contemplation. Indeed, feeling his heart grow warm as he gazed upon her. Therefore, in the year 1617, on the 15th of April (which was a Saturday) in the evening, around the ringing of the Angelus, Rosa, with Gundisalvi's wife and daughters, were insistently praying in the aforementioned oratory, as was their custom. The venerable image of the Savior had been uncovered, to either side of which two candles burned on the altar, and a third shone separately on a small table some distance away. This virgin, more than usual, began to be stirred more vehemently by the pangs of love, so that she could no longer restrain her voice; instead, rising to her feet, she addressed the sacred image with the greatest fervor and in a loud voice, as if she were alone in the oratory.

The gist of the fervent prayer was this: "Lord, [praying fervently before the painted image of Christ,] oh, when will everyone love you as much as you deserve to be loved?" How long will you put up with being angered by people's sins and treated with disrespect? Oh, who would grant that all might recognize how worthy you are of love, and embrace you not for fear of servile punishments, nor for the mere hope of mercenary rewards, but for the sake of loving you—because you are you? Come, do it, Lord, make yourself loved as is fitting; shake your quiver, hurl on every side the purest fiery arrows of your love, so that brilliant fires of the heart may break forth everywhere. Let all things serve you. May every breast be enervated in you by the pious violence of charity. All affections sweat balsam for you. to you (I say) to you, most loving Jesus, who with so much love for the human race boils and burns. Amid these and similar voices, the quaestor's wife slipped quietly from the oratory into the adjoining atrium with her daughters, so that they might not interrupt the Virgin while she was speaking, or, by their more unrestrained impulses, hinder these impulses in deference to her importunate presence.

[236] Yet one of the daughters, with her mother's consent, crept in, [her prayers drew sweat from her,] as if she were going to purify the altar with candles: and immediately, as if seized by a sudden terror, she interrupted Rosa; What do I see? he said Behold, the face of the Savior was completely covered with sweat. The mother heard these things outside. And immediately upon entering, she saw abundant drops of sweat, like tiny pearls, shining and protruding all over the face of the most sacred image: new ones sprang up beneath the others, and by their mutual friction they dissolved into a flow, trickling in various lines through the painted hair and beard. Stunned by the spectacle, and not daring to approach any closer, she sent for her husband, who was fortunately away from home, to be summoned back as quickly as possible. For he had summoned the secretary, John de Tineo, who had then returned to Lima from the port of Callao, on business. They both came, unaware of what was going on. and having scarcely entered the oratory, where his wife, with John de Benavides and Peter Chiandro, relatives, was waiting in silence, they saw, not without a pang of piety, the effigy, from every side, as if thickly drenched and coated with drops of pre-dawn dew. And already the drops had trickled down to the very edge of the wooden frame that enclosed the painting, and new drops were constantly forming from the forehead, cheeks, and eyes.

[237] Rash credulity is detrimental to the faith of miracles; circumspect doubt is beneficial, [as many eyewitnesses testify], so that by the severity of the examination, like the abrasion of a polish, the solidity may be explored and the truth made clear. This is how it happened: for Gundisalvus first summoned Angelinus Medorius the Roman, who had painted the image, into his house, so that he might investigate the origin of the sweat, if perhaps it was due to the nature of the colors. A painter was present, marveling at the clarity of the most limpid humor; he tasted a few drops with his finger, rubbed them, and brought them to his nostrils. And since he could detect nothing either foul or oily, having carefully examined everything, he declared that what was taking place in the image seemed entirely supernatural to him. that experiments of color, smell, touch, and other circumstances can in no way be deceived, and that the industries of nature and art are far from such a prodigy.

[238] Not at all satisfied with this, the quaestor sends his trusted man, Andrew Lopes, and orders that the Fathers Diego Martinez and Diego de Peñalosa be summoned by name from the nearby Jesuit college. Yet they did not strictly examine either of them as to what they were called for at that hour. Father Peñalosa came with his associate, Francisco Lopez, coadjutor. For P. Martinez was held up, and the third hour of the night was at hand. Both of them entered, and after a brief speech, approached the altar; having long and attentively contemplated the still-sweating face, he finally wiped away the moisture with the requested rag. But the more it was wiped away, the more fresh sweat broke out; so Peñalosa again applied the gossypium, which was dry. Again, drops succeeded drops, and rivulets succeeded rivulets. Then he applied a sheet of paper to see whether, perhaps, some oily, greasy substance was present in the moisture: the paper adhered, but shortly afterward it dried; from this he concluded that it was purely aqueous. The pious spectacle lasted for four consecutive hours and beyond, with everyone in the crowd marveling that, without any loss of color or beauty, that most sacred face appeared more beautiful and more vivid than usual after the sweat. Nor has this sweat so vanished but that it has left traces under the beard, right to the very edge, plainly visible many years and lustres later. A legal examination and inquiry by public authority remained, so that the certainty of the miracle might be more clearly recognized. Therefore, the Archbishop of Lima, D. Bartholomew Lobo Guerrerob, for taking the legal depositions, appointed Doctor John de la Rocca, parish priest and metropolitan archdeacon, who, in the presence of the notary James Blanca, priest, and having heard the unanimous testimonies of all who had been present at the spectacle, took down the entire sequence of events in order and committed it to the clerk to be duly entered in the protocol.

[239] [This prodigy frees the terrified family from fear,] Meanwhile, Gundisalvus, his wife, and their household were seized by the gravest fear, lest perhaps on account of some hidden crime of their own or of their household this portent had occurred in their own home, or was threatening the miserable with some impending calamity of divine vengeance. But Rosa, readily meeting them, fully aware of the entire secret, commands them with a calm and placid voice not to be afraid: nothing terrifying, nothing sinister, or horrible is foretold. all of this, whatever miracle it was, served only to show that the Savior, who so earnestly desires to be loved by all, was revealed by his very own sweat-stained face and unusually brilliant radiance; that this stage was the sole property of divine Love, inviting the reciprocal loves of men. He cried out, not with a single mouth but with all his pores, in the image of the Redeemer: "Redeem the one who loves you." For who would perceive their pores opening to this clamor unless they were sweating? Rosa had not yet ceased speaking, when the fiery words with which the Virgin had addressed the image before the sweat came back to Gundisalvi's wife. He compared the former with the latter and found them to harmonize wonderfully with one another. Nor did those who had enjoyed the spectacle of sweat before him any less openly confess that, under his gaze, they had felt new, sharp, unusual stirrings of divine love—and this was what the Virgin had so ardently and anxiously longed for. Those who love this way never grow weary of praying continually: "May charity blaze with fire, may ardor kindle those around."

[240] [and ordering his wounded arm to be anointed with that sweat,] Nor did the miracle stop there, but with the birth of a new miracle it both confirmed its own truth and delivered Rose's arm and faith. A few days before (namely, the second day of Easter), Rosa, having accidentally fallen to the ground, had severely injured her arm. The surgeons, alarmed by the swollen and bruised indications, judged that either the Virgin would remain lame in that part, or at least the labor of the cure would be exceedingly long and uncertain, and that the danger would have been far less had the bones cracked, breaking into pieces. Rosa, strong in habit of suffering and in desire, calmly received these words, as if spoken of another's arm. Nevertheless, one day, while familiarly discussing with Gundisalvi's wife the miracle of the sweating image, she suddenly felt herself drawn to the hope of regaining her health, provided that the gossamer which had been drenched by the image's miraculous sweat were applied to the crushed nerves and muscles of her arm. The Questor's wife, as if snatching a word from the Virgin's mouth, immediately spread the gossip, ordering the bandages to be loosened and the plaitings to be put on. The Virgin hesitated, fearing that her pains would be prematurely snatched from her, which she wished to endure longer for the love of her Spouse. She claimed that she would do nothing without the confessor's permission. However, so as not to appear to spurn the offered remedy, she immediately went herself to the confessor in the church of St. Dominic: with the candor she was accustomed to, she laid the matter before him and awaited his nod. The confessor decreed that nothing was to be postponed: go, hurry, prepare the gossiper, and submit yourself to divine favor. It was done, as he had commanded.

It was noon when D. Maria Usategui, with her own hands, stripped her arm to bring Rose home [she is healed immediately], wrapped it in gauze, and reapplied the bandage. Soon the virgin rushed into the oratory, and when she emerged about two hours later, she displayed her arm completely sound and supple. The lady, pleased, inquired about the manner and duration of the treatment. to whom Rosa said: "I had barely composed myself in the oratory for prayer before the sacred image, when by a powerful attraction I felt my nerves, which had been twisted, return to their places, my fleshly parts* swell, my muscles contract, yet I did not wish to come out at once, until I had duly offered thanks to the heavenly physician: in these I distinguished myself somewhat: now take away the gossamer, take away the bandages, for everything is safe." Such a sudden blessing stirred the entire household to secure congratulations in joy. All were amazed, and especially the surgeons, who the day before had feared that the oil and their labor would be lost. For they had seen the arm as intractable and stubborn against all treatment, so that they did not hesitate to murmur ambiguously about an incurable case.

[242] From then on a more severe storm of dread descended upon Gundisalvo [and the fearful guest, lest on that account the image be transferred], but was soon to be dispelled by a new consolation of Rose. A rumor was spreading uncertainly through the city that the sacred image was to be removed from the private oratory, as though it were most worthy of a public and more celebrated place. This rumor tormented the quaestor, his partner, and his daughters. that they would suffer grievously to have this domestic treasure removed from their daily sight, devotion, and joy; especially since they had learned from Rosa on several occasions that this gracious image of the Savior was salutary for the entire household. in it, to work its divine mercy more secretly, its great wonders; Frequently, a blessing of heavenly sweetness emanates from her. But the spirited Virgin, upon learning that the anxious Gundisalvus was being tormented by this fear; "Do not doubt anything," he said, "my father" (for he was accustomed to honor his parents by calling them by their names), "do not be afraid, I say: for this kind Savior has not granted us such displays as if he were about to leave this house any day now." Rest assured, he will deign to live with us. "If, because of the favor of the most recent miracle, that painting must be removed from here, then the entire oratory, together with the other holy icons, must also be taken away, for they all compete in bestowing wondrous benefits upon us and abound with secret prodigies."

[243] [He confidently predicts that she will remain in his house.] They had long since inferred from the affection, devotion, and reverence that Rosa showed in a particularly special way to this image and that one there that a more abundant breeze of divine favor had blown upon her. On the altar of the oratory stood an image of the infant Jesus, painted in charming colors; to this the Virgin would often turn her ardent gaze. He sent her frequent sighs from the bottom of his heart. was perishing for this; Not even her most cautious veil of modesty was enough to hide her innermost passions for her. Maria of Usategui observed these things in silence and, with prudent skill, finally elicited from the Virgin, by indirection, what was hidden there. The ingenuous Rosa confessed that at every glance of that painting she was filled with a new warmth and joy, her heart leaping for delight, her spirit was inflamed, because that divine Little Boy seemed now to greet her with a gentle smile, now to pierce her soul with a sweet effulgence of rays, and at times even to stretch out his short little arms, as if about to rush into Rosa's embrace of his own accord. As she spoke these words she grew animated and could not restrain herself from raising her voice more forcefully in her exhortation: "Come now, O mother, esteem this heavenly little one;" Serve this Lord, cultivate, venerate, love this King of glory, who without ceasing from a small icon pours upon us such great gifts, ready to pour out greater and more, if only we are willing to receive them. A manly effigy of our Savior, of whom we have spoken above, returned thence directly to her. He asserted that he was thereby thoroughly renewed and restored, and experienced certain effects in soul and body very similar to those he perceived in the reception of the Holy Eucharist. Finally, the querulous one sighed, that there were so few who would love the Lord with a whole and pure affection. this pain seemed to him to be utterly unbearable. he wished that he had been granted sex, eloquence, and strength, by which he would persuade the entire world of the love of Jesus Christ alone. For her seraphic mother and teacher had once desired the very same thing, and at last, amid these ardors, she would reproach herself for not burning enough.
