Chapter 19
His tender affection for two images of the Virgin Mary.

[There was an illustrious statue of the Virgin Mary in Lima,] the following are almost twin tales, which we will relate here; But a few things must be selected from the many concerning the singular prerogative of this most celebrated image, which, from the very beginnings of the Christian faith, first became known throughout the entire kingdom of Peru by its public benefits, and was privately so exceedingly dear and gracious to our Rose. The statue is crafted from an unknown wood to the height of a human figure, skillfully carved into a beautiful effigy of the Blessed Virgin Mary, holding the infant Jesus in her arms and with her other hand offering her Rosary to the world. This, brought from Spain by the Christians as a banner for the conquest of the Indies, was, among the first undertakings of the city of Lima, solemnly placed in the first temple of the true religion there (which bears the name of the Most Holy Rosary, contemporary with it), with the founding of a distinguished monastery of the Friars Preachers. That was the most auspicious name and beginning for spreading the faith there: for at that time, in this one and only temple, under the protective patronage of the Blessed Virgin, dedicated to the title of St. of the Rosary, a parish was established: here the font was opened for the first time to the instructed catechumens, by which the infidels, brought to the worship of the true God, began to be admitted through the door of holy baptism into the fold of Christ's sheep; From this, at last, the entire administration of the Sacraments flowed forth to the other sacred edifices that were gradually being founded, and, as if from a single, most august, parthenic rose-garden of the Queen of Heaven, the spring of the nascent faith spread itself in a flourishing bloom in every direction.

[245] In the year 1553, near Caxaguanab in Cusco and the neighboring provinces, [which, once appearing in the air, had terrified the Indians,] more than two hundred thousand pagan Indians were under arms, so that they would trample the Catholic army, numbering no more than six hundred men, rather than defeat it. Present were several religious from the Order of Preachers, and having implored the help of the Queen of the Most Holy Rosary, while the armies rushed together from both sides, the divine Virgin was seen in the air on either side, under the well-known statue of her Liman image, brandishing a rod at the enemies, and, if they did not yield, threatening utter destruction. Struck by the divine spectacle of the idolatry, they preferred the peace of arms by experience, and having cast off obstinacy along with iron, they joyfully submitted to the yoke of faith. From then on, popular veneration of the sacred image grew, and with the fame of such a miracle, a marvelous propagation of salutary devotion spread throughout the kingdom and the entire region.

Finally, on May 9, 1643, the Catholic King, desiring to fortify his Peruvian kingdom more securely against impending dangers under the heavenly protection of the Virgin Mother [and having chosen her as Protector of the Peruvian kingdom], commanded that she be publicly chosen by all the inhabitants as Protector. and at the same time to establish most firmly a perpetual public refuge, collected by a common vow from some sacred image of it (whichever was most celebrated throughout the region and most renowned for the fame of its benefits). It was decreed: the Viceroy, together with the Archbishop and both the ecclesiastical and secular members of the kingdom's council, after deliberation and a vote, chose the most holy image of Our Lady of the Most Holy Rosary of Lima, and ordered that it be celebrated annually on the second Monday after the Sunday of the Rosary, As newbornsd, with a public procession established (in which both the civil and religious authorities, the Regulars of each Order, and all the dicasteries of the tribunals participate), they visit the same image in its chapel at the Friars Preachers. Likewise, when she is carried in a festive procession into public (which is done annually on the eighth day of the Holy Rosary in October), she is greeted by the thousands of onlookers in the square with repeated blasts of gunfire, while the banners unfurled by each cohort gleam with the name, the emblem, and the image of the Rosarian. Nor is the rest of the year free from a constant influx, especially whenever earthquakes, the contagion of epidemics, or other such events—whether a public or private necessity—compel citizens to seek refuge in their own asylum. This, by the way, about the Rosea image; now to the Rose.

[247] [He showered St. Rose with many blessings,] From her earliest years, she was a magnet and siderite for Rose (though nothing less than iron). So powerfully did this sacred image draw her, so strongly did it fortify the maiden. We have touched on some things above. Let others succeed. When in this chapel of the Rosary Rosa, before the Virgin, received the habit of this Order of the Dominicans, her mother was present at the public ceremony weeping, and saw her daughter, by the gentle favor of the Mother of God, being lifted up to heaven; where, filled with joy, she easily restrained her tears and began to congratulate the happy maiden with silent applause. A sublime omen & worthy of observation; For she who was honored with such a vote, her high station above the stars, here served as a blessed gate of heaven. Whenever Rosa resolved to ask for some great and special help from heaven for herself or for others, she would fall prostrate at the altar of the Most Holy Rosary in prayer, there she would gaze most attentively at the face of the beloved image, and not without great confidence, after tender words, would hang upon its face for an oracle. The questioner's wife often noted that Rosa, returning from the chapel, would betray some new comfort in her very effort to suppress it: for she was usually bubbling with such fervent joy that she could neither hide the signs from her familiar and piously curious observer, nor deceive her altogether. Therefore, by virtue of their domestic relationship, he dared to address the shy woman directly, saying, "Today, O Rosa, as I see it, the favors have rained down again." To whom the modest Virgin said with a slight smile, "Immediately that affable Queen of Heaven showers her favors upon this wretched sinner." Remember, then, that the same Gundisalvi's wife was subjected to the ordeal, about which see above, chapter. XIV, and having learned from this more and greater things about her secrets through this image, commerce, visions, and conversations, she thereafter confessed to this matron with a little more confidence a large part of them, which she had long since revealed to her examiner with the candor that was due.

[248] [as she herself ingenuously confessed.] When, among other things, she was asked how she perceived the Mother of God speaking to her in this image, she answered most simply that this marvelous kind of address was without idiom, sound, or movement of the lips, purely sympathetic. and that only the icon's face, by various radiations of its most serene brow, as it were by certain signs and nods, so aptly, skillfully, and clearly expressed the feelings and affections appropriate to the occasion, that the most exquisite eloquence of words could not do so better: that the same power resides in the face of the divine Son; to read the answers distinctly for himself in a living and animated book on both sides, which was far more meaningful than if he were to discern them from characters that were merely written or painted; to be stirred in his soul by these, as though by intimate fiduciaries of his innermost thoughts, a certain luminous attention, by which, without discursive reasoning, he may clearly perceive each thing; Finally, on the lips, cheeks, and eyes of both figures there seemed to be a kind of clock of mysterious affability, and a corresponding diversity of signs, inexplicable by any knowledge of languages, yet far more certain than human speech. It was said that Rosa could obtain whatever she earnestly requested from the sacred image of the most august Queen of the Rosary. Hence, often asked to seek this or that if it seemed to be for the good of the republic or for anyone's private welfare, she would not take on the task without some reluctance; then, returning from the sacred image, she would promise good results with such assurance as if she had received a diploma of granted grace.

[249] It happened by God's permission that the envious Satan had sown the tares of discord among a certain religious community in Lima. [Holy suppliant before this image of discord,] An unhappy tares grew, and what at first had been nothing more than a battle of opinions was gradually, by the very sweetness of opposing, degenerating into the bitterness of wills, not without a breach of peace and the offense of the Deity, who is unwilling to dwell outside the bounds of peace. The confessor of the Virgin perceived the evil creeping in, and he ordered Rose to go to the chapel of the Holy Rosary and there, before the image of the Mother of God, commend the need of that congregation in peril to the Mother of Mercy with fervent prayers, and not to desist until she had obtained grace and help. The Virgin, wonderfully inclined to acts of charity, went to the chapel, offered prayers, but returned home sadder than usual and wounded in spirit. The next day she returned and prostrated herself longer at the feet of the sacred image, prayed more fervently, added tears, and with patient equanimity awaited favor, her hopes hanging solely on the face of the Mother of God, and she would not rise from her place until she understood herself to have been heard. Finally, overcome with sudden joy, she rose to her feet and, having most devoutly given thanks to the Virgin Mother, she hurried home happily. The questor's wife had silently observed the Virgin's brow on both occasions as she returned home, and, marveling at the difference, asked what new matter was afoot. The virgin replied to him succinctly, as much as she needed to know.

[250] But a little later, more carefully examined by her confessor, [having arisen in the religious assembly, she dissipated it with prayers] and not daring to conceal anything, she ingenuously laid out the whole series of events, that the day before, during simple prayers, she had found the face of both the Mother of God and her little Son not only as hard as steel, asper, inexorable, but also severely threatening, austere, and possessed by manifest indignation: so that she had tried in vain to soften the Son through the Mother; having returned home in the deepest sorrow after this unusual rebuff; then, the next day, having at last moved the Mother of God to intercede with many tears, since the Son refused to be appeased toward that assembly so stubbornly at odds. as his afflicted heart trembled, perceiving the alternating words of the Mother and the Son, by which she urged grace, and he justice and punishments. so that at last the Son's anger was disarmed by the most sweet Mother's most powerful groans. so that this one, now more placated, would look upon the Mother with a kindly smile, and then upon Rosa with the utmost indulgence; from that point on, the confessor should have no doubt that grace was now safely secure. A brief, hands-on effect. Thus it happened: for shortly thereafter the divided minds were wonderfully reconciled, the apple of discord vanished, and Satan's wedge, which had opened the fissures, splintered. Let those who sow discord among religious communities know how abominable their work is to God, how pleasing to the devil, and how harmful to their neighbors. But to other matters.

[251] [(from another painted and domestic image of the Virgin Mary) It would not be out of place to briefly relate what happened to Rosa with another image of the Virgin Mary, not carved but painted, in Gundisalvi's oratory.] The Virgin Mother was depicted in colors on canvas, embracing the sleeping little Jesus in her arms. In this image, the roses seemed to breathe and live, and she often confessed that she held this one in particularly high esteem, because from it she had at times been treated to the sweetest consolations. To kindle in Jesus sleep, and in Mary watchings a fire in their very marrow, so that neither the little one dares to interrupt the most gentle slumber, nor yet can the watchful Mother pass by without a song-filled, ardent heart's discourse. but under this duel of affections, her soul is melted by the Beloved's mysterious speech, as if to say: "I sleep, & my heart is awake."

[252] [he also received various favors] It happened that in the oratory of Maria de Usategui, during a spiritual conference on the benefits of the most blessed Virgin held before Rosa and two family members, he came upon the great miracles that the divine hand deigns to work at the image of the Mother of God at Atocha, a village near Madrid. Rosa listened eagerly to what was being said, her eyes fixed on the present image; but when the matron slowly turned to other matters, Rosa interrupted her with an unusual emphasis of words. "Go on, mother, go on and discuss more about this matter that you know." The matron easily noticed that something peculiar was happening between the image and Rosa. After the others had gone off, he secretly forced the Virgin to tell him why she had interrupted him, insisting that there must be a mystery behind it which he was determined to uncover. Rose, attributing the prodigy she had seen not to her own merit, but to that of the matron. "Indeed," he said, "a mystery lay beneath." for in the meantime, while you were narrating the miracles of the absent image of Atocha, this one, present, was giving signs of unusual joy, moving her dovelike eyes toward us, rising on a new kind of corpulence beyond the surface of the canvas, as if about to come forth from it to us with her slumbering little Son, scattering secret sweetnesses, shining, radiating with greater majesty, now to her little one, now caressing us maternally. And was it not fitting, under such grace, to linger longer in praising our Queen?

[253] [And since there were no flowers to adorn it,] But for the time being she had to return to the chapel of the Rosary, to which Rosa, every Sabbath throughout the year, would send or bring in bunches the flowers she herself had cultivated. Those who knew Rosa's garden were amazed that its flowerbeds were never without flowers which she would dedicate to the altar: for whether the changing seasons of the year had carried this seed elsewhere, or whether the more scorching Sirius had with its torrid heat corrupted this solace of the earth everywhere, always in Rose's modest garden remained untouched the buds of spring, which she would consecrate to the heavenly Antiphora on the coming Sabbath. He would have preferred to offer the sacred image a gilded and jeweled cycad from time to time in place of flowers, but poverty stood in the way. Nevertheless, she also supplied this with the industry of her ingenious devotion: for, in order to clothe the Queen of the Most Holy Rosary twice each year in a spiritual vestment, she devised a material, workmanship, and form far more precious, in a manner described above in the chapter. We described 18.

One idea was found in the Virgin's pamphlets, to the following effect: "In memory of the vestment that I, Rosa of St. Mary, unworthy handmaid of the Queen of Angels, have woven for the Virgin Mother of God, with the help of the Lord." First, let a tunic be made of interweaving 600 Angelic Salutations, and let the antiphon "Hail Mary" be recited as many times, together with 15 days of fasting, in reverence for the purest joy of the angelic announcement that the eternal Word of the Father was to be clothed in human flesh in her most chaste womb. Secondly, let 600 Angelic Salutations, as many Hail Marys, 15 Rosaries, and 15 days of fasting be woven into the cloth of this vestment, in reverence of the most joyful gladness, when she visited her kinswoman Elizabeth. Thirdly, the edges and fringes of this garment are surrounded by 600 angelic salutations, each one saying "Hail," etc. (as above) in reverence of the highest joy, while she was giving birth to her Son, my Lord Jesus Christ. Fourth, let us present 600 greetings (as above) in reverence of the innermost joy, while she offered her blessed little Son in the temple. On the fifth step they offer 600 greetings, "Hail," and fasts (as above) in reverence of the most blessed joy, while they find their Son, who had been missing for three days, in the temple among the doctors. On the sixth, let them prepare a small branch to be carried in their Virgin's hands, 33 Our Fathers, as many Hail Marys, with the verse, Glory be to the Father, Hail Mary, and as many rosaries of divine praises, and again as many rosaries of the Virgin's praises, in reverence of the 33 years that my Lord Jesus lived on earth." And a little below: "This garment is now finished;" Blessed be God. and may His most holy Mother, by her preeminent piety, make up for my shortcomings and grant me forgiveness. Praise to you, Christ. Likewise, but much more laboriously, she began to sew the garment of the Virgin Queen on the first day of the year 1616. In this sumptuous texture of Dominican prayers and angelic salutations, the Virgin defined the number not by hundreds (as above) but by thousands. However, it is not possible to pursue each and every one of his devotional exercises in detail, especially since only a few have become known among the countless.

Notes.

Honorius Philoponus Benedictine ascribes the same origin of the Christian faith to his Order, as is evident from the epilogue of his work On Navigation to the West Indies, which he published in 1620, and in which page. 96 has the following: My intent here is only to demonstrate that the monks of Saint Benedict were the first, and the primates, archbishops, and vicars of the most holy Lord Pope for all of America (of whom to this day some boast, and boast too ambitiously) and that there they consecrated, ordained, and established parishes, churches, parishes of the New World consecrated, ordained, and established churches; namely, those who held supreme authority over other clerics and brothers of the Orders in the ordaining and establishing of American churches and monasteries, with officialdom, jurisdiction, and spiritual primacy; which we have already demonstrated to you sufficiently above with testimonies. From the testimony of Thomas Bozii and others, this writer asserts above that the abbot of Monte Serrato was the first, together with other Benedictines, to preach the faith in America, which we will note below on another occasion.

I suspect that the same passage is cited by John Latius, while in the book. 10. Of the western Indies. 29 writes as follows: From here one goes on to Limatambo, where the remains of many royal buildings can be seen; it is nine leagues from Cusco. From here, one descends the vast ridges of the Bilacongas into the Xaquixagua Valley (which Garcilaso calls Sacsahuanam), enclosed by the narrow space between the highest mountains, in which the ancient kings of Peru had their gardens and orchards &c.

Vox ἄγαλμα is purely Greek, which I have not yet found in use among the Latins, and which signifies a statue or an ornament . From this meaning, that period is sufficiently understood.

Designated as Low Sunday, whose Mass begins with these words: As newborn infants &c.

A dicastery (from the Greek δικαστήριον) is, in the proper sense, a judicial forum, taken from the Greek verb δικάζω, meaning "to judge." This appears to be taken to mean the gatherings of the judges who were present at this supplication.

An epidemic is a disease that spreads widely or is scattered among the people, from epi, "upon," and demos, "people," as if moving among the people. By this term, a plague or contagious disease is generally designated, which is also sometimes called an epidemic disease for that reason.

A magnet is called a siderite by the Greeks, and is a stone that draws iron to itself by a hidden natural force, from the Greek word σίδηρον, which signifies iron.

The adjective sympathetic comes from the noun sympathy, which is composed of the prefix συν with, & πάθεια passion, and literally means compassion or sympathy. Here, however, it is taken for the congruence of affections, just as antipathy is taken for the repugnance of affections, on which Castell can be consulted.

A great crowd of people gathers at this most celebrated image of the Virgin Mary, as I myself saw when I venerated it near Madrid.

The star Sirius, which the Latins call the Dog Star, is a star or sign of the zodiac that produces great heat. Hence the days get their name. Its location and etymology are disputed among astrologers.

Suckers, among other meanings, is taken for the shoots that grow from the roots of trees, from the verb to sucker, which is to emit shoots or a sprout. Here it is transferred from trees to flowers.

The biographer here calls the Virgin Mary a celestial flower-bearer, that is, flower-bearing or bearing flowers, from ἄνθος, flower, and the participle φορὸς, bearing. Hence Bacchus is called ἀνθοφόρος by the Greek poets. That is to say, he is called the flower-bearer.

Chapter 20
His devotion to the salutary sign of the cross and to the image of St. Catherine of Siena.

[Holy virgin, sign of the cross,] Solitariæ in the garden of the cell (of which above) the first and almost entire piece of furniture was a cross taller and more upright than the Virgin, so that it might more fully present to her the Passion of Calvary, and at the same time be more conveniently embraced by the pious embraces of the new Magdalene. How many kisses Rosa had sweetened it with, how many tears she had moistened it with, how many fiery sighs she had scorched it with, how tightly she had pressed it to herself day and night until it was bruised, how often and with what suppliant devotion she had worshiped it—this was not entirely hidden from the household servants, who at times watched her unwittingly through the keyhole. Whenever in temples, chapels, or houses he happened to come upon the frequent image of the life-giving cross, he greeted it with a sorrowful gaze and a warm sigh. In every family gathering, conversation, and society, if a cross was laid out, Rose's sun, she herself, was a heliotrope to the cross. At the sight of him, the Virgin's heart seemed to be transformed into a clock, beating more strongly and agitated. Especially during the greater week of the Passion of the Lord, it could not be removed from the public adoration of the faithful; He sat motionless beside her; when the crowd thinned, he showered her with the most tender kisses; he fixed his eyes on her from a distance and could not look away, not even for a moment. For the devotion to the holy cross had so taken possession of the Virgin's mind, that wherever she encountered the figure of a cross, whether from the chance meeting of beams, in the bars of windows, in the crossed rods of hedges, on door hinges, on beams, or even in the very straws and poles, or wherever she encountered the figure of a cross on any other scrap or refuse, she would nowhere pass it by without a careful and silent veneration.

[256] He would accompany Rosa to the sacred buildings as a mark of honor, [wherever she was stationed,] and he grieved that he had to stand with her so often whenever the Sister bent down there to lift the stakes from the ground. He noticed that the accidental cross-shaped figures had been formed in the projected splinters by the wind or by chance. But Rosa could not bear to see them lying on the ground and being carelessly trampled by the feet of passersby. Hence Ferdinand (for that was his brother's name), impatient of so many delays, and as it were murmuring at his sister's superfluous piety; "Do you think," he said, "it is fitting for a virgin to uncover her face so often in public to relieve the burdens?" What will they think, what will they say, the passersby, that you stand rooted in the streets, like a crossroads of sticks everywhere, I decree, your thighs gaping from labor? If, by this method, you desire a view of the figure of the cross, you have certainly undertaken a tremendous—nay, a ridiculous—labor, the sole reward of which is the jeers and sarcasm of passersby.

But the Virgin replied gravely and modestly. Ah, brother, I worship it and even imprint it on my feet, for I am tormented by overwhelming pain as I see it lying contemptuously on the ground and being trampled underfoot by my heels, that most rudimentary form of that adored cross on which the innocent Lamb of God redeemed us with the inestimable price of his own life. I know that these shortcomings are trampled by others without scruple or guilt; I neither dare condemn their carelessness nor presume to reproach them. But neither can they justly take offense at the simplicity of my devotion. "Finally, let them mock, let them laugh, let them think of me as they please; as long as I am able, I will not cease from my care, lest the cross of my Lord be trampled anywhere, even upon the most abject and vile stubble." He added, strange as it may seem! that his zeal for honoring the cross (which to others seemed childish and overly feminine) was stirred by such lofty and serious incentives that he never had a way or reason to resist; Therefore, this, like many other things, was to be forgiven on account of the fervor of his affections, especially if it were true, as he had received by some obscure persuasion, that from the sacred treasuries of indulgences a certain profit had been reserved for the faithful, whoever should show reverence to the holy cross in this simple religious observance.

[258] [one of which, which had withered in the queen's garden,] She had planted a rosemary in her little garden (commonly called rosemary) and had arranged its three small shrubs in separate mounds in the shape of a cross, so that each mound, taken by itself, would accurately reproduce the form of Calvary from every angle. He rejoiced, continually turning his eyes among the flowers and fragrant plants alike and the cross. Many were pleased with the new art of beauty, which, watered by grace, had happily grown under the gardener's devotion. However, with the master P. F. Alphonso Vasquez, confessor to Rose, at work, only one of the three remained in Rose's garden, for he had asked for one for himself, obtained another for the Queen Regent as a gift from her, and strove with the utmost care to nurture it; but in vain: for scarcely a few days had passed when the crusader's tuft of grass at the queen's prow withered up completely, a clear sign that its own gardener of the Cross and Calvary had been lacking. The Virgin's confessor told the queen regent of her pain. But the Virgin smiled and warned that such crosses could not flourish amid the secular pomps of the court and the constant commerce of the world. Yet she forbade the withered little tree to be cast away and ordered it to be brought back to her at once to be revived.

[259] [miraculously restored to its former vigor.] Barely by the fourth day had the shriveled little plant beside Rosa dried out, when, more beautiful than before, it burst forth anew from every side, with new, bushy shoots of twigs, revived by the fresh sap of its roots and pith, and the obedience of its boughs reforming it into its original cross-shape. to whom Rosa, an ornament crafted from the pliable pith of a fig tree, had most fittingly arranged a number of heavenly genii in a circle, and had skillfully added a small image of Saint Mary Magdalene reclining at the base of the cross, thus the queen's confessor returned her, reborn, adorned, and flourishing, to the astonished regent, her own Lebanon had so quickly, so charmingly, so fully come back to life under Rose's hands. But the Virgin attributed the whole of it to the glory of the cross, since she remembered that St. Catherine of Siena had always done the same.

[260] [To adorn the image of St. Catherine of Siena] We must now turn to the image of this seraphic teacher, marked with the characters of the cross and stigmata, with whom the Rosary had the closest connection, and not without miracles. Lima has flourished for more than a hundred years under the invocation of the Blessed. A secular confraternity devoted to Catherine of Siena; It is the custom here to carry on a festive procession three times a year around the public circuit the conspicuous statue of their patron saint, suitably adorned with crowns, flowers, and much glitter and preciousness of gems. Furthermore, for the task of procuring, handling, and arranging the ornaments for the statues, no one was found more suitable, more worthy, or more skilled than this one Rose (during her lifetime). For no prototype is more devoted or more similar. Thus, by the unanimous vote of all, this office of religious piety was entrusted to one Rose. She herself, having diligently collected from every quarter jewels, veils, torques, flocks, love-knots, and all other precious furnishings (of which no part was ever lost without the whole being returned to its owners), eagerly applied herself to the task, taking on the most faithful helpers as partners in the labor and its reward.

[261] [A prodigious sum of money is provided,] But while she was circling the precious icon, while she was dressing it, while she was arranging its folds, she could not, for her ardor, restrain herself from tears, from kisses, from addresses; She spoke to, venerated, and sweetly caressed the sacred image, as if she had her teacher present from the heavens. At one point, between sighs, the Virgin let these words slip (though perfunctorily and in a low voice): "You know full well, my sweetest mother, that if I had the means of fifteen or sixteen patacons, I would clothe you, by the dictate of my genius, in a new, whiter garment." And behold, after a little while, while the companions were thinking nothing less, the illustrious woman Jerome de Gama's Ethiopian slave girl appeared, and she brought letters with money, the contents of which were: "Greetings, sister Rosa." I suppose you are now busy decorating the statue of the glorious mother of Siena. Use the sixteen coins I found on me to decorate the seraphic image, if necessary. Goodbye. Rosa looked up at the sky and said, "O sweetest Jesus, what a faithful friend you are!" He soon bought an Attalic one of snowy whiteness and fitted a new garment to the icon. While Rosa was sewing the oblong plank (which we call a scapular) for the icon's vesting, she sent into the room where it stood her fellow townswoman Philippa de Montoya to fetch her a skein of silk thread. She went, and saw the face of the image shining more brightly with unusual splendor. She burst out with unexpected joy and, trembling, ran to Rosa, who was spinning in the next room. But paying no heed to this, she replied briefly, "Have you seen anything, sister, that our seraphic mother deigns to approve by a clear sign—what we are doing?" He is pleased that we are both happily occupied with making his scapular.

At another time, Rosa wished to have an abundance of carnations on hand, [and flowers which that season of the year did not produce,] with which to adorn the sacred image. The current season of the year refused, since in those regions the month of May knows no such flowers. Seeking for a long time in Rose's garden, whether by chance in the bed of carnations, or buds would appear, about to burst into a flower; but nothing was found. The search is repeated twice, three times, in vain. But that evening, there was no hope left. Nevertheless, Rosa, perceiving with good hope what was to come, said, "The Lord is able to grant what we desire." "Rather, this very night, in honor of the Holy Trinity, this very little twig" (he pointed to a shoot, then entirely bare of any sign of fertility) "will produce three carobs for us." Having said this, he dismissed the women (Catherine and Francisca Montoya) to their home, laughing, because at that time of year they expected, indeed promised, cloves from a languid, barren shrub.

The next morning at daybreak (for that day had been appointed for the procession) they hurried to Rose, [who are found in the garden of the holy Virgin,] so that they might complete with her whatever remained to be done in adorning the image. Rosa then, with her eyes fixed in prayer, beckoned to those who were coming to approach the garden and, in the name of the Most Holy Trinity, to bring her three carnations from there at once to be attached to the sacred icon. Catherine replied, "You, dearest, surely don't remember that yesterday we went through the entire garden with you several times in that pointless search for a flower, nor did we find anywhere so much as a bud on the stems or a tiny denticle on the scapes that would promise the down of any little bloom for this whole month." But Rosa insisted with more effective authority: "Go, sisters, and quickly bring me the triple flower I mentioned—the ripest, cross-pollinated one." What are you hesitating about? What are you waiting for? He who commanded the rod of Aaron to blossom, also provided for us. They went, and, as the Virgin had said, they found three cloves of that very thing which Rosa had indicated the day before, plucked them from the trembling stalk, brought them, and, begging pardon for their own distrust, They prostrated themselves with Rose in thanksgiving to God, at once amazed and joyful that on that sacred day the image, with such a foreign adornment, was to be brought out in public. What is even more remarkable is that, for as long as Rosa lived, her garden never lacked fresh carnations at any time of the year.

[264] [Then B. Catherine to a certain assistant in the adornment] On another occasion, Rosa had summoned Mary Euphemia de Parejas, a widow, along with other helpers to dress the icon of Saint Catherine. A household servant named Francisca, who also served as a nurse, was dangerously ill; at that time she was nursing the infant son of a widow, Joseph. But the physician, terrified by the increasing severity of the disease, had ordered the day before that the infant should not suckle from the sick woman. However, Euphemia, anxiously entrusting her sick sister to her household staff, went to Rosa to lend her assistance in the common duty of charity. When at last everything was finished and the image had been properly adorned, Rosa, commiserating with her weary companions, said, "Go," she said, "breathe, and refresh yourselves with a leisurely stroll, for you are tired." Euphemia replied, "You order me to walk, when you know full well what affliction awaits me at home?" Rather, you, who are endowed with both grace and confidence by this seraphic mother, pray for the life and health of my handmaid. Rosa nodded, and immediately, turning to the woman before her with her usual friendly familiarity, said, "Hey you, glorious mother, don't you see this woman's bitter affliction?" Come to this need, and do not delay comfort. Now I will try. How dear to you are the stigmata of our Redeemer; I implore you by these, to secure the safety of the endangered Francisca. He said, and having comforted the widow, he bade her hope for the best. Saint Catherine of Siena, to whom she herself had just been a servant, would by no means fail her servant in her hour of need. Euphemia hurried straight home, where she found her Francisca, the danger having passed, recovering so well that the next day, at the doctor's orders, she once again provided the infant with nourishment, as before, by nursing her at the breast.

[265] [She conferred a twofold benefit,] At one time the same Francisca de Montoya had helped Rosa all night in decorating the icon of St. Catherine of Siena, and when the work was finished, as she was departing to rest a little so that she could take part in the festive procession a few hours later, Rosa, having strayed, saw the Blessed. He earnestly recommended Catherine, asking that she be protected from the imminent danger. Nor did the anxious intercession go without result: for while the circling procession was being greeted here and there with magnificent applause, the sulphurous crackling of fiery missiles met it with varied din; one of these struck Francis squarely in the eye and struck her eyebrow, yet without any harm to her. Not that he lacked the power to do harm: for the same blow, rebounding from Francisca's oblique thrust, struck another woman, and with the folds of his toga he swiftly set fire to the inner tunic beneath her. Francisca was quite certain that she owed her safety to Rose's prayers, recounted the event to the Virgin, and gave thanks for her benevolent care. Rosa said to him, "Why shouldn't our seraphic mother take special care of you this entire day, now that you have spent the whole of last night laboriously in her service alongside me?"

[266] [and Saint Rose, who suffered from arthritis,] Blessed Catherine was so munificent toward the vestments and carvers of the rest of her image that, of course, she was not stingy toward her own Rose. I will give a single paradigm, and I will finish. In the year 1616, in the month of August, Rosa B. He had dressed the image of Catherine, to be carried in the principal solemnity of St. Dominic, according to custom. The same, after the festival was over, awaited its dismantling in the house of Gundisalvus the treasurer. She was brought in and led into the private chapel so that there she might remove the precious adornment she had borrowed from various Rosas. But two or three days before, a horrible arthritis had invaded the Virgin's right hand through all its joints with the most excruciating pain, so that it had rendered her utterly useless for any work or even the slightest touch, and had also terrified the doctor with the monstrous enormity of the swelling, suspecting a more serious atherosclerotic disease. The hand had swollen to the size of the casks, by which the choicest honey is brought from Spain to Lima. so that he could no longer move any of his fingers, much less hold a pair of scissors or anything else, or grasp it.

[267] It was the day of St. Lawrence the martyr, when toward evening the physician, [which had invaded his hand,] having inspected the hand, a purulent meliceridi, or whatever other kind of abscess was thought to be underneath, he decreed that slow poulticesk be applied, and ordered that the next day, by drawing blood, a vein in the left arm be opened with a scalpel. Gundisalvus, who by chance was present with the physician, distressed by the sad spectacle, withdrew from the oratory, as though he were unfit for such excessive compassion. But Rosa was even more distressed that on the day which was the anniversary of the Dominican's assumption she was forbidden to handle the festive habit of her seraphic mother in the usual religious service. And so he knelt before the sacred image (which stood upon a small table) and prayed briefly. Rising soon after, he asked in a more cheerful voice for scissors from Gundisalvi's wife, who was standing there with others at work. Thinking it a game or a joke, she smiled and asked which hand he wanted to use to grab them, well aware that her fingers on her right hand were so swollen that they couldn't fit into any scissor handles, and so, as if in play, she offered the smallest pair of scissors she could find. Without delay Rosa put on these gloves, seized the task, and with the utmost diligence began to unloose the gems, torcs, and bracelets from their setting, deftly to undo the threads of the fastenings, to cut the knots, to lay out the adornment in its proper parts, and to bare the image. The matron exclaimed again, "What are you doing, Rosa?" Spare the hand that is now useless for these things. This is our labor, and take care of it. The Virgin went on eagerly, and said nothing else, except that the same man who had helped her dress the sacred image then healed the right hand with which he would serve in its undressing.

[268] After an hour the magistrate arrived, and seeing Rosa working with both hands as swiftly as possible, he immediately released her, was astonished, and gently interrupting her, said, "Is your right hand so strong that it can handle these tasks?" Stop, let's see the hand. He saw her and found her to be nothing like the left one: healthy, strong, and agile. The quaestor was overcome with the happiest astonishment, and, silently glancing at his wife, hung spellbound on the narrator's lips, as the Virgin had prostrated herself in prayer when he departed, and in the briefest interval had risen to her feet in perfect health and had vigorously undertaken the task, scissors in hand. He then preferred to learn the method of the sudden cure from the Virgin's own mouth. She replied that in the short time she had been praying before the image, she felt the strength return to her joints, and that they immediately swelled up again with a violent puff, as a bladder full of air does when it is pressed, and that in this way all the pain vanished along with the swelling.

[269] [as the Virgin herself candidly confessed.] The next day, the doctor was summoned, examined the hand, and then the Virgin; She most simply confirmed what she had said the day before, gratefully giving thanks for the entire series of events in favor of her seraphic mistress. It was a blessing that, from then on, arthritis never afflicted that hand again for as long as Rosa lived. But the other was more wonderful, that within, Rose's soul was being sweetened at the very moment that the hand was being healed without. She confessed that during that brief time when she had humbly petitioned St. Catherine of Siena to heal her right hand, a generous dew of unusual sweetness had simultaneously descended into the innermost recesses of her heart, which had even from there trickled into her body. It is no wonder that heavenly medicines are accustomed to work from the center to the surface, and to touch the soul before the body. But it is strange that these things were by no means remarkable to Rose, for she had almost daily consolations from the blessed Catherine of Siena, as from a mother, through their mutual communications.

Notes.

our Holy One is here metaphorically called a heliotrope, from the flower which the Greeks ἡλιοτρόπιον call, because it turns toward the sun, from ἥλιος, that is, the sun, & τρέπω turn or convert, as is well known.

Libanotis, Greek libanotis, is a shrub that gives off the scent of incense, from libanotis, by which incense is signified among the Greeks. It is a herb commonly known, whose various species the curious reader will find explained by Dioscorides.

Pataco is a type of coin, commonly called by the Spaniards patagon or real de a ocho, because it is equivalent to eight silver solids, which they themselves call reales de plata. As for the value of this coin, which the French call an ecu, Cangius can be consulted in his larger glossary under the entry "pataco."

All things precious in furniture or clothing are called Attalic, from Attalus, king of Pergamon, who abounded in money and splendid furniture, as Pliny says. 8, chapter 48 testifies. Hence, by Attalic here, a precious or magnificent garment is understood.

from the Caryophyllum is a fragrant flower, as if καρύου leaf, that is, the leaf of the nut, called giroflee by the French, and sanamunda by the Spaniards.

I did not find this word among Latin writers. Therefore I suspect that here a denticle has been written or used in place of a small tooth, because the flowers emerge from the stems in a pointed fashion like little teeth or tiny teeth. This is merely our conjecture, to which we would gladly prefer a more certain meaning of this word.

Arthritis is called a Latin disease of the joints, whose particular species are sciatica, gout, chiragra, and other such diseases, as is more fully explained by Gorræus in his *Medical Definitions*, page 50.

The praised Gorræus, on page 8 of the work most recently cited, writes thus about this type of tumor: "An atheroma is a tumor of a uniform color, free from pain, in which there is something like a pulp, called athera:" is enclosed by a certain membranous tunica, with which at times are found certain stony and hard particles, and other debris resembling sulfur. How an atheroma differs from a meliceride, we will say shortly.

i Castellus, p. 520 of the Medical Lexicon, has the following regarding meliceris: Meliceris, μελικηρὶς, having a membrane enclosing a honey-like humor, is a species of apostema, like & athērōma & steatōma, of which this one takes its name from fat, that one from a porridge-like substance enclosed within a membrane. When the honeycomb is cut open, the honey contained within flows out just like honey from the combs. Furthermore, Gorræus, in his Medical Definitions, page 8, assigns the difference between this and the preceding abscess in these words: "An atheroma is an oblong, prominent, and hard swelling which, because it does not easily yield to the fingers on account of the thickness of the humors, likewise, when it has yielded, does not readily return." By which characteristics it is distinguished from the *melichridus*, which is rounder, lower, broader, and softer, and more easily, as it yields to the fingers, so it recurves afterward.

According to the same Gorræo (p. 214), a cataplasm is a soft, moderately consistent medicine that is applied to repel, digest, digest and dissolve, loosen, evacuate, cleanse, warm, dry, and soothe pain. Then, in the same place, it is indicated how a cataplasm differs from an emplaster.

Chapter 21
A fervent devotion to the Most Holy Eucharist, for the faith in which he prepares himself for martyrdom.

[She often received the Body of the Lord] If such things happened to Rose, the virgin of Siena, who clothed Christ with things from another's substance, she received far more marvelous things from that wondrous Sacrament, in which they clothe Christ with things from another's substance. God prepared this food in His sweetness for the poor, yet it is to be remembered, to whom He said: "I am the food of the great, grow, and you will eat me." Rosa had not yet grown beyond her childhood years, and was already devouring him. But the confessors themselves had already found her great in spirit, when they permitted so tender an age to recline at the sacred Banquet, which provides delights for kings. Rather, with their consent, the girl was nourished twice a week with this divine food. He would have preferred more often. But at that time, he was not yet allowed to go to church without his mother. How sweetly Bernard exclaims in his joyful exultation: "Those who taste you hunger!" For Rose's thirst for the Sacrament of Pastoral Care grew so great from frequent Communion that she was permitted the sacred Synaxis three times each week, once she was a little older.

[271] Nor was that enough: for if any festival or solemn station intervened, [by the advice of his confessors,] he would even four, nay five times within a week, most eagerly partake of this divine Edule. The confessors, having thus come to know the state of the soul thoroughly, more readily indulged this frequency for her, unanimously testifying in the process, that they had found in her spirit such limpid purity, such radiant innocence, such a hunger for the Eucharist, and such a robust fervor of devotion, that from then on they had never dared to withhold from the Virgin participation in so great a mystery. Thus he easily obtained permission to partake daily, throughout the entire octave, of this delicate Bread of Angels, both at Easter and on the Solemnity of Corpus Christi. In this matter, to avoid human applause and not be noticed for his singularity, he most diligently varied the hour on each of these days, approaching the sacred Communion now earlier, now later: for the heavenly treasure (as Gregory advises) is hidden, so that it may be preserved. And he who does what no one else does, is admired by all.

[272] [and she always did this with such fervor,] It is truly worthy of note, however, that the Virgin, in such frequent communion and at the same time with such serenity of her immaculate conscience, nevertheless, whenever she was to approach the divine banquet, she would always precede it with sacramental confession; not perfunctorily, but with the earnestness of an examination, the spur of contrition, and a display of groans and tears, as if he had been entirely deprived of both Sacraments for weeks and months. The day before Holy Communion, she more harshly scourged her little body with whips and a strict fast, so that the hunger of the soul would be accompanied by the thirst of the body: she adorned the secret chamber of her mind for the coming Bridegroom with affections of reverence, humility, and desire. He would kindle the lights of pious meditations, especially from the little book of Louis of Granada, which is titled On Prayer. It exhaled the precious scents of its heart's ignited aspirations, and gathered the whole family of its powers and strengths into obedience to its dear guest, who was to take up lodging there the next day. He devoted such study and care to these exercises as if he were to communicate with her for that one and only time in his entire life. Furthermore, at the very approach to those awesome mysteries, the ardor of the Virgin's most devoted fervor, the reverent composure of her face and gestures, the blazing fire of her spirit—none of this can be described otherwise than by transcribing here the very image of Catherine of Siena herself, prostrate as she received the Eucharistic Host. Indeed, for this work, an angelic pen and brush would be required. Yet the Most High did not allow it to remain entirely hidden, for He repeatedly made visible in Rose's face the signs of the inner flame—its vibrations and constrictions—whenever her soul was kindled by the reception of the divine Host.

[273] [as the priests were presenting him with the sacred Host,] On a certain Tuesday after Pentecost, in the chapel of the Rosary, while the Mass was being celebrated by Father Fr. While Antonio Rodriguez, the preacher general, was preaching, Rosa, along with other faithful, knelt at the altar rail to receive the Eucharist. When the priest arrived at this spot with the Most Holy Host, he saw the Virgin's face entirely aflame and was utterly terrified, not yet knowing what this marvelous and unexpected flash of light signified. But later, from the more frequent occurrence of the same prodigy, he learned that it was Rosa, in whom, upon contact with the most sacred Host, a flame of love would suddenly blaze within, so vivid and powerful that it would even change her outward appearance into a flame. Master Father Louis of Bilbao, after Mass in the same chapel, while offering the sacred Communion to the Virgin, often noticed her face bathed in a heavenly splendor, so that her eyes trembled before the intensity of the glow, as if unworthy of that starry clarity which the beauty of grace had conspicuously kindled. and had wonderfully transformed the Bread of Angels into an angelic (as he said) beauty.

[274] [they would see his face shining,] Master Fr. John of Lorenzana, happily struck by a very similar vision, thereby had the opportunity to get to know Rosa more deeply, whom until then he had never known face to face. He himself would pray in the chapel of the Rosary, and after the Sacrifice, would distribute the Sacrament to the common people there to receive Communion. As he approached the rose (which, to receive the Holy Host, at that moment revealed its face, its pallium's folds drawn back), he saw a form more beautiful than any human, bright, snow-white, radiant, and adorned with a certain heavenly beauty. He passed on, certain that such a brilliant splendor did not belong to the realm of our mortality. Soon, pondering, he said to himself: "Whatever this Virgin may be;" He must be endowed with sublime grace before God; may he be permitted to know the innermost state of his soul, so bright and burning. Thus his vow was fulfilled when he least expected it: for by divine providence he was later given to Rose not only as a confessor but also as a teacher, to whom the Virgin showed obedience unto death, just as she did to her regular superior.

Father Friar Bernard Marques, while still a novice, would from time to time minister to the priests celebrating Mass in the chapel of the Rosary, [and another would feel an external ardor.] and according to custom, after the priest had washed his hands, he would pass the chalice to those receiving Communion; whenever he stood before the Rosary altar with the chalice, he felt as if ardors were emanating from the Virgin as from a kiln or furnace, so that at times he thought his hand was being burned by the paten, whether he was offering it or receiving it. But then, ignorant of the mystery because of his age, and contentedly passed by in silent wonder, but fifteen years after the Virgin's death, now a priest and more mature in judgment, he recounted the prodigy, as it had occurred, under oath, adding that from such sacred ardors his young and uncouth heart had nevertheless been stirred by the secret reverence for the present Sacrament, which he had seen received with such great religion and fervor.

God willed that through these outward and sensible things, however manifest they were on the outside, [the Virgin herself could not explain] how great a pyre of devotion was burning within. But when she drew near in person to the one who came to send fire on the earth, she appeared to herself no differently than among the Seraphim—like a rose walking amid burning stones in the very heavens. Hence in his very soul those marvelous effects, so innate and proper to this Sacrament, which no one knows except the one who receives it. Obeying the orders of the confessors, the Virgin tried to point out some of them from among these. But he was at a loss for words in almost every sentence, caused by a scarcity of words, and he knew no other way to speak of them than to call them ineffable. He said, however, that from the virgin flesh of God a certain admirable meekness of the heavenly Lamb was diffused into his soul, and that he was at the same time invigorated with unusual strength from the nobility of this nourishment, his powers being restored and renewed by a kind of new transubstantiation of his spirit into this divine food, sweet, succulent, and most potent. Meanwhile, a placid serenity of a higher sphere, a peace and temper, inexpressible by any comparison, was growing clear in his heart. Furthermore, regarding the immensity of joy from the close union with the most beloved Spouse, the height of fruition, the spiritual savor of true sweetness where it is tasted at its own fountain, the excellence, abundance, and richness of the fruits, and the like, it was deemed unworthy to attempt any barren mode of expression. it was enough in these things, or to be made completely mute, or to protest in short that there is no sweetness, joy, or jubilation in this lower world that can even remotely be likened to the joy of that precious Banquet, where the infinite hunger of the soul falls upon a meal worthy of God, upon the food of the sons, upon the breast of the Incarnate Word, which alone fills the hungry with good things.

[277] The confessor, P. M., had heard these words from the Virgin's own mouth. de Lorenzana, who was present with the same woman as she was dying, while she was slowly swallowing the sacred Host as her last viaticum, said these few words: [all the effects of this heavenly Food in her,] "Daughter, now enjoy your Spouse, and delight alone with the Alone, for He is truly most sweet;" Ask that, in his usual way, he now fill you with the same good things he used to. Another of Rose's confessors also remembered her saying that in the most holy Communion it seemed to her that she received the very sun from heaven into her breast: for whatever the visible sun does in the world, while it recreates all things with light and heat, while it adorns the earth with flowers and fruits, while it unites the seas, the bowels of the mountains with gems and precious metals, while it delights the little birds of heaven, quickens plants and animals, and illuminates, enlivens, and gilds with its diffused loveliness every corner of the vast hemisphere, the real presence of the Lord's Flesh accomplishes all this in the naked spaces of her soul. And perhaps for this reason it was divinely given to the Virgin that the sacramental species would usually last in her stomach for seven or eight hours before being dissolved by the heat of her stomach. so that in the meantime the devoted heart might grow stronger under this divine Sun, and be more vigorously nourished by the life-giving union of the Sacrament.

[278] [by which she was thus comforted and satisfied,] So that these solar effects might not at all remain hidden and secret, Christ provided, while he caused two prodigies—strength and fullness—to overflow from them themselves even into Rose's little body. The mother perceived the strength drawn from Holy Communion in her daughter: for whenever Rosa went to the church to receive the sacred Communion with her parent, she was so weary, emaciated, and weak from the previous days' fasts, scourges, and vigils that they were often forced to pause in the lanes along the way so that the exhausted Virgin could catch her breath. But having been refilled with the divine morsel, Paul, as he was returning home from the temple, outpaced his mother and urged her to follow, as if, by the strength of that Food, they were to walk all the way to Mount Horeb. Rosa herself confessed to both her confessors and her household that she was sated in body by the Eucharistic meal. For when she returned home, and had scarcely taken off her cloak, she hurried straight to her private chamber, where, shutting herself in until late at night, she pondered the greatness of the favor she had received, and she appeared to no one nor conversed with anyone.

[279] [so that she would not need bodily food for a long time.] When asked to take food, especially since she seemed, after the previous day's fast, to need refreshment and would remember that Sunday was exempt from the law of fasting, she would answer that, because of her great satisfaction from the Lord's Table, she could not yet admit any other food. He realized that, short of the utmost violence and torture, he could neither swallow a loaf of bread nor sip a drop of water. So, since they had confirmed that this was indeed the case, they stopped bringing up food so importunately. Thus the Virgin, with her most complete fast, persevered until evening, and often until the following day. It happened that Rosa, by the permission of her confessor, was nourished daily by the Sacrament of the altar for an entire octave, and during that whole eight days she abstained from all bodily food, because that heavenly saturation had not only banished all appetite for any other nourishment, but had rendered her completely incapable of taking any for that time. These are very similar to those which appear in the life story of St. Catherine. so that it would be more evident that he had excelled in both by a single spirit.

[280] [With the utmost reverence] Moreover, how religiously Rose was accustomed to venerate this Sacrament, whether it was being offered in the sacrifice of the Mass or exposed for adoration on the altar, as we have already touched upon above. He indeed attended all the Masses celebrated in the church of the Preachers. It remained like this until noon, with such peace and silence that not even a stone held fast in its center. And truly, the sacred Host was the very center of Rose's being, wherever she could see it. She was carried here, almost overwhelmed by the weight of her love, so that for entire hours she barely—or perhaps not even barely—let her eyelids flutter once, much less is it recorded that she ever turned her face away from the altar, even for a moment. Known and unknown alike passed by her, kneeling before the Virgin, and almost ran into her gaze; yet she herself recognized no one at that moment, nor did she so much as bat her eyelids, so intent was she on what was taking place at the altar, whose sight perhaps no intervening object even briefly eclipsed.

[281] The same was the Virgin's unmoving tenor of attention, when the venerable Sacrament was exposed in the oratory and adored by the people, [and with unmoving mental attention] as is done in public Forty Hours' devotions: There, from morning until evening, she remained on her knees in a continuous and utterly motionless posture, Rose, having forgotten her lunch, nor did she accept even a drop for refreshment, content with the joy and delights she received from the bodily presence of her Spouse. Thus, throughout the entire solemn octave of the Feast of Corpus Christi, she spent those full days before the open tabernacle of the exposed Sacrament, to the amazement of the confessors, who wondered whence such a frail little body could draw the strength to endure such prolonged, continuous, and arduous genuflections, all the while fasting. The strength of his stones seemed to be his strength, and his flesh was like bronze, which the most invincible Job complained was lacking in him. Furthermore, during the last four years of her life, in the greater week (as we call it), while the sacred body of Christ was being adored, enclosed as was the custom in the monument, and the Rose was affixed to the tomb, nor did she depart from it at night; Nor did he rise from the place where he had knelt on the Thursday, until the next day, Good Friday, that divine Treasure (with whom his heart was ever) was transferred in the usual rite to the tabernacle. Meanwhile, oblivious to hunger, thirst, and fatigue, she was entirely occupied with her beloved, with such veneration and reverence that throughout the entire twenty-four-hour period she would not presume to sit before him or even lean for a moment against the adjoining wall.

Nor was he any less diligent in the observance of this adored mystery, [adoring this venerable Sacrament] even when it was out of sight. Whenever, in conversation, whether spoken by her own lips or by someone else's, the most holy Sacrament of the altar was mentioned, Rosa would show her reverence with a deep bow of her head and entire body. Whenever he heard the signal of bells from afar given for his adoration, or the little bells ringing at his solemn rites, he could not restrain the sudden signs of inner joy, so that his heart and flesh rejoiced in the living God. His face grew more vivid, and his spirit exulted in God, his Savior. He could never get his fill of listening to the solemn panegyrics on this ineffable mystery, and once he had heard them, he would recite them almost verbatim after an interval of years, with remarkable felicity of memory.

No task was more pleasing to him than decorating the altar, the tabernacle, or, during Holy Week, the Holy Sepulchre. Hence, with such devotion, he would prepare the linens we call CORPORALS. Altar cloths and veils, silk covers for chalices, and anything that could pertain to this sacred furniture were made with all skill, art, and elegance. Moreover, not at all content with living flowers, she fashioned more precious ones from silk threads with a multicolored artistry. He added to it sprigs of the same cost and workmanship: greenish, leek-like, herbaceous, and variegated with every pleasing hue of foliage. The mother did not approve of her daughter's devotion to luxury, and she resented so much time being taken away from Rose's other labors, which supported her parents and her needy family. Thus the Virgin, in order to please her mother, would set aside part of the night after her daily labor to make altar ornaments. But this, too, seemed to the spiritual father (perhaps suggested and exaggerated by the mother) to exceed the daughter's strength. But Rosa answered him sharply: "I do not wish to be so delicate that the labor of my groom's adornment for the one night should be too great or troublesome for me. For what bride could be so idle or unconcerned that she would find it hard to spend the night on the adornment and grooming her husband requires, so that the next day she might appear in public decently?"

[284] [Rather, with the Calvinist faction] Finally, toward the mystery of the Holy Eucharist, Rose was so disposed that she preferred to shed her blood and her life for it rather than flowers. A present opportunity was not far off. In August of 1615, a hostile Dutch fleet appeared off the coasts of Peru in the Pacific Ocean, and terrified the entire coast with its sudden arrival. Now everywhere the call to arms rang out, and in the meantime both clergy, as is done in public dangers, to implore divine aid, had publicly exposed the most holy Sacrament of the Body of the Lord in all the churches of Lima, to be adored by the unarmed multitude. Finally, by B. himself. During the sacred vigil of Mary Magdalene, that formidable host of heretics, drawn up in battle array, was seen rushing straight for the very harbor of the port of Lima (called Callao by the natives), as if about to land its military forces on the ground for the sacking of the city. Soon the whole city was in an uproar, and the clergy, together with the citizens, were ordered to seize arms; for it was certain that this Calvinist foe was most savagely intent not only on the fortunes and throats of the inhabitants, but also on the desecration of sacred things, the plundering of churches, and the extermination of religion. Meanwhile, Rosa, who with the honorable matrons in the Church of St. Dominic was awaiting the outcome of these disturbances, feared alone for the Most Blessed Sacrament on the altar, which the sacrilegious hands of that perfidious people would surely not spare—perhaps not even its feet—but would trample with a horrendous audacity, and, cast upon the ground, would be kicked about with blasphemous fury.

[285] [having landed on the Peruvian shores,] Meanwhile a more distressing report reached Rosa: namely, that the enemies of the faith, from the ships, were jumping down into boats, then onto the sand in squads, and advancing toward the city gates in a body. This empty rumor had been born of the very fog of public fear. But nevertheless, he struck the unarmed sex, who had remained in the temple with Rosa, so that he nearly killed her. Rosa alone was so far from being afraid for herself or looking for a way to flee or hide that, on the contrary, she, like one triumphant, could not restrain the signs of immense joy, convinced, of course, that the most blessed and long-sought hour had come, when she could pour out her life and soul for the honor of the Most Holy Sacrament exposed there. And so she drew her companions with her into the chapel of St. Jerome, where, with a face beaming with joy, she began to urge the fearful ones to undergo martyrdom with her, saying that this was an opportunity too precious and fortunate, that is to say, not just any kind of martyrdom, but to fall under the very gaze of the Holy Host, to be slain before and for that divine Victim, to lay down one's life for the exposed Body of Christ.

[286] With his face flushed by these words, he drew scissors from his sheath [in defense of and for the truth of the sacred Eucharist], and eagerly cut off the part of his garment that hung longer on the ground, and, rolling up his sleeves to the elbow, drew what remained of his tunic inside his belt, down to his ankles, He took off the cork-soled shoes. When asked what she was doing and what she wanted with this novelty? "I gird myself for the contest," he says. I do not want the cloths of the wrestling and dying for the Sacrament to be a hindrance to me. In this short garment, I will more easily climb to the upper ledge of the altar. There I will lay my body, a substitute for Christ's, open to wounds, and I will not yield, until I fall, pierced on all sides by the two-edged weapons of the wicked. Nay, I will even pray my enemies not to finish me off with a single blow, but to dismember this little body of mine limb by limb with slow cruelty, and to crush each part into the smallest fragments. so that while they are busy dismembering me, they may at least postpone the injuries, which alas! They will later ask my current sponsor.

[287] She spoke these words in such a way that from the sparks in her eyes, [she was preparing to shed blood,] from the steadfastness of her fearless countenance, from the lively tone of her heroic voice, and from the entire bearing of the spirited Maiden, it was easy to infer that Rosa had donned the lioness's guise for the Eucharistic Lamb of the altar: nor did the women standing by believe their own eyes, seeing an Amazon who until now had been nothing but the living embodiment of meekness. They marveled that the most modest virgin in public, with her pallium thrown over her shoulders, her dress cut short, her sandals flung aside, her arms bare, her waist high-cinched, and armed ferociously only with the precious beads of the rosary, would of her own accord provoke the fatal hour of martyrdom and most fiercely threaten death. Meanwhile, she herself, as if already impatient of delay, would now try to walk, now approach the altar and the sacred Host, then soon look toward the church doors to see if any enemies were coming, to whom she would rush more swiftly to the altar, certain to fall there. Already the blood, eager to be spilled, was surging through all the veins and arteries of the Virgin. But while she waits, while she prepares herself for the struggle, while she invites her companions by example, gesture, and encouragement, word is brought that the enemy is moving farther from the shore. And so it was: for the archithalassus, struck by disease, had sounded the retreat, and a little later was buried by his men on the cliff opposite the harbor. Rosa, her fear of others dispelled, began to feel self-conscious about the brevity of her truncated garments, and in order to return home decently, she had to wait out the night in the aforementioned chapel with the pain of her martyrdom being snatched away, although she rejoiced in her liberated homeland and especially in the inviolable sanctity of the temples.

[288] Thus it was not a lack of martyrdom that failed him, but a lack of spirit; [and he often longed to undergo martyrdom for the same cause.] so often before and after, most ardently desired in her prayers: for she often lamented her lot, that on account of her sex she could not go into the regions of the infidels, where she would have sought death a thousand times for Christ's sake; she grieved that she was not born in those times and places when tyrannical persecution stained the orchestras, the forums, and the amphitheaters with the blood of Christians; Inestimable seemed to him the happiness of those who had been given the final drop of blood to seal the character of faith. He often said with a groan to the servant of Don Francisco Urtado de Bustamante: "If only a suitable plan or method could be found for preparing an escape to the provinces of the barbarians, so that there we might be most horribly slaughtered by the idolaters for the sake of Jesus Christ." Finally, since she could do nothing else, she delighted herself with the image and meditation of torments, all of which she desired to experience in herself for the love of her Spouse. He so desired that the world might find the Bread of Christ with St. Ignatius the martyr, for that charity by which Christ, the Angelic Bread, becomes the bread of men.

Notes.

The author of the Life here alludes to the story of Elijah, to whom, fleeing Jezebel's persecution, food was brought by an angel, and who, by the strength of that food, reached Mount Horeb in Arabia, as in the book. 3 Kings, chapter We read nineteen.

Greek πράσινος is the same as the Latin viridis, from the color of leek, which is called πράσον.

The Batavi are a people of ancient Germany, who are now commonly called the Dutch, and whom the biographer designates here.

d Archithalassus, that is, prince of the sea, a name composed of ἀρκὴ (principality) and θάλασσα (sea), and by which the prefect of that fleet is designated.

Chapter 22
Eminent zeal for the salvation of endangered souls.

[Rose, concerned for the salvation of the barbarians,] Who burned so zealously for the mystery by which the true Body of Christ is veiled, could not neglect its mystical members wherever she saw them exposed to the twofold danger of perdition. He knew to value souls by the infinite price for which they were redeemed: Hence, whenever he turned his eyes to the interior mountainous regions of South America, he was deeply tormented, weeping over the perdition of so many barbarians who, beyond those snowy summits of the highest ridges, swarmed in countless but inaccessible valleys, still blinded by ancestral idolatry. He lamented inconsolably the neighboring kingdom of Chilib, where souls perished daily by the myriads; since the fierce inhabitants, having shaken off the yoke of the king and of religion, had returned to the vomit of their former paganism. Nor did she lament only the western suns of the Indians, but whatever nations the vastest tract of China or the empires of the East contain were daily matter for Rose's lament. He longed to be torn to pieces and spread out like a net across the broadest road, down which so many wretched souls were rushing to hell. This was indeed the spirit of her seraphic mistress, who herself had wished (if it could be done) to be sealed up in her own little body to block the jaws of hell, so that such populous throngs of souls would no longer migrate there and frustrate her ransom.

[290] [He was encouraging one of his confessors,] It happened that he was talking with one of Rose's confessors about his mission to announce the Gospel to the neighboring barbarians. Therefore, to urge this Virgin to go forth, to exhort, to spur on with the most ardent words, to beseech earnestly that she fear nothing, hasten her journey, come to the aid of the perishing—that no more acceptable obedience could be offered to God, that it is the duty of the apostolic heart and dignity, to which divine assistance can in no way be lacking. Finally, it would be enough reward and consolation for so much sweat, if he were to send even a single infant among the infidels to the upper world through the gate of Baptism. On the contrary, he, seeking an experiment of the one who spoke in the Virgin, Christ, like a doubter, belittled his own powers as if they were insufficient for such a ministry. He exaggerated the difficulty of the work, the hunger, the thirst, the dangers from wild beasts, the heat, the fatigue, the exiles, and finally the poisons, with which that most cruel and inhospitable people used to rage against the preachers of the faith. Nevertheless, he added that he had placed the greatest reliance and hope in the faithful intercession of the prayers, fasts, and other good works of Rose.

[291] This Virgin, although she felt most humbly about her own pious works, [so that she might fearlessly establish the mission among the Indians,] nevertheless, to spur the departing one on as much as she could, she promised with great confidence that, by the merit of all her exercises, she would assist him in the holy expedition, provided that he, in turn, would make her a participant, in proportion to her contribution, in all the spiritual gain he was to receive from the conversion of the infidels. The confessor nodded, well aware of how great a weight the Virgin—with whom he was negotiating a mutual loan—would be before the Lord, the weigher of spirits. Thus, by mutual agreement, it was finally settled that Rosa would cede to this confessor half of all the fruits she had hoped to accumulate in the meantime through the full exercise of good works; and, in return, he would transfer to the Virgin half of the gain from the conversion of souls he was to lead onto the path of salvation through his preaching.

With the same fervor of spirit, the pious Virgin strove to have religious men (whichever she knew to be suitable) succeed in the conversion of the Gentiles; she also stirred up other suitable men for the same work, especially she begged, admonished, and implored the Brothers of her Order to send their men, well-versed in letters, to this work with timely dispatch. that they would set this generous, excellent, and sublime endeavor a goal and an end, so that with the remaining plagues of America, they would drive out whatever was left of idolatry anywhere. to bring into the Lord's barn the abundant sheaves of the Indians; to rescue thousands upon thousands of souls from the vortex of eternal damnation; one should not be too hung up on subtle points. not to languish over useless controversies of words; It is a pity to squander so many scholars' labors, so many sleepless nights, the hoarse clamors of so many disputations, if the learning won by such sweat is not devoted to the salvation of others and the propagation of the faith; if it rests, puffed up, at the goal of idle leisure, in the vanity and applause of empty pomp, or in the temporary honor of a laurel on its brow.

[293] She asserted with great earnestness and force that, had nature not made her a woman, she would have accomplished that on her own, had her sex not been an obstacle. that this would have been her first desire, that, having completed the encyclopedia of literary studies, she would devote herself entirely to the evangelical mission, eager to go to the fiercest, most savage, man-eating peoples, provided she could bring help and remedy to her own Indians, still entangled in paganism, through her catechesis, sweat, and blood. Nor did she allow the Virgin to rest in such a compassionate affection; hence she devised a plan by which, had she not been prevented by death, she hoped to supply by another in the work of the apostolic mission what she could not accomplish by herself. She had indeed conceived in her mind to adopt from elsewhere an impoverished and utterly destitute ward, whom she would raise from his very infancy with a mother's care to virtue and learning, from the pious alms of the matrons of her family: For this boy, she had soon, from his tender years, firmly and gradually proposed to implant eager desires for the evangelical mission, as he would grow little by little, and to provide in advance that he be formed most carefully in piety and sacred disciplines, until, having become older and duly initiated into the priesthood, he could be considered a suitable worker to labor in the conversion of the Gentiles. Then indeed the Virgin had resolved to demand this one thing from her protégé as the reward for her long-standing support and maternal care: that he go, that he hurry to the peoples ignorant of the Christian law, that he raise the trophy of salvation in the midst of barbarian nations, and that he mightily free as many souls as he could from the snares of the devil.
