Rosa believed that this supplement would in any way satisfy her generous ardor, by which, beyond the measure and bounds of a woman, she eagerly thirsted for the well-being of her neighbors.

[294] [He also admonished wicked Christians,] Nor was his zeal and concern any less fervent toward Christian souls in peril, whether he knew any who had, by mortal sin, wretchedly fallen from the state of divine friendship, or who were entangled by some capital vice and thus drawn into certain ruin. For these truly wretched ones, he inflicted bloody scourges on himself daily, groaned and sighed before the offended Deity, sparing no tears, so that by such deeds he might implore from God a sincere repentance for his sins and a serious amendment of his life. He was saying. if it had in any way been permitted for her to fulfill the office of a preacher, she would surely have wrapped herself in a horrid hair-shirt, barefoot, filthy, by day and by night through all the streets of Limensium, carrying the image of the crucified Redeemer on a crossroads, and everywhere in the marketplaces, at the crossroads, and in the alleys she would repeatedly cry out with a lugubrious cry: "Repent, O repent, sinners; Depart from the worst of paths, by which you are led like cattle to the slaughterhouse of a Stygian butcher; Flee, you who stand on the slippery precipices of the most wretched eternity, separated from hell by a single, most uncertain moment of fleeting life. Recognize the danger you have involved yourselves in, have mercy on your own souls, O lost sheep, whom the good Shepherd seeks to save through thorns and thistles, through bloody sweat, through wounds and the cross. Oh, hurry back to this merciful Redeemer, for if you now despise him, there will be no redemption left for you in hell.

[295] [or she would see to it that they were warned to repent,] This, the merciful Virgin, would most often offer with such a brilliant burning in her breast, and with such a passionate affection of her heart, that she would not seldom move those who heard her to compunction. Nor would Rosa now be seen privately among the faithful, but Jonas among the Ninevites, actually beginning a sermon of repentance. Father Fr. was present by chance. Anthony Rodriguez, by title, rank, and office the preacher general: to him the Virgin turned, with equal fervor and confidence (guided by modesty and charity), and thus addressed him: "Mark well, O Father, that divine clemency has willed you to be a preacher, so that you may bring lost and obstinate sinners back to the right path." Therefore, be careful not to waste the rich talent of your eloquence on flowery, clever notions that are merely pleasing. Away with these calamus-wielding pedants of empty cleverness; let the theatrical display of cothurnus and phalera make way. Remember that you were appointed by the Lord as a fisherman of men; you must cast your net more vigorously to capture sinners. Do this, I beg you, strive for this one thing: to rescue as many wretched souls as possible from the whirlpools, to snatch from the very Charybdis of vices those who are about to perish, and to bring them to the safe shore of salutary repentance.

It happened, while Rosa was still living with her mother, that a young man of noble birth, even more distinguished by his manners, Vincent Montes Venegas, took up residence on the same street not far from Rosa's house. He marveled at the Virgin's exceptional beauty. But certain of one thing, [and seeing that the young man who had come to him with a lustful mind could not hope for marriage,] he sought an opportunity by which he might at least more freely feast his eyes on Rosa's most sweet beauty. So she went to her mother, pretending that she needed some precious collars made from a thin piece of cloth, saying she wanted them made by Rosa, who was known to earn her parents' living by that craft. Rosa sat intent on the girls' handiwork, when her mother motioned for her to listen to Vincent and to meet him face to face, as was the custom, to discuss the price. The young man, having been ordered to sit before the Virgin, politely began, in the urban fashion, to inquire what was required, saying he had come on a commission to have several collars made: he asked how many ells of the finest cloth would be needed for each, at what cost, where the material could most conveniently be purchased, and within what time the work could be completed.

But the Virgin, divinely instructed, rebuked the secret that weighed on Vincent's heart and began to pity the stinking soul, in which the impure desires of a slippery youth were stagnating. and, with eyes raised to heaven and a groan, said, "O good Jesus!" How exceedingly long your patience is! But you, noble Vincent, forgive me, and give a hearing to the truth I am about to tell you. Your heart turns its thoughts far away from collars. Shall I tell you frankly what you've come for? But I spare your blushes. You act like a youth, and I pity your lot. Grieve also, and repent. A lie is whatever does not lead to God. It destroys the soul, whatever flatters the licentious flesh. Recognize your danger, Vincent, and correct a mind distracted by vain amusement and excessive hunting. Strive to walk more carefully in the paths of God's commandments, lest you perish: behold, whatever you try to cover up, your intention has not escaped my Spouse."

Struck by the Virgin's sudden sermon, Vincent lowered his eyes and, looking into himself, fell silent for a while. Finally, now changed from the one he had been a little while before, he thus addressed the Virgin: "I feel Christ speaking in you, who alone could have revealed me to you in this way, so that you might penetrate the inmost depths of my wicked heart." I yield, therefore, to your pious and fiery exhortation; I will follow the God who invites me to better things. May you make Him propitious to me, so that He may complete what has today begun in me. The Virgin added the faithful aid of her prayers and kindly dismissed the man, who from that time, bidding farewell to his youthful desires, established a grave and sober way of life, so that every eight days he would at least once cleanse his soul with the sacrament of Penance, and to refresh himself with the divine Banquet, rejoicing to have fallen upon a Rose, who had both learned from heaven the state of his unhappy soul and knew how to apply the salutary thorns of serious compunction at a timely moment to the less expectant.

It was a miracle if anyone, no matter how flawed, approached Rosa and did not come away better. Maria de Mesta, wife of the painter Medor Angelini, had by her impatient and headlong temper become almost unbearable to everyone: she was disturbed and disturbing over the slightest thing, ready with her hand, menacing with her shouts, and with her loud quarrelsomeness an implacable storm throughout the entire house. When the storm of her spirit subsided, she would sometimes ache and condemn the unbridled impotence of her own ferocity. but a choleric temperament, and habit had bred a callousness to wrongdoing. At the suggestion of others, or perhaps by chance, he once visited Rosa, who was living in a garden shed. Immediately the Virgin, seizing the opportunity, launched into a serious and nerve-racking discourse on the tranquility of the mind, prescribed brief and wholesome aphorisms for acquiring gentleness, offered fitting counsel on equanimity and patience, and thus dismissed her, having instructed her. What a wonder! From that day on, the woman was more tranquil and gentle, seeming to have put on a tractable disposition of peace. She bore the domestic thorns and troubles of her household with a very quiet heart, and if any sudden gust of anger was kindled, she would quench it by the mere remembrance of the most meek Virgin with remarkable ease. In a short time she reached such a degree of patience that she would now of her own accord ask God for more and more adversity. Indeed, it was only the zeal of souls that had made the shy and taciturn Rosa eloquent. He was never at a loss for the eloquence to effectively persuade his audience of the pursuit of virtue and the avoidance of vice.

[300] [A religious man's vile passion] A certain man whose religious profession had been led by that foul, as well as foolish and monstrous, gluttony for smoking tobacco to the very brink of the destruction of both body and soul, so that a remedy for both was almost universally cried out for. The man's mouth inside was utterly sooty, like a chimney; his foul nostrils were caked with a sooty cream; a fog-vexed brain dripped onto his chest; and with his lungs blocked, a hoarse asthma besieged his windpipe, yet the wretch did not cease from the noxious draught of the kindled pipe. The doctor protested, they dissuaded him, friends begged him; finally, the regular prelates added to the severest prohibitions fines, precepts, and the thunderbolts of anathemas and censures. but in vain, for the stubborn vice, by thirty-three years of possession and delay, had grown too strong, and no other remedy seemed to remain for the perishing man, except that Rosa, after so many exhortations, counsels, and invectives from others, after so many rebukes, threats, and clamors from her superiors, would exercise her marvelous, more ardent eloquence, speak to the man's heart, and commend at least the salvation of his soul to the hand of the Almighty.

[301] [and an age-old custom,] The Virgin readily agreed, pitying the one in peril; especially since he knew that he had not fallen into this by willful obstinacy or contempt for obedience, but only through his weakness in resisting a violent and long-standing vice, and had thus been so brutalized by the most wanton intemperance. And behold, scarcely five days had passed since the Virgin's address, when the man was seized by a complete nausea for tobacco, soon followed by horror, and a vehement abhorrence, so that from then on he not only most easily abstained from the smoking of the clay pipe, but could no longer endure the smell of this ill-omened little furnace, which could be kindled by the delights and appetites of human folly only by him who is thus described in Job 41: "From his nostrils comes forth smoke, like the smoke of a cauldron that boils over:" and finally, even here in Europe, he found those who would smoke with him.

[302] [and with her speech she removes the diseases that arise from it,] Rosa healed this madness in a single instance, and will cure many more, if she is properly implored. Nor did the Virgin's benefit stop there: she at once cast out an old, chronic asthma, hemorrhage, and colic, with which that poor man had been struggling for more than fourteen years. Indeed, the energy of the most salutary correction penetrated to the very soul: for that mind, wonderfully restored to sobriety, began to attend more diligently to the business of eternal salvation, to purify its conscience, to reform its cowardly habits, to shake off its old sluggishness, to the joy of all who had ever before lamented its decay in the corruption of that vice. Indeed, this was how he had heard Rosam, who knows how to make a whole person well on the Sabbath.

I will add one thing and finish. Father Peter de Loaysa, a Dominican, [and another near death,] being well aware of the zeal and charity with which the merciful Rose burned for the aid of her fellow travelers in peril, had informed the Virgin that N. was lying in the convent, anxiously struggling with the pangs of death. A Religious of the same institute, abandoned by the doctors the day before; He added that the sick man was tormented by burning scruples, afflicted not by the certainty of imminent death, but by the uncertainty of a better life, because he recognized himself to be devoid of merit; he trembled in fear and sweated from the constant memory of the stern Judge he was about to approach. Finally, it must be overturned, lest excessive fear involve the desolate one in a more serious danger.

[304] These things wounded the heart of the Virgin most intimately, so prompt to compassion and so pliable and obedient: [who, on account of the imminent judgment of God, was exceedingly anxious,] wherefore he enjoined Peter to visit the sick man and, in his name, to bid him hope more confidently in the immense mercy of the most good and most benign Deity: that she would indeed pray for his happy passing, but at the same time offer herself from that moment, whatever merit she might have acquired through her pious exercises, for whatever the dying man needed to make up for any deficiency in his merits; indeed, she would claim it all for herself, and, if any good work had ever proceeded from Rosa throughout her life up to now, she wishes to transfer it entire from herself as a most free gift to the sick man, so that he may present it, as if it were his own, to the Judge, and in the meantime cease from thinking meanly and humbly of the magnificent liberality of the Most High. He added to this full consolation that he wished, God willing, to be visited by the deceased's soul upon his death, so that if it should happen to need further suffrages, it might be helped at once.

Peter faithfully reported everything to the sick man, [commanding him to be made secure of eternal salvation.] and immediately that excessive fear gave way to the joy of a more joyful hope; The patient accepted Rose's generous largess, not unaware of how much wealth lay hidden in a gift of so many merits. Thus fortified at last with the salutary Sacraments, and filled with great confidence of salvation, he returned his spirit to the Creator, tranquil, placid, and serene, having previously made a pact with Peter that he would visit both himself and Rosa in death, if the Lord would grant him that opportunity. But after his brother's death, Peter was deeply troubled by the question of why, according to the deceased's own stipulation, his soul did not appear. But the secure Rose dispelled Peter's anxiety and said that the deceased's soul was doing well and happily, already enrolled in the region of light and enjoying perpetual refreshment, so that it would by no means need to return to either place to beg for the alms of suffrages. Indeed, the generous charity of Rose had enriched her with such a provision for her departure from here, for to her nothing could be more precious or dear than what she would not expend for the salvation of souls.

Notes.

The ribs are those bones in the human body which, as is well known, connect to the spine and enclose the intestines. But here it seems that that name is used for slopes or the sides of mountains. In what sense Persius called the ridge of the mountain.

Chile or Cile is a vast region of America, extending four hundred French leagues from north to south, which is bordered on the north by Peru, on the west by the Pacific Ocean, on the south by the Magellanic region, and on the east by Tucumania. John the Latter, book. 12. West Indies, chapter. 1 & following provides a more extensive description of this region, prefacing it with a geographical map.

c Cannibals are called those nations that feed on human flesh, from the Greek origin ἄνθρωπος, that is, man, and the verb φάγω, which is to eat.

An aphorism, from the Greek ἀφορισμὸς, is a statement that embraces all the properties of a thing in the briefest terms. Others therefore call them definitions, such as the aphorisms of Hippocrates in medicine. This name can also be applied, for a brief introduction, to other sciences.

This Indian herb, now widely known and used throughout Europe, was first named after the American island of the same name, on which it grew most abundantly. The physicians of Lyon, in the Dictionary of Etymology under the word "tabac," write thus of this herb: "As it is a great ornament to all gardens, so it is celebrated for its remarkable properties; the herb which Thevetus reports to be called PETUM by the Indians, and Nicholas Monardus PICIELT." Oviedo on the island of Hispaniola, PETEBECENUC. The Spaniards named it TABACO after an island of that name, on which it is found most abundantly. The French, because John Nicot, once a royal envoy to Portugal, was the first to bring its seed to the queen, mother of the King of France, and to teach its properties, called it the Nicotian herb or the queen's herb. Furthermore, Menagius in the same work, under the heading "Nicotiane," reports a satirical epigram on this occasion against Catherine de' Medici, Queen of France. Similar things about the name of this herb can be read in Trivulzio's Dictionary of the French Language. From this we conclude why Baudrand in his Geography called the island of Tabago also Nicotiana.

A clay pipe is an instrument for drinking tobacco smoke, as Europeans as well as Indians already know.

Hemorrhage from Greek αἱμοῤῥαγία is an abundant and excessive flow of blood, from αἷμα blood, & ῥέω to flow. Consult physicians of your choice about the various causes and effects of this disease, if you wish.

Chapter 23
Material works of mercy spent on one's neighbors.

[She fasts, so that she may feed the poor,] She will marvel, whoever reads, that the funeral of the most wretched Rosa was celebrated with the public wails, dirges, and tears of the beggars of Lima, the crowd of needy crying out that a true nurse and mother of the poor had been snatched from them by the most bitter and untimely fate. For what could the poor daughter of poor parents share with other needy people, when she herself had barely enough to support herself and her parents? And yet, depriving himself of what was necessary, he distributed it to the poor, and, mindful of the heavenly Lapicida, who (as related above in chapter… Having taken upon herself the care of her parents' support as one of her dowry stipulations, she, with her mother's consent, opened her hand to the needy and stretched out her palms to the poor, sharing with them the alms she had unexpectedly received. Once, when a certain person's hidden or urgent need was reported to the virgin, but since no other help was at hand, she withdrew eight days' worth of food (living in the meantime on only bread and water) to provide relief for the needy.

On another occasion Gaspar Flores, the Virgin's father, brought home a large roll of whiter cloth (whether donated, or the cloth given to him among the poor, I do not know), so that his wife Maria de Oliva could more generously provide for herself and her flock of children in all things pertaining to cleanliness. Knowing that her daughter Rosa, because of her frequent illnesses and her love of cleanliness, needed more than one of the same linen, she set aside for her a notable portion of that cloth (it was thirty-six ells), so that she herself could make handkerchiefs for her own use as she saw fit, handkerchiefs, peplums, bandages, aprons, sails, mantillas, coverings, ribbons, collars, napkins, and all other necessary articles of pure whiteness, and to sew them. Rosa, either admiring the generous gift or, more humbly, to thank her mother, said, "Are you giving me this entire portion of linen, mother?" She replied, "To you, my daughter." Use it as you please; do whatever you want with it. The virgin seized upon these last words and, without even keeping the palm of that cloth for herself, immediately gave the whole thing in secret to two virgins, noble both by birth and virtue, yet exceedingly poor, whom she knew to be secretly enduring a tremendous scarcity of everything at home.

[308] The mother, seeing that her daughter was thinking of nothing less than making herself linens, and the mother, displeased at this, suspected what was happening and asked why she was delaying to adapt the white, fine, good cloth entrusted to her for her own use. The daughter replied, "It is already fitted as well as it can be; indeed, it has only now first shone with beauty since it was turned into a charitable gift." The mother replied, "But I had instructed you to spend on your own needs, making sure that nothing would be lacking when you fall ill." The virgin smiled and said, "Haven't you clearly given me the power to use it as I please?" I have used it, and from now on, when I fall sick, do not think that I will lack anything: the Lord will provide abundantly. And so it happened: for shortly thereafter, having been moved from her mother's house into the household of Lady Maria Usategui, she, by virtue of the matron's piety, lacked no furnishings, indeed had more than enough. On the Virgin's admirable trust in divine providence, separately in the following chapter.

Rosa's mother possessed in her own feminine world two pallia, one of which she had also given to a poor virgin—that is, long, head-to-toe cloaks, a feminine and becoming covering whenever one appears in public. Rosa, having by chance at home spotted one of them left on a seat by her mother, freely took it and gave it as a gift to a certain poor virgin. The mother, having searched in vain throughout the entire house for her cloak, indulged in suspicions, almost to the point of rash judgment, that one of the neighbors had crept into the house secretly and had picked up the cloak, which had been carelessly left hanging from the chair. The daughter, so as not to burden any innocent person with an accusation of theft, sweetly said to her mother, "Why do you hesitantly accuse your loss and its uncertain author?" Behold me, a thief of your cloak—a domestic one, but harmless, for a lost cloak will serve you better than one kept intact. In the meantime, I have given this to the very poor Montoya, who, because she lacks such clothing, cannot attend church decently or attend Mass and sermons. "To you, O Mother, another new cloak remains, still almost intact; and before it has even slightly worn, divine goodness will bless you not with one, but with several cloaks."

[310] [He promises repayment with interest.] What the Virgin had foretold came to pass; For a short time later, a man, never before seen in those houses and unknown to any of the household, entered, asked for Rosa's mother, offered her forty silver pounds to buy a new cloak, and left. Likewise, around the same days, D. Maria de Sala sent, through a servant, a suitable material for the feric undergarment, enough to make a new pall. Finally, a devout person had offered to the Convent of the Friars Preachers of Lima the same number of yards of the same material, which would have been sufficient for a woman's cloak; Here Rosa gave her mother this gift: thus, within a few days, Maria de Oliva received three new and better cloaks in exchange for her only cloak, which Rosa had given to the needy woman of Montoya, and she learned to trust more boldly both the prophecy of the Daughter and the providence of the generous Deity.

[311] [A poor and ailing virgin of noble lineage] Outside the walls, in the outermost suburbs of the city of Lima, lived Joanna de Bobadilla y Azevedo, a virgin of illustrious parentage but an orphan, and richer in virtue than in wealth: for having lost her parents, her circumstances were straitened, and she was already sorely pressed by the hidden hardship of frugal economy. Adding to the heap of affliction was a voracious and purulent cancer beneath the breast, which, with its bluish and gangrenous growths, was developing into a carcinoma and, with treatment delayed, threatened destruction. Joanna, as lacking in counsel as she was unprepared for danger and calamity, could find no way to meet the crisis without bringing on an even worse one: for to summon a doctor from the city each day to the place where she lived across the river, the long distance was an obstacle. For at that time (as it is today), the size of Lima had not yet grown to those more distant spaces beyond the river. Whether he moved to the city for slow treatment, the funds were lacking for a six-month stay. There was one who offered his house for free. But the honorable and timid virgin would not trust strangers with her identity. Finally, while praying in the church of Saint Dominic, Rose perceived everything, and, understanding that this desolate and perplexed case was soon to be entrusted to her, she promptly found a way to help.

[312] [He orders her to be cared for in her mother's house,] he secretly approached Joanna, told her to cast aside her fear and anxiety, to secure a suitable and very convenient room to rent in her mother's house at a reasonable price, to have her mother agree on the monthly rent, and to leave the entire responsibility for arranging the payment to him. Joanna opened up and accepted the unexpected favor with modest cheerfulness. Rosa, however, was all the more joyful, because she had found someone to whom she could so secretly and piously extend the bowels of her mercy. Thus, trusting in divine providence, he discovered that each month he had been secretly bringing to Joanna the exact payment for her lodging, having only agreed to keep faith with her silence. After four or five months, Joanna had fully recovered and returned to her lodgings abroad in good health, grieving only that she could not publicly express her gratitude to her hostess. But after Rose's death, freed from the bond of her promised silence, she freely proclaimed the benefit to all.

[313] [& to any sick person,] For Rose, as long as she lived with her mother, nothing could have been more delightful than if she had been permitted to bring into her father's house poor people of any sort or condition afflicted by illness, so that she could there constantly minister to the sick with her own hands: and she strove to abundantly refresh those in need, not only with shelter, a bed, and bedding, but also with the comfort of medicines and food. Whenever she learned in the neighborhood or from a familiar household that any humble servant was down with illness and being cared for more negligently, she would at once demand to look after him, and with many entreaties she would persuade her mother to allow the ailing one to be brought to her own home for more attentive care. Her austere mother would sometimes object, believing that a daughter who was seldom well and constantly ailing, before she undertook the care of others, ought to begin the work of charity with herself and look after her own health more than that of others. However, from time to time, so as not to grieve the merciful one mercilessly, he granted her permission to work according to her domestic frailty, as much as she could, not as much as she wished.

[314] Soon she would invite the first beggar she met into her own home, [even if they were suffering from a filthy disease,] she would wipe this one, anoint him, bind up his pus-filled sores, she would clean this one's clothes, mend his tattered undergarments, scrape away the filth that had crept into the folds of his skin, washed his feet and his impetigined head, and was lacking in no service by which she might bring relief to his hunger, thirst, and pressure. Nor (which is almost unusual for that region) was there any distinction in Virginia of origin or nation, but she would equally and impartially come to the aid of needy Spaniards, Indians, Ethiopians, whites, and mixed-race people (whom they call mulattos) with ready charity. He treated vernacular, foreign, servile, free, familiar, unfamiliar, rustic, and indigenous people with equal compassion, regarding only the more obvious need. Among these beggars and wretches there was none so ragged or filthy, none so deformed or ulcerous, none so hideous in appearance or so intolerably foul-smelling that Rosa could not but loathe or turn away in disgust. No sickness so foul and putrid, no sore so dark or festering, no scabies so crusted or oozing, to which Rosa would refuse to offer her remedy, her service, her care; to which she would not apply her virgin hands, to remove the filth, to apply, to cleanse, to change the poultice. With this pious ambition, the Virgin would visit the women's infirmaries whenever there was an opportunity to be in the company of honorable women. She would seek out the sick along the corridors—those most dreadful and protracted of all—and with remarkable alacrity she would make their beds, wash their vessels, and prepare their food. Nor could she render them any mean, grim, or base service that she did not herself willingly undertake.

[315] It happened that one day, while tending to an infirm woman, the Virgin was for a short time diverted to her mother's house: [serving with the utmost care,] the curious mother detected in her daughter's garment an unusual, musty odor: then, examining the tunic more closely, she noticed that here and there some purulent filth had accidentally adhered to the cloth from another's pus, which the Virgin had unknowingly contracted while washing the ulcerous woman. An indignant parent was scolding her daughter. "What is the reason for this negligence of yours?" "I'm not at all asking you to don that pious, snow-white robe so you can soil it with other people's filth, nor did I call you Rosa so you'd smell like a cabbage. When I told you to use mousemeat and fragrant gloves, you little one, you resisted; but now that you're grown, if you don't want to smell of musk, at least don't stink of someone else's sweat." The Virgin smiled, and sweetly added: "We are the good odor of Christ, while we minister to the sick;" Charity is not delicate, nor does it despise its neighbor, remembering that we are all made of the same clay and mire. By hereditary mortality we all rot, and there is no one who does not carry about the stench of his own corruption: therefore, mother, do not think it a small thing that I inadvertently stained my tunic; far fouler still, the face of the Redeemer itself was disfigured by tears, spittle, and the salivations of vile executioners, on account of our sins.

[316] [& once about her nausea] Rosa performed another feat of this kind, scarcely imitable, which should not be kept silent. A certain domestic servant of the widow, Mrs. Isabella de Mexia, was lying ill in her house, overcome by phlegm and a feverish heat. Rosa had taken upon herself the care and service of this one, as she was in the family home with her mother's permission. It happened that, on the doctor's advice, the patient was subjected to a bloodletting, and the blood drawn from the vein was set aside in a corner while the doctor returned to examine it. It delayed for more than two days, and in the meantime the blood from the very source, already noted for its putrefaction, had rotted even more horribly, becoming a foul, serous pus streaked with yellow and black, and everywhere thick and discolored with branching matter. At last the physician arrived, and after examining the blood and noting in it the criteria of a dangerous disease, he ordered the foul-smelling mass to be thrown away as usual. Rosa, moved by compassion, gazed more intently at the basin in which the blood clot was being carried away, and felt her stomach turn from deep within, her saliva alter in her throat, and her entire being incline toward vomiting. She concealed it as best she could and, calling to the servant who was hurrying away with the basin, asked that it be brought to her, saying she would not only empty its contents but also clean the vessel itself until it was spotless.

The man readily agreed, thinking that such a stench would rid him. [He reports an outstanding victory.] But the Virgin, indignant with herself because she had recoiled, secretly withdrew with the basin to a corner outside, and there, harshly reproaching herself, said, "Have you then so strenuously and earnestly devoted yourself to the love of your neighbor that the infirmity of the miserable makes you nauseous?" Did your seraphic teacher teach you this? Thus you have not yet learned that with all this rot you are both more worthless and more rotten. Come, delicate one, come, tender one, experience better, learn more fully, whether it is fitting for you to so disdain your wretched and fellow human being, in whom the image of your Creator shines no less than in yourself. Having brought these forth with a roar (reader, stop up your nostrils), he placed the basin to his mouth and with heroic daring slowly drank down all that pus-filled gore (shall I call it pus or bloody pus?). He then wiped his mouth with a cloth so that no trace of residue might be noticed, and brought back the basin, washed, scrubbed, and shining. His mother's noble deed was hidden. But to Isabella (as God miraculously arranged), it became known, and she, having at last by chance found the cloth with which the Virgin had wiped her lips, more devoutly preserved it as a memorial of the event. Henceforth, no one (I think) will doubt, Rosa B. To have been a direct disciple of Catherine of Siena.

[318] [Visiting the sick friend of his host,] Hence, indeed, what follows becomes more credible. To the Quaestor Gundisalvus, both by the bond of office and by a like devotion to virtue and friendship, was intimately attached and closely related John of Tineo Almianza, chartophylax of the royal (as they call it) chamber and supreme circuit judge of Quito, a man distinguished for his piety and religion. He, as he was a frequent and almost domestic visitor to the questor's house, had there often seen Rosa on business occasions, and had heard more than once of her sanctity and the wonderful singularity of her life; so he thought of the Virgin in a sublime manner, as was fitting, especially since he knew, Gundisalvus had been freed from very serious illnesses by Rosa's prayers and merits on several occasions. It happened that John fell dangerously ill from stomach pains, so that, having summoned his confessor, he led his soul to be purified by confession, as if uncertain of his life. Meanwhile, mindful of the charitable deeds with which he knew the Virgin abounded toward the sick, he earnestly implored the husband of the questor to bring Rosa, who in such a peril would pray well for him. At last, with the confessor's nod, she prevailed upon the Virgin to promise the questor's wife that she would accompany her on her visit to the sick man. What prevented Rose, out of virgin modesty, from submitting to the sight of the man, though he was the most familiar and infirm, was that it was harsh and unusual. But above all shone a pious compassion, which could not deny the sick just consolations, and a humble obedience, which she acknowledged she owed to Rosa Maria de Usategui, as to a second mother.

[319] And so, with these safe precautions, she set out on the road with the most honorable matron and her attendants, [she immediately cured him of his illness;] but first she wished to attend the sacrifice of the Mass at the church of Saint Dominic, and in the meantime she sent ahead someone to announce to the sick man that she, as he had requested, would come to him from the temple shortly with the matron. The sick man felt his pains eased at the very moment he had received the message. At last Rosa entered the sick woman's chamber, greeted the patient, but with such a sweet abundance of compassion and mercy that even to the healthy bystanders it was plain that in that most modest Virgin spoke the God of all consolation. But John, observing Rose's face, perceived in it an angelic majesty, a token of peace, and a living hope of safety. Without delay Immediately all pain vanished, and a gentle sleep, which had completely fled, peacefully seized the invalid; soon he fell into a deep slumber (while the Virgin, having returned home with her companions, had awakened). He awoke fully recovered. It was a shortcut by which Rosa, swiftly aided by a miracle, would neither be lacking in the work of charity nor linger longer in a stranger's house than is the custom of virgins. He was no less anxious to depart as quickly as possible, to avoid the applause he justly feared.

[320] I defer the many miracles of this kind of cure to another place and time for the time being. [Indeed, her charity extended even to brute animals,] here, where we speak only of Rose's mercy, let us be permitted to add one charming detail: for Rose's clemency and tender compassion extended even to the brute creatures; for, as Solomon testifies in Proverbs. 12 The righteous knows the soul of his animals, but the entrails of the wicked are cruel. There was in Mary of Oliva's own domestic hen-house a pullet of marvelous beauty; it played upon its back, and its wings displayed a delightful variety of striped feathers. A certain band encircled its neck with a purple-like beauty, and the hind part of its body, instead of feathers, seemed to end in an arc of charming iridescence. Finally, this one charming little creature was the delight of the entire household, and everyone was pleased that the matriarch was nurturing and keeping it in hopes of offspring when it grew up; for it was hoped that the chicks it would hatch would resemble it very closely. The chick grew, but it was so sluggish and belly-heavy that it lay on the ground all the time, and was scarcely ever seen to rise to its feet of its own accord, nor was it ever heard to chirp. Fed up, the mother, believing that a cowardly offspring would be of no use, decided at the table before her husband and sons to slaughter the useless chick that evening and serve it for dinner the next day.

[321] [as is demonstrated by a charming example.] The young maiden Rosa, standing here, pitied the beast, and with innocent simplicity turned childishly to the bird; "Sing, my little one," he said, "sing, lest you die." Scarcely had the girl spoken a word when, before everyone's eyes, the chick suddenly sprang to its feet, flapped its wings vigorously, and chirped cheerfully. Soon, pacing the entire chamber with lofty and proud strides, he sang eagerly several times at Rose's nod, his whole chest distended. All who were present laughed; with the death sentence suddenly revoked, the chick, its song repeated, clapped along with the others, strutted as if proudly belted high, and with its neck held erect, the shrill little one again and again renewed the household's shrieks of delight. From then on, he often filled the neighborhood with sonorous songs throughout the day. He counted the family's turns and discovered that they had sometimes sung fifteen times in the short span of a quarter hour. Nor did it disappoint the housewife, for shortly thereafter this most handsome rooster produced the most beautiful chicks. The single, merciful voice of Rosa was enough to save even a brute animal. Indeed, one might ask here: Who gave the rooster the intelligence to obey the holy maiden's command to give his throat to song, rather than to a knife? But to graver matters.

Notes.

Cancer is a disease arising from black bile that bubbles up, then invades other parts of the body and frequently attacks women's breasts. Cancer is said to derive its name from the resemblance of the crab of the zodiac, because it creeps gradually or clings tenaciously like a crab that seizes something with its claws. Others derive this appellation from the shape of the animal's crab, among whom Castellus in the Medical Lexicon relates the following: Veins in that part are clearly visible, full and swollen with thick black blood, so arranged around the tumor that they seem to form the shape of a crab. For the swelling itself of the cancer, its swollen and thick veins represent the body and its feet to the eye.

Carcinoma is a type of cancer, about whose risks and treatments one can consult a doctor. This name is also taken metaphorically for a vile or foul person, in which sense our humble Virgin is referred to above, no. 46 called herself an abominable carcinoma of the human race.

I searched in vain for the word pluderones among Latin and Greek writers. However, from the context I infer that by it cheap clothes are meant here.

Mulattoes are those who are born of mixed African and Indian parents. who are called Mestizos from the Spaniards and Americans, as can be seen more fully in the later edition of Cangii's Glossary under these words.

An emplaster, from the Greek ἔμπλαστρος, according to Gorræus, is a medicine pressed into a mass, which, when softened, is applied to wounds. It differs from a cataplasm, because emplasters are thicker and drier, as the renowned Gorræus asserts in his Medical Definitions, page 214.

In both ecclesiastical and secular affairs, the chartophylax is the keeper of the charters or prefect of the archives, who preserves privileges and the like.

Quito is a province and city of America, in which there is a legal assembly and a episcopal see, as John Latius states in his book. Chapter 10, West Indies 6 & 8 fuse exponent

A watchman or patroller was formerly appointed by the prefect of Rome to patrol the city or to keep watch over its guards. I suspect that this man is called a circuit judge because of the similarity of his office, since he was supposed to observe whether the ministers of the Quito tribunal were performing their duties.

Chapter 24
Great was this virgin's trust in God, the fruit of which she often experienced in her own and her loved ones' needs.

[Saint, firmly trusting in God,] in her earliest childhood Christ had anticipated his Rose with blessings of sweetness, teaching her to trust in the help of the Most High. Hence, for the rest of her life, she was most secure, always dwelling in the protection of God of heaven. Hence he was so deeply moved at the beginning of the Psalms. 69 "God, come to my aid; Lord, make haste to help me." She constantly had this verse on her lips; while working with her hands she sweetly hummed it under her breath; whether sitting, standing, or walking she devoutly repeated it, and she never grew tired of its frequent repetition, especially when she later learned that the same verse had been in the blessed Catherine of Siena's constant use and delight. He would sometimes ask that the emphasis of these words be explained to him more learnedly and profoundly. but he never came across an interpreter who could better know the innermost sweetness of this honey by experience, or explain it with fitting indications. When asked why, out of so many thousands, she loved this single verse above all others, she replied that the entire consolation of her seraphic mother was contained in it, that each of its syllables swelled with a certain secret sap of familiar confidence in God, and that nothing could taste sweeter to her palate than these words so uniquely her own. There were three things of paramount importance, and the Virgin did not allow herself to doubt their attainment, for a heavenly certainty of her trust had been granted. First, his eternal beatitude; the other, the perpetual and never-to-be-interpolated friendship of God; ultimately, infallible help from on high in whatever needs or sudden dangers. Go through each one.

The Spouse's piety sought an opportunity by which to make the beloved Virgin secure in a singular privilege of eternal happiness. [by heavenly revelation,] for which reason He once permitted it to be somewhat shaken by a fearful dread concerning the mystery of its predestination, a dread terrible to the whole soul. Innocent Rosa was accused, considering that she might perish; She trembled in fear, reflecting on the great abyss of God's judgments. But the Lord did not delay to hasten to help; rather, He opportunely comforted that contrite and humbled heart, speaking to her with gentle words: "Daughter, I condemn no one, unless one is willing to be condemned; Therefore, be of a calm mind from now on. It cannot be said how sublime, invincible, and steadfast a confidence these few things had imprinted on the heart of the Virgin: for she understood that they signified to her more by the very manner of their utterance than by words, and thereafter the Spouse did not allow her, fixed as she was in the anchor of eternal salvation, to be troubled by any fluctuation of uncertainty.

Doctor John of Castile once asked the Virgin, among other things, whether she had received a certain knowledge of her predestination revealed from heaven? The unlettered maiden was puzzled by the scholastic term PREDESTINATION, [as she indicated to Doctor Castillo,] whose true meaning and etymon she did not fully grasp. He had heard nothing else about it, except that it was a sign of an impenetrable mystery, not to be rashly probed by any mortal. And so the simple and timid woman finally denied that she knew anything about the most subtle notion of predestination. But when the prudent examiner, by means of suitable periphrases, more clearly explained what he was asking, Rosa could no longer deny, nor indeed could she, that by a heavenly illumination she had learned with great timeliness that she had been chosen from eternity for celestial glory, and that she held this with such certainty by a marvelous gift of God that she could not desire a clearer revelation of it.

[325] [and at the point of death she showed,] These things were more manifestly evident in the dying Rose's final bed: for the most blessed passage of her soul to blessed rest was so certain that she even foresaw she would be free from all purgatorial delay. And when one of those present had said that this grace was exceedingly great and altogether rare, and that souls departing from here fare well enough while they are sent to the purifying flames to have the remaining scum of mortal life washed away, he earnestly wished that for himself. Immediately the Virgin, animated by great confidence, responded boldly: "I, however, have a Spouse who can give things that are exceedingly great and rare, and not merely small or mediocre things should be expected from him through lack of faith." Hence she looked upon the neighboring death with joy, as upon the certain gatekeeper of paradise: for as soon as the doctors announced to Rosa that nature had succumbed to the disease, and that nothing remained but the decisive day. Fearless and unperturbed, she replied that she was delighted with the town by such a welcome message, and only wondered why they had feared to inform the expectant sooner.

[326] [and another mystical vision] It is more memorable that he made Rosa secure in eternal salvation while she was dwelling in her little garden cell. The Virgin was praying, and in a trance of mind she saw all around her the ground strewn with roses. As I marvelled whence such a sudden spring had burst forth, behold, the little Jesus appeared, supported in the arms of the purest Mother, and gently urging the Rose, he at once commanded her to gather the scattered roses into the wide, sinuous fold of her garment. She gathered what she could and, with her arms full, stopped the divine little boy. He asked for only one gift from all of them. Upon gratefully receiving it, she added: "This rose is you." I will take charge of this with careful foresight; you may arrange the rest as you see fit. The wise Virgin understood where the delicate Spouse's words were tending, so serious and precious as they were. He rejoiced to be held in the Savior's right hand like a chosen rose. He remembered the things about which he had promised Joan. 10: "No one will snatch them from my hand." I give them eternal life.

[327] [She was made certain of her predestination] Moreover, from the greatness of her joy, seeing that a lovely rose was safely springing to life in the Little One's palm, she neither knew nor anxiously cared what to do with the other roses, except that with a brief, spontaneous plan she made a wreath from them and reverently placed it upon the heavenly Infant, who, smiling most sweetly at the Virgin, gave his blessing and vanished. She later suspected, that the other roses, which she had seen scattered across the ground, signified the most pious virgins scattered throughout Lima, who at long last ought to be gathered into the new monastery of Saint Catherine of Siena, so that there, bound by the vow of religious profession, they might crown the Spouse of virgins with chaste service, and in turn be crowned by him with glory and honor. So it happened. But only after Rose's death. On this matter, see the proper place below, chapter. Twenty-six.

[328] She enjoyed the same unshaken certainty and was happily comforted by Rosa in the security of grace, [and with her state of grace confirmed,] and of divine friendship: for besides that after those fifteen years of the most dreadful eclipses of obscuration (of which above chapter XIII) Every day, in her return to divine union, she discovered a new illustration, finding herself confirmed in the favor of the Bridegroom. On other occasions, too, the Lord had promised Rosa that he would never allow her to fall away from the bond of their sweet friendship, even for a moment. One of the Virgin's confessors stood speechless, dumbfounded by such an inestimable gift. He was Father Brother Peter de Loaysa of the Order of Preachers, who, desiring to experience more clearly how firmly the Virgin stood in the faith of so great a promise, in one of her confessions began to raise a childish and insignificant matter as if it were doubtful, and worthy of a more careful examination, to see what this sudden and not perhaps useless terror would work in the Virgin, whom such security of divine grace, as he knew, possessed.

[329] The level-headed Rosa was amazed at the unusual amphiboly of the stern censor, [though the confessor tried to frighten her.] and she allowed him to speak to his heart's content. But at last, certain that she had not violated God's friendship, she perceived where that covert industry of ambiguous exploration was tending; so, having first asked for leave, she modestly replied, "I am rightly admonished to work out my salvation with fear and trembling." And may I work like this, as I ought! I am a sinner, I confess, and to confess, I cast myself down here. But I feel within me such infallible tokens of His uninterrupted favor toward me that it would be easier for anyone to persuade me that I am a rock or chaff than that the sweet Deity has turned away from me. My Father, not even if someone were to fold the vast orbs of the heavens onto this earth could he bend me to the belief that, with God's permission, I have committed a mortal sin. I know what he promised me, even though it was unworthy. I trust in the Lord, not because I deserve to be preserved in this way, but because he is faithful in all his words. The self-aware confessor of the truth acquiesced, briefly excusing himself that he had stirred up this censorious debate solely for the sake of testing the spirit.

[330] That same strength of confidence in God, from her earliest years, had powerfully armed Rosa against any difficulties, fears, and hardships of mortal life. Maria de Oliva, mother of Rosæ, of a singular temperament, feared darkness and spectres, and would not easily venture at night into the more secluded parts of the house, nor did she trust herself alone in the garden after dusk. The same spirit of terror had, as if by heredity, taken up residence in Rosa, the daughter, through her mother's temperament; yet the girl, devoted to prayer, did not avoid solitude and darker places. It happened one evening, as night was falling and growing darker, that Rosa, still delicate, lingered longer in the garden where she had withdrawn to meditate. The mother came to look for her daughter, but with her husband as companion, because (as has been said) she was extremely fearful of nighttime terrors.

Rosa, seeing from a distance both her parents coming toward her—a fact she had confirmed by a certain resemblance—stepped out to meet them. but a thought sent down from heaven seized him as he went, which thenceforth disarmed all fear, taught him confidence, and instilled security. She said to herself: "My mother comes through the garden, and does not, as usual, hurry; she is already fearless in this alone, because she has her husband present at her side." and I, who am constantly and everywhere fortified by the presence of my Spouse; Will I, who carry such a faithful, caring, and brave companion not at my side but in my heart, ever again be afraid of the night? She trusted in a mortal man, and learned to set aside her usual fears for the time being for his fragile assistance. And confidence in God my Savior will not likewise confirm me, so that I will no longer be afraid of anything shadowy. This robust thought had taken such deep root in the maiden's mind, and so completely dispelled her innate propensity for fear, that from that moment on, Rosa marveled at herself, for she feared no encounter, by night or day, at home or abroad, with any danger, monster, or beast, emboldened by the sole protection of her present Spouse.

[332] [as if twice in manifest peril of life] Nor was there lacking an occasion to demonstrate that heroic steadfastness of the virgin's breast amid arduous and sudden dangers. She was living in a certain Indian village with her parents, the twelve-year-old daughter, when one day, as she was returning home with her mother and brothers, she saw in a clearing a most ferocious bull, which, having escaped from the stable and with its ties partly broken and partly torn away, was rushing at full speed toward the very spot where she was with her companions, Rosa. Struck suddenly with terror, the mother with her children looked around for a way to escape. Rosa alone stood firm with unshaken step, and urged her mother not to provoke the already too-near danger by fleeing, promising together that the immense beast would pass by harmlessly. Rosa had not yet finished speaking (her eyes fixed on the sky) when a fierce, muscular beast, rushing past with a terrifying roar like a whirlwind, charged off at a distance at other people, as if it had not even seen Rosa or her unarmed companion. The other's panting hearts still fluttered even after the danger; But Rosa, her color not having changed in the slightest, warned that one should place a firmer hope in the help of the Most High, especially then, when the proximity of danger had cut off all escape.

[333] [She would by no means be flustered,] On another occasion, she was returning in a chariot from the festive station of a celebrated church in Lima, accompanied by her mother and the most honorable matrons. One had to pass through the city's largest forum, where, then, a fear-stricken, clamorous crowd had made way for a raging bull, and with confused shouting and the hurling of stones, they more fiercely provoked the furious beast for the sake of the spectacle's enjoyment. With a forward rush and a winding course to right and left, the menacing beast hurried on, its tail rigidly erect, mouth frothing, with a horrid bellow, nostrils smoking with rage, flinging sand into the air with its hooves, and whenever it struck a log, a rag, or a cap eagerly thrown in its path, it drove them into the ground with the cruel and repeated force of its horns. At last the bull had already filled everything with terror, when, spotting a chariot approaching from a distance, it charged straight at it. The women who were with Rosa, pale with fear, rushed outside as fast as they could, placing their hope of salvation in flight. The charioteer himself was anxiously looking around, seeking a safer spot to descend to the ground and set himself on his feet. Rosa, alone again, with eyes cast up to heaven and immediately brought back down, dispelled the trembling fear, halting the flight as if it were by no means necessary, affirming that the bull would by no means rush toward the chariot, but would turn back the way it had come, She had spoken: and the outcome responded, to the astonishment of all, that the bull's swift return without harm was no more wondrous than the Virgin's spirit, superior to any fear. But she herself, supremely secure amid the greatest dangers, sang to her heavenly Spouse: "I will not fear any evil, for you are with me."

[334] Who had such confidence in divine protection amid life's dangers, [and in having bread at home,] that he could not be wanting in the necessary means of life. One day, the bread ran out for the numerous family in the father's house, and there was neither time to bake nor money on hand to procure it elsewhere. It was reported to Rosa that the larders were empty, and that not even a crust remained from the last baking. The virgin was amazed that the provisions they had laid out the day before could have been consumed so quickly, provisions that would surely have lasted for two days. But now, made certain of the scarcity and undaunted, she seemed with a brief movement of her lips to commend the matter to God. He soon opened the bread chest with great confidence and found it full of loaves—unusually white, unleavened, and flavorful for that household—as evidenced by their shape, which was entirely unfamiliar and exotic there, proving they could in no way have come from the household baker.

[335] At another time in the same house (a most necessary provision in those regions) there was a shortage of honey, [a great quantity of honey,] and its barrel was drying out, having been emptied to the bottom. Maria de Oliva, whether ignorant or forgetful of the scarcity, sent three times into the cage those who were to bring honey from the jar known to everyone. They each reported that they had found the pot empty. Compatient with the common need for so many heads, and by a most secret instinct commanded to hope for the best in God's providence, she said to her mother, "If you command, I will go down in the name of the Lord to see if I can find any honey left." He descended and found the honeycomb itself full of fresh honey up to the very top. The entire household stood amazed at the truly wondrous spectacle, and all the more so because, by a new miracle, the honey that had been given from heaven lasted the whole family for eight full months for their daily use. Has anyone ever seen the duties of bees be supplanted by roses?

Gaspar Flores, the Virgin's father, was afflicted by a troublesome bodily illness, [and would miraculously obtain the necessary money,] while at the same time his spirit withered under the sorrow of a fifty-pound debt, which, because of his poverty, he had no means to pay. The perplexed mother was grieving. Thus, from the creditor's importunity on one hand, and the needs of the household on the other, and especially of the sick husband, she was gradually becoming inadequate. When the daughter learned of both their distress, she tried with the key she was accustomed to use to open the opulent treasury of the eternal Provider's confidence in God. And scarcely had she poured out prayers in the temple when a man, entirely unknown to her, appeared as she was returning home, his countenance modestly handsome and his voice courteous. Greeting the Virgin in passing, he at the same time offered a silver coin wrapped in a small cloth, ordering that it be used to relieve the present need of the parents. Without further delay, he left at once. It seemed from the weight and the very slight bulge of the wrapper that there was little metal in the handkerchief: But when Rosa unwrapped it at home, she found the very fifty silver pounds her father owed, and going to the sick man's bed with the money, she repeated again and again that one must trust greatly in divine goodness, which had just come to the rescue by the most opportune means, enough to remove the hard creditor's trouble. Gaspari's nightmares seemed to be coming true until the daughter, having unfolded her handkerchief, promptly counted out the fifty pounds she had received.

[337] The house was repeatedly refreshed by such foreign and unexpected aid, [and the poor Virgin hoped,] lest the holy Daughter's steadfast and robust confidence in God be defrauded. This, indeed, the heavenly Stonecutter had promised to his former young bride among the paraphernalia and antidora, as mentioned above in the chapter. IX) so much so that Gundisalvi the quaestor's wife, who knew the Virgin's secrets better than anyone else, turned the matter into a familiar proverb, saying: "Has the empyrean, at your bidding, once again rattled your Lapicida's little casket?" Indeed, Rose's confidence in this exceedingly wealthy sponsor was such that, with a generous spirit, the very poor virgin frequently and openly accepted from him the provision of the entire cost for erecting from the foundations a new monastery of Saint Catherine of Siena, provided that in her lifetime she obtained the only thing then lacking—a royal permission diploma. But since we have come to mention this, the things that were divinely promised and shown to the Virgin concerning the founding of that monastery must be told separately in the next chapter. Here, briefly, what suffices for the present chapter remains, and I append and conclude it in the coronis.

[338] [that she could found a new monastery.] At home, Rosa spoke with the devout virgins with remarkable and earnest confidence about the founding of the aforementioned monastery; But the mother, with unfeigned disgust, rebuked her daughter before everyone, as if she were clearly delirious with impossible dreams: "Stop your foolish nonsense," she said, "remember you are not of common birth, nor is it a matter of ordinary patrimony to found monasteries." But you speak with such confidence, as if you already had two hundred thousand pounds of silver on hand for the lavish expenses of such a grand undertaking. The modest Daughter humbly replied: "Indeed, if I were to rely on mortal aid, if I were to look to human hands, I would freely admit that what I undertake is impossible." But know, mother, that I have placed my trust higher. I have him as a surety for all the treasures. I can no longer doubt this generosity. These eyes of yours will see success. You will rejoice; indeed, you will prove for yourself what I say.

Notes.

a Greek periphrasis, the same as the Latin circumlocutio, which had to be used with this examiner, since the holy Virgin did not understand scholastic terms.

b Amphibolon, Greek amphibolon, here it means an ambiguous speech, from the adverb ἀμφὶ on both sides, and the verb βάλλω to throw or strike, as if striking on both sides.

c Ex crasi, that is, by maternal temperament or disposition, as I explained this Greek word above.

Paraphernalia, or parapherna, are in law those things which a wife holds as her own, from *pará* (beside) and *phérne* (dowry), as if one were to say, "things beside the dowry."

Antidora, Greekἀντίδωρα,are returns, which are given in return for a gift made to someone, from ἀντί against, & δῶρον gift, as if a counter-gift, or a gift in return for a gift.

Chapter 25
A prophecy about the future convent of St. Catherine of Siena, which was later founded in Lima.

[The monastery of Lima is described,] Among the more illustrious ornaments of the port of Lima, the noble, spacious, and elegant monastery of the blessed Catherine of Siena was finally added, dedicated to a numerous college of holy virgins under the regular institute of St. Dominic. In the year of our Lord 1622 (that is, five years after the blessed Rose's passing to heaven), by the illustrious and wealthy widow, Mrs. Lucia Guerra de la Daga, this famous convent was established in Lima in a most convenient location, and with the introduction of a novice of the white veil, divine services began to be celebrated. The newly founded convent has grown by sudden and prodigious increases, so that today it numbers over two hundred nuns, who, with God as their spouse, serve under the inviolable religious enclosure in strict custody. In this chaste sanctuary of piety, the daily and nightly worship of God flourishes, the devout pomp of feasts, the canonical order of the Offices, the unceasing psalmody of the virgin choir, the meticulous splendor of the sacred furnishings and church, and the abundant magnificence of the apparatus.

[340] Nothing in the external adornment is at odds with the inner beauty of exact observance: [which was begun after the death of St. Rose,] the assiduity of prayers, mortifications, and exercises, the pursuit of perfection, the uniform purity and sanctity of morals, the strict discipline, and throughout every corner living and truly angelic examples of all virtues. Moreover, the august and noblest fabric responds fittingly and appropriately to the company of so many celebrated and illustrious heroines with its spaciousness, its fountains, gardens, vineyards, porticoes, dormitories, peristyles, and workshops. Likewise, with possessions, latifundia, services, annual revenues, and the most fully developed economy of abundance, so that this illustrious monastery, within the first forty years, perpetually irrigated by so many heavenly blessings, by example, fame, and splendor has advanced to such a degree that it can be equated with the grandest and most principal monasteries of all Europe.

Finally, this is what, for almost a full decade before the foundation, was so often divinely foreshown to Rose—[and whose construction the Holy Virgin herself,] now by symbols and figures, now manifestly in its own form and type. This is what the Virgin so securely and confidently, so often and openly, so gladly and freely promised to her sweet homeland by prophesying, with such firmness and constancy of assertion, as if she already had it built before her eyes. The Virgin was hidden from the time and year in which it was to be built for a long time, and learned too late that she would not see it with her physical eyes; He then pointed out with his finger several others who were to be there to serve God (among them his own mother), and he set the number. He predicted the place. He drew the plant and system of the future factory there on a tablet. First, he explicitly named the initiate of the bloodless Sacrifice: what more? He recognized the first future abbess (whom we call the prioress) by her face, imbued her with his spirit, and in a manner initiated her with the kiss of peace. More precisely, each item must be gone through in order.

[342] [Through frequent visions] The prophet, having long since experienced them, warned that insight is needed in visions, Daniel. Ten. However, in this matter under discussion, it will become clear from the following that Rosa did not lack the ability to grasp the genuine meaning of the symbolic visions. A solitary virgin sat in the home garden, intent on gathering flowers, and had already filled her lap with freshly picked roses, when, raising her eyes to heaven and moved by a secret inner spirit, she began, with devout sighs, to toss each rose high into the air, as if commending them to a heavenly gardener. The Virgin's brother arrived, and, thinking his sister was amusing herself with an innocent game and that there was nothing serious about it, he approached familiarly and asked what she was doing. Hiding the mystery, she said, "This, (she said) that you see me doing"; And he went on, as before, to toss roses high into the air. Germanus, suspecting nothing more than a simple jest and pastime, replied, "I too will scatter roses into the air, and perhaps I will throw them higher than you."

[343] [& various revelations] The Virgin was startled, but prodigies began to speak: for behold, those which her brother had thrown upward, by the slightest fall, were soon rolling back to the ground; On the other hand, the roses that had been scattered aloft hung in the air, fittingly coming together there into a conspicuous figure of the holy cross, and a decorative border was formed around that same cross by the roses that followed. The brother here lacked an understanding of the mystery, but Rose did not. When later questioned by those aware of the prodigy as to what she thought this aerial rosary pendant portended, she replied with unflinching candor that it was a foretaste of the future, distinguished monastery of Saint Catherine of Siena among her fellow Limeños. that in this place the most numerous roses of outstanding holiness may strive to settle, so that, fitted into the cruciform rule of a stricter life, they may look down upon the earth from the lofty height of perfection, and that this world may be crucified to them, and they to the world.

[344] [Divinely instructed] On another occasion, while praying, Rose was divinely shown a meadow of marvelous beauty, variegated on all sides by the whiteness of lilies and the purple of roses (though scattered and without order). The Virgin delighted in the twofold beauty and charm of so many budding plants, especially when she received the reply that all of them, one by one, were being reserved for the crown of the one Beloved, fair and ruddy. But it was displeasing that the spacious and noble meadow was open to any traveler. Rather, the common and public passage there, frequented by both beasts and men traveling in every direction, was used by all; and so he was seriously afraid that the so-beautiful little flowers, the earth's most exquisite gems, would be thoughtlessly trampled by the feet of passersby. But at the same time, a heavenly radiance dispelled the fear, instructing Rosa that the time would come when that chosen germ of the flourishing stock would be gathered into one enclosed garden of St. Catherine of Siena, and separated by its own fence from the commerce of the profane world. that the roses and lilies signify the pure and God-beloved souls of the virgins of Lima, who indeed dwelt scattered here and there throughout the city without order, and were to be gathered in due time into the cloistered heaven, where they will be safest from all the wear and tear of the secular world, and will breathe for the eternal Deity alone the fragrance of sweetness, until, translated from there into the Paradise of the Spouse, they are bound to Him in an unfading crown of glory. While Rosa hungrily absorbed and listened to the mysteries of these heavenly decrees, she meanwhile seemed to be gradually weaving this flourishing garland with her own hands and placing it upon the Bridegroom, who in turn showed that it was most pleasing and deeply dear to him. This vision filled Rosa with immense consolation, for she understood by it, especially in regard to her own labor and merit, that God was granting that Lima, at some future time, would be adorned with such an astery of holy virgins as a precious jewel. And whenever she presented this sweet spectacle to her own people, she bore witness to the splendid blossoms in that monastery—the devoted handmaidens of God who gloriously excelled in the fragrance of virtue and the renown of holiness.

Father Master Brother Louis of Bilbao, the Virgin's confessor, [firmly to his own confessor] although he gave the utmost deference to her other sayings, nevertheless more scrupulously doubted this same prophecy of hers, because, looking to human things, he could not even from a distance see the slightest semblance of probability that would promise any success whatsoever for so great an undertaking. Rather, every consideration and the authority of the government everywhere seemed inexorably opposed, especially since, given the times, there were already more than enough monasteries in the new city, which then had not yet completed its first century and was still a long way from the vast extent it now boasts. Rosa noticed that her father was uncertain about the outcome and, with great confidence, addressed him thus: "What are you hesitating about, my father?" While you are alive and present, that which I have often said will stand in this city, B. The Monastery of Catherine of Siena. Suppose, for whatever reason you wish, that the commands of those who forbid are obstructed. Add to these, in a manner of speaking, all of our America, or rather the whole world. Add, by hypothesis, the forces, arts, and power of the entire underworld; Finally, imagine whatever obstacles you can conceive of. However, with you alive and as an eyewitness, the monastery that I mentioned will be built, inhabited, and flourish. I have marked the place for you; he will be there. And indeed God has chosen you by His eternal decree, you who will first offer the most sacred Host there in solemnity: for the first stone of the church to be built there will be laid in the foundations with the most solemn rite. Then you will remember that these things were just now foretold to you by a lowly little woman. Everything happened just as the Virgin had foretold: for in the year 1622, as Louis was about to assume that office, during the solemn Mass he remembered the prophecy, and after the Sacrifice was completed, he publicly explained to those present how confidently, clearly, and eloquently the surviving Rose had, many years before, predicted what she had done that day and in that place.

[346] At one time, in the presence of his household during familiar conversations, [and to many others he had foretold,] while B. The glorious prerogatives and just encomiums of Catherine of Siena were being discussed, and gradually the conversation turned to the future monastery under her patronage. Here the Virgin first ingenuously confessed and protested that she would by no means behold it with mortal eyes. However, the others who were there would certainly enjoy his gaze, presence, and comfort. The matter was met with laughter by all. Nor were there lacking those who thought that Rosa, from her excessive devotion to the seraphic mother, was insane. especially after he, who had undertaken to solicit from Madrid the royal faculties necessary for the new foundation, returned to Lima empty-handed, not even bringing with him the hope of ever obtaining permission. These would have terrified any other, or at least robbed them of the confidence to speak. But the very firmest certainty of the heavenly revelation did not permit Rosa to be either terrified or to fall silent in a timid manner, so much so that, since she could not adequately prove it with words, she attempted to more clearly outline it with the architectural marks inscribed on the tablet. So, seizing a tablet and wax before the unbelievers, he improvised an extemporaneous archetype of the future monastery, with such skill and confidence that he left the onlookers in astonishment. He was sketching out the outline of the walls with a few strokes, which, at the outset of the future monastery, was to encompass less space. He noted the corners, the bends, the boundaries. where the temple, and in what location the gate, workshops, portico, and dormitories were to be arranged. She grieved for nothing else, but that the others did not, as she did, have the entire image of the monastery present in living color.

[347] [& forewarned his unbelieving mother;] Above all others, Rosa's mother was indignant, and could not bear with a calm mind the frequent and serious mention, both at home and abroad, of the new convent, which her Daughter so assertively and fearlessly presented to friends and strangers alike. There appeared to be no manager for such a difficult undertaking, no founder, no supporter or persuader, nowhere an augur or patron. to abhor all, as if it were a humanly impossible thing; To judge the Virgin as either a fool for chimeras or one who stubbornly indulges her affections. Doctor John de Castillo himself (whom Rosa held in such high esteem for his renowned piety) was trying to dissuade the Virgin from this conviction, believing the difficulties to be utterly insurmountable. The same was the judgment of the quæstor Gundisalvus and of the confessors. Hence the anxious mother, fearing disgrace for herself and her family, rebuked her daughter. that it would want to be wiser than is fitting, against the judgment of so many prudent people, by relying on its own wisdom alone. it should not be stubbornly resisted, according to the unanimous opinion of all experts; That Rosam, like a vain prophetess, will soon be a disgrace and a public spectacle. Finally, that these are to be obeyed for the sake of reason, not out of stubbornness. The promised monastery is more quickly in Utopia or imaginary spaces; to be established in Lima.

[348] [that she was to be enrolled there among the nuns;] But Rosa, all the more eager for so many contradictions, replied in a humble but joyful voice: "Come, most sweet mother, let the battles of words cease here at last; you, for your great benefit, will experience how hard it is to kick against the pricks: in the monastery of Saint Catherine of Lima, which I foretell, you yourself will be among the first to don the pure habit of the nuns; "There you will be veiled, there you will solemnly profess, there, as a Religious, you will consummate the rest of your life." Upon hearing this, the mother, thinking herself insulted, was so enraged that she drove her daughter from her sight, crying out loudly, "Shall I become a nun?" I, to whom no thought of a regular life or state has ever found access, I, lacking the necessary gift, ignorant of singing and psalming, nurtured in worldly cares, empty of spirit, impatient of enclosure, burdened with many things—should I enter a cloister? In this age, shall I embrace and profess a strict, narrow, and difficult religion? Away, enough of nonsense, we will see these things fulfilled on Greek Calends.

[349] [as happened in the year of Christ 1629.] The beginning of the year 1629 did not see the Greek Kalendsc, she did, however, see the nun Mary of the Olive in the new monastery of St. Catherine of Siena, where, after her husband's death, the sixty-year-old had most devoutly joined the religious daughters of St. Dominic, and, with the sacred habit of the Order, took the name of Sister Mary of St. Mary. After completing her year of probation, she made her solemn vows and, having lived to a ripe old age, rested in holy death. The poor widow was deprived of her legitimate dowry, which had been fixed at four thousand pounds of silver for each of her weddings there. Indeed, so that poverty would not stand in the way of Mary, the founder, inspired by the care of Providence, had provided in the very program of the foundation that certain places for the free reception of worthy but needy persons be reserved. And Rosæ's mother enjoyed that privilege.

[350] There remains of the very founder of the convent, and its first prefect, the memorable and lucid prophecy of Rose. Mariana, a maid in the household of the illustrious matron Isabella of Mexico, was named Rosa; when she fell gravely ill, Rosa (who, as was almost a member of the household, was in her own way a domestic) ministered to her out of charity in her customary manner. On that occasion, respectable women frequently came to visit both Isabella and the sick woman. On a certain day, among others, was present the most noble Lady Lucia Guerra de la Daga, who was no less distinguished for her virtue than for her lineage and wealth. She saw Rosa (of whom she had heard) there, piously occupied in the service of the infirm Mariana, and began to feel a more devoted affection for her. Seizing the opportunity to speak, and after the mutual courtesies of urban courtesy had been exchanged, she earnestly begged the Virgin to remember her in her daily prayers to God and in her holy exercises.

[351] Rosa, already sensing she was being warned from within, bound the matron with a reciprocal affection, [on the pretext that she had visited St. Rose,] offering what little she could. And with greater insistence, she promised that she would select a specific day each week on which she would commend herself to God.
