Rich with this comfort, Lucia, upon returning home, could not rest until she met Rosa, who was now truly her own. She came under the pretext of recommending her husband and little daughter; for they perhaps then stood in greater need of the Virgin's intercession with God. Rosa, as if she had looked more deeply into Lucia's heart, answered her more familiarly than the day before, saying nothing about her husband, nor about her little daughter or sons. But he drew the matron into a new benefit for himself, and at last admitted her, not to the profit of a single day for a few weeks, but fully into the friendly communion and participation of all his exercises, stimulated by a secret impulse of God.

[352] She herself did not yet fully understand where or why she was acting as she did, [she felt herself stirred to found a monastery,] until, contrary to her habit, she fixed her eyes intently from the ground upon Lucia's face and began to exult in spirit, to flush with an unusual joy, to palpitate with warm affections, and at last, with the most modest cheerfulness, kissed Lucia, "Well done, (she said) mother, God has reserved you for his great work." And, repeatedly echoing it with overflowing joy, he at the same time, with a fitting kiss, stunned her and, without the slightest resistance, won her over. It cannot be said how many spirits the fortunate matron soon drew from the Virgin's spirit; she shuddered, and felt herself being changed to the very marrow by Rose's words and breath. Unknown little flames were sweetly creeping through all the veins of her soul. His heart, suddenly expanded in various deliberations, was lifted entirely to heavenly things, as if, with a quicker pulse, crying out: "Lord, what do you want me to do?" Thus affected, she returned home, speaking to God along the way in this way: "Lord, if it is according to your eternal will, and for the glory of your name, that I should at some time pass from this secular to a religious state, go on working in me what you have so wonderfully begun;" with all the affection of my heart, I offer myself to you, together with my children and all my substance; It will be your task to remove the obstacles, in which you know me so ineluctably entangled.

[353] [even though her husband and numerous children were still alive.] From that moment, a kind of connected procession immigrated into the woman's mind: the effective desire for a religious state, the most vivid hope, and the indubitable certainty of reaching this goal, of evading the snares, of breaking through countless obstacles that only a divine hand could remove. Lucia had not yet passed her thirtieth year. A husband was left, who, it seemed, was to live for many years. The mother was surrounded by numerous offspring, namely four sons and one daughter. She was pressured by a vast economy of great wealth, yet these could neither shake nor diminish the certainty of her religious vocation in Lucia's mind. So secure was she in her dealings with her own people about the execution of her holier purpose, as if, having arranged everything, she were awaiting the veil to be placed upon her at the appointed time.

[354] [Then, regarding his confessor, whom Rosa had obscurely forewarned,] Meanwhile, R. P. John de Villalobos, who at that time was the Rector of the Society of Jesus' novitiate in Lima, to the house of D. to turn aside to the home of D. Isabella de Mexia, where, in a conversation with Rose, after various arguments had been exchanged on both sides about the certainty of the future monastery of St. Catherine of Siena, she learned from the Virgin that she had spoken there the day before with Lucia de la Daga, who was indeed young in years, but mature in character; He says he now doesn't want to say more about it. The religious and prudent man understood to what Rosa was alluding, both by what she said and by what she left unsaid. She remained silent, however, until after Rose's death, when Lucia would approach to purify her soul through the sacramental confession.

[355] [This noble matron reveals her plan to her confessor,] Here the matron disclosed to her confessor that she was contemplating a great and arduous undertaking: the foundation of a new monastery under the patronage and in the name of St. Catherine of Siena. to be driven by divine impulses, to which one must entirely obey; She will turn all her strength, resources, and thoughts to this. In turn, the confessor, at that moment recalling the words he had long ago heard from Rosa, informed Lucia that this was precisely what the Virgin had promised the matron when she had told her that she herself was being reserved by God for some great work. Therefore, he would not distrust the prophecy of such a Virgin. that a task so arduous would not be without the strongest obstacles, delays, and difficulties; nevertheless, in the very height of the storm's fury and motion, it would be most peacefully hoped for that help would come from above, which the Rose, already co-reigning with the immortal Spouse, would most easily obtain.

[356] [and after the death of her husband and children, she carried out the plan.] So it was: for God, shortly thereafter, called Lucia's husband and all her offspring from this light in due order, removed countless and most powerful obstacles to the new foundation from every quarter, and bent the hard hearts of the dissenters to the favor of the pious work. Lucy was left with the most distinguished of relatives, who, considering her rank, age, and wealth, urged the widow so insistently toward a second marriage that they nearly forced her. But she herself, mindful of Rose's prophecy and steadfast in her resolve, resisted, conquered, and triumphed, and with things happily brought to a successful conclusion according to her vow, indeed beyond her vow, devoting her vast resources to founding, building, and endowing a new monastery of St. Catherine of Siena, she finally gave herself to it, and, taking the sacred veil and the name of Sister Lucia of the Most Holy Trinity, she served as the first prioress of her new convent, and by exhortation, care, and example, wonderfully spread the spirit of religion to the nuns under her charge, and finally, celebrated for her sanctity, she ended her life there. Such was the success of Rosa's prediction. Hence it happened that the monastery of St. Catherine of Siena in Lima, by chance, was most often called the Monastery of the Rose by the aware populace (this having been foretold long before by the prophecy of the Rose).

Notes.

Utopia is a fictitious name for a region, which certain writers use when they want to signify a place that is nowhere or will be nowhere. ab ou, not, & topos, place.

b Ad Calendas Græcas is used by proverb for never, because the Greeks do not have Calends in the Latin manner.

The biographer clearly states with this phrase that at the beginning of the year 1629, the prophecy of St. Rose was fulfilled or proven by events.

The Spanish word is *ichnographia* for the Latin *vox Hispanica est Latinis*.

Chapter 26
Predictions about various future and secret things, the truth of which has been proven by events.

[Holy to many persons] The same (about which a little earlier) R. P. Villalobos of the Society of Jesus testified under oath that he had discerned the prophetic spirit in the Virgin through his own experience. He had asked Rosa to pray for the successful outcome of a secret but arduous affair, and, so that the others would remain silent, he himself was the first to keep quiet. But Rosa (which was not her custom) fixed her eyes for a moment on the speaker's face, as if she had read the secret there in an open book, and smiling modestly, she replied to the Father in such a way that he gathered, not without surprise, that at that moment the Virgin had been divinely made aware of the entire sequence of the most secret affair. whereupon, stunned with amazement, he reported the matter to Father Anthony de la Vega Loarsa, a most upright man of the same Society; who R. He publicly asserted that something very similar to what happened to the Virgin had happened to Father Philip de Tapia, rector of the Callao College.

[358] The daughter of the quaestor Gundisalvus, Michaela de la Massa, was entertaining a certain secret thought in her mind, [the secret thoughts of her heart], which she had not shared with any mortal, nor had she resolved to share. Rosa familiarly applied herself to this, and accurately recited the whole of that thought as if from little notebooks, and prescribed to the astonished man the aphorisms of the most salutary document. Maria de Mesta, wife of the painter Medor Angelini, to confer with her husband alone, with no one else aware, about both of their journey to Spain, and about the necessary amount of money to support a life there honorably and comfortably. Then she went to Rosa to talk about other matters that were pressing on her at the time. The maiden, her speech casually diverted to that which was thought to be hidden, responded as if she had been asked about it as well. He approved the plan to set out, mentioned the sum of money that the woman and her husband had agreed upon as a joint provision for the rest of their lives, and said that this would be enough for both of them, since they had no children. The woman was amazed, admitting that nothing more could have been said by the Virgin, had she herself sat as a third party to their secret counsel.

[359] It seemed far more miraculous to Fr. John Miguel, a religious of the Order of St. Dominic, when, having returned to Lima from a long pilgrimage, and in D. While speaking privately with the Virgin in the chapel of St. Jerome, he heard from her in order, [and she revealed other secret matters,] and with exactness, the things that had happened to the brother far away in a foreign land, which by human means could not have come to the knowledge of the Rose; He clearly recognized that even the most hidden recesses of his own conscience had been laid bare to the Virgin. In the church of Saint Dominic, Rosa, with her mother and other honorable women, had paused to commend to God by prayer the final agony of Mary of Vera, who, lying ill in the house of Diego de Requena the argentine, awaited the last hour of her life. Sad news arrived: the sick woman has died. The terrified women looked back at Rosa with sorrow. this to heaven; But suddenly, turning her eyes to those around her, she said, "Let us not weep, for our friend is not dead; Mary of Verona is alive. Let us only pray to God that she may recover." He lived and recovered, but by another miracle of Rose, about which in the Appendix.

[360] [& to one who thought he would die in the year 1615;] A certain religious of the Society of Jesus, a man truly apostolic and adorned with the highest virtues, had (it is not known from where) a firm, tenacious, and immovable conviction that he would most certainly depart this life in that year (which was MDCXLV). He truly desired to be dissolved and be with Christ; so, seizing the opportunity, while speaking once to his wife, the quaestor's wife, with Rosa present, he humbly and earnestly commended his passing from this life, as if it were to occur without a doubt before the Kalends of the coming year. Gundisalvi's wife shuddered. But Rosa, smiling gently, said, "By no means, my father, will this year take you from us." So I safely promise you. He, on the other hand, contended that the matter was most certain, and that nothing could happen to him more fortunate than if, shortly after the sacrifice of the Mass had been celebrated, he were commanded to return his spirit to its Creator. To ardently desire, indeed to hope for, that one thing. A notion gradually gained ground among the most devoted father of his household that, when moving from the college to the novitiate, or the house of novices, he should bid farewell not only to his companions but also to the very trees, walls, and roofs, as if he would never revisit his former home.

[361] [He commands a longer life to be promised;] These things tormented the pious wife of Gundisalvus, who grieved only to lose so prematurely the guide of her conscience. Therefore, looking anxiously at Rosa every day, she asked a thousand times whether the things she had predicted about her confessor outliving her were true. The virgin repeated it a thousand times in turn and told the matron to be safe. Nevertheless, she was anxious whenever she attended her confessor's Mass, fearing that he, having satisfied his desire, would depart this life as soon as the Sacrifice was over. Finally, on the most sacred vigil of the divine Nativity, while a matron was approaching the father to confess, Rosa had him signaled through her to lay aside at last the most vain persuasion of his neighbor's death, and that she should know she was reserved for greater services of God, that she would not die until she had brought several souls outside to the way of salvation through her laborious conversion, and that among them five in particular stood out, worthy of her apostolate and to be won over to the true God. This, Rosa, the hundred-times timid wife of the questioner, had sung to console herself. This very outcome fully proved it: for the father also survived Rosa by nine years, and in the conversion of lost people, he reported the fruits that she had foretold, which were marvelous. Indeed, he was the first of the men of his order to go to the mountains, which today are called Santa Crux de la Sierraa, where he delivered many souls from the devil, and finally rested in Lima in the year 1626 in holy death.

Father Fr. Bartholomew Martinez of the Order of Preachers, prior of the convent of Saint Mary Magdalene in Lima and confessor to Rosa, was waging a doubtful battle with a very severe and extremely dangerous illness, and now, with the care of his body almost abandoned, the final remedies for the departing soul were being hastened at the urging of the doctors. He visited the sick P. M. Father John of Lorenzana, moved by a sudden instinct of confidence, told the sick man to hope for the best, for at that moment he had seen in the church, before the tabernacle of the Blessed Sacrament, a bright candle burning for the patient's health—Rosam, whom he had observed from afar there pouring out the most ardent prayers. He had scarcely finished speaking when the sacristan, Father John Fernandez, arrived and said that he had been sent by Rosa, who was kneeling in the church before the Blessed Sacrament, to assure Bartholomew that, despite his illness and all his lamentations, he was by no means near death. He would therefore be of good cheer, his health returning more quickly than could be hoped, because he must first zealously serve the divine glory in a certain unique ministry. Bartholomew, to whom the Virgin's soul had been intimately revealed, without hesitation, believed the words, and at that very hour cast off fear and sickness.

Master F. Louis of Bilbao, likewise the Virgin's confessor for fourteen years, [who had been suffering from a dangerous illness,] once the fevers he had believed to be extinguished were laid to rest, had ceased to fear for himself: when the disease returned as if from ambush, far more violent than before, and the relapse brought him to the brink of death. The doctors were already perplexed and had stopped administering medicines. The patient, already endowed by nature with a sonorous and clear voice, was so obstructed by a constricted chest and enfeebled on both sides that he could barely utter words that could be understood. Nevertheless, with all the gentleness of her voice and by her nods, she signaled to Rosa her own peril, and commanded her to tell her most freely what she herself felt about the course and outcome of this illness, so that, if she were to die, she might at once prepare herself for the moment from which eternity depends. He implored the Virgin not to abandon him in this perilous situation, or to conceal anything that would be conducive to his salvation. This, if ever, is the time to speak the truth, and now above all she expects from him the sacred observances of filial affection.

[364] The virgin, though she grieved with her father in spirit over his anxieties, [she promises his former health,] nevertheless, not frightened by any mention of danger, replied briefly, but with a cheerful countenance: "To prepare oneself by every effort for that serious moment of the last breath, with care and concern, is never untimely, but always praiseworthy and salutary. Nevertheless, this illness is not unto death; the sick man will recover, and at the next triumphal feast of the Most Holy Rosary he will eloquently speak from the pulpit before the entire congregation. "In the meantime, I will send him my physician; let him keep him constantly by the head of the bed, within sight, and not despair of his recovery." She then sent her father—whom she dearly loved—a small, neatly dressed icon of the infant Jesus, her "physician," as she called him, which she later retrieved from the convalescent, saying that without it she found herself far too lonely. Louis believed Rose's prophecy, and he devoutly honored the doctor. He recovered his strength with the utmost integrity, to the admiration of all. Only one thing remained, and he was most perplexed about it, namely, not seeing how he could possibly be assigned the duty of preaching at the upcoming solemnity of the Most Holy Rosary, since for that year those duties did not belong to him but to his Provincial, M. F. Gabriel of Zarate: for to him, by lot (as is the custom there), the burden of speaking had fallen one month before this. Nevertheless, this too had to be fulfilled in Ludovicus: for the Provincial, having been struck by illness shortly before the feast, could not preach, and, unaware of the prophecy, entrusted the sermon to Ludovicus.

[365] [& he claims to have cured a novice of epilepsy.] In Lima, at the convent of Saint Dominic, John de Soto had received the religious habit; but he had kept silent about the epilepsy from which he suffered. In the probationary year, when fraud was discovered, Abbot Alphonsus Velasquez, acting secretly on the council of the fathers, decided to honorably send the useless novice back to the secular world. The day of execution had already been decreed, and the novice master, Peter of Loaysa, was ordered to dismiss him. Nor could anything of these have been known to Rosa by human means. The pious Virgin was present at first light, and through the Friar. He asked Blasius Martinez, the sacristan, together with the Prior, to come down to the temple to him, before he carried out the conceived work. Both came, amazed, wondering what the Virgin could want at such an unusual and premature hour. She replied that she had come to intercede for the novice who was about to leave, and at the same time asked that the decree of expulsion be rescinded. One of the fathers, scoffing at this, replied: "You don't know, Rosa, how badly you're misapplying this benefit of intercession." But know that the young man for whom you ask is completely unfit for the profession to which he is to be sent. The most gentle Rose bore this harsh rebuff with equanimity, yet she added, "It will appear that the decree of the Most High is different, and stronger than yours: for that novice will duly attain to solemn profession, and will notably illustrate the Order by his example of piety and religion." Everything happened just as the Virgin had predicted.

[366] [Assigned to certain virgins] Three sisters, Philippa, Catherine, and Francisca de Montoya, were instructed in piety through the example and conversation of Rose. Of these, Philippa and Catherine desired, having spurned the world, to embrace the religious habit of Saint Dominic and the institute of the Third Order. Francisca alone clung to the world a little more tenaciously, and with all her heart abhorred the divorce from the world. He disliked the religious garb, its shabby simplicity. He delighted in the ostentatious display of more polished grooming and was held by a feminine love of charming hair. Rosa would sometimes reproach Francisca for her excessive zeal in curling her hair, saying, "I will see these very hairs of yours, which you now cherish with such affection, cut off someday, so don't you doubt it." Finally, he more clearly foretold that Francisca (though then reluctant) would, together with her sister Catherine, become a nun of the Third Order of Saint Dominic, while Philippa, who at that time was aflame with desire for the same institute, would eventually consent to the honorable marriages offered to her. Success confirmed the prophecy: for Philippa's ardent desire for a religious state, to the astonishment of all, passed over to Francis. She obtained a husband; the other, together with her sister Catherine, received the habit of Saint Dominic: she renounced her hair, her pomp, her worldly life, with ineffable fervor and joy.

[367] [the state of life they would later choose,] Another noble pair of virgins, Mary and Joanna Urtado de Bustamante, although they enjoyed the closest and most intimate friendship with Rosa, thought of nothing less than embracing the religious state. However, one day, while walking in the home garden with them, Rosa, by a sudden and divine impulse, thus addressed them both: "Know, good sisters, that you two, together with your grandmother Ludovica, will become nuns in the monastery we call the Holy Trinity, and I will live to see that day." A third sister, named Francisca, remained with them, who, on the contrary, had so kindled her desire to join the Third Order of Saint Dominic that she could scarcely think or speak of anything else. Finally, after sharing the plan with Rosa, he received her reply, which was later confirmed by events: namely, that she would be neither a nun of St. Dominic nor of any other order, but the wife of a legitimate husband. Thus it happened: for in 1615, that is, two years before Rose's death, two sisters, Mary and Joanna, together with their grandmother, were veiled in the Monastery of the Most Holy Trinity, and the first took the name Mary of the O, the second Joanna of Jesus, and the last took the name Louise of the Cross. Francisca married Jerome de Villalobos.

John de la Raya and Mary Euphemia de Parejas had one son, Roderigo, who was growing up, [and he warned the mother of a certain youth,] whom they had destined from his earliest years to be enrolled in the sacred militia of the Society of Jesus in due course. The boy grew up, but showed no sign of a religious inclination. When he was assigned to the schools, he was seen to shun both the clerical office and any literary pursuits more openly than either parent, whether they publicly desired it or more earnestly urged it. The afflicted mother went to Rosa, who was hiding in the garden shed, laid out her sorrow, and earnestly pleaded for help. Rosa, ever most ready to hasten the consolations of the sorrowful, immediately devoted whatever her prayers to God could avail to Euphemia. Soon, looking up at the heavens for a moment, and after a brief pause in thought, she said, "Doubt not, your Roderick will don the religious habit in just a few months, but not that of the Society of Jesus which you so dearly desired."

These last words wounded Euphemia. whereupon she replied: [what she wanted, was for her son to join our Society,] "Ah!" How deeply my husband will grieve with me when he hears that our son will not be admitted to that Society. The Virgin replied gently and compassionately, "Rather, worship the divine providence that willed these things to be made clear to you in due time, so that you may gradually bring your husband to a just equanimity, lest he vainly constrain the spirit where it longs to breathe." Let the divine will, rather than your own, work in Rodrigo. And as soon as your son has embraced the regular institution, see that you remind me, so that I too may give thanks to the Most High for the benefit with which He has favored us. About three months had passed when Rodericus felt himself seized by a sudden and impetuous desire for the religious life, and he himself, since it had been so agreed, without delay and in earnest spoke with the Fathers about his admission into their illustrious Society. Rosa learned of this, and again said to Euphemia: "You will certainly see your son a religious man." But it is equally certain that he will not be assigned to the Society of Jesus: a rougher tunic of Seraphic Francis awaits him.

Rodrigo was thinking of nothing else at that time. or she wished, with her husband, Euphemia. Rather, since everything had already been discussed, concluded, and prepared with the Society's superiors, only the letter of consent from the Father Provincial was awaited. But (as Providence had ordained), they were delayed, and in the meantime Rodericus secretly went to the monastery of Saint Francis with his parents, requested the habit, obtained admission, and after receiving their counsel and finally winning his parents' consent, he was received by solemn rite into the poor Order of the Friars Minor eight days later. The mother, grieving and anxiously doubtful of the novice's steadfastness—since she feared the delicate boy would scarcely be equal to such austerities—went again to Rosa, anxious, timid, and restless, especially after she had learned that her son was somewhat failing under the rigors of Lent. But the Virgin, gently rebuking the woman's lack of confidence, said, "Do you have so little faith in the help of the Queen of the Most Holy Rosary, to whom I entrusted the novice Roderick?" Know that he will persevere in his order, and suppose that you already see him professed. What's the matter? As soon as Rodericus exchanged his undergarment for a woolen one, he recovered; after completing the probationary year, he took his solemn vows and lived on for a long time as a devout and exemplary priest in the Seraphic Order.

[371] [And the concubine announces legitimate marriage,] Maria Perez, the slave of the noble Spaniard Leonard de Roya, was living secretly with a hero in a deplorable state. At last, deeply troubled and seeking a healthier path for her soul, since she saw no way either to free herself from a dangerous association or, oppressed by her servile condition, to obtain a legitimate marriage as a free woman, she confidently revealed her calamity to Rosa. The compassionate Virgin commanded the handmaid to hope confidently in God's mercy that Leonard would duly take her as his wife. The afflicted woman believed in hope against hope, and by a happy experiment discovered that Rosa had not promised comfort in vain.

[372] [And the return of the fugitive slaves to the woman.] Two Ethiopian handmaids had simultaneously run away from the house of Maria de Mesta, the wife of the painter Medeo Angelini; and one of them, named Antonia, had even carried off her mistress's keys, which were then extremely necessary. This is a virgin's loss: that upon returning home, she was forced either to break into her chest of clothes, whose key had been taken away, or to borrow the necessary garments from elsewhere for the next day. But suddenly Rosa replied, "You will do neither: for when you return to the threshold of your home, before you step down from your seat, you will meet at the door one who will announce that Antonia has voluntarily returned to the house from her flight." the other fugitive, know that she will be brought back to you tomorrow. Mesta departed, and at the threshold of her house, before she could step down from her sedan chair, she met her husband at the gate, announcing that Antonia had returned with the keys. Mesta replied to her husband, "And tomorrow will restore another fugitive to us." Rosa had predicted both contexts, and Mesta saw both fulfilled the next day.

[373] [He predicts that his guest will be involved in a difficult undertaking,] The most illustrious Viceroy of the Kingdom of Peru, Gundisalvus de la Massa, had resolved to send the treasurer far from Lima on an honorable but arduous mission, one that demanded a man of the highest experience, faith, and integrity. He therefore sent to win Gundisalvus's consent the senator of the royal (as they call it) audience and the fiscal procurator, to whom he added his own confessor for the sake of honor and authority. The quaestor, struck by the dignity of the mission and at the same time perceiving the seriousness of the viceregal intent and the difficulty of the undertaking, asked for a delay, chiefly because he was then occupied with the fleet about to set sail for Spain, burdened with the dispatch of countless letters and the accounts of his office, so that he could not be away from home for even a single day: In truth, however, he was using these as a pretext to escape the very dangerous undertaking itself through the delay of a longer stay. After the fleet set sail, the three aforementioned men returned to Gundisalvus, not together but separately, and on several occasions: for the gravity of the matter and the king's orders pressed them.

Finally, on April 15, having been warned as if peremptorily by the man the viceroy had decided to involve, that he was to appear at the palace for a conference with the viceroy at the appointed hour the next day, he readily recognized that no escape from the odious expedition remained. So that evening after supper, pensive and sad, he revealed the anguish of his soul to his wife and to Rosa alone. The wife paled with fear. but by no means Rosa, who, coming from the oratory the next day, addressed the treasurer, who was just about to leave the house and was about to set out for the pro-king, with a cheerful face: "Go," she said, "and do not be afraid; you will return to us from the palace in a happier mood than you left us here." And so that you may not be delayed by superfluous things, be assured. That arduous task you dread will be transferred to another, and you will be completely safe.

To the quætor's wife, who was hesitating, he most steadfastly insisted on the same thing twice or three times, [that it was not to be applied with certainty,] adding: "Nor if you see both of a husband's feet planted in the stirrup, or him already riding down the street, would you believe he was going to set out." He will remain with you in Lima, and will not be sent outside to the danger of the most troublesome function. A strange thing: the treasurer, suddenly brought in for a secret conference with the deputy-king, was detained by him in various conversations for an hour and a half, at the very moment when he was already anxiously awaiting the intimation of an adamantine decree, was politely dismissed, and the deputy-king, during the entire course of the conversation, he did not so much as mention that odious commission, even though he had for four whole months been striving, through the aforementioned, to most effectively persuade the unwilling quaestor to it. The same thing was later imposed on the other, as if it had never been discussed with Gundisalvus.

Ferdinand Flores de Herrera, Rosa's brother, following the military life of his father [and of his brother, whose marriage he had foreseen from afar], had penetrated as far as the kingdom of Chile, where he also served as standard-bearer in his own company. To him, who was more than five hundred leagues from Lima, his sister Rosa wrote a letter (for she had learned by divine revelation that he had entered into an honorable marriage in Chile). In it she earnestly urged her brother that, since he had embraced this state, he should not neglect to fulfill the obligations of a Christian head of household amid the clatter of military life. to diligently form his household in the fear of God and the observance of piety; He would strive to instill the finest morals in the children whom the divine blessing was to grant him, and to raise them up piously. and first of all, that the fruit of her marriage would be a little daughter, specially marked by God on her face, which, of course, the purple character of the conspicuous Rose was to adorn. Therefore, as soon as she was born, her parent would dedicate and offer her to the most august Mother of God; for this little daughter, by the purity and innocence of her life, would greatly please the Most High. Thus it happened: for about two years later Ferdinand had his first child, and she brought with her from her mother's womb a rose-like feature on her face so beautifully and accurately formed that it could not have been painted more charmingly with a brush.

She grew up with this sign, to the amazement of all who had long seen Rosa's letters. [He commends by letter the daughter who was to be born of him.] Diego Gonzales Montero, Christopher de Aranda Valdivia, and other centurions and commanders of the Chilean militia eagerly flocked to Ferdinand's house to enjoy this spectacle in person: for the girl (according to Rosa's prophecy) was remarkably inclined from her earliest years toward the exercises of religion and piety. Finally, prematurely deprived of both parents, she remained under a guardian's care until Francisco Lasso de la Vega, governor and president of the kingdom of Chile, on account of the pious and throughout Chile celebrated memory of the then-deceased Rosa, of his own accord assumed the duty and care of sending the orphan from Chile to Lima at his own expense, where she most devoutly lived with her grandmother, Mary de Oliva (already a nun at the time), in the monastery of St. Catherine of Siena, and shone wonderfully by her singular example of virtue.

[378] [She received divine knowledge about the unbaptized Ethiopian woman,] Isabella of Mexico, a noble matron, had as a servant an Ethiopian woman named Speranza, a name she had given herself (as if she were a Christian). These, carried from the promontory of Africa (formerly called Hesperides, now called Cape Verde) across the Atlantic Ocean from inland Libya, had first served at the Isthmus of Panama. She was then taken to Lima, where she had been a servant to Isabella for six years. It happened that she became seriously ill; therefore, the suppliant Rosa implored both Isabella and her mother to grant her Speranza, so that she could be more conveniently cared for in her mother's house by her own ministrations. As it was brought in, the Virgin, suddenly perplexed and muttering to herself, finally said aloud to her mother, "I feel that Speranza is not baptized." The sick woman, on the other hand, arming herself against shame with a worse obstinacy, contended stubbornly that she had been baptized in Panama, and to lend credence to her lie, she fabricated the names of several people whom she claimed had been present. He remembered godfathers never born, the place, the year, the rite, everything except the truth.

[379] [who had falsely pretended to be a Christian,] It was very easy for her to deceive both her mistress and Mary of Oliva and the others, so much so that they accused Rosa as if she were an importunately troublesome person. But the Virgin groaned, repeatedly sighing and openly lamenting the weak unbaptized one. After eleven days, Francis, the slave of the quaestor Gundisalvus, arrived, by the providence of God. He, who had been Speranza's constant companion from Africa to Panama and thence to Lima, when questioned replied simply that he had always believed—and indeed was certain—that within the past six years Speranza had been baptized in Lima; for he knew for a fact that she had not received baptism in Panama. At last convinced of the lie, but admiring how Rosa had been able to divine it, she began to speak before everyone: "While I was coming from Panama to this house of Isabella, the other Ethiopians there were mockingly calling me a mare, because I had not yet been baptized." But I, impatient of the insult, replied boldly, just as they had, that I was a Christian. From then on, so that I would not be shamed, I have tried to defend my lie with persistence to this very day. But now I confess that I have been discovered by Rosa (I don't know how), and I beg for Baptism. Immediately the Virgin called for the neighboring parish priest of St. Sebastian, Quixanus. He laid out a series of reasons for this matter, with the weaker ones being allowed. Speranza is baptized, and dies the next day.

[380] [& she had a heavenly gift for reading and writing.] No one dared to ask the joyful Rosa, by this success, from where she had learned the secret of Speranza's status: for it was a matter of public knowledge that this could not have been revealed to her by human means in Lima: On the other hand, it had long been established to the mother that abstruse knowledge was divinely infused into the Virgin, who, among other things, had imparted to her minor daughter the skill of reading and writing, not by acquisition, but by divine infusion, as she had learned by a grateful experiment. In the end, the matter became clear. The mother had taught her little daughter Rosa to recognize each letter of the alphabet, and was now about to instruct her in combining them into syllables, while at the same time she had given her a crude model on a small slate so that she could learn to form the characters with a pen. Rosa would rather devote all this time to prayer. But the mother, suspecting that her daughter was, in her childish way, shirking the tedium and labor of learning, brought in the confessor to rebuke the young woman before her for her neglect. He did what he had been ordered to do in good faith. But the next day, after a prayer, Rosa went to her mother, and read the offered book as quickly as possible. Furthermore, he presented a program elegantly written by his own hand. Indeed, the anointing had taught better than the olive, and the same one who had supernaturally bestowed upon Saint Catherine of Siena the art of reading and writing was pleased to divinely instruct Rose with the same infusion. Furthermore, so that we may not linger over these minor details, this Master, at last informed of the day, place, and hour of his own death, foretold nothing more sweetly than his own happy fate. But let that be more conveniently reserved for the next chapter.

Notes.

Santa Cruz de la Sierra is a mountainous province of America, located in the Kingdom of Peru, extending eastward toward Paraguay. Its head is called the Abbey of the Holy Cross of the Mount, from which the province probably took its name. It is a fairly cultured town under Spanish rule.

The Green Cape, which is called by the Spaniards Cabo Verde, is a promontory of Africa in the region of Nigritia, which Dionysius Ferdinandus the Portuguese discovered in 1446, and it is now widely known.

c Libya or Libya, when taken in the broadest sense, signifies all of Africa. But Baudrand describes the interior and more restricted region of this name with these words: "Upper Libya, which is also the interior region, a part of Africa and the largest part of the region of Ptolemy, which was extended southward between Lower Libya, the Atlantic and Ethiopian oceans, and Ethiopia." It was divided by the Niger River into two unequal parts, and within it were included the region of the Nigritas, the Garamantes, the Getuli, and desert Libya, etc.

The Isthmus of Panama is a small eighteen-league stretch of America, by which North America is connected to South America in the province of Tierra Firme. Panama, the most famous emporium of South America, is located there, and it is a bishop's city under the Archbishop of Lima.

An apostolic priest of our Society, who for several years has served as a missionary in America and still lives there, told me for certain that among the slaves already converted to the Christian faith it is customary to call their fellow countrymen who have not yet been baptized by nicknames like "horses" or "asses," and they refuse to associate with them.

Which of the two?

Chapter 27
Saint Rose's prior knowledge of the illnesses and torments she learned she would have to endure toward the end of her life.

Whether the certainty of death, or the uncertainty of the hour and day, is the more severe problem; There it decrees, here it conceals from mortals, except that for the friends of God alone to die is a gain, and moreover to some of them, by the extraordinary favor of the Deity, the secret of the day and hour is revealed. Hence, the festival of Saint Bartholomew was every year so precious and dear to Rose, for she foresaw that she would die on that day. He observed this unique devotion more devoutly, and not content with his own fast, he persuaded several innocent little boys to fast with him on the vigil of this feast. Indeed, even after Rose's death they continued to hold the same Apostle's vigils by voluntarily fasting, and they knew of no other reason for this pious custom than that they had been so instructed by Rose. The curious mother wondered what was so special about her daughter's relationship with Saint Bartholomew. Until at last she heard from the Virgin that this day would someday be her wedding day, on which she would be summoned to the Bridegroom's chamber.

[382] [& to those present she seemed about to die,] There was still about three years left before the Virgin's death, when she fell ill from a serious disease; she seemed to everyone to have already reached the very brink of life. No one doubted that she would be dead within a few days: her household mourned her as if she were already dead, while at the same time they were uncertain whether she was still breathing. Among others, Father Master Fr. served as confessor. Louis of Bilbao began to address the Virgin in a tearful voice with the words by which the dying are more earnestly prepared for their final struggle. Rosa, groaning, quietly and eagerly listened to her spiritual father's last consolations, and, readily yielding to his exhortation, she now offered the most fervent acts of contrition, now the tranquil sighs of the humblest resignation, now the eager longings for the heavenly homeland, all performed with effortless frequency.

[383] [she herself affirmed that her death was not yet imminent at that time:] But when she saw those around her sobbing more freely as if all was lost, and her own confessor unable to hold back his tears, she too could not restrain the tender feeling of compassion: and in order to console the others, she addressed her father as follows: "Lay aside your fear, banish this untimely sorrow, and be certain that I shall by no means die from this illness, though it is lamented by your judgment and that of all. Alas! The goal I rush toward is still far off; I will finally reach it, but not yet. For if I were to die now, I would have to die twice: for the day that will bring me my final death is still far off. Nor should it be thought that this is the Spouse's will, that I should die twice." The confessor was amazed that the Virgin should utter these things with such assurance and confidence, for he knew she was neither accustomed to speaking rashly nor to assert anything in so profound a matter, unless she had been informed of the day of her passing by divine revelation.

[384] [for the time of her death,] At last, Rosa reached the thirty-first year of her age, which she had long ago foreseen she would not complete. Four months before her death, while still strong and healthy, Gundisalvus's wife spoke to her thus: "Know, mother, that in the fourth month from now I shall surely enter the common path of all flesh." The pains of my final illness, which will consume me, will be the most atrocious, but the greatest torment of all will be thirst, for which I earnestly and anxiously beg you to relieve me then. Remember not to deny me the dew I shall need, lest you, with scorched throat and parched bowels, implore your motherly bowels for it. "Promise me today, mother, that in that deadly, extreme fire you will not abandon me, but will provide water to anyone who asks." The matron, astonished by these words, and having gazed intently at the Virgin, when she saw her so earnestly and humbly pleading, promised in good faith that she would give to the petitioner as much water as she asked for, whenever she asked for it. Meanwhile, she was distressed that the fateful day of the rose was looming so close.

[385] Moreover, she had also foretold the place (though a whole year earlier) to the same matron: [a place in the host's house,] for one evening, in the midst of spiritual conversations, suddenly interrupting in an unusual manner, Rosa, her face beaming with delightful and modest signs of joy, burst out with these words: "Know, mother, that I shall not die anywhere else, but here in your house, and in this very spot; And if you see me seized by the last illness in my parents' house, do not doubt that here, not there, I shall be loosed from the knot of mortality. Now then, I beseech you by all our friendship not to entrust my little body to other women for dressing and preparing for the coffin when I have breathed my last. For the love of God, only you and my mother, take on this care. I humbly commend to you alone this final act of kindness for me.

[386] In the famous vision of the arches (about which above chapter XVII) The devout Savior timely revealed to the Virgin the entire series of immense pains, [and had previously known that these torments were to precede her,] by which she was ultimately to be pierced and prepared for blessed immortality. these would be so acute, fierce, and manifold that the others he had endured until then, when compared to them, would scarcely deserve the name of pain. Each exhausted member of his body would have its own little horse, so that it could neither help another nor share in its suffering by natural sympathy. the same thirst that wrung the Redeemer, bleeding on the cross, would seize the dry viscera and throat of the Rose. devouring the very marrow of the bones with an intolerable ardor, and would admit no regular order, period, laws, or intervals of nature, since the lawless gathering of so many mortal torments would be supernatural.

[387] So that Rosa would not be startled by the chalice of such great bitterness, in the chapel of the Rosary, [and to certain friends she had indicated.] like in the garden of Gethsemane, she ardently commended herself both to the will of the eternal Father and to the help of the Virgin Mother, when, with the consent of the Mother of God, she learned more clearly there that the day was near on which she would have to drink down this entire deadly absinthe of the Passion. John of Tineo Almansa, a familiar, happened to arrive by chance; he greeted the Virgin in passing and commended himself to her prayers. In turn, Rosa, her face aflame (for that earnest conversation with the Virgin had greatly stirred her), implored him for the aid of her prayers, weaving in some perplexed words that were enough for John to understand that the precise day of her neighbor's death had then been revealed to her.

Finally, three days before the fatal syncope of her last illness, Rosa made her final visit to her parents' home, perhaps to bid a silent farewell to her garden cell there, so conscious of so many heavenly delights. [After she had entrusted her mother to St. Dominic,] There, considering herself a solitary virgin without a chaperone, she began the swan-like modulations and sweetly sang them for her own death, now near at hand. Her mother, observing her from a nearby corner, heard her daughter sweetly caressing the Holy Father Dominic with the joyful flow of her eloquent rhythms, earnestly commending her mother, Mary of Oliva, to him, repeatedly urging and repeating it. this alone will remain brief (as it dies). Therefore, he would bestow upon this forsaken world his heavenly protection, which he would eventually adopt as his daughter. A secret horror seized the mother, and at that time she concealed it, perhaps doubting that what is expressed in verse and in a ditty was very serious—not because it was true, but because it was commonplace: until the third day (which was the first of August) revealed that the swans' migratory laments, or rather, if you will, their jubilos, had been sung not poetically, but prophetically.

[389] [On August 1, 1617] On August 1, toward evening, Rosa, healthy and vigorous, had shut herself in her room, to await there the promised pains of her final labor, the forerunners of which she had already begun to feel. And behold, in the middle of the night, a most wretched wailing is heard. The questioner's wife runs up with her daughters and maids, and they find the sick woman stretched out on the floor, her whole little body. Their limbs lay rigid and motionless. The solitary, panting flutter of her breast and the languid, querulous rasp of her voice argued that a spark of life remained in the Virgin. An anxious matron stood by: she asked where and what pained her most. What, after all, could he have against it? But Rosa, for want of spirit, could at that moment answer nothing else, except that, her words broken, she said she had no evil indeed, but that death itself ruled most freely in the innermost recesses of her bowels. When asked, would she like a doctor to be called? He answered, "Heavenly," and obtained it.

[390] [stricken by an unknown and deadly disease,] Meanwhile, lifted from the ground and placed on a bed, he could neither move nor rest. Cold sweat stood on the pale forehead; His ability to breathe was struggling, as if pressed by a great weight. The discordant pulses of the arteries and the times vied for the palm of intensity, and at uncertain intervals now a swelling, now a tremor would overtake the pale little body. Only in this virgin was it well, when she would from time to time tenderly pronounce the name of Jesus with her own lips. At daybreak, the summoned confessors of the sick woman stood by, marveling at that living, silent likeness of all sorrows. The physicians were present, and immediately, having probed the force of the contending diseases, they stood in a mutual, haggard gaze, admitting that the heap of torments seemed beyond all human endurance, and that these pains were by no means entirely natural, but that a deeper malady was raging in paroxysms which their own art's experience had not recognized. Surely this was the cup that the Bridegroom had drunk, that he had mixed for the Bride, and had foretold so long before.

[391] [Since he could not explain it,] one of the confessors asked the Virgin to at least reveal to the physician what she was feeling. He compelled her with an order when she hesitated, believing that her silence stemmed from humble modesty rather than from the excess of her torments. Upon hearing the command, obedience soon proved stronger than the pains, and although Rosa, through the most frequent experiments, had learned the names of almost all diseases, she was nevertheless only able to explain this one (being a foreign one) by means of periphrases of similitudes, and so she replied: "I know I have brought upon myself what I suffer;" But I did not know that so many punishments could fall upon the human body and be distributed among its individual limbs. It seems to me that a ball of white-hot iron is brought up through my limbs, and from the very crown of my head to the sole of my right foot a fiery spear is transmitted, whose force somehow lifts me up. A dagger, kindled with a similar ardor, pierces me transversely from the left side through the center of my heart. (let the reader observe, in this double and crisscrossed torment of ardor, the form of a cross is expressed) my head seems to be enclosed in a glowing helmet, where it is being battered from all sides without ceasing by powerful pricks, as if by the blows of hammers; My bones are slowly dissolving into dust. The marrow withered like ashes. Each joint is beset by its own torments, for which I can find neither a name nor a torment to compare them to. I find that these things are indeed gradually driving me to my wit's end, but since a definite extension of a few more days is about to be added to the intensity of my pains, I am also made more patient with the labor and trouble I will be imposing on that house for longer than I would wish. May the Lord accomplish in me all that has been pleasing to His most holy will. I will not refuse either death or punishments more dire than death.

[392] These discourses increased the doctors' bewilderment: they wavered, confused in each other's gaze, [& foresaw that the doctors' industry would be useless,] perplexed, astonished: for from this, the most well-known virtue and holiness of Rose did not allow anyone to doubt the truth of their words. There were no signs to confirm the suspicion of a lethal disease, since there was no trace of fever in the veins, nor did the unusual succession of so many simultaneous changes present any outward criterion of a disease that the school of Aesculapius would have recognized. Furthermore, the Virgin secretly revealed to her confessor, Father Master de Lorenzana, that the doctors were laboring in vain to explore the nature of this illness. since the whole was unnatural and filled with orifices, with a multiplicity of paths leading from the periphery to the center, and the mutual encounters of the others intersected one another with the greatest variety of paroxysms of feeling; Therefore, she realized that nothing other than Job's incurable patience would be required of her, so that she might in some way experience the individual pains of her crucified Spouse, one by one. Therefore she earnestly begged Gundisalvi's wife to allow her, at least for a few days, to lie thus without human speech, so that under this solitary silence she might more quietly probe her inmost and inexplicable pains in the final torments of the crucified Love, to which she felt herself nailed limb by limb upon the cross.

But Rosa's mother, arriving and interrupting whatever peace of the cross, [yet by her mother's command], who, upon being admitted, found her daughter to be weaker than she had been told; And so he would press the sick woman painfully and anxiously, asking her which part hurt more severely. The daughter replied briefly that she was miserable in every way, except for one thing: that these were truly the pains of the cross. But by no means content with this answer, the mother, perhaps convinced that the patient's silence was delaying medical help, insisted on having every detail explained to her. And seeing her struggle, weak because she did not know where to begin or what names to give to a disease so anonymous and supernatural, he more imperiously, by the virtue of holy obedience, compelled the Daughter to reveal the secrets she did not know.

[394] Rosa, mindful that nowhere was a broader field for obedience open than on the cross, drew from it the power and spirit of her speech, and by the same concise similes (since she could find no more fitting ones) explained to her mother [her sufferings by way of a simile.] that she felt herself pierced as it were by a long, fiery javelin from the crown of her head to her heel, by wondrous torments which seemed to lift her entire left side; to be penetrated transversely by a very similar tower from side to side; by this roasting, his bones were being incinerated (for it was, in effect, a holocaust of the cross), his marrow and tendons torn away bit by bit with a white-hot forceps, the keenest frost wandering through his fibers, and the very antiperistasis of so many flames made all the more savage. the hinges of the jaws, as if most tightly bound by a steel wire, to be dislocated from the joint by any movement of the mouth; ears & temples as if being dottedly scorched by a glowing orb thrust in; to ulcerate the mouth and throat with bleeding wounds on the inside; the throat, with the tonsils, had withered like pumice from drought and thirst, (and why should it not be heard on the cross, "I thirst"?) the skull, like an anvil, was thoroughly pounded by the most frequent and powerful blows, and not one of all the tortures paused for even a moment. "These are, my sweetest mother," he said, "the things I have been able in any way to outline for you so that I might obey; the rest I cannot even crudely, and even if I could, you would not accept them." The troubled mother wept, grieving above all that she had nothing but tears with which to anoint her daughter's pains. But the Virgin, wounded by piety, tried to stop these useless tears of her afflicted mother, begging that the weeping be restrained, as if speaking in the words of the Spouse: "Do not weep for me."

[395] [Then on August 17 they broke into it] Meanwhile, the sixth day of August, sacred to the Transfiguration of Christ, dawned; But he found Rosam not on Tabor, but on the mount of Calvary, and fastened her more strongly. It seemed that the Virgin's innermost pains had summoned to her aid the rest of the host of natural ailments, so that neither any kind of punishment would be lacking to such great patience, nor would there be any further doubt that Rose's infirmity was lethal. The first thing to appear at this festival was a paralysis of the left side, by which the entire left half of the body was enervated from top to bottom and fell into a lethal stupor, so that from there Rosa began to die piecemeal. Only the tongue, by a singular gift of God, remained intact for the use of piety until his last breath; the other members on that side had grown so numb that they never returned to function, no matter how many ointments, rubbings, or fomentations were applied—more to increase his discomfort than to bring relief. She felt ill from the mere weight, still having one arm and one leg. The rest, whether to be propped up for a while in bed or turned onto the other side, required the hands of another, feeling more sympathy for the inconvenience of others than pain from his own. Various minor symptoms of paralysis followed, or rather developed.

[396] [Various different kinds of diseases,] But on August 17, with the pleural membrane on both sides inflamed, a marked pleurisy set in; to this a hoarse, constrictive asthma gradually joined, so that the burning pains, which inside like a furnace consumed the remnants of the viscera, would admit relief only through the rare sigh, not without fresh torment. Pleuritis was aggravated by a severe pain in the sciatic nerve, colic, a dreadful convulsion of the hypochondria, and an inflamed, nodose arthritis in the right foot. Then the continuous caustic feverh confounded the distinction of the other diseases, and such a potent, varied, and cumulative rust was required that only this single fatal Rose would finally succumb to the blight; It's a wonder it didn't collapse sooner. But the most glorious kind of martyrdom is to die slowly, indeed, piece by piece.

[397] She regarded the heaped-up piles of so many sorrows and illnesses, [which she accepted amid sweet sighs for God] with a resigned and serene spirit, and steadfast Rose, as though measured out to herself by the hand of Christ from that marvelous balance which she had watched most attentively in the vision of the arches, secure, that to such afflictions would succeed an equal heap of graces, namely the eternal weight of glory, of which the Apostle speaks in 2 Corinthians. four whose name is, Grace perfected. Hence that placid and sweet rest of the soul amid the most bitter torments; hence that unshaken strength of confidence and hope, which snatched many, and especially its spiritual fathers, away into astonishment; Hence those festive and gentle soliloquies with the Beloved; O Lord, more and more: fulfill the adored and holy good pleasure of Your most just will. Fill the scale, heap pain upon pain, but remember to increase my patience at the same time.

He sighed at times, but without a groan: "Lord, attend to my help; for I can do nothing without it." Whenever he was seized by side pain and driven to vomit blood, he addressed the Spouse in the familiar rhythm of his own idiom: "Lord, only do not punish me in your anger, nor chide me in your fury, while you will, that I may wash away my crimes with this blood." It was sometimes heard that the Crucifix, which she constantly carried in her arms, with sweet tenderness spoke to her thus: "My Jesus, when I asked you for sufferings, I believed they were to be sent to me, by which you have exercised me from my earliest youth;" But now it has seemed different to you: blessed be the abundance of your mercy. To a certain person, who was familiarly sympathetic toward her because of the severe stupors of her paralysis, she responded with a marvelous serenity of face and spirit, sometimes saying that she had been negotiating with God about adopting the beggar boy, whom she herself would raise in the hope of spreading the Gospel among the barbarians (as above in the chapter). As was related in chapter thirty-three, he now had two given to him, one of which he had taken up on his left knee and the other on his left arm to carry and nurture. He almost jokingly pointed to the stiffness in his dead knee and arm, where the heavier weight of his paralysis had settled.

Gradually the wise virgin was weakening (and not without cause), [and in whom her mind was always composed,] lest the continuous and atrocious violence of so many diseases at once should rob her of the use of reason and dissipate the entire temper of her brain. especially when so many days and nights were completely sleepless, so many changes of motion, such a inflammation of the occiput, and the restlessness of the times threatened a near certainty of darkness and an eclipse of judgment. So humble Rosa, with a trembling and pitiful voice, implored her household to pray with her to God to temper the power of diseases, so that they might harm only that part of her which could affect her mind and the free use of her reason. Divine piety looked upon the humility of its little handmaid, and preserved for her the full vigor of her judgment and speech, by a special miracle, immune from the constant lapses of a brain worn out by exhaustion, until her very last breath. However, often amid these anxieties, the Virgin was withdrawn from the use of her external senses, either by the severity of her illness or by an excess of contemplation, so that she was thought to have been gently lulled to sleep. Hence, some even dared to hope a little better for the sick woman's health, because they believed she had slept a little.

More than anyone else, the anxious mother was working to persuade her daughter that these were signs of her health returning. Smiling truly, the Virgin replied with a certain grace. She was forced to endure the torment she had foreseen. What they had seen was nothing more than a doze, and her torments were not such as to stand on their own for a single moment during a respite, or to admit even the slightest companionship with sleep. Therefore, they would not doubt that they would soon be consumed, but first this bitter cup of their passion would have to be drained to the very dregs by the love of the Spouse. May the arch-caterer of the Cross be your most wondrous thirst. Rose was twisting me, no, roasting me more fiercely each day. And so, looking at his wife Gundisalva with a miserable, downcast, tearful gaze—often, languidly, and painfully—he prayed imploringly for a little water to refresh himself, considering bile (to which he had long been accustomed) along with vinegar a real delicacy, if he could have obtained it. The matron, though grievously wounded by compassion, refused to allow the doctors to do that for her. Rosa urged him to remember the promise she had made four months earlier to a strong and healthy man, that she would promptly give him as much water as he demanded in his final illness. But she excused herself, saying she could no longer fulfill her promise. Thus, the Virgin, wasting away painfully, had only this left to her, as she cried out with her dying Spouse: "I thirst."

Notes.

At the Romans, promulsidaria are called vessels or small bowls in which the promulsis is contained. A promulsis is a food or drink that stimulates the appetite. From the epithet "mortiferous," which is added here, it is clear enough what sort of promulsidarium that was.

Syncope, from the Greek συγκοπὴ, is, among physicians, a sudden loss of all strength, which Gorræus and Castellus discuss at greater length.

A strategist is properly the leader of an army from the strategos of the army, by the verb ago, that is, I lead. This is taken to mean the leaders or harbingers of the final or lethal disease, as is clear from the sense.

Critirion, or criterion, from the Greek kriterion, sometimes means a tribunal of judges or a controversy to be settled by judges, from the verb krino, which means to judge. By this criterion, it is indicated that no certain sign of a specific disease has appeared, about which the physicians or the school of Aesculapius would pass judgment.

The Greek compound word, literally translated from the verb, could be called 'surroundings' in Latin. Antiperistasis, however, is, according to Calepinus, a reciprocation, a compression diffused from all sides, by which, where it is hot, cold is expelled from there. Where it is cold, heat is driven out. From this description and the preceding period, it is easy to gather what that word means in this context.

Cholika or cholera, from the Greek name cholē, that is, bile so called, is a very acute disease, as Gorræus in his Medical Definitions, page. 509, where more can be read about its various species and causes.

Hypochondrium, as if you were to say in Latin, subcartilagineum, because it is situated under the cartilage. Hence Gorræus, on page 484 of the work just cited, describes that part of the body as follows: "Hypocondrium" is the name given to that entire region of the human body which extends from the costal cartilage on both sides down to the ilia. The reason for the name is that it is under the cartilage, that is, it lies beneath it and behind it. Not only the muscles, but also the internal organs subject to them are called by the same name, because they are enclosed beneath the cartilages of the ribs. From this, one can understand what that dreadful convulsion of the hypochondria was like.

h Caustic, that is, burning or inflamed heat, from the adjective kaustikos, that is, having the power to burn, from the verb καίω, which is to burn, to set on fire, or to consume.

Chapter 28
The Saint's final illness, happy death, and the beautiful appearance of her body, and the great crowd of people who gathered for it.

[Often having confessed her sins,] the time was at last acceptable for Rose, withered not so much by Syria's sun as by thirst's ardors, to cast off the thorns of her sorrows along with her life, to be reborn more lovely in her Spouse's paradise. The dying man's lethal symptoms could no longer be concealed, and this was most welcome to the Virgin, for it granted her, as it were with sureties, a freer field in which to treat of the soul alone, the care of the body having been set aside. Thus, by frequently calling for confessors, he most diligently purged his most tender conscience through the repeatedly repeated act of confession, once indeed in a general confession of his entire life; then more frequently, but more briefly, always, however, with a loud sob of deep remorse and tears accusing himself; so that the groans they heard had moved many, marveling that the Virgin, in the greatest torments of her body, was so sparing of groans, yet now, in describing the slightest troubles of her spirit, she sighs so bitterly and deeply.

After these things, three days before his blessed passing, with hands folded in the rite of supplication, he was strengthened with the last Sacraments of the Church, and with a mournful voice he asked for the divine Viaticum and the last Anointing. When she heard that the Eucharistic feast was being brought to her, she, suffused with a living color, blushed graciously like a shining dawn. Soon, overcome by his joy, he was lifted into the highest ecstasy, to the astonishment of all who were present, since he nevertheless answered the priest, who according to custom, holding the most sacred Host in his hand, was asking the questions that are usually asked at that point, fittingly and promptly. After receiving the divine Sacrament, she lay pale and bloodless, her lips motionless, so that for a long time those present doubted whether she had passed the sacred Host into her stomach. Finally, Father Master de Lorenzana, fearing that the Virgin might not be able to swallow with her throat so dry and seized by illness, anxiously asked what condition she was in. Rosa, suddenly brought back to herself by the confessor's voice, replied that the most sacred Host had already descended through her throat into her inmost being. But he himself, mindful of the wondrous effects which this life-giving Sacrament used to work in the Virgin like the sun, (as we have related her own confession above in chapter XXII) He briefly urged her to enjoy her sun now. Fittingly, for on that very same day in the zodiac the sun of the month of August passed from Leo into Virgo, and the Eucharistic Sun, truly august, was fulfilling the final virgin, Rose, with her virginal body. Surely this Virgin stood between sickness and death, as between a lion and a balance: for that one, like a lion, had crushed all her bones; this one was weighing the eternal weight of glory for the Virgin in the balance, of which chapter. Seventeen. But to other matters.

[403] How immovably rigid she lay, while in ecstasy she received the sacred Viaticum, [professes the Catholic faith,] she received the last anointing of the dying with such alacrity of all her senses, a true oil of joy, by which she felt herself animated rather for triumph than for the conflict of the struggle, being already so certain of her salvation that she also knew, without the contact of purgatorial fire, she was to proceed on the straight path to the nuptials of the Spouse. Often in the presence of those standing by, she repeated the profession of the orthodox faith in a voice as loud and clear as she could, not without joy, and almost adding a swan's song, protesting that she, just as she had lived until then, would now die a daughter of the holy Roman and universal Church: that she had always believed and believed, whatever the apostolic See, the teacher of faith, proposes and teaches: that she would never, so long as she remained in her right mind, depart from the most solid firmness of this faith. Her next concern was to profess openly and devoutly the offspring of her Holy Father Dominic, and so she willed to see and be seen extending before her, outside upon the blankets, the white insignia of her Order, which we call the scapular, as if it were the proper standard of her religious militia, under which she had merited until her last breath: and this is that very vestment with which the Virgin Mother had, as with swaddling clothes, wrapped the nascent Order of the Preachers in her own hands. Furthermore, Rosa was delighted when she learned from her confessor, Father Lorenzana, for the first time something she had never known: that it was a perpetual custom in the Order to lay the sacred scapular in that manner on dying religious. Hence, Rosa, about to die, kissed her scapular more tenderly, for she felt as if the Milky Way were being marked out for her to the heavenly seats.

[404] [forgives his adversaries or persecutors,] He had visited Rosa, who was near death, Father Bartholomew Martinez, prior of the Lima convent of St. Mary Magdalene, a very devout man and a zealous guardian of the Dominican observance there, to whom for five years the Virgin had been accustomed to reveal the secrets of her conscience. From that moment on, the sick woman earnestly begged that the most exquisite and supremely effective formula be read to her word for word, by which, as she lay dying, she might implore God's forgiveness for all who had afflicted Rosa with troubles, losses, or any kind of injury throughout her entire life. The pious Father nodded and followed the words of the Virgin who was speaking, holding the icon of the Crucified in her hand. He could not be satisfied with these most tender words: "Father, forgive them." He was enraptured by the whole sweetness of these affections; their repetition kindled him to the marrow, and he was not wearied by the length of the discourse, so long as he could return to the same words again and again. Having completed this pious duty, he thanked the Father gently and sweetly, because he had, in so precious and solemn a work, shown him a fitting method and path by reciting it, by which he might in some way imitate the divine Lamb on the cross, who was earnestly interceding for his adversaries.

[405] [he asks forgiveness for his mistakes from everyone in turn,] Then, turning inward, she humbly requested that the household of the treasurer Gundisalvus be summoned, and although she had never offended any of them with a word or the slightest gesture, she implored each and every one with a tearful and supplicant voice to forgive her for any harm she might have caused, whether by her disorderly conduct, her slowness in attending to their needs, or the unpleasantness of her singular disposition. He lamented that he would still be a burden to them for nearly two days, and he begged them not to lose the fruit of their long-suffering and the merit of their patience so close to the end. The hour was not far off that would free Gundisalvus's home from the foul Rose's long tedium and useless burden. There was no one present whose eyes would not be filled with tears by such words of humility; for, as they loved their Rose most tenderly, it had been enough for each to have known her personally and to have confirmed her holiness, innocence, humility of spirit, purity, and devotion to obedience by so many trials.

The quaestor knew that the Virgin, having lived in the habit of the Dominican Order and in obedience (and having requested a burial place with the Fathers Preachers), wished to be buried at the feet of her brothers, and nowhere else. but the prudent man feared that perhaps after Rose's death, disputes would arise between the parish and the convent of Saint Dominic over the founding of a treasury for such a funeral; Yet he did not dare to reveal that honorable peril to the most humble Virgin. Finally, he suggested to the Virgin an ingenious plan: that she should notify the Friars Preachers of her legitimate desire for burial by mendicating rather than by testament, using the document already prepared by Gundisalvus for this purpose. Rosa readily agreed and signed, believing it was merely an exercise in humility and that it was the custom of her Order. At other times, she thought of neither her body's care nor her coffin, intent only on her spirit, so much so that she welcomed the visits of her female relatives with more difficulty than the new intensifications of her pain. For just as she would often, with a polite excuse, ask to have those things, as if they were burdensome and superfluous, kept at bay, so she took ever greater pleasure in having this one heaped upon her. Hence, amid the most acute torments of sciatica, that frequent cry: "Burn, press, squeeze, O Lord;" Do not spare, however much you prick or pull, for it is too little for my deserts.

[407] Most have noted in the Virgin, by the same degrees, [Then she was rapt into frequent ecstasies,] by which her frail little body was failing unto death, that her spirit was lifted up into vigor and secure gladness, so that she could scarcely conceal by voice or countenance the hourly increases of her joy, as though protesting with the Apostle: "When I am weak, then I am strong." A certain devout man, commiserating with his dreadful torments, urged him to endure bravely, saying that the end was now in sight and that by this heat the soul would be happily purged of its crudeness, so that it might delight the eternal palate of the Bridegroom. The Virgin replied, "But this is precisely what I ask of the Spouse: that he never cease to refine me with his most exquisite ardors, until I ripen for him into a fruit worthy to be brought straight from here to his own table." Toward the end of her life, the intervals between the Virgin's most sweet raptures grew more frequent, in which she already tasted on her highest lips the savor of heavenly glory. A few hours before his death, recovering from such a foretaste and unrestrained in his joy, P. Fr. Franciscus Nicto, with others, while keeping watch nearby, said frankly but in secret to the priest: "Oh, Father!" If the brevity of my remaining life would permit, what lofty, precious, delightful, and extraordinary things I would have to tell you about the sweetness of God, about His joyful court, about the region of eternity! I now go with a dance to gaze forever upon that most beautiful face, which I have sought throughout my entire exile of wandering, and longed for in the whole heat of my desires.

The afflicted mother was present with her dying daughter; the father was absent, for his own infirmity kept him at home in bed; [and the parents having been summoned,] However, at the request of the Virgin, who was awaiting the final blessing of both her parents, she had herself carried to Gundisalvi's house. At the first sight of her daughter, emaciated and pale, she broke into sobs and tears, and there was no one among those present so hard-hearted that the tenderness of that mournful spectacle did not soften him into joining in the weeping. Meanwhile, having somehow wiped away her tears, Rosa calmly and with the utmost reverence kissed her parent's hand, and then softly and unperturbed explained that only the humblest portion of the life her parent had given her now remained, and that in this final phase she awaited both their blessings with humble submission. Having obtained this, he, with equal respect for Gundisalvus and his wife—since he had honored them as second parents—also wished, with their blessing, to set out on that arduous journey. Then, having summoned his two brothers who were present closer, he gave them a serious, grave, and sententious exhortation, and among other things, he most effectively commended the careful care, honor, and obedience of parents. as if, following the example of the crucified Spouse, he had said: "Behold your mother." Having finished these tasks, he summoned the two daughters of the quaestor, whom he had dearly loved for their innocence and upright character. He instilled in them, with marvelous energy, the fear of God, a love of virtue, and a love for their parents, by which they would secure for them a tranquil and happy old age. She addressed the other household servants in turn with no less emphasis, admonishing each one decently of their duty and of Christian piety. It seemed not a woman, but an apostle, preaching.

[409] [After pious admonitions, she dies peacefully.] Such was the spirit and vigor of preaching in Rosa that she had persuaded Father Lorenzana, her confessor and master, that she herself would not die at least that night. It was night, and the vigil of Saint Bartholomew; Therefore the confessor, who was to preside at the solemn Matins service at home, was preparing to depart for the monastery, to return the next day.
