But the Virgin, knowing that she had no more than four hours left, earnestly begged him for a final blessing, as though she were about to pass on to the Lord shortly thereafter. But when he replied that it could more conveniently be postponed until tomorrow, that time would not be lacking, and that he would arrive the next morning in good time; Rosa, smiling gravely and modestly, said: "Know, my Father, that tonight, with the coming of the Feast of St. Bartholomew, I shall depart to the eternal feasts: I have already been invited from heaven to that splendid and solemn banquet, the hour is already set, and you do not wish me to go while the doors are open." She spoke with such serene and gracious countenance, with such calm and cheerful composure, as though she were already waiting at the threshold of paradise with a lamp, while at midnight a cry rang out: Behold, the Bridegroom comes. And indeed, when midnight came, Rosa, roused by this cry, was summoned; she asked for the blessed candle with the gentlest nod, and, as if about to undertake something great, she marked her forehead, lips, and breast with the sign of the cross. He motioned to his brother Germanus, who was still uncertain what was happening, and ordered the cradle-board to be removed, so that, leaning his head against the bare wood of the bier, he might feel himself dying on a cross: Finally, with his senses completely intact, in full possession of his faculties, his eyes fixed on heaven, and with no sign of fear or horror on his face, he quietly expired while uttering these last words: "Jesus, Jesus, Jesus be with me." Indeed, just as little Rosa had, at the sound of those words, begun her first prayer, so she, having died uttering them, carried back to the threshold of the heavenly homeland, unchanged, the token of her innocence and childlike simplicity, in the fifth month of the thirty-second year of her life, scarcely begun.

At the same early hour of the festive night, Aloysia of Serrano, far away in her parents' house, still wrapped in morning sleep, learned that she had then laid down the mortal remains of Rosa with St. Bartholomew. It had been agreed between them that whichever of them was the first to depart this life would, God willing, warn the other of her passing. Rosa kept her promises, and that very night, in the form of a shining light, she gently awakened Aloysia, indicating that she had then gone to the realm of light. But let more ample discussion of the luminous apparitions of the Rose after her death be reserved for the Mantis. We must return here to the corpse of the Virgin Rose, which was believed to be breathing when she had ceased to breathe. The vivid color of her face, her lips slightly parted as if in a gentle smile, her eyes unblemished and faintly aglow with a bright, pleasant light, long kept those present in doubt about Rosa's death, until, with a mirror applied, they discovered that no breath remained in her. Here, as with the rose, the mirror was used last, yet first.

[411] While the tractable corpse was being dressed, while it was being laid in the coffin, [and it moved the household with a strange joy rather than with grief,] while it was being carried out, sprinkled with flowers, into the front room, with the mother looking on, a mournful license to wail was granted to all, yet not one of those present chose to give way to lamentations: for by a sudden prodigy a certain secret sense of joy so pervaded them all, that the entire house seemed to swell with unexpected consolations and to float in a kind of nuptial joy. What happened to the mother is already mentioned above at the end of the chapter. The third has been reported. The same stimulus of intimate and secret delight seized the rest of Gundisalvus's family and all the outsiders who were present. In all, nineteen noble persons, besides the household slaves of both sexes, had surrounded the dying Rose's little bed in mournful silence, and when the Virgin yielded her spirit, each one found herself, by a certain hidden sweetness of spirit, drawn of her own accord from weeping to applause. One of those present, having seen the dying Rose's bier surrounded by a numerous crown of angels, likewise observed that they were now dancing most festively around the deceased's bier in an alternating chorus. She secretly confided to the questor's wife that the vast hall, in which lay the exposed corpse of Rosa, had been all aglow from every side with a luminous outpouring of heavenly glory. He swore that it had been revealed by Christ to a certain religious person three days before the blessed passing of Rose, that the Virgin's death would be admirable and her burial glorious. Furthermore, he had decreed that at the Virgin's funeral, cloths of a mournful black color should not be draped, but rather white, snowy, and solemn ones, which would not indicate a funeral, but a triumph. At last a marvelous sympathy for rejoicing grew so strong that, while the dawn of the approaching day was awaited, several devout women there freely delighted themselves in turn with the sonorous singing of sacred hymns, proclaiming that it was not a place of mourning but of jubilation.

[412] At last a brighter day dawned, and behold, the quaestor's palace was suddenly filled with a crowd of people, [and immediately such a multitude of the populace] without anyone calling them. The household wondered where or how the news of the late, still-warm Rose could have spread throughout the entire city at such an ungodly hour. Nor could the public heralds, even if sent out in relays and filling the forums and crossroads with stentorian cries, have by early morning gathered a crowd as dense as the one that of its own accord streamed from every street to Gundisalvi's house, roused by no human diligence. Among the foremost was the most eminent P. de Lorenzana, and upon seeing Rosa in the coffin, as if asleep, he could not restrain his voice but cried out aloud: "Blessed are the parents who bore you!" Blessed be the hour that received you at your birth! Blessed are you by the Lord, most blessed daughter of Dominic, who now enjoys the beatific vision of your Creator! You died as you lived. baptismal grace has never been interrupted by any lethal harm. You brought to heaven the immaculate innocence of your entire life, the childlike purity of virginial whiteness: follow, follow now the Lamb, wherever he may go.

Meanwhile, the crowd that had gathered—nobles and commoners, newcomers and citizens, Indians and Spaniards alike—competed to press rosaries against the holy little body, and they tore from every direction the tiny flowers that had been woven into the religious woman's lying-in-state dress. He was eager to hastily kiss this one's hand, that one's feet. Nor were there lacking those who, by a pious theft, would snatch from here the hem of a tunic, from there the ends of a veil, with scissors secretly at first, then openly, so that the attendants soon had to fend off this rapacious devotion. Those who stood farther off were fixed, gazing at the snow-white and graceful face of the Rose, which a flowering crown and the whiteness of her veil most charmingly commended. They had several times tried to cover the eyes of the deceased Virgin with her eyelids, drawing them down. but in vain: for they slowly returned to where they had been drawn, covering only the middle of the eyes: as if the Virgin, even after her death, sweetly loved to gaze with her dove-like pupils at her Limeños.

[414] [so that the house had to be guarded by armed soldiers.] The crowd of onlookers swelled, and with it grew the crush, so that neither the house's doors, though wide open, could admit those rushing in, nor could those wishing to leave find an exit in such a jam. The entire courtyard, the corridors, and the corners were filled with a confused crowd, so that Gundisalvus was forced to open the back of his house, through which those who were arriving could make their way out by another route. For a while that remedy worked, until even that section began to be beset by the most eager throng of onlookers, which left no room for letting in or out even the more distinguished persons. Therefore the most illustrious Viceroy of the Kingdom of Peru swiftly dispatched the Praetorian cohort of his armed guard and ordered it to stand at the gates of the buildings, lest the unordered clash of so many troops ignite into a riot. The entire city was amazed that it was moved so wholeheartedly to honor the funeral of the poorest Virgin, the humble daughter of obscure parents, who, while she lived, had so hidden herself from everyone's eyes, knowledge, and praise. But it was to be fulfilled, as had been divinely foretold, that his burial would be glorious.

Notes.

At this point it is said that the holy Virgin in ecstasy shuddered, because ecstasy is an abstraction of the mind from the body, into which people, when seized, are as if frozen and rendered inflexible. The Greek name ekstasis comes from the verb existai, which means to be stupefied, or to be carried away.

Here is an allusion to the heavenly signs of the month of August, in which the natural sun was then present, and to the Eucharistic Sun, and to the time when St. Rose perceived this and departed from this life, as is quite evident.

On the vest which the Virgin Mary gave to the Order of Preachers, we have treated at sufficient length in the preliminary commentary on the life of St. Dominic on August 4, § 28, or in the first volume of that month, pages 460 and following.

Since the feast of St. Bartholomew is celebrated in some places on August 24, and in others on August 25, I do not yet know whether in Lima the vigil of that same Apostle fell on August 23 or 24. However, it appears that the annual feast of St. Bartholomew is celebrated there on August 24 by virtue of the testimony of Pope Clement IX, which we shall soon present.

From these we openly conclude that St. Rose died on the very eve of the Feast of St. Bartholomew, and therefore it follows that this feast was then celebrated in Lima on August 24; for Pope Clement IX, in the decree of Beatification which we cited in § 2 of the Preliminary Commentary, states the same thing there, no. 22. He expressly decrees that the Office for this blessed Virgin be recited annually under a double rite, and that a Mass be celebrated … according to the rubrics of the Roman Breviary and Missal on August 26, which is the first day not impeded after August 24, the day on which she returned her spirit to the Creator. From this we conclude that St. Rose passed away on the feast of St. Bartholomew, and consequently that feast in Lima then fell on August 24th.

Posthumous glory from various monuments, which legitimately confirmed the common opinion of the sanctity of this virgin.

Rose, virgin, of the Third Order of St. Dominic, of Lima in Peru, Province of the Americas (S.)

A. Leonardo Hansen, from the printed works.

Chapter One
Posthumous glory from his honorary funeral rites, the later translation of his sacred body, and its careful preservation.

Section I. Such a great and confused crowd of people gathered for the funeral of this holy Virgin that her burial had to be postponed to the following day.

[As the rumor of his death spread through the city,] Toti was already known throughout Lima, the rumor having blown up, that Rosa's corpse was to be carried from the magistrate's house at dusk to the sepulchre. And behold, the streets, forums, and alleys through which he had to pass had been filled several hours earlier by the overflowing multitude of people. The streets, spacious and wide for a city of such dignity (the route from the quaestor's residence to the sacred edifice of the Preachers was about a thousand paces), nevertheless could not adequately hold the numerous crowd, which had gathered in time at every intersection; invited by neither a herald, nor the lofty bells of the temples, but by each person's own individual devotion alone.

[2] The Most Illustrious Archbishop of Lima had come forth, to lend the funeral procession the honor of his presence, [the Lima prelate with his chapter, the religious Orders,] but, not trusting himself to force a way through the densest multitudes of spectators to Gundisalvo's house, and after a fruitless attempt to pass, he preferred to turn aside to the church of St. Dominic, and there await the virgin's funeral at the doors. Meanwhile, just as if by a public supplication, pious societies of various titles flocked in long processions and by different routes to the house of the quaestor, with no one either commanding or requesting it, or offering a stipend. The mendicant religious orders processed under distinct banners; soon after, the metropolitan college came of its own accord, contrary to expectation and custom, since otherwise it never intervenes in the burial of bodies (unless its own archbishop is being interred).

Finally, the most august senate accorded its homage, which it had scarcely ever extended to any other, save to its own late pro-consuls. [and the senate gather for the funeral procession;] The others, who had flocked together with the same spontaneous devotion, the number or list of the illustrious men from every order of the equestrian class in the dense multitude could not be ascertained. There was one cause of astonishment for everyone: to see themselves gathered there, where no one had summoned them. The same ambitious piety and eagerness to see of the matrons and the rest of the female sex: wherever the funeral procession was to pass, the windows of the palaces and houses on both sides were filled with the faces of numerous onlookers; from every portico, wall, and parapet, hundreds and thousands of eyes gazed from afar at the approaching spectacle. The roofs of the buildings themselves lay softly nestled beneath the unarmed, populous crowd of all ages, which blackened in the distance. It was, indeed, of such great importance that the poor daughter of an obscure and barely known soldier was carried out to burial. Certainly, all of Lima has never before been seen to move in this way.

Finally, toward evening, the bier of the precious pledge emerged from the open gates of Gundisalvi, its shoulders first voluntarily offered by the metropolitan chapter, in that honorific observance which is customarily shown only to archbishops when they have passed away. From there, after crossing the first street with heavy and slow step, they came to the crossroads, where the bier was set down, and the secular senate took up the pious burden. After this, the foremost members of the religious orders most devoutly took up the work, dividing the duties among the villages and the boundaries of the crossroads. On both sides, the most excellent lieutenants of the deputy-king, armed escorts, flanked the sides of the bearers, both to clear the way through the dense crowd with force and shouting, and also to protect the virgin's body from the devout rapacity of the people clamoring for relics: For just as individuals, in the midst of the tumult, cried out "Holy" to Rosa, so the more violent and stronger everywhere gnawed at the sandalwood, eager and ready to pluck from it whatever they could, had not the double-edged spears of the spearmen formed a kind of hedge.

[5] Certainly, if this most eager crowd had been allowed to do as it pleased, there was a danger that only a small part, or none at all, of the sacred body would reach the church. Yet even under such vigilant and iron-clad guard it could not be prevented that, when they came to the church doors, it would be noticed that the corpse was missing parts of its clothing, the crown, and the palm, and that others would have to be put in their place there. While she lay dead on the threshold of the temple (which Rosa, still mortal, had so diligently swept), at the sprinkling of lustral water her face seemed to some to be newly enlivened with a virginal gladness, rosy in the snow-white purity of her veil, and made more beautiful by the bright serenity of her purest robe; her neck and whole body supple, so that one might almost exclaim: "The maiden is not dead, but sleeps."

[6] A mound, gradually raised from the floor, received the offering in the main chapel; [where such a great crowd of people had gathered,] but at a more illustrious sign, the Virgin Mother of God was seen there to have received her Rose. For under those very motions, in another chapel of the miraculous image, which is called the Rosary, a splendid face suddenly shone upon all who were standing by. Soon a torrent of immense people converged there, and he, caught between fear and joy, openly broke down in tears. The cry of the supplicants grew stronger, so that there were those who suspected the image had been made to sweat with a prodigious, additional sweat. But upon closer examination, it was discovered that they were nothing but mere splendors, though unusual, festive, and benign, to which the Virgin herself, Queen of Virgins, publicly applauded.

[7] But we must return to Rosa, more magnificent now in her tomb, [so that the funeral could scarcely be held,] and reciprocally resplendent with the sweet radiance of her features: for to this spot the entire crowd was pouring back. But the monks of the monastery, more distinguished by dignity and age, had opportunely surrounded the tomb, both to prevent that treasure from being plundered and to admit in an orderly fashion the weak and the lame who approached with no vain hope of receiving a cure. But of these, in the Mantissa. A little farther back, the pro-regent's spear-bearing retinue had taken up position to hold back the confused mass of the rushing crowd, if they could. Yet he could scarcely manage it: for the press was so great that the wailing singer had to struggle up onto the altar's edge in order to carry out the mournful rite with the customary chant.

[8] Presiding over the funeral rites were Archbishop Limanus, the royal senate, [and the burial of the virgin's corpse,] whom they call the audience, the metropolitan and curial colleges, the prelates of the regulars, and a conspicuously numerous nobility of the equestrian order. And so, with everything arranged, as the Office of Burial was about to begin, by which Rose's body was to be carried from the temple to the grave prepared in the chapter house, suddenly throughout the farthest corners of the vast church there resounded such a clamor from the mingled multitude, such a disjointed and mournful wailing of those who pleaded, that for fear of confusion and tumult it was deemed best to desist, and the funeral procession was stayed, the people being informed that the burial of that most dear pledge would be postponed until the following day.

[9] Under the comfort of this promise and certain hope, many were persuaded to retire to their homes, to return the next day for a more convenient view of the tomb. Thus, as the plebeian crowd filed away in orderly procession and gradually dispersed, the nobles were granted free access and a closer approach to venerate the body of the Handmaid of God, [which still seemed to breathe,] and to kiss her most innocent hands. Meanwhile a curious crowd had again gathered, and once more impetuous throngs were pressing around the tomb. When the archbishop saw them, he motioned to the Fathers with gestures and signals—for his voice could not be heard above the loud din—to carry the Virgin's body into the sacristy.

[10] [if it was to be postponed until the next day.] But it was not safe there either, with the crowd following and pressing on. Therefore, he had to be transported to a safer, more spacious recess beneath the novices' cells, accompanied by a few archpriests; Hordes of followers were pressing forward, about to force their way through the gates, had not the wedge of the religious and the reverence for the place intervened. Finally, he was also brought there at last to the novices' simple oratory, and there he spent the night under the watch of the guards appointed by the Prior. At last the archbishop, his piety sharpened by solitude, knelt down, reverently took the Virgin's hand and repeatedly pressed devout kisses upon it, and he felt it tractable not only at the joint but at every finger joint, as if it were alive. Aderant nonnulli e senatorio ordine, magnæque felicitatis loco habebant, posse illic de genu fimbriam virginei habitus osculari, nec ulli horum ea fuit pectoris constantia, quælachrymas temperaret.

Notes.

The labarum was originally called a military standard, most splendidly woven with gold and glittering with gems, which was carried before the emperors. Others want this name to be derived from a foreign word, and to mean the sacred banner that was hung in temples. Whatever it may be, the sense in this place sufficiently indicates for which standard that word is here used.

Here and elsewhere, Hansenius calls the appendix "the supplement of miracles, which he appended to the Life of St. Rose," as we noted at the end of the preliminary commentary.

In Spanish, audiencia is called a tribunal or a place where justice is administered. This is taken to be the assembly of senators or judges that had convened for the funeral of St. Rose. For other meanings of the same word, Cangii's Glossary can be consulted under the name 'audientia'.

Section II. The next day, the departure of the people was shrewdly arranged; after that, the sacred corpse was secretly buried, and the funeral rites were later repeated more solemnly.

[The next day, again in the temple] The following day, at the approach of the morning twilight, I ordered the guards to bring the venerable relic from the novices' quarters to the larger chapel of the church, where it had stood the day before, and to raise it on the reclinatory of the same tomb. Then, the temple doors being opened, the people rushed in in waves. He filled all the stalls, benches, platforms, and seats densely packed. Everyone considered himself fortunate if he could find a place to stand on higher ground. But when the bell struck its usual signal for the funeral Mass and the solemn Office of the Exequies, the vast remainder of the church suddenly proved too small for the crowd that poured in: for not only the townspeople, as on the previous day, but also outlying villagers from six or more leagues away had come, unbidden. The regent's supporters surrounded them again. But they, if on the previous day they had barely been able to withstand the onslaught of the mob, on this day could not even barely manage it.

[12] All ages and both sexes rushed the entrance in a frenzy. This one precious stones, [a great tumult of the people arose,] that one amulets of the lambs of God, another a handkerchief, a coin, garlands, and whatever was dear at hand, by touching the sacred body, humbly stretched out from a distance and up close. Hence the crowd of the sick, or of the miserable crippled by bodily defect, almost in a clamorous dispute demanded access, hoping to seize the hoped-for health by handling the bier. From there, infants were carried aloft over the heads of the crowd to the salutary contact with the sacred corpse. Nor was the utmost vigilance and guard of soldiers and religious men enough to prevent the veil, hair, and garments of the late Rose from being repeatedly torn to pieces by the more cunning, under the pretext of obtaining relics. so that before burial it had to be completely rewrapped six times. All subsequent care was devoted to preserving that lifeless little body, lest it be torn by importunate tugging before burial: for it had already been noted that one finger was missing, whether secretly severed by iron or by a bite is not clear.

[13] Among these movements, the monks gathered in the choir, and the solemn Mass sacrifice proceeded, [so that the funeral Mass could not be heard.] with the Most Illustrious Lord Peter of Valencia of Guatemala, bishop, present in his pontifical vestments, who had arrived, admitted through the sacristy's rear entrance (for the entrance through the temple, filled with a crowd, was not open) to perform the burial office after Mass with his own hands. The church resounded from every side with the ceaseless voices proclaiming "Holy Rose," so that neither the people outside could hear the religious choir singing, nor could the priest's or the altar ministers' voice reach the choir: a signal was given to the choir by the pulse and bells when a response was to be made to the priest. But all was in vain, the clamor of the seculars prevailing; at last it became necessary to have the singers leave the choir and surround the very altar where the service was being held, so that they might in turn hear and be heard up close. Thus, with difficulty at last, amid the temple's sonorous clatter, the divine mysteries were completed in a kind of melodious silence and by the gesture of the rites. Then the high priest, stepping down from the throne and advancing to the bier, performed the ceremonies of incense and lustral sprinkling amid the customary psalm and responsorial chants with fitting pomp, as if about to prepare for the final procession to carry forth and lay to rest the sacred little body.

[14] But again, the crowd, packed together, groaned with fresh and louder cries; [Then, deceived by the signals, the mob readily believed,] and again, in a denser wedge, it rushed in from all sides onto the platform of the mound. To let another have one's rose at the last. others would, if they could, give kisses to his extremities, either his hands or his clothes. others at least, in the end, most importunately insisted on saluting her from a distance; Again, labor was being performed under the eager rubbing of so many rosaries, crosses, and coins, and once more the Virgin's habit was being tested with scissors. Therefore the prudent prelate, fearful of further violence, turned to the Prior and the more senior Fathers and warned that the present danger must be averted by this second postponement of the burial, saying that he by no means considered that time suitable for the sacred corpse to be carried intact to the grave through such crowds and throngs of people. The Fathers assented to the plan, and immediately, by nods, gestures, and words, persuaded the crowd that Rose's burial, by the will of the most illustrious prelate, was to be deliberately postponed to a more convenient hour. The crowd received this news with the greatest delight, especially when it saw the bishop, having laid aside all his pontifical ornaments, mount a chariot and hurry home.

[15] [that the burial of the body had been delayed again,] A public argument was advanced to confirm the popular credulity: the constant virgin's body, both by its beauty and its miraculous fragrance, seemed to vouch that there would be no danger of deformity, corruption, or foul odor, even if the burial were postponed for a few more days. The unchanged appearance of the deceased Rose had been visible to all eyes until then, just as it had been from the very moment her spirit first departed. For thirty-six hours he had maintained the same placid expression, the beauty of his lips, the lively gleam of his half-open eyes, and the whiteness of his hands. amid so many sighs, breaths, and glances; Under air so humid, hot, and dusty from the reek of the competitors, no soot had settled on Rose's face, no sallowness or ghastly pallor had stained her cheeks. in the jaws, in the nostrils, in the eyelids, nothing flabby, discolored, sad, or emaciated; The same tractability of supple limbs remained, the same pleasantness of a snowy face; Finally, she still seemed not dead, but asleep.

[16] [And with this hope he departs home.] Moreover, the marvelous fragrance of the heavenly scent that emanated from the sacred little body did not even cease in the tomb. For after nineteen months (as will be related in the following chapter), when the rose was exhumed, it retained and diffused the same fragrance that was present at its burial—a fragrance which on the day of burial some perceived as very like that of a rose, others as if composed of a mixture of lilies, roses, balsams, and the entire flower garden. But the more sensible of these judged it to be higher, sweeter, and for us exiles of paradise, a stranger and an unknown. Thus the people, now secure from any further delay, at noon stream from the temple to their own homes. Once the church had been cleared, the doors were, as was customary, drawn to and fastened, and no more opportune hour for Ros's burial could be hoped for than the one when the entire religious congregation was expected to be regularly at lunch. And so, whenever there was a brief respite from the raids, a funeral procession would suddenly be prepared, the bells would fall silent, and the Brothers would chant in a subdued voice. The relics of the precious Virgin Rose are carried into the chapter house. There, reclined in a coffin made of cedar, they are sealed with iron nails on all sides. Finally, the trench is sealed, and the baked bricks, bound with strong mortar, are laid. Thus, with the relics of Rose safely deposited, and all the rites of prayers and hymns from the Order's rubric performed, they proceeded to the refectory.

[17] [But in the meantime a secret burial had taken place,] Yet the crowd did not remain long; by the afternoon, as if by agreement, it poured more numerous toward the temple. But when he saw that the cherished pledge was gone, he rushed in a crowd into the cloister of the peristyle, filled the chapter house, and, crying out "Holy!" to Rose in the loudest voice, while she could do no more, she hastily carried away the dust and earth of the sepulchre as if they were relics. Hence, in a continuous procession of carriages and with an escort, for a month and more, more distinguished persons journeyed to Rose's father's house, to see and venerate the garden cell which the Virgin had inhabited out of a love for solitude. to gather from all sources at the parents of Rosa, and from the treasurer Gundisalvus, any of her personal belongings, garments, or the poorest of furnishings, to be kept as a cherished memory.

[18] The devotion of the people around the sepulchre was increased by daily increments on account of the extraordinary, [& the funeral rites were more tranquilly repeated] which openly multiplied from there, benefits of healing; It did not seem that the due honors for so great a Virgin had been satisfied, until more solemn funeral rites (at which the pro-regent himself had requested to be present) had not yet been celebrated. Thus, the 27th of August was set, as it seemed least to interfere with her own affairs. But since this was a Sunday, which (except for the present deceased) ought not easily to be interspersed with mourning offices, at the archbishop's urging a nearer, less occupied day was chosen. But she was not then free to proceed, and so, after several postponements between this date and that of the archbishop, they both at last, quite unexpectedly, met on the fourth of the following September, which was all the more pleasant for both of them afterward, when they learned that this day, according to the Roman Calendar, was the feast of another St. of the Rose of Viterboc—a day dedicated to the anniversary honor of the other—and that they had not fallen upon it by human counsel, but by divine impulse in unison.

[19] Both were therefore present on the appointed day, together with the entire ecclesiastical and secular magistracy. [On September 4th,] Once again, the entire city, uprooted from its seats, filled the church of Saint Dominic. A speech was delivered there before the assembly on the blessed Rose's wonderful life and praises. The altar's funeral pomp was completed: it tempered the sonorous, confused restlessness of the people with the painful absence of the sacred corpse. But the vows were no less fervently made, and while the priest commended the deceased to the pious Deity at the altar, the people, in no less fitting a voice, commended themselves to Rose.

[20] While these things were happening in Lima, the most celebrated fame of Rose—by which the holiness of this virgin became known everywhere—spread with rapid speed through the towns, villages, and municipalities of almost the entire kingdom. Everywhere, the new Citizen of the Heavens was acclaimed with festive applause and public signs of joy. Potosí, three hundred leagues from Lima, had until then known neither Rosa nor her name. But when he perceived the blessed Virgin's passing, he suddenly, with a loftier impulse, broke forth into a general outburst of congratulations. The bells rang in the towers, the lights twinkled, and voices and hymns of praise resounded. Thus throughout the Peruvian kingdom (even where Rosa, during her life, was utterly unknown) the people celebrated her death with universal joy, acclamation, and vows, because she had popularly comforted them all. They would promise protection and the vote. Nor did the pious trust fail, as will be briefly reviewed in the appendix, Mantissa.

b A peristyle is a place surrounded by columns, called in Greek peristyleon from the prefix peri meaning "around," and the name stylos for column, as if you were to say columniatum, that is, a place enclosed on all sides by columns, such as is commonly found in monasteries.

Section III. By the command of the Archbishop of Lima, after a year and several months, his relics were solemnly translated to a more honorable and conspicuous place.

[When the body of St. Rose rested in a place too hidden;] After the funeral rites were performed so magnificently and splendidly, it was believed that the people would gradually cease from the crowds and importunity, as is the way with most things to languish with the passage of time; But the opposite occurred: for the very fame and multitude of miracles drew ever more people day by day to visit Rose's tomb. Indeed, a common complaint was heard throughout the city that the sacred little body of the Virgin was kept in a place too hidden, where, because of the laws of enclosure, it could not be visited at all hours or by people of every sex. The nobles and leading men gradually came to agree with the complaining commoners. Finally, the prelates of the churches, the superiors of the regulars, the senators, and other men of higher rank, by private ballot, came to this conclusion: that it would be necessary at some point to yield to the unanimous wishes of both the neighbors and the inhabitants. that the transfer of that precious pledge to a more celebrated place must now be seriously and finally considered, and that the common Rose of the Limeños—treasure, solace of the devout, remedy for the afflicted—ought to be dedicated to the public.

But the official decision of the Archbishop of Lima had to be awaited. He, however, informed of the state of the matter and urged on by the most ardent zeal of all, immediately on that same day, February 27, 1619, ordered the decree to be issued in the following terms: "Before the Most Illustrious Lord." To Bartholomew Lobo Guerrero, Archbishop of Lima, counselor to His Majesty, etc. The petition, etc., was read and examined. He said that, considering the reasons stated in it, and by the universal and public acclamation by which throughout this city and kingdom Rosa of St. Mary, a nun of the Third Order of St. Dominic, is proclaimed Saint, he granted permission to transfer her body from the tomb which it now occupied to another suitable place, within S. Dominic's church, where it will seem more fitting to the Father Master F. Augustin de Vega, Provincial of the said Order. Signed by the same Bishop D. in my presence, Doctor Ferdinand Vecerril. For free

[23] [still uncorrupted in the year 1619] Therefore, the very vigil of the holy patriarch Joseph is designated for the act of translation, namely the day of March 18th. For the new bakery could not be finished with its decoration any sooner. Just as the Virgin's death had expressed the death of Christ expiring on the cross through various symbols, so too had her glorious tomb been revealed long before. Now, however, a certain image of the Lord's resurrection was also reviving. For just as Christ lay in the tomb during part of the sixth hour, the entire Sabbath, and at the beginning of the Lord's day, so Rose rested in the sepulchre during part of the year 1617, the whole of 1618, and at the beginning of 1619, in which she was granted neither to see corruption nor did her body come forth any less fragrant than when it entered. The virgin hands alone, worn down by the kisses of the faithful a thousand times (perhaps even pierced on all sides by the eager with iron or teeth), had somewhat lost their original whiteness.

These were the notes taken by those who, on the very morning of the translation, removed Rose's corpse from its old wooden tomb and transferred it to a new one, solemnly carried in a procession, likewise cedar, but gilded inside and out, and secured with a double lock. Thus, with everything duly arranged, on the day above mentioned, the domestic assembly of the religious, crowded on every side by the most numerous throng of the other regulars, proceeded in a straight line from the choir to the chapter house, the standard of the cross preceding with a quaternion of acolytes and two thuribles, the Provincial following in the sacred diptych with the sacred ministers, also vested in precious tunics.

Shortly thereafter the archbishop arrived with his entourage, accompanied by the ordinary judge of the Holy Office, Don Feliciano de Vega, metropolitan canon, by illustrious men, and by the archbishop's vicar general. After the customary prayers were recited, and with the sprinkling and suffumigation completed, six priests, robed and adorned with stoles and maniples, lifted the cedar ark onto their shoulders. the church was thus adorned, draped with tapestries, silk curtains, and linen veils hung from top to bottom. The archbishop pressed on, followed by the vestments-bearers, then the remaining nobles and distinguished men. When the gilded tomb appeared before the temple gates in full view of the assembled multitude, a festive cry from every quarter rose to the heavens, and the mingled shouts of those applauding, weeping, and imploring drowned out all other sound of ecclesiastical song. While the sarcophagus is being lifted before the altar, which has been sumptuously adorned on the high mound for this purpose, the archbishop sits on the throne at the side of the Gospel. The provincial, having put on his cassock, approached the altar with the ministers to celebrate Mass: the trumpeters and singers from the arranged choirs began the symphonic modulations; But the people, intent on touching the icons and rosaries of the Virgin's tomb, were working around the tomb. Thirty wax torches occupied this circuit. Indeed, the tombs were gradually surrounded by twenty-nine silver candelabras, each holding as many two-pound candles. Finally, the entire system of flooring was covered with Phrygian tapestries and with rugs lavishly fringed with gilded braid. Finally, the whole scene seemed less like a funeral than a triumph.

After the Gospel had been chanted, a eloquent preacher appeared on the raised pulpit, [and after various ceremonies] about to speak to the people. He was P. M. Brother Louis of Bilbao, a primarius and full professor at the University of Lima and a censor in the Holy Office, who, having for many years heard the confessions of Rosa, thus had more thoroughly than anyone else explored her spirit, exercises, secret illuminations, and the favors of her Spouse. You would have seen, soon after, everyone stand perfectly still with their ears pricked up. And in the presence of such a talkative crowd, a sudden silence evoked the quiet of a deserted solitude, sustained by nothing but the most ardent desire to listen. There was spoken of the Virgin's baptismal innocence, never violated by any mortal sin; of the immaculate flower of most pure chastity; of the austere severity of penitential exercises; of wondrous illuminations; of seraphic ardors; and of whatever could be compressed within the limits of an hourly discourse. They were few whom, in the presence of the sacred corpse and the recent and vivid memory of its deeds, the public assembly did not pierce to the marrow. Many were also shedding tears. The name of Rosa, repeated by all, was met on the platform with a broad but silent gesture of reverence.

[27] [It is placed in a more conspicuous location.] When the panegyric was finished and the solemn sacrifice at the altar completed, the archbishop, donning his pontifical vestments, proceeded to the tomb with the crozier and pectoral cross, accompanied by the sacred ministers and four of the most dignified members of the metropolitan college. After the remaining rite of prayers, antiphons, and psalms was completed there, the Provincial, together with the other prelates of the religious orders (each wearing a priestly stole), took the bier, lowered from the tomb, upon his shoulders, and thus, with the archbishop present and the magistrates of the various tribunals and the civil court standing around, It was brought to its own crypt, skillfully prepared for this purpose on the right side of the high altar, which, entirely gilded on the inside, reflected the splendors it received from the lights in its arched recess. Outside, it was fortified with iron grates similarly gilded, and the enclosed pledge was as open to view as it was narrow and impassable to seize. Indeed, the influx of devout people, which from that time on was both more free and daily more numerous, testified that nothing could have been more pleasing to them than this translation. to the point that it was finally necessary to consider another translation, lest the services at the high altar be disturbed daily by the undignified throng of people, drawn partly by devotion, partly by the frequent occurrence of miracles and graces.

Notes.

Diplois is a double garment or cloak, as is clear from its Greek etymology: for what the Latins call duplex or duplicatum, the Greeks call diploun. I think that this refers to the sacred or priestly beam, which we commonly call the choral robe.

System, from the Greek *systēma*, besides other meanings, here seems to indicate the structure of a work composed of certain parts, and is derived from the Greek verb *synistēmi*, which means "to constitute."

Peripetasmata are curtains or tapestries, which are hung on the walls for decoration, & hence they are also called by the Greeks peripetasmata, from the verb peripetazein, which means to stretch.

Section IV. Because of the excessive crowd, the burial site was changed again, and the public cult of the people was prudently hindered by the decree of Urban VIII.

[Again the place of the tomb is changed because of the crowds of the people,] St. Jerome protested that he was merely moving from altar to altar for a short time, when he was passing from his treatise on the altar of incense to write the eighth letter to the virgin Demetriada. The same seemed to be the case with the feelings of those who flocked to the Virgin Rose: for they were suddenly diverted from the high altar to the Virgin's nearby tomb, as if to a second altar. There was a perpetual tumult here of votaries offering their vows, of those suspending anathemas, of those laying down their staves and scythes: in short, a different arrangement had to be made for the reverence of the sacred tabernacle, which stood on the high altar, lest the backs of those constantly turned toward the Virgin's sepulchre should be constantly turned away from it, which also inconvenienced the presbytery: for in the Order of Preachers, it is customary to build this at the Epistle corner (as they call it). Therefore, another, more suitable place was finally chosen for the burial of Rosa's remains, namely B itself. The chapel of Catherine of Siena, set quite far to the left of the high altar. It seemed that this bosom of her seraphic Mother owed the Daughter such repose, if repose could be called such, as the daily din and clamors of the devout crowd resounded around, though they were a lesser hindrance to the ecclesiastical rites, for which alone the greatest altars ought to have the freest space.

Meanwhile, letters apostolic arrived in Lima, by which the judges commissioners were authorized to establish a new tribunal there according to the formula prescribed by the Sacred Congregation of Rites, and the judges legitimately constituted, for the purpose of examining witnesses concerning the life and deeds of the handmaid of God, Sister Rose of Saint Mary. And so the examination was begun on May 17, 1630. But because of the multitude of witnesses (there were one hundred eighty-three) and various obstacles delaying the process, the inquiry lasted until May 1632; to which, as was customary, was added a visitation of the tomb and relics of the Servant of God. Therefore, the deputies and inspectors John of Texeda and John of Vega, doctors of medicine; John of Valenzuela and Bartholomew of Tivico, secretaries; together with Aloysius of Molina, surgeon.

[30] Before Father Master F. Gabriel de Zarate, then Provincial, [while examining the fragrant body of the Saint,] they opened the wooden chest and found the virgin's little body, almost fifteen years after her death, with all her garments consumed, but her bones intact and still covered here and there with flesh that was dry. Moreover, they exhaled a fragrance, not the kind usually associated with corpses, but that of roses dried in the sun. Yet the foremost of the physicians swore that he had infused a peculiar fragrance which, while not merely wonderfully refreshing the senses of those present, had also unexpectedly flooded their spirits with solace and, by a mysterious sense of devotion, had moved them all.

[31] From there the same inspectors went to the chapter house to visit first the Virgin's sepulchre, from which the faithful eagerly gathered dust to ward off various kinds of diseases. There, one by one, they explored by hand the depth of a small hollow, from which no more than four or five pounds of earth appeared to have been dug up or carried away. Moreover, it was clear from numerous testimonies that several bushels of powder had been dug up from that very opening (the only one that lay open where the head of the deceased Virgin had lain) and had been distributed in various ways throughout the kingdom. Hence it was not a slight conjecture that, just as with St. Raymond, so too this was divinely granted to the old tomb of the Rose, that in the place of the dust that was removed, another springs up continuously and perpetually.

[32] It finally happened in the year 1640 that the General Procurator of the Order of Preachers in the Roman Curia, [because of a decree of Urban VIII public worship is impeded,] in a private letter written to Lima, admonished the Fathers that the most exact account must be taken of the apostolic Constitution of Urban VIII, emanated on July 5, 1634, which begins: Cælestis Hierusalem cives &cd. The Fathers were distressed that for nearly six years no copy whatsoever, nor even the slightest rumor of such a Constitution, had become known to the Limeños until that day; but what wonder, given the vast distance of the regions and the many enemy fleets, pirate ambushes, and frequent perils of shipwreck? However, the Fathers, deeming it necessary to prepare at least a private draft of that letter at once, in accordance with the norm of the said Constitution, abolished that very night whatever might have seemed untimely in the venerable cult of Rose.

[33] [And it was with difficulty that the inhabitants of Lima were persuaded,] This sudden change seemed intolerable to the devoted people the next day; She sought her usual refuge and solace, Rosa. She was emulating this other Catherine of Lima after Saint Catherine of Siena. The chapel is filled with complaints, and what is usually the rashness of the common people in suspecting and judging under the guise of piety is spread abroad, that the venerable relics of Rose were either stolen from the Fathers without their knowledge, or, with their knowledge, secretly destined for Spain. Hence the credulous and ignorant multitude gathers in a throng, the uproar swells, and the danger to the harmless religious is not in the least diminished, while the rough and impetuous mob, already fiercely resorting to insults and threats, at the first explanation either did not grasp or refused to accept the reason for the matter.

[34] [that they should await the judgment of the Apostolic See.] At last, and not without effort, it was persuaded to the importunate that it was fitting to advise the more illustrious, who are expected to receive the honors of the Rose from the apostolic throne, that there is no detriment to the obedience of the Roman decrees, that disobedience cannot please the holy Virgin, and that her precious relics are preserved for the Limeños in the most secure place under the guard of the first sepulchre. But concerning those (the mind of the Supreme Shepherd having already perceived the like), nothing outside the line of sacred rites is to be heard or permitted. The will of the people was most reluctantly acquiesced to, content with this one thing: that the relics of his Rose were safe.

Thymiama is a most fragrant incense, which Calmet describes in his Biblical Dictionary as follows: Thymiama was a kind of fragrant ointment made from stacte, onyx, or scented nail, and galangal, all in equal weight. It was a sacred and religious matter, the private use of which was forbidden under capital punishment. In the morning and evening of each day it was placed on the golden altar in the sanctuary, and the duty of carrying it fell to the priests serving the weekly office.

Chapter Two
Posthumous glory from the illustrious apparitions of the saints and various miracles.

Section I. Frequent apparitions of the Saint, attested by many to whom she made herself visible.

[Glorious after death] Aloysia of Serrano (whom I remember above in chapter XVIII) Just as Rose had been familiar and dear to a mortal, so she merited to be honored more familiarly by the immortal through frequent appearances. Among these, the most illustrious was that in which she saw before the divine throne the most august Queen of Heaven standing, and holding in her hand a crown of wondrous splendor, as though awaiting a new guest to be solemnly crowned upon the rim of that throne. On the other side, a snow-white choir of virgins, having enclosed the newly deceased Rose in their midst, escorted her to the Virgin Mary with a cheerful and melodious procession. Each virgin held in her hand a palm frond, at the tip of her crown. Rosa was yielding the palm, like the others, but not yet the crown.

[36] Aloysia, overwhelmed with joy and astonishment, while she gazes at the rest of the coronation spectacle, [Aloysia de Serrano's] vision vanished. But on the third day after, Rose's apparition filled them with joy. Surely Aloysia then saw Rosa, in the same bridal and most festive procession of virgins as the day before, beautifully surrounded, and now adorned not only with the palm but also with a crown of immense glory, to which the ranks of angels stationed there separately applauded, and the blessed multitude of many saints most sweetly congratulated her with public jubilation. Aloysia, returned to herself but not trusting herself, submitted both visions to the secret examination of her spiritual Fathers; and they, by their unanimous judgment, declared them to have been divinely inspired and free from all danger of deceit.

[37] A man, [another anonymous one,] secretly confessed to Qestor Gundisalvo de la Massa (who before the apostolic judges testified this both in word and in writing), a man renowned in those days for his expertise in mystical theology and the sublimity of his contemplation, that within the three weeks immediately following Rose's death, the glory of this Virgin had appeared to him at least twenty-two times, by an insight now imaginative, now intellectual; but that among the other visions one had excelled as purely intellectual, in which the virgin brightness of its purity, with its inestimable grace and most flourishing aureola, was displayed without any corporeal form, and through the simple radiation of tranquil knowledge. He considered this mode of revelation, as plainly perfected and of a higher order, to be ineffable and most worthy of such an object, and, as was fitting, he esteemed it.

[38] Doctor John del Castillo, a physician, in the fifteenth year after the Virgin's happy passing, swore before the apostolic tribunal of the second examination that the Rose had appeared to him by an imaginary vision in the midst of a most brilliant light, which itself reflected the clarity and likeness of the Divine Majesty. In the most delightful center of this scene appeared Rosa, clad in the habit of her holy Father Dominic, which shone and radiated wonderfully, whose incomparable beauty she declared she could not express in words. The face of the Virgin shone with infinite grace and splendor. Countless white and purple roses had surrounded his body on all sides. A branch occupied the right hand, the index of the most brilliant virginity. Nor did the branch shine any less than the roses; continuously, dense flashes of glory sparkled from it. Quin, and on several occasions under such a vision, Rosa gently addressed her spectator, unfolding to him many sublime things about the lofty pinnacle of her happiness, which cannot be expressed in the idiom of human language.

[39] [appeared more often,] A certain one of the Virgin's confessors received from the mouth of the same doctor that the glorious apparition of Blessed Rose had shone upon him from heaven more than fifty times. He himself, of his own accord, in the final deposition of the year 1631 before the same judges, most solemnly affirmed that, for a full space of six months, day and night, he had been divinely admitted to the spectacle of such great beatitude, and that as often as he raised his mind to the innermost recesses of contemplation at the appointed hours, the Rose was shown to him among the ranks of the blessed. As this gracious frequency of apparitions gradually ceased, Rosa nevertheless, by the law of urbanity, did not stop entrusting her turns to the heavenly youth for several days thereafter. He was an angel, a tender twelve-year-old boy in appearance, who visited the doctor named Rosa every day and always revealed something new about her ineffable glory. And so, these visions were finally brought to a gentle end.

[40] [and was seen by others] Moreover, it was granted to several others to see the same Rose in her Spouse's paradise, surrounded by flourishing roses and adorned with a golden diadem, especially to a certain widow, then celebrated for the renown of her virtues, to whom Rose often appeared in a heavenly light, not without the dutiful escort of myriads of angels. Once, in an imaginary vision, the virgin spoke thus: "We must work, O mother, for the heavenly reward is a very great thing." I have indeed been amply rewarded here for my labor. He said, his face stretched into a festive, clear-skied smile, and looking exceedingly handsome in the snowy whiteness of his Dominican habit. Sometimes it seemed to her that Rose was pleasantly strolling in the heavenly gardens with Christ, who placed the Virgin a few paces ahead of him. Sometimes she could be seen delighting among carnations and lilies, as if competing with them for the undying prerogative of floral loveliness.

[41] [The Peruvian kingdom] I consider it worthy of no superficial consideration that in these visions I find evidence of Rosa's affection and concern for her homeland. The aforementioned widow, a few days after the Virgin's death, was praying privately in the church and was earnestly commending the city of Lima and the kingdom of Peru to the Rose, whom she had seen several times in the choir of the Blessed. Soon, in a flash of intellectual vision, she saw Rose shining with divine light. and she heard him answer her kindly: "It will surely be so, O sister, and whatever is fitting for the glory of this most sweet Deity, whom I enjoy, that supreme goodness will grant at my request." I clearly remember those things that were recommended to me to ask for.

[42] Another vision, which is reported in the Process from the sworn deposition of Sister Catherine of St. Mary, is consistent with this vision; [and to commend the city of Lima to God,] of course; The blessed Rose had often appeared (but in a dream) to a certain pious person, showing how pleasant and good her affairs were. Twice, however, she appeared to the same person, brighter and more illustrious than usual. First, when she came to comfort his spirit amid the adversities he was then suffering most severely; second, when he saw her clearly in the air above the Virgin's tomb, kneeling with her knees bent in the manner of a supplicant, interceding with God for Lima, her beloved homeland. It is no wonder, then, that on these two occasions Rose shone more gloriously than usual: for when in the first instance she was speaking of patience, she had to show herself adorned with the same special privilege of patience as her seraphic Mother. In the latter, which concerned his country, his most ardent love for his fellow citizens was, of course, to shine more brightly after his death. Moreover, to establish the sincerity of the vision, experts found in the woman the true criteria of divine revelation: a deeper humility, the recognition of her own nothingness, a lofty tranquility of mind, and a spiritual joy inflamed by new fires of divine love.

[43] What I am about to relate is even more prodigious, and the result of the beneficial concurrence of many signs. [and to a certain Christopher de Ortega,] Father Master Friar Augustine de Vega, of the Order of Preachers, then the Peruvian Provincial, was lying in the convent of the Holy Rosary in Lima, and was already preparing for his final struggles. It was proclaimed that the man's life was at stake, that he had stopped taking his medicine, and that the sick man had been abandoned by all the doctors. Rosa alone, now safe in paradise, took on the entire care of the sick man. Far from his home, Christopher de Ortega, a layman of below-average fortune, was sleeping. This Rose, who lies with him at night, clearly reveals herself. He commands that at first light he go to the monastery, visit the dying Provincial, and announce to him that from this, however much to be lamented, infirmity he is by no means to depart from this life. that he must first sweat for a time under the episcopal miter for the glory of God, before he is freed from the troubles of this mortal life.

[44] Having said this, the glorious Virgin disappeared. But Christopher, so swiftly deprived of the presence of such wondrous sweetness and heavenly, gracious dignity, [the dying Provincial of the Preachers] broke out into cries: "Ah!" He said, "Saint Virgin Rose has now departed from me." Woken by this outcry, Thomas de Mesa's son, who was sleeping nearby, learned the whole story from his father. But, disbelieving, he earnestly implored his father not to rashly believe the vanity of his dreams. The father replied that he, for his part, had not seen and heard Rosa earnestly instructing him in these matters in a dream, but with his senses fully alert and unrestrained. His son was nearby, so at least for the time being he gave himself over to rest and sleep. Christopher denied that he could. Thus, deeply occupied with ruminating on the vision, he awaited the day with impatient vigilance. As soon as it dawned, urged by her son, she said and did everything to keep her husband from leaving home to relate the vision. now with entreaty, now with reproach, interrupting the man, lest he bring ridicule upon himself and his family with the most inept account of a deceptive dream. Nothing would be more certain than that it would itself be accused of feverish enthusiasm or delirium.

But Christopher, as tenacious of his purpose as he was certain of the truth of the nocturnal vision, approached the monastery with cheerful confidence, [he ordered that he be informed of a longer life.] greeted the infirm, and laid out his commands with such steadfastness of brow and security of spirit that, in a few words or even a single one, the speaker's alacrity alone dispelled all doubt in the minds of the hesitant. What a wonder! They did not delay to bring manifest experiments to bear on the words to give them full credence: for from that very moment when Christopher had ceased to speak, the sick Provincial began to notice the disease's departure by gradual signs. Then, commending himself to the virgin Rose, he recovered completely. Finally, having been raised to the episcopal mitre in Paraguay, he, full of days, fell asleep in the Lord.

[46] [St. Rose was also seen by certain persons;] Doctor Balthasar de Padilla, a canon and penitentiary of the metropolitan church of Lima, by order of his archbishop had gone to the house of a certain widow with a notary, to receive under oath whatever was brought concerning Rose's life and miracles. Thus, as the examination proceeded, the widow's bright Rose appeared, her face cheerful and familiarly pleasant, her gestures and encouraging nods seeming to embolden the woman to speak the things that were asked, and to ratify each item by approving the praises that were inscribed on the tablets. Separately, she later revealed this to the apostolic judges in 1630, adding; The virgin then appeared to him in that same attire in which she was accustomed to be recognized and wrapped while alive, yet her face was much more cheerful and open, as if she were breathing forth grace in return for the homage.

[47] [which before the apostolic judges] Another person, zealous in spiritual exercises, happened to be seen elsewhere in a visionary apparition of the Blessed. I brought Rosa to the place which the judges then appointed for receiving the witnesses' answers and depositions concerning her. It seemed as though the Virgin herself was pacing the chamber, carefully surveying everything, arranging each object in order with her white hands, polishing and adorning them, and at the same time signaled that she was doing this because on that day she was to receive from mortals the just tributes of honor and truth. It is very similar to what D. Maria de Usategui Gundisalvi, the quæstor's wife, reports, adding that she saw the glorious Virgin in that role eagerly preparing the scrivener's instruments for the notaries and arranging the chart, proclaiming the joy with which she was delighted by the earthly juridical confirmation of the divine benefits bestowed upon her. However, whether this vision is different from the previous one or the same, I cannot easily tell.

[48] [They appeared for the purpose of bearing witness,] It must not, however, be omitted that in the year 1631 this happened to a professed nun of the monastery of the Most Holy Trinity in Lima, Maria de Bustamante, as she herself testified under oath. In good faith she had explained before the apostolic judges in the case of the Venerable Rose everything she knew. For this sex, as it were, easily trembles at the stern religion of a public oath, and, as if under the thunderbolts of censure, fears its own conscience. After the examination, anxious scruples assailed Mary, lest she might have said more or less than the exact truth and rigor of that tribunal demanded, either through inexperience in answering or through a stumble of the tongue or memory. The poor one wavered between the rock and the sacred. Hence, a restlessness of spirit urged him to either moderate or retract some of the things he had said. but, with the utmost reluctance and modesty, he was at a loss, not knowing specifically what he was required to correct in his own words. Hence, my mind dictated that no retraction was necessary. Meanwhile, the delicate conscience lacked secure tranquility.

[49] While in the midst of these anxieties, one night, as he had fallen asleep in his solitary cell, [to advance the cause of his Beatification,] he felt himself moved and commanded by another, and, without any frightening alarm, was most gently awakened from his sleep. Awaken, she sat up in her bed, and with her slumber easily shaken off, she sat in silence and with attention, wondering to what, from where, and by whom she had been roused from her sleep at that hour. And as she thus turned all things over in her mind, listening and hesitating, behold a gentle and most sweet voice floated to her, saying: "Doubt not, Mary, nor be troubled: for Rosa is truly holy." How strange! At that very moment Mary felt herself freed from her scruples; anxiety and the dread of fear vanished, and the most tranquil serenity returned to her heart, so that she was able to spend the rest of the night in the placid silence of joyful security, certain that such a sudden peace of mind could not have been poured into her from above. She also knew that the pangs of her conscience were known to no one but herself and the heavens; and since she was in the habit of being startled by any unfamiliar sound, especially at night, at the aforementioned voice she not only was not startled at all, but was in fact completely reassured.

[50] More of this kind would remain; but let the coronet be placed upon Diego Hyacinth Pacecus of Seville, [and the amanuensis working on the same case], who in Lima, living on the daily earnings of the art of hypography, was employed by Diego Morales, notary in the case of the venerable Rosa, to copy and transcribe the volumes of the Processes or Acts. He was therefore ordered, in the aforementioned case, to complete two thousand pages in writing within a fixed period of time, on the condition that he not use the fluid, loose, and easy handwriting to which he had always been accustomed, but another, more fussy, square, and cramped style, which the common scribes there call NOTHUM. The writer obeyed. but on the first day he began the work, he despaired of ever being able to write out so many leaves, overwhelmed by the difficulty of the unfamiliar script: for indeed (as he himself eloquently deposed before the apostolic judges under oath) his fingers grew so weary while writing, his arm grew numb, and his nerves were strained, that he often exclaimed he feared that from such great fatigue the pulse of his hand would fail sooner, and he would lose the use of his hand, than he would complete the assigned task.

[51] So one day, having labored at writing from early dawn until evening, his strength for writing quickly exhausted and his whole arm aching, he went to bed, uncertain whether he might not be too weak to write the next day. Dozing off from boredom, he saw the happy Rose entering toward him with a calm and festive step, her face benevolent and joyful, which could in no way be frightening. And so that she might be more surely recognized, she appeared in that likeness, bearing the same dress and adornment, just as our scribe had seen her skillfully depicted on a tablet a few days earlier. Both were silent. But Rosa, gently approaching, kindly seized the right arm of the reclining figure between the elbow and the fist, squeezed it firmly but gradually, and held it so for a long time, and only after a very long delay released it most peacefully, and at the same time vanished. Waking up, Pacecus, ignorant of the mystery, pondered in his mind what this sweet apparition had meant for him, what the grasp of the arm, what that prolonged and tenacious constriction. "Or perhaps," he said, "aware of my instability and of my nature, by which I am wont to pass easily from one city to another, from kingdom to kingdom, she admonished me of constancy, she commanded firmness."

[52] [He supplied it in a marvelous way.] As he dressed himself amid these fluctuations of thought, he felt his right arm more agile than usual; Not yet suspecting what the matter was, he once again applied himself entirely to the writing he was to continue, just as he had the day before. He wrote from dawn until the evening ringing of the Angelus, with no fatigue in his fingers, no torment of his nerves, his arm exceedingly light and unusually agile. He recognized from that moment the timely benefit of the salutary apparition, devoted all his remaining days to laborious writing with scarcely any effort, and discovered an inexhaustible strength bestowed upon his hand by the virgin contact of the imaginary vision. Thus he completed the work of two thousand pages, which now seemed to him few in comparison with the admirable ease of writing. When the volume was finished, all who knew Pace were astonished, and they gazed at his portrait, the character so hastily, fluidly, and tirelessly daubed across so many pages. Hence, we must move on to greater miracles.

Notes.

Aureola is a church term that signifies a reward justly bestowed. It is commonly said of the rewards of the heavenly, concerning which Joseph Angles, in the Glossary under the word aureola, writes that on the bodies of virgins, martyrs, and doctors there will be certain insignia, by which they will display that they have such aureolas in their souls; Each virgin will wear a small white crown on her head, the martyrs a red one, and the doctors a green one.

Sanctuary (Gr. ἄδυτον) is the most secret part of the temple, to which no one has access except the priest. Henceἄδυτον is said, as it were, to be inaccessible, from the privative alpha, & the verb δύω to enter.

c Caryophyllus is the same as caryophyllus, which we have discussed above in the Annotations.

Enthusiasm is properly a certain divine vigor, such as is found in the sayings or writings of those who are inspired by divine spirit, from ἐνθουσιάζω, which means to be inspired by spirit. Among physicians it is regarded as a fanatical shock or a foolish persuasion, as Gorræus and Castellus relate. Hence it is clear in what sense that word is used here.

Paraguay, or properly speaking the province of Paraguaia, is situated between Brazil to the east and the Paraguay River to the west. Although this region is almost devoid of cities, it is nevertheless distinguished by episcopal dignity under the Archbishop of Plata, and its bishop resides in the town of Assumption.

Hispalis is the most famous city of Baetica in Spain, from which that man was, by chance, a native. I added, perhaps, because there is a small town of the same name in North America, situated in the Gulf of Mexico, which the Spaniards once built.

This man made a living from a small hypographic art, that is, the art of transcription; for ὑπογράφω means "I transcribe." Hence, a scribe is a copyist who takes dictation or copies the first edition of a book.

Section II. The spiritual benefits by which the holy Virgin brought great sinners to penance, and others lukewarm to a more perfect life.

[Through the intercession of St. Rose of Lima] How and how great a work of divine omnipotence the conversion of a sinner is, and how far it surpasses the creation of heaven and earth, and also when it itself is to be considered miraculous, let anyone who wishes read in St. Thomas, Part I. two. question One hundred thirteen. Article 72 nine. and 10. From these, to the Rose, the celebrated advocate of sinners before God, that they may be converted and live. In his encyclical letter to the entire Peruvian Province of the Order of Preachers, written on September 1, 1617, Father Francis Nicholas de Aguero—then presiding as Vicar General—strictly recounts the life, death, and miracles of the admirable Rose, among other things, that upon the mere contact of the virgin's little body in the coffin, the greatest number were so struck by a sudden spur of compunction that they immediately, right there before the entire multitude, began to denounce their sins in loud voices, to deplore their past life, to break into groans and sobs, and to humble themselves before the Most High in a tearful confession of their crimes, as the countless crowd of onlookers heard, saw, and marveled.

[54] Some (as is the way) of a more dissolute life, moved only by a spirit of curiosity, [great sinners repented] so that they might gaze more closely with others at the dead, most beautiful Virgin in the coffin, At the first glance they were seized with a sudden and deep sorrow for their sins, their faces were bathed in tears, protesting that they were departing with a spirit quite different from the one with which they had come, and that from then on they would devote themselves effectively to reforming their lives and their morals. Perhaps God, the Father of mercies, had foreseen this, so that for nearly two full days the little body of his Rose would lie openly in a tomb, unburied, so that her thorns might become a means of salvation for so many: for, as the honey-tongued Bernard says, "You will never be pricked more happily than when you are pierced."

[55] The Mother of the Virgin Mary of Oliva, on February 15, 1618, before the deputied judges, among other things recounted, [(add to these lukewarm religious stirred to fervor)] that after the funeral of her Daughter, at successive intervals, several devout persons had come to her, even from the families of the Regular Orders, before completely unknown to her, who, having come seeking to relieve their notorious poverty with some liberal aid, each in turn professed that they were paying these tributes of pure gratitude to the Virgin Rose, whose intercessions, knew they owed the best change in their lives to the virgin Rose, and were joyful, now being different from what they had been, and asserted that they had attained this happiness only after imploring Rose's intercession in the heavenly court. It is certainly a more laborious and perhaps rarer miracle for a spirit, languid and tepid in its obedience to God, to be suddenly lifted up to the serious pursuits of religious perfection and to be powerfully enkindled, than for a criminal to be brought back from the enormity of his crime to any measure of fruit; for the former generally knows his own peril, whereas the latter sinks deeper into inertia and security.

[56] It is worthy of admiration above many things that Father Francis Bartholomew Martinez, formerly confessor of the Rose and then prior of the Dominican convent of Saint Mary Magdalene in Lima, revealed concerning a certain notorious sinner, from his own certain knowledge (as he affirms), to the same judges, in the same month and year. [as is proven by a singular conversion] A certain man of depraved conscience did indeed frequently partake with others of the sacraments of life-giving penance and the Eucharist, but only to his own soul's death and destruction: for he had never, in the entire course of his sacrilegious life, made a proper and complete confession, and for a very long stretch of years he had wallowed in this mire. Crimes were piling up on crimes. but it seemed that everyone had become calloused by synteresis. Rather, it grew more brutish with each passing day, and its impiety, having sunk to the depths, held nothing sacred. Yet the piety of others did not fail, which would commend the most wretched Rose, newly interred.

[57] [of a certain stubborn sinner,] Soon he, as if awakening from a long-standing lethargy, began to feel within the hiss of that subtle and most powerful breeze, by which God breathes upon the hearts of the obstinate, so that they may melt like wax. The unfortunate one shuddered when he looked around and saw himself immersed in such a filthy and inescapable labyrinth. But divine goodness, together with the keenest stings of penitence, instilled confidence in mercy. What's the matter? He made a general confession of all the sins of his life with the most bitter sense of sorrow. He did not omit any of the most horrific and outrageous crimes, having surpassed all measure and number. And the fear of the Lord softened the callousness that had hardened her conscience, so that from then on she most timidly felt even the slightest transgressions as though they were enormous, and hastened to wash them away.

[58] [& before the apostolic judges] The same P. Martinez in the same examination solemnly asserted that it was clear to him, not only from his own experience in hearing confessions, but also from the unanimous testimony of many confessors, that after imploring the help of the Rosary in heaven, countless numbers both in Lima and throughout the entire kingdom, from the lowest abyss of sins, they had emerged into the harbor of true penitence, and had purified their souls with sincere confession and tears. As early as December 11, 1617, Father Anthony de la Vega Loaysa of the Society of Jesus, formerly commissioner of the Holy Office and successive rector of various colleges, had already informed the judges of this very fact, and he also warned that this kind of favor should be regarded as one of the strongest and most excellent arguments for solid sanctity, rightly counted among the common judgments of the holy Doctors. The same report was formally signed in that tribunal by P. Fr. Franciscus Nieto, & master Fr. John of Lorenzana, formerly confessor of both Virgins, about whom mentioned above.

[59] [Various confessors have testified in general,] Father Fr. agrees with these in every respect. Peter de Loaysa adds two things worthy of note. First, a few days after the Virgin's death, there was such a great frequency of these conversions that in Lima, the public shops where discipline whips, hair belts, and various kinds of hair shirts were sold, these instruments of mortification and penance suddenly ran out due to the great number of buyers. The other was that he himself, of the many who then turned to serious repentance, knew two notable sinners, whom the devious, knotted nets of hell had most tenaciously entangled in the debauchery of a shameful life. But both, moved by the fragrant fame of the Rose and commended by her powerful prayers before the throne of the Most High, happily cast off the knots of impurity and firmly fixed their foot in the path of virtue. It was not possible, as in the miraculous cures of the body, to proceed in these treatments of the soul to the individual names of the persons; indeed, it was not fitting.

[60] Let it suffice, Lima and other ministers of sacramental penance throughout the entire region of Peru—whom experience has taught that reform of life—that, having received general indications, they were astonished at such a great change of morals in the people from the day on which Rosa began to act as advocate for her fellow citizens in her Spouse's paradise. You would have seen women, casting aside the luxury of their clothing, eagerly displaying a simpler and more modest attire at home and abroad. you would have heard in the cloisters and cells of the regulars, at night, in every corner, penitence, like a tympanistress, declaring war on acedia and tepidity with bloody blows of the flagellum. You would have found the confessionals everywhere besieged by groans and sighs.
