Finally, this prodigy was so publicly known and so frequently spoken of by the people, that the aforementioned Martinez, a man of the greatest gravity and renowned among the most eminent for his fame of religion and piety, did not hesitate in the said tribunal, under oath, to affirm, that in his opinion, since the time when Peru was discovered and opened up to us, no preacher had ever shone forth who had urged those peoples to virtue with so many and such great incentives, or who had kindled such a universal spirit of penance and such a brilliant fire of devotion. How remarkable that the salutary thorns of a single rose were enough to bring about the repentance of so many.

It happened, as related before the judges by the wife of Quaestor Gundisalvi, that two matrons happened to sit down to chat. [The Holy Spirit kindled the woman to the love of God.] The other of them, picking up a little book that happened to lie nearby, saw that it briefly contained the life, deeds, and death of the virgin Rose. She began, then, to read aloud leisurely, while the other listened. But this almost happened to two courtiers who were reading the life of St. Anthony the Abbot out of mere curiosity: for while the one was reading, as it were, to while away the time, the other was being kindled as it were by the torches of heavenly flames; he felt that he was gradually becoming unfit for such unusual, and indeed divine, ardors. He nevertheless concealed it, so as not to inappropriately interrupt the fiery sweetness of the precious lesson. Meanwhile, the flame of charity grew, and the secret fire could no longer be hidden. The woman exclaimed, not accustomed to thus melting and bubbling in God. Both are amazed that the spark had been contained in those rose-colored little leaves of the brief pages. But he who first said that the lamp of love was fittingly called Rose did not fully understand it, Philostratus.

Father John de Villalobos of the Society of Jesus, [our John de Villalobos,] Rector of the novitiate house of Lima, a man most highly trained in the ascetic palestra, recounted these things about himself before the same tribunal of judges. that he had been present as Rose drew her last breath, near death, and on that occasion had secretly asked of the dying woman that, as soon as he reached the Spouse's heavenly bridal chamber, he would strive to obtain for himself from the Divine that free and singular gift which he then desired in his mind. The Virgin, agonizing intrepidly, promised, and a little after she had expired; "I discovered (he said) by clear indications that this gift had been miraculously bestowed upon my spirit through Rose's intercession in heaven."

[63] But the quaestor Gundisalvus affirms under oath that several, both secular and Regular, confessed to him, [and obtains promised gifts for others,] to whom the Virgin, when asked, had promised the same in their agony, immediately after her blessed passing they felt from the heavenly myrrh-bearing of the Holy Spiritd the secret anointings of their hearts, unusual restraints of heavenly lights, the white computations of a new name, which (as we are warned in the Apocalypse 2.5 17) No one knows, except the one who receives. Surely these were the apophoreta that Rosa had promised, while, dying, she had said she was being summoned to the solemn feast. The above-mentioned Father Anthony de la Vega of the Society of Jesus, and others, confirm these things (but briefly). But let us return to the softening of obstinate hearts, accomplished through the mediation of the Rose.

[64] [and in favor of a certain Francis,] Maria de Xuara, a very wealthy and opulent woman, was a maternal aunt of Francisco and Alexander de Coloma, the two brothers, but her spirit was implacably hostile and obstinate toward her cousins. Francis likewise supported six cousins and two cousins-in-law in Lima at his own expense. But the stubborn woman was not swayed by any of them, so much so that she would not even deign to speak to them. Indeed, when she made her will, she took great care not to leave anything to any of the aforementioned (even though the latter eight were notoriously in need), but did not even mention them. Francis and Alexander were eventually forced to depart for elsewhere for a while, leaving so many needy cousins behind in Lima.

[65] [who had implored her patronage,] Here Francis, bitterly commiserating his orphans and not knowing where to turn, implored the Rose, the sole Ariadne of his perplexity, and found her: she herself, namely, on the night before her departure, Anxiously commending to the Virgin (whose image he had before him) all his miserable afflictions, he implored that by her intercession with the Almighty disposer of hearts she would soften the stony hardness of his avaricious heart, restore the sense of blood and humanity to his benumbed uncle, and come to the aid of the innocent and those endangered by the slipperiness of poverty. What a wonder! The next day, at the crack of dawn, she summoned Francis, whom she had refused to see for a full twenty-eight years. He told his visitor that he had spent a most restless, sad, and sleepless night, with the desolation of his cousins constantly turning over in his mind. whom he considered, in every human respect, as needy as they were destitute. Therefore, as soon as he returned home, he would bring the entire band of these wretched people before him, wanting to embrace them all in turn and to feed them honorably in his house.

[66] [Changes her obstinate aunt's will.] Furthermore, she instructed Francis to summon a public notary, saying she would draw up another will before him, rescinding the previous one. Franciscus, unable to contain so many joys at once, and having silently gone to his aunt to fetch Mother Rosa, hurriedly and willingly brought the miserable crowd. Here one goes into kisses, into sweet tears, into tender embraces; Eight needy people are taken into the household; an inofficious will is changed on the spot; cousins are written into the will as heirs to the entire estate; Finally, to Francis himself (for he was a priest by order) a yearly income of one hundred and forty patacones (as they are called) is assigned under the title of chaplaincy, with certain houses mortgaged. While Dioscorides obscurely hints that roses have the power of a philter to subdue, soothe, and conciliate hearts, our Lima Rose makes it clearer.

[A woman averse to the Dominican institute,] Ludovica Barba had nothing less on her mind than to assume the white habit of St. Dominic. Rosa, while she was alive, had at times foretold to her confessor and had firmly asserted that Ludovica would be entirely bound to that habit, indeed that she would ardently pursue it. When this became known to Ludovica from the mouth of her confessor, she began anxiously to fear the fulfillment of the prophecy, and to loathe the habit itself with all her heart: Indeed, if from a distance any mention of undertaking it arose, or a thought of it drifted in, she would stiffen in terror at the mere prospect, her spirit would rebel, and her heart would harden. Meanwhile, Rosa closed the last day; And as if the death of the Seer were the death of prophecy, Ludovica began to cast off the useless fear, secure that such a purpose could never enter her own mind. It is easier for poets to create fabulous metamorphoses than for such a change in one's own heart to actually occur.

[68] But the woman was undone by her own confidence in her resistance and her certainty of obstinacy; [she is brought to the Third Order of St. Dominic,] for behold, suddenly, at the Virgin's death, that stronghold of contumacy withered away, the age-old horror vanished, and the door of her heart opened of its own accord to heavenly inspiration. He cried out with Paul, "Lord, what do you want me to do?" Then he went to the tomb of Rose, humbly commended the Virgin's cause, and asked that the Spouse grant her a fitting choice of that state which would best enable her to render divine homage and advance her on the path to eternal salvation. Without delay: he heard Ludovica Rosa, the Rose of God. and suddenly a fiery yet foreign desire for the sacred Dominican habit poured into his heart. The woman was suddenly seized by a burning heat, she bubbled and burned with that effect which she had always shunned, and if she had ever feared even to think of it lightly, she now feared more grievously that the opportunity to carry out her pious purpose might slip away or be delayed. She was tormented by a suspicious anxiety, as if the happy hour that would make her wish come true could not draw near. Thus, upon catching sight of her confessor from a distance, she hurried over, poured out her heart, and with many tears obtained the favor of being admitted that very day into the number of the Tertiaries, taking the name of Louise of St. Mary. Moreover, as soon as she found herself in the white habit of her Father Saint Dominic, she was completely filled with abundant consolations from on high, and could scarcely express the soul's most contented delight, grieving only that she had long stood in the way of such great happiness with a fearful and obstinate heart.

[69] Ludovica de Mendoza, wife of Alfonso Gonzalez de santo Martino, [and another who doubted her sanctity], a little after the Virgin's death, seeing her celebrated by the people with so many encomiums, incredulous—whether from the feminine delight in resisting or from the stubborn chill of pious affection—could neither conceive nor would she believe in her sanctity. For how much perfection (she thought) could a frail girl, barely having passed the age of thirty-one, achieve in so short a span of life? Where, then, for the Roses—born and raised among the common people, supported by the labor of their own hands, living and dying in a secular home—was there a school of such virtues, or a school of such sublime contemplations, readily at hand? They will cry out for miracles. But Ludovica hesitated. The more the people cheered, the more she herself grew deaf; therefore, the one who had so fortified her heart against believing had to be struck with a stronger ram, so that she would surrender.

She was seized by a sudden anguish, so that she believed herself to have been maimed in hands and feet. [He punishes with sickness, and soon he heals the penitent.] Nor was that enough: he felt a storm more severe in his own spirit; A fog, fear, and turmoil had besieged the mind. The heart was shaken, as if by an earthquake. At last, clearly recognizing this, she suddenly abandoned her single-minded, stubborn unbelief, let go of her hardness, and with a trembling heart lifted to God, she humbly declared that she believed and with her whole heart confessed that Rosa was truly holy. And suddenly the dreadful commotion ceased, the storm fell silent, and serene tranquility returned. Thus the Groom, to whom the winds and the sea obey, zealous for the honor of the Bride, was zealous. But from spiritual to corporal benefits, one must ultimately descend here.

Notes.

Synteresis is derived from the verb *syntereo*, which I observe and revere. Hence conscience, or that faculty of the soul which opposes vices and immediately reproves committed sins, is called by the Greeks synteresis, that is, as it were a reverent observance, which we commonly call the reproach of conscience.

Saint Augustine, Book Chapter 8 of the Confessions 6 narrates the story of these two courtiers, who, upon reading the life of St. Anthony the Abbot, were so inflamed with zeal for imitation that it will be recorded on the 28th of this month in the Acts of St. Augustine.

In Philostratus's Letter 27, roses are called ἔρωτος αἱ λαμπάδες, that is, torches of love. But that pagan philosopher did not think of the divine love of Castor, which here is applied to our St. Rose because of the similarity of the name.

Myrothecium is a repository of ointments, a name which Cicero also used metaphorically in his letter to Atticus, when he wrote that he had consumed the entire myrothecium of Isocrates in his commentary on his consulship.

Here "apophoreta" is taken to mean gifts: for the ancients called the small presents given to guests to take home from a banquet "apophoreta," from the Greek verb ἀποφέρειν, which means "to carry away."

This alludes to poetic tales in which Ariadne, daughter of the Cretan king Minos, is said to have led Theseus, whom she loved, out of the labyrinth with a thread. The biographer therefore wishes to indicate that St. Rose was an assistant to this Francis in so perplexing a matter, just as Ariadne had been to Theseus in the labyrinth, from which he escaped by means of her thread.

It seems that Dioscorides of Anazarbus must have hinted at this very obscurely; for I have read through chapters 130 and 131 of the first book, where the effects of roses are discussed, and I found no mention of any philter. It is, however, a philter, from the Greek φίλτρον, a drug that conciliates love, which is derived from the verb φιλέω, that is, "I love."

Poets fabulize that Medusa's hair was turned into snakes, which had the power to petrify anyone who looked upon her head. Hence this biographical comparison is clearly known to allude to that story.

Section III. By the intercession of St. Rose, a girl was brought back to life, and several young children were freed from illness.

[A six-month-old girl died,] Although P. Although Father Anthony de la Vega Loaysa of the Society of Jesus, in his most eloquent Responsa, had eloquently and repeatedly warned the appointed judges that the entire life of Rose should be regarded and held as a continuous and perpetual miracle from her earliest cradle to her last breath, they nevertheless saw fit to proceed to more specific matters, each of which shone forth after her life in the benefit of others. First, the resuscitation of the six-month-old daughter, Magdalena de Torres, rightfully claims its place. His father was Gregory de Torres, a farmer, and his mother was Joanna Michaela; both earned their living by farm work in the suburbs of Lima on Melambo Road. While they were living in the countryside near the town in October 1627 on the estate of Balthasar de la Cona, a fever with diarrhea seized their tender little daughter and ultimately carried her off in her mother's arms. The remedies applied for his age had been of no avail, nor the mother's solicitude and tears. A morsel of death nibbled the soul of the beloved child in the arms of its afflicted mother. The little body lay lifeless from that evening until the bright light of the next day, without warmth or movement.

A small spade stood ready to dig a grave for the little corpse, [whose mother had implored the help of St. Rose,] and little flowers were being gathered to weave into a modest wreath. But the mother, remembering that very stillness of the night, rummaged through the little box of her poverty for some relics that had touched Rose, and found a scrap of clothing with a sprig of broom (on which the Virgin had once slept): With these she hurried to the cold corpse and began to invoke Rose for help with long and anxious prayers. Finally, her faith now stronger, and as if about to draw forth from the relics the life-giving odors of Rose, she placed the twig and a few threads of the other fragment on the embers in the brazier and soon suffused the dead daughter with their smoke. The living smoke closed her eyes, the dead opened them: for behold, at the very moment the puff touched the infant, she came back to life. And opening the eyes which the grieving mother had closed the day before, he looked upon her joyfully. The child's father, sister, and another young man were present at the spectacle; they fell to their knees together, and, overcome with their joy and wonder, they praised the Lord with raised voices for His so fragrant Rose. Meanwhile, little Magdalena with a little water, to which her mother had added dust from the tomb of the Blessed. Having sprinkled herself with rosewater and eagerly drunk it, she sat up in good health where she had been lying on her back, and no trace of her former illness remained in her.

In the year 1631, Anthony Bran, a servant of the young lady Joanna Barrett, was lying sick with fevers and chest pains, [and the servant, who was believed to be dead,] and for more than three months the stubbornness of the disease had eluded all the power of various medicines, and little hope remained. He was being watched over by his wife and his cousin, who, while on watch by the sick man at night, suddenly both noticed that Antony was lying motionless, without breath, and without the slightest sign of life: they called him by his own name, pinched him, struck him, but elicited nothing but the responses of death from the already cold body. The wife, overcome with horror and grief, ran to her mistress and, with a brief sob, indicated that her husband had breathed his last. The terrified lady leapt out of bed (for it was indeed midnight), and, with her sister, Lady Louisa Baretta, she hurried to Anthony's bedchamber. There, at the head of the little bed, Antony's cousin was weeping over the dead man, and as the women entered, he met them in a mournful voice: "Do not trouble yourselves," he said, "for our Antony has already given up his spirit to the Creator." Joanna comes closer, explores, touches, exclaims; But when he found it extended, cold, and rigid, as if its loss were certain, he said to his sister, "Now God has also taken away from me this servant, who was so useful and necessary to me; blessed be his name."

Then, turning to the head of the little bed, she saw hanging there the venerable Rose's paper image, one of those that had been printed in Rome with the Superiors' permission and brought to the Indies. [Under the patronage of this Saint they are revived.] Hence, filled with great confidence, he began to invoke Rosa in a loud voice and freely to demand the resuscitation of his servant. He soon reverently laid the recovered image upon the chest of the one lying there, and with the other three he awaited the outcome in sorrowful silence. Thus, half an hour was spent in silent prayers by the bedside. Then, behold, Antony, who for a full two hours had lain frozen, pale, and motionless, opened his eyes with a deep sigh (such as the very weary are wont to make), looked around, and, surprised to see his master standing there at that inconvenient hour of the night, asked what was going on. As soon as he realized what had happened, he sat up in bed, feeling himself not only unhurt but also healthy and vigorous, and the fever never returned. However, at the urging of the women who were there, he took the medicine that had been prepared beforehand, though it was now superfluous, rested, and after two days, bidding farewell to his bed of pain, He went to Rose's tomb and spent the entire day there in thanksgiving.

[75] [Elisabeth Duran, widow,] Elisabeth Duran, widow of James Carlos, had for a long time been carrying a dry and useless arm, bound up in bandages, by whose not only inert weight but also constant pain she was afflicted. So, when he heard that the virgin Rosa's little body, placed on a bier in the church of the Friars Preachers, was being prepared for burial, he, full of faith and accompanied by two of his daughters, hurried there as best he could. She thrust herself into the surrounding crowd, trying by any means to reach the tomb of the Virgin; she arrived, her daughters and others clearing the way. Struggling with the same supports up the steps of the tomb, she pressed her arm to Rose's face two or three times, and then at that very moment exclaimed: "Hurrah, my arm is healed, and my arm has come to life again." And to prove the miracle, he then threw the lizard in every direction with agility, vividly and swiftly, while the densely packed crowd watched and applauded. But above all, Melchior de Amusgo, the physician, who by chance was in the crowd, was more vehemently stunned; for a long time he had applied various, though futile, treatments to the woman's arm, admitting that it could not be cured by human means. Furthermore, Elizabeth, delighted by her success, was thereafter seen for several years openly using her arm for any task, as if she had never been weak. That miracle there had as many witnesses as Rosa then had spectators at her bier.

[76] [A certain Ethiopian slave,] The Ethiopian slave of Licentiate Diego Ayala (his name is nowhere recorded) had long since lost the use of his right arm and hand from a sprain and nerve strain; Upon hearing of the Virgin's death, she rushed with others to the church of Saint Dominic, commended herself to the prayers of Rose, and immediately, as a sign of her restored health, she carried her arm, held high and outstretched, around the entire church, casting off the old bandages. The entire people were watching, and while from this place one voice of all burst forth in public praise of God, while from the other side the mingled cries of those offering congratulations rose, some who had known the servant more intimately festively seized his hand and kissed it before the crowd, and with pious curiosity they examined the old scars where the attraction had once most tenaciously bent the Ethiopian's hand with his arm into folds.

[77] [George de Aranda, priest] But from the right to the left arm. Before entering the priesthood, Father George de Aranda Valdivia had received several wounds in his left arm during the Chilean wars against the infidels, which, poorly treated, had gradually brought on pus, convulsions, and tumors, so that by then, due to the swelling, he could neither extend nor straighten his arm. A most troublesome swelling had crept up to his fingertips, so that the afflicted priest, at Mass, could neither properly handle the sacred Host nor raise his other arm without the support of his forearm. Thus, on the very day that Rosa was buried, at dusk, aided by a religious brother, he crept into the chapter house, prayed at the Virgin's tomb, implored help, and suddenly received it. For suddenly, drenched in cold sweat, he, while successively testing his fingers, hand, and arm with the gentlest motion, finds everything sound, vigorous, and supple.

[78] Soon, trembling with piety and joy, he made his way into the church (where, by chance, Father Francis Christopher de Azevedo, Prior of Panama, was standing with many laymen), and there, before the altar of the Holy Rosary, he fell to the ground in thanksgiving with raised voices. Those who were standing closer rushed to the spectacle, and among them was Bartholomew de Toro, the king's clerk. George, not one to be asked much, before them (and he, and the stupor of his arm, were already well known to most) faithfully laid out the entire sequence of events: at the same time, the notary and the witnesses, before anything was entered into the records, investigated the miracle most thoroughly. They ordered George to stand up. they inspected the arm, free of all swelling, which the priest rotated up, down, to the right, to the left, and in every direction with equal, painless agility. He opened and closed his hand very readily. He was stringing, separating, and moving his fingers very quickly. Nor did he notice any trace of his former languor in them as long as he lived.

Alphonso Diaz, a beggar then well known throughout all of Lima, leaning on a humble staff and dragging his hand and foot, rendered useless by paralysis, across the ground, did not walk but crawled on the earth, pitifully begging for alms from door to door. On that very day, when he had covered Rosa's grave with earth, he came panting to the church of Saint Dominic, where he wept bitterly for a while. Finally, after inquiring about the burial place, he crawled into the chapter house as best he could, He lay prone on Rose's tomb, and for an hour he prayed, groaned, wept, and finally sweated, unaware that he had already been cured. He who was in charge of covering the Virgin's tomb with a brick covering arrived; he orders Alphonsus to leave the place. He denies that he can. They both struggle, but the former, in his prime and stronger than Alfonso, embraces the man, pulls him away despite his resistance, and slowly but gently lifts him to his feet.

Here Alphonsus first realized that he had been cared for. [Well-known throughout the city of Lima,] nevertheless, from long disuse of standing, leaning against the nearest wall, and briefly marveling at himself, upright and unsupported, he broke out at once into shouts and tears, rejoicing and crying out; Blessed be God! I am well, I am healthy: what I have not been able to do for about four years, I can now do: I am like this to my plants: I now plant in the ground those plants that had grown used to living without soil. Let the Lord be praised in his saints. Meanwhile he was still sweating all over his body, and because softer flesh had grown under his soles in place of calluses, he trod the ground with somewhat unsteady steps; others, thinking him ill, tried to support him as he walked with outstretched arms. He refused, protesting that he was already more than enough for himself. A gathering takes place, and amid the hymns of the applauding, Alphonsus is brought back into the church to be seen by all. He proceeded gravely, who had never before walked more festively or more stately.

[81] [and two Ethiopians] While Rose's corpse lay in the tomb, an Ethiopian boy, older than twelve, crept up, and was more famous throughout the city for the deformity of his drawn-in feet than for his name. And so he was praying on the floor beneath the bier (for he could neither raise himself a palm's breadth from the ground, nor struggle up the steps to touch the sacred little body), when suddenly he appeared, brisk and upright on his feet, which he had previously dragged most wretchedly along the ground, crawling, since he was not even permitted to use his hands, but was allowed only to drag half his body along the ground. He then sprang out, sound in mind, and confidently mingled with the crowds, repeatedly crying out in a loud voice that Rosa was his savior. For eight days thereafter, he was seen going daily to the Virgin's tomb to publicly and dutifully give thanks.

[82] [They are miraculously freed by the contraction of their feet.] At his example, another boy, also an Ethiopian, and suffering from a very similar weakness of the feet (except that he supported himself on crutches, not on the aid of walls), after Rosa's funeral was buried, came to the grave, threw himself into a corner of the capitular hall, He prayed, and for more than two hours he lay there supplicating. Finally, in the sight of the crowd that was present, he slowly rose, shouting: "I am well, I seem to be able to walk;" I feel my shins are unencumbered. Rather, they are itching for the race. One of those standing nearby replied, "If that's the case, stand up, walk, and bear witness to the grace you have received." Without delay, the boy, neglecting the gulls, rose up, stood, walked, and, unable to contain himself for joy, leaped, capered, and finally asked the densest part of the crowd to step aside for a moment and give him room to run. He obtained it and ran back and forth through the hall like a nimble goat. Those who had known the boy before could hardly believe their eyes. Voices of those praising God are lifted up to the heights, the devout rush in, they bring the boy into the church, and the entire temple resounds with melodious acts of thanksgiving.

[83] Among the many boys, the little son of Joanna de Castillo, the widow, was two-year-old Francis Fernandez de Sigura, who was frequently and dangerously afflicted with cataleptic seizures and palpitations for a whole year. Human remedies were in vain until recourse was had to the Rose then newly interred. So the afflicted mother brought her little child here, stood by the tomb, and prayed. Meanwhile, the little boy eagerly lay prone in the tomb, and there he soon began to brighten up. And when, after an hour and a half, his mother asked him if, being tired of lying down, he wanted to get up and be taken away, he asked to be left there for just a little while longer. Finally he recovered of his own accord, rejoicing at the signs of his health, and from that time until the seventeenth year of his age (when he and his mother testified to these facts before the apostolic judges) he was not afflicted by this or any other infirmity.

[84] [Oppressed by various diseases] Rufina Brava had given birth to a little son, Peter Tamayo. The infant was barely fifteen days old when it was discovered that he was suffering miserably from a hernia. The torn peritoneum was becoming more lax day by day. The entrails were hanging out, and could only be forced back in with great labor and danger, to come out again soon. The little one wailed continuously, unable to bear so much pain, and often for three whole days, feeling nauseous, he utterly refused the nurse's breast. Nearly two years were spent applying remedies at great expense, with no progress. Finally, two days after Rosa had passed away and been laid to rest, he went to her tomb and sat there quietly for nearly two hours, while his mother and female relatives remained in the church, entrusting the child to God and to the Virgin Rosa. The infant was returned to his mother, with the mesh closed and the intestines replaced, and was found to have no hernia. Only a conspicuous scar remained as a reminder of the favor, which, however, also confirmed the integrity of the repaired peritoneum so that it could not be torn again.

Peter de Vega's five-year-old daughter had been gravely ill for four months from fevers, [through the intercession of the same Virgin], and the little one's condition was made no less distressing by a very severe, morose disposition, which was dangerously likely to worsen with age. The anxious parents carried this feverish girl to the floor so that she might touch the virgin body of Rose, not yet buried: she touched it, and immediately, seized by a paroxysm of feverish pulse, she recovered before everyone, and when she was brought home, she was not troubled by any new attacks of the disease. Indeed, that innate harshness of disposition was also corrected by the same miracle, and she was transformed into the utmost meekness and sweetness.

[86] It seemed that by these miracles Rose was crying out from the tomb: [they are restored to health.] "Let the little ones come to me." They did indeed come, and were passed from hand to hand in turn, so that the infirm might reach the salutary touch of Rose, the innocent of the people, to carry back health to their own. Among them was Francis Cardoso, a tender nine-month-old infant, who for three months had been suffering from a constant fever, now shivering, now burning. Suddenly, as soon as he was brought to the sacred corpse, he ceased his crying and his fever, to the amazement of the people. Not only that, but the next day the infant, cradled in the arms of an Ethiopian slave girl, signaled by gesture and motion that he wished to be set down on the ground. Hardly had he touched the floor with his tiny feet when he began to walk alone across the atrium, and from that day on he needed no help or the arms of a bearer. Indeed, the day before, Rosa had summoned such strength for the little boy, who had been scorched by such prolonged fevers and utterly drained. Surely he has perfected praise for himself from the mouths of infants and nursing children. Let us return to the adults.

Notes.

Diarrhea, from Greek διάῤῥοια, Latin profluvium, is a copious flow of the bowels without phlegmon or ulceration of the intestines, as Castellus and other physicians explain more fully.

b Ginesta or rather genista is a low-growing, self-seeding herb, so called because it is flexible at the joint like a knee, and therefore suitable for tying vines, or because it is used to treat aching knees. Some think it is a Spartan of the Greeks, others are doubtful. Whatever the case, so that this plant may be known by its name among various nations, the Spaniards call it hiniesta or retama, The French call it genest, the Italians ginestra, the Germans pfriemen, and the Belgians brem.

c Catalepsy: among rhetoricians it is a figure, among physicians a disease, which Gorræus in his Medical Definitions, p. 213, describes as follows: "Catalepsy is an affection of the brain." It comes from a severe cold spell with some dryness. and the posterior part of the brain is rather occupied, while in the anterior part, in the flesh (this disease is a species of lethargy), it labors more. In apoplexy and epilepsy, however, in both, as Galen writes in his book. 4 of the affected places. They remain, however, in catalepsy with the eyelid open, which is closed by the brow, and breathing is preserved according to nature. From this, it is concluded what is indicated here by cataleptis, whether that name is an adjective or a substantive.

A hernia, from the Greek *kēle*, which the common people call a rupture, is a swelling of the scrotum, or more properly so called, when a part of the body has fallen into the scrotum through a rupture of the peritoneum, the explanation of which last word we shall now give.

Gorræus, in the Medical Definitions, page 369, describes this part of the human body with these words: "Peritoneum is a membrane or tunic (for it is called both)." but the membrane is indeed called the essence, the tunic, however, from its use) containing the entire lower belly: for it περιτείνεταιthe whole, that is, it surrounds it; From which it also gets its name. Furthermore, a more detailed description of that very thin membrane can be read there.

Section IV. Many adults of both sexes, having implored the help of this Saint, were delivered from dangers and diseases.

[To Saint Peter of Vega,] In the year before Rose's death, Peter of Vega had dislocated a bone in his shoulder in a severe and precipitous fall, and from that time had lost the use of his arm on that side, constantly complaining that nothing was left of his muscle but pain and burden. Finally, one night, remembering that he had at home the rosary that had shortly before touched the lifeless body of Rose in her coffin, he devoutly applied it to the spot where the sharpest pang of pain was felt. The torment ceased at once, Peter fell asleep peacefully, and upon waking in the morning he noticed that strength had returned to his useless arm, which, with no other remedy applied, had grown so much that within a few days he had made a full recovery, and he felt no remaining difference in the agility of his left and right arms. The same Peter was struck again by a severe case of sciatica, and at the same time the other of his shins was swollen with painful inflammation. Overwhelmed by these misfortunes, the unfortunate man could neither eat nor sleep. At last, Rose, remembering her rosary, offered up the stations of the Cross, rested, and when she awoke at dawn the next morning, she found herself well all over; indeed, the troublesome swelling on her shin had gone down so completely that you would have sworn it had never been there.

[88] [To Helen the Ethiopian,] Helen the Ethiopian, a slave of John Merinni, was so exhausted by a seven-year discharge of a continuous and copious supply of worms that were hatching (which they call de Guinea), having expelled blood, with the bowels eaten away, had so exhausted her, that her life was thought to be entirely at an end, especially four days before Rose's death, when to so many diseases the most ardent fever, and (a mortal sign) an inflammation of the ankles and shins had been added. Throughout the seven years, all kinds of medicines were tried. But by irritating the disease, these new daily perils did not afflict the patient, but aided the very illness itself. Finally, unable to bear the loss of her most useful servant, she urged and persuaded Helena to perform a novena at the tomb of Rosa, who had been buried two days earlier (the people call such a nine-day station with specific prayers a novendial). Weak in body but eager for life and health, she undertook the task with more spirit than strength.

[89] [who had been sick for seven years,] But on the fourth day she declared herself unfit to carry it out: for, worn out by a long illness, pale from bloody and frequent hemorrhages, scorched by fever, gasping for breath, and with swollen feet, she had completely despaired of being able to visit Rose's tomb during the remaining days. Nevertheless, encouraged by hope and by his wife, he recovered his spirits and, for the remaining days, continued his treatment, though it was exceedingly difficult, in whatever way he could, with no sign of recovery appearing, not even in the slightest. Rather, it was pouring a greater force of worms with the blood each day. Until at last the final station on the ninth day at the tomb of Rose, in an instant, completely cleansed the entire foulness of so many diseases. Helen returned home a completely different person: her bowels entirely free of worms, her feet sound, unaware of any fever, her blood, vigor, and flesh marvelously restored, and from then on conscious of none of those symptoms, she enjoyed perfect and lasting health.

[90] He was sailing with Father F. Dominic de Leon of the Order of Preachers, a servant [and a certain dying servant], and John, a fourteen-year-old youth, a companion on the journey, who was stricken with a quartan fever, which the sea's tossing had then made more dangerous, so that on one day it was believed the violence of a paroxysm had nearly cost the boy his life: for suddenly, at a cry, Dominic was called down from the upper deck of the ship to attend his dying servant; As he descended into the ship's covered hold, he encountered a married man, Francis Flores, who asserted that John had already expired. The anxious Father went on, however, and found the servant speechless, colorless, and breathless, lying on his back and motionless. Unsure whether he was alive, he repeatedly tried to rouse him with loud cries, pulling on both of his floppy ears. But seeing that he was in vain awaiting signs of life, he fled to Rosa's protection and, on his knees, pleaded for the boy's life, He promised a novena: and behold, at that very moment John breathed, sat up, and took food, to the astonishment of the religious men who had intervened in the spectacle—Father Agnello de Oliva of the Society of Jesus, Father Francis Laurentio de Texeda of the Order of St. Francis, and many laymen of both sexes. All who were on the ship were astonished, especially when they saw him appear shortly afterward on the upper deck, healthy, unharmed, and restored to perfect health without any human remedy.

Beatrice of Gaves was afflicted for four consecutive years by a most troublesome, unceasing drip of humor, and she restored the other woman's health. But when this was made worse by remedies, it had destroyed all hope of cure. So, at a loss for what to do, he pushed his way with the rest of the crowd into the house of the quaestor Gundisalvi, where Rosa lay in a coffin, about to be carried out. There she entrusted herself to the Virgin with all her heart, touched the reliquary, returned home in good health, and never again felt troubled by that discharge.

[92] A year after Rose's happy passing, Fr. John Miguel of the Order of Preachers, [He rescues a certain Dominican friar from danger,] was riding through the valley of Xauxacon account of the economy and business affairs. But as he was passing by the cattle pastures near the place of Verastegui, a bull suddenly burst forth and fiercely pursued him. He was terrified, and spurred on by the spurs, John took to flight, crying out loudly his danger to God and the B. Commending the roses; But the faster bull was already nearby, and was no more than fifteen paces away from the man. He was lost, had he not invoked Rosa again and much more ardently. What a wonder! A monstrous beast, at the name of Rosa, stopped as if struck back by lightning, gazing fiercely at John, who was faint from fatigue and terror. He did not move from the spot until he had gone far away.

[93] Another, but more charming, incident befell the same man on the river that flows past the place called Misqued [and favors him while he fishes], and it abounds with fish (especially boci, or the whiter menaee). He had gone there fishing with his religious companion, Father Jerome Jambrano, and another Indian. Jerome and Indus were casting their nets together, and Indus hauled in some catch, while Jerome, with his net empty, grumbled. John urged her to lie down again, and with each throw he would raise his voice and say, "Come on! In the name of blessed Rose, may it fall successfully." Jerome appeared, but not the fish. For indeed, having cast the net more than twelve times, he caught nothing. John, suspecting what it was, said, "I beg you, my Jerome, speak freely: does your heart not waver in unbelief concerning the virgin Rose's sanctity?" He affirmed with a scornful smile that there were so many certain Celestials whose names these offerings could have been more usefully dedicated to, and that there was no need to resort to uncertain names. But John replied, "You, my brother, must conceive an unwavering faith in the holiness of Rose, and so in her name cast the net again and earnestly, and you will see wonders." He made, threw, and suddenly drew such a swarm of locusts and armadillos that the weight of the full net could barely be supported.

[94] [a fugitive slave from the monastery,] Let us here briefly turn aside to the fishing of men. In the year 1630, Christina Angela, an unwilling slave, was serving as a handmaid in the Monastery of Saint Clare in Lima. It happened that by chance the monastery's back door was open for his escape, through which, like a small fish slipping from a net, he quietly slipped out and hid himself in the city. Raphaela de Esquivel, a nun, was then serving as the doorkeeper. She, embracing the damage, obstacle, and disgrace of this escape—all at once confused with fear—when, after several days of searching, the fugitive could not be found, fled to Rosa's aid, and, commending her distress to her, demanded that the lost servant be restored to the monastery. Furthermore, that same night, Raphael saw in a dream Rosa, dressed in a white robe and with a face shining gloriously, who said to him, "Tomorrow morning Christina will be recovered." The nun, awakened from the joy of the most beautiful vision, once again devoutly entrusted the matter to the Virgin and fell asleep again. Early in the morning, the maid, found at around five o'clock before noon, was reprimanded and sent back.

[95] [and the lost promissory note had been restored to others.] By no less a favor from Rosa, though of a different kind, the brothers Alexander and Francis de Coloma recovered the lost property of their brother. They had duly paid the annual tribute of five hundred crowns to the Monastery of Saint Clare in Guamanga, and, as was the custom, had received from the steward a receipt as proof of payment. After a period of time, that very debt is again demanded. The German brothers offer a solution; a receipt is sought, but it appears nowhere. Francis was certain that he had put it in such a compartment of his desk. he feared that perhaps she had disappeared, having been taken by some other's fraud. Then, a hundred times back to the same little desk, all the papers were examined, stretched out, and put back one by one: everything was there except the receipt. Anxious, therefore, Francis in a brief prayer returned to the Rosary, his most familiar refuge. Then, full of hope, he asks Alexander to examine the little box with the utmost care, scanning it from every angle with his eyes. Alexander replies that it is a waste of time. Another one insists, demanding that at least this be given to him as a consolation so that he may ask again. He searched casually, as if in desperation, and behold, in that very compartment where they both had searched so often, on top of the other writings in plain view lay a receipt, its most beautiful foreign inscription neatly rolled up, as if it had just at that moment been secretly returned to the desk. But we must return to the hospitals.

Father Fr. Didacus de Arrasia of the Order of Preachers, from the Convent of Panama to that of Lima, setting out in the height of the heat, [Didacus de Arrasia the Dominican,] from the change of climate, he had contracted a double tertian fever, which in a short time brought him to the very brink of death, the physician ordering the Sacraments to be administered quickly, especially because the sick man's stomach was greatly distended from drinking too much water, and whatever remedy was applied to either his stomach or his fever, it aggravated the illness. And so Master Father admonished the sick man of the extreme danger. Gabriel de Zarate, then Provincial. But Diego, secretly offering supplications to the Rose, had conceived vows to God, and now his hope of salvation swelled higher than the waters. The final remedy for the swelling of his belly remained to be tried, but it was a violent and bitter one. Doubting his ability to endure it, Diego implored Rosa again and most earnestly. And behold, that remedy having been omitted, his belly subsided, the two-month-old, deep-rooted fever departed, and to everyone's astonishment, Diego leapt up joyfully to fulfill his vows.

Isidora de Montalvo, advanced in years, had been burdened for eight months with a continuous and very violent fever. [and many others of both sexes] The doctors denied that an old woman, burdened by so many years and worn out by age, could be cured of her illness by methodical remedies. Therefore, abandoned by them, she appealed to Rosa for life and health, and at that very moment she recovered completely and lived on in good health for a long time.

[98] Joseph de Torres was consumed throughout his body by a burning fire from putrid blood inflamed by fevers: [Feverishly burning,] bright blisters rose up from every side, and within three weeks they had brought her to such a state of weakness that, lying motionless before her household without speech or pulse, she was thought to have breathed her last. The terrified onlookers eagerly implored Rosa to grant Josephine some time, so that she could at least summon the priest and confess her sins to him. But Rosa did more than was asked of her: for the sick woman suddenly came to herself, made her confession properly, and soon regained her full health.

Maria Inda, a twelve-year-old girl from the kingdom of Chile, [stricken with another kind of illness] and serving in the Monastery of the Incarnation in Lima, had been reduced by rigors, stomach torments, and finally spasms, a constriction of the throat, and convulsions, to the point that she could neither be restored by food or drink nor swallow the smallest thing. Desperate, she was being fortified by the doctors with the last sacraments of the dying. However, at the very moment of her last anointing by the priest and the nuns, she was admonished to commend her anguish to the Virgin Rose with the greatest faith and affection she could muster. He obeyed, and without any other human remedy began to recover from that moment, and within a few days returned to his usual duties as a servant.

A long-standing paralysis had afflicted Antonio de Umbela the worker's arm and hand, and, suffering from bodily infirmities, they were never able to be relieved by any remedies. Having heard of the miracles that daily shone forth at Rose's tomb, he at last went there, laid his arm upon the tomb, prayed briefly, and immediately sweetly opened his hand, which had long been closed; then he stretched out his arm and resumed the exercises of his craft in good health. A nine-year-old girl, Maria Sanchez, was so afflicted by a chance fall to the ground that, with her shins drawn in and all use of her feet taken away, she could neither stand nor sit anywhere. Rather, he had to lie down immediately on the little bed. Three whole years were wasted on remedies. Finally, each parent, having promised a novena at the sepulchre of the Rose, has their little daughter carried there and laid prone upon the virgin tomb. When the ninth day arrived, it brought the girl health and joy to her parents: for, as she lay in the tomb according to custom, she suddenly sprang to her feet in perfect health, walked, ran to her father, and, when he took her by the hand, he led her for a long time around the courtyard and then home. The daughter ran ahead eagerly, as if her feet had never grown weak.

[101] [Saint Rose invoked,] Maria Farsan, a three-year-old girl, while being treated in 1617 for a dangerous blockage from undigested crudities with an infusion of mercury (which another woman had suggested), soon lost the use of her legs, which swelled, and lost her ability to walk, so that from the navel down she was as good as dead, crawling on her hands with her legs dragged along the ground. For six full months she languished thus half-alive, until her body was carried to the high altar of the Church of Saint Dominic, where, as soon as she touched the face of the deceased Virgin two or three times, the swelling suddenly subsided, and the little girl, returned to her parents, ran about the house healthy after two days, with no other medicine applied in the meantime.

[102] [swiftly and miraculously] Agnes of Figueroa (Rosæ's dearest friend while she lived) was being torn apart by excruciating pain in her loins, and, unaware of what was wrong, had already reached the point where she could not void her bladder for three whole days. The long acerbity of her sorrows had nearly overturned the woman's mind, when, at her husband's suggestion, she began to commend herself with heartfelt entreaties and many tears to the intercession of her dear Rose. And immediately, as if seated on hot embers, she was seized with a burning heat throughout, and suddenly, in less time than it takes to say the Angelic Salutation, she gave birth to a calculus the size of a hazelnut, pointed on every side. At that very moment the pain ceased, and Agnes, who for a long time had been unable to sleep or lie down, fell asleep. The surgeons and physicians, all who saw the stone, testified that it could only have been expelled by either death or a miracle.

[103] [They experience its help.] A Mexican tailor named Luis Rodriguez, working in the house of the widow Joanna de Vargas, suffered from a painful nerve traction, so that for two months he could never walk or set foot on the ground. The surgeons, after having tried cheaper remedies without success, declared that the man could be treated in no other way than with a masterful ointment (which would have had to be prepared at considerable expense). Louis's mother wept bitterly, aware that she would be far from equal to such great expenses, and of her son's poverty. And so, at the widow's suggestion, she looked up at Rosa, promising to make the Novena to the tomb together with her son. The next day, upon returning home from where she had set out, she found her son, with his foot drawn up, already standing firmly and able to walk a little. The last day of the novena had not yet dawned, and Louis, already fully recovered, was leaving the house to visit the Virgin's tomb. By a similar benefit, the aforementioned widow herself was suddenly cured of the severe swelling and torment in both feet, as soon as she had pitifully invoked the helper Rosa, having promised a similar novenary of days or stations, which she also began the next day, laden with votive offerings, and cheerfully completed on healthy feet.

[104] [He rescues a woman in labor from danger,] for the afterbirth had remained in the uterus of Isabella Morales, who was giving birth (and had already delivered). The midwife panicked and spared no remedy to draw out the membrane. After three hours of fruitless labor, and with all hope now lost, Isabella, sensing she was about to die, asked to be carried back to her bed, where she would die more fittingly. Meanwhile the confessor, summoned, arrived; and when everything was turning for the worse, Isabella was advised to resort to Rose's protection in such a dire plight. It obeyed, and at that very moment, other remedies being set aside, the seconds of their own accord came forward. And the entire danger was suddenly dispelled.

Christopher Perez, secretary of the criminal court, had retired to his estate in the Lurigancho Valley with his wife and household servants. [Christopher Perez into a deep ditch] From here, on a certain day, while traveling to the neighboring hills, which are most lovely with greenery, the ground being wet and slippery from the rains, he and his horse slipped into a ditch sixteen palms deep. The frightened wife ran up with the household servants and saw her husband's horse rearing. A softer earth had followed the sloping ground and almost covered both. While the horse (being noble and young) tries to shake the earth from its hooves and with great effort attempts to rise, it presses Christopher down more heavily with its backslide and sometimes strikes him unwittingly with its hooves. For a quarter of an hour Perez lay like that, and, his hands and feet knotted and weakened by arthritis, he could do nothing to help himself.

Meanwhile, above the ditch the household was wailing, his wife was mourning [that he had fallen from his horse], and at last everyone called for Rosa to come to his aid. Christopher heard, and he too, under the weight of the horse and the ground, began to invoke Rose. And behold, the horse immediately rose to its feet, the man unhurt: they descend into the spring's ditch, Perez is pulled out unharmed, whom others were freely proclaiming would die within an hour. He himself, by the sole benefit of the rose, declared himself so uninjured that he would allow neither phlebotomy nor any application of fomentations.

The rescue of the old slave Anthony of Terrabia was all the more miraculous because his fall had been all the more miserable—he was already close to death. As he lay laboring in the final throes of his struggle, Mary of Oliva, Rose's mother, sat alone by his side through the night. As Anthony tries to change sides while tired, he falls from the very narrow bed onto the floor. Anxious, since she had neither the strength to help her dying servant nor to lift herself back into bed, she implored powerful help from heaven from her daughter Rosa, and immediately, full of faith, she placed her arms under the weighty burden of both, She lifted the old, corpulent, half-dead Antonius from the floor all by herself and placed him in bed, arranged him, and covered him, and only then, marveling at her own work, she trembled all over, realizing that her own Rosa had been there to help her.

Let this chapter be closed by Rosa, the heavenly exorciser of poison. [and the other, to whom poison had been administered.] A cruel woman of Lima (whose name is omitted throughout the proceedings) hated her husband to the extreme, and, in order to remove the unwitting from her way, secretly mixed a very potent poison into his drink, one that, before any remedy could be sought, would quickly kill the man. Scarcely had the husband inhaled the pestilence when, with his breath caught, he swelled up like a drum. Soon, seized by a fainting spell, his bowels loosened in a flow, he sweated, chilled, trembled, and with distorted eyes breathed nothing but the final agonies of death. But thus, at the critical moment, the memory of Rose came to her in time, and as the poison crept through her heart's vitals, she summoned her last strength and burst out in a loud cry: "Saint Rose, help me, and I promise a novena." The witch-wife, enraged by Rosa's plea, and now despairing of her poison's effect, wounded herself with a fatal wound. The husband, having escaped the poison with nothing but an antidote, rose from his bed the next day and, mindful of his promise, hurried to the Virgin's tomb.

Notes.

Guinea is a large region of Africa, from which a great number of slaves are transported to America. Whether the earthworms of that region differ from others, we do not know. Furthermore, Gorræus and Castellus, in their Greek work *Helminthes* on the various genera of worms, explain how these animals are born and nourished in the human body.

Cacochymia is a vicious juice, or an abundance of a vicious humor throughout the entire body. Therefore, Castellus in the medical lexicon, page 398, affirms that cacochymia in the human body is, as it were, a vitiation of the humors.

Caxa is the most famous valley in all of Peru, stretching fourteen leagues in length and four or five in width, once densely populated. This valley is surrounded on all sides by snow-capped mountains, and is thirty-six or forty leagues from the city of Lima.

d John Latius, book. Chapter 11, West Indies 7, describing the journey from the city of La Plata toward Potosí, he thus remembers the little town of Misque: "Moreover, about twenty-five miles from La Plata lies the small town of Misque, from which many wines are transported to Potosí." Halfway along the journey flows a river which the Spaniards call De Condorillo, eight leagues from La Plata, seventeen from Miské. This river is undoubtedly different from the stream indicated here, which flows past the little town of Misque, since another one is seventeen leagues from it.

About the bocas and mænas, which are small marine fish of the genus Halec, Ulisse Aldrovandi discusses at length in his piscatorial work, as in book. Chapter 2 It can be seen in 39 and the following.

From the Spanish it is called armadillo, an American animal covered with bony scales like little plates, & whose figure John Latius lib. Chapter 15, West Indies 5 shows. But since this animal is terrestrial and approaches the size of a piglet, it is by no means indicated here. Here, therefore, by "armadillos" are meant fish which have been unknown to me until now.

A flying fire and a volatile one are the same thing, if I'm not mistaken. However, the volatile fire in the more extensive glossary of Cangius is called impetigo, or lichen, which is a disease that flies from one part of the body to another, as can be read more distinctly in the now often-cited physicians, and a clearer exposition of which will recur later in the text.

At first I hesitated, not knowing what that masterful unction signified. But later I examined the book entitled Pharmaceutical Medicine, which was printed in Flemish in Brussels in 1681. In this work, on page 143, I read that a remedy composed and invented by an experienced physician is called a masterful composition. Then on page 936 and the following of the same work various surgical or chemical masteries are set forth, and the masteries of corals, pearls, and other such things are mentioned. Add that the Trévoux Dictionary of the French Language teaches that the term magisterii was in use among apothecaries and chemists, and was taken to mean a certain exceptional or exquisite medicine. From all this I conclude that by this masterful anointing a certain remedy or precious ointment is meant, which would have had to be applied to drive away this disease, and for which the meager abilities of that tailor were not sufficient.

By the name of membranes the second one is called that membrane by which the fetus in the mother's womb is enveloped, and which ordinarily follows the newborn infant, as is well known and as has already been mentioned several times in our work. It is called by the Greeks δευτέριον, or χορίον, as Gorræus testifies in his Medical Definitions, page 513, and explains more fully.

An antidote, from the Greek *alexipharmakon*, is a remedy given against poison. This name is composed of the word pharmakon, which means medicine or poison, and the verb aléxō, which means to help or to repel, so that alexipharmacum can be called in Latin a helpful medicine, or a poison repeller.

Section V. Paralytics and other infirm people were miraculously healed, and women in dangerous childbirth were helped by touching the relics of the same Saint.

[Cephalæa,] Asanato being afflicted with a headache, let us begin this chapter. Eleonora Ruiz de Saudosa was tormented by a severe headache. But so as not to lose the benefit of the jubilee, he had to go straight from the sarcophagus of Rosa (who had been buried the day before) to the cathedral. He went; But first she had placed a piece of Rose's clothing on her ailing head, and having entered the path under the protection of this helmet, after she had sat for a while in the main hall, she realized that she was completely free of all headache. Philippa de Vargas, tormented by an unbearable fever, was at the same time being incessantly pricked throughout her skull by dreadful, thorn-like pangs; at last she applied a particle of Rose's relic to her crown and fell asleep. Upon waking, she found herself completely freed not only from her headaches but also from the entire fever.

[110] Lucia, appointed by the most holy Trinity to the monastery of St. Catherine of Siena, was severely afflicted by a headache, with cerebral intemperance and a gouty discharge from the right temple. But when she applied a small, rose-infused strip of cloth to the affected part, it immediately stopped the bleeding and relieved the pain. She, who at nightfall had long been wracked by sudden stomach cramps and was nearly faint, suddenly, as she rubbed the said particle on her chest, her colic subsided. Maria, a barefoot nun of St. Joseph, had, by an unexpected fall to the ground, severely injured the nerves of both eyes, so that she could not turn them to the right or to the left, and a constant, severe pain persisted in them. Finally, he snatched a fragment from the relics of Rose and said, "I will test whether the sanctity of Rose, which is so widely preached, is truly hers." It was the same moment the splinter struck the eyes, leaving them mobile and healthy, and neither stupor nor pain ever returned to them.

[111] The Virgin Lucia de Montoya, from early childhood, suffered from a foul and flowing discharge from her eyes, a condition called lippitude, not without the risk of being blinded. The weakness of her eyes and the constant pain in her eyelids would not allow the girl to go out into the bright daylight or to come near the candlelight. A constant headache aggravated the illness. When he was burdened by these troubles on a certain day more severely than usual, he sought a remedy in Rose's garment with great faith, and found it. He had at home, among his most precious treasures, a piece of the cloak that had wrapped the still-mortal Rose. Lucia reverently applied this to the head, rubbed the eyes with it, and at that very moment, along with the squint, she wiped away all the headache. As proof of the miracle, she, still on the very day of her cure, seized a needle and by evening had finished sewing four squares of a finer cloth. In fact, from then on he had eyes so clear and bright that it seemed he had never been cross-eyed before.

[112] [& other bad eye conditions,] A little three-year-old girl named Margaret was going blind more severely in the care of Isabella Mendoza. She was completely blind in one eye (in which a film had settled), and in the other, though dimly, she could barely see by barely blinking. With the ointments proving ineffective, it was believed that she would gradually lose all her sight. Her mistress (for the girl was of servile status) often protested that her little Margaret's death would be more useful than her life; for more than a year, intolerant of bright light, she had been wandering through the house with her eyes mostly closed. One evening, they entrusted the household maid to the heavenly Rose as she was about to go to bed, and tied a piece of the virgin's garment as a band between the girl's eyes, thus arranging the bandaged girl on the little bed. In the morning, as the bandage is removed and they look into Margaret's eyes, they first notice that the cloud has vanished. Then they gazed in wonder at the brightness of the other eye. Finally they discovered that blindness on one side and a total loss of sight on the other had been completely removed: for with a small, firm, and unclouded field of vision on both sides he now looked upon the air and light with pleasure.

[113] [Toothache, and other ailments] A biting worm from the head had filled Joanna of Velasco's gums with pus; Hence, the most excruciating toothache kept the poor woman from sleeping and could not bear to remain in any one place. The swelling grew larger and larger, and poor Joanna was in agony until she remembered that she had a scrap of the Virgin Rose's linen veil. She applied it to her mouth, and at once all the pain vanished, sleep came over her, and when she awoke the next morning Joanna was amazed to find herself healed. Epilepsy had killed two of the children of the widow Ludovica Faxardo: a seventeen-year-old boy and a ten-month-old girl. A three-year-old son, named Francis de Contreras, survived, whom the same disease was preparing for a similar fate; For the boy would sometimes lie for fifteen straight hours, speechless and senseless, foaming at the mouth, his arms and shins throbbing violently, and at times even striking his back on the ground. An anxious mother, now desperate for any human remedy, finally resorted to Rose's heavenly pharmacy, obtaining a piece of scapular from elsewhere, believing it would benefit her son, anything that smelled of Rose. And indeed, it was helpful. For as soon as he placed a drop of Francis's then foully foaming medicine on his trembling chest, the boy was immediately quieted, came to himself, sat up in his little bed, asked for a drink of water, and recovered. And he was never afflicted with this disease again.

[114] [at the touch of the relics of St. Rose] About seven years after Rose's blessed passing, a volatile fire tormented Aloysia of Escobar, a nun of St. Clare, now roaming the seasons, then the cheeks, then the eyes and other parts, not without danger. Much effort has been expended on the remedies for a long time. but to ever greater harm, and with absolutely no benefit. Finally, the abbess took from the reliquary a preserved particle of the Tunic of the Rose. And, after a preliminary prayer, he gently bound it to the face of the one lying down, and thus ordered him to rest for the rest of the night. The next day, when she removed the bandage, she found Aloysia's entire face, as well as the palms of her hands, completely free of any burning or swelling, with not the slightest trace of yesterday's ailment remaining. At one time, a tertian fever had brought on terrible and most violent stomach pains for the same Aloysia, so that from the anguish of the convulsions she believed death was imminent; but immediately, having abandoned all other medicine and fled to the Rose, she applied the said particle to her stomach, and the pain of the fever departed at once.

The same Mary of Jesus, a Discalced Carmelite, had previously experienced the same help against erysipelas. [They are suddenly driven away.] His entire face was swollen by this inflamed disease, and at the same time the rest of his little body was being consumed by fevers. Finally, when a small scrap of Rose's garment was applied to the sick woman, the swelling subsided, the burning heat abated, and she was soon able to fall asleep. She awoke healthy, with the erysipelas and fever both gone. In the year when Rosa was transplanted into the heavenly paradise of her Spouse, asthma was nearly strangling the painter John Rodriguez Samanes. For three years a fierce malady had settled heavily and tenaciously in the lungs and bronchi, and granted only coughs, hoarse wheezing, and the most feeble power of breathing to a crushed chest. At the right moment, Mary of Mesta offered certain relics of Rose (from which she herself had often drawn healing) to her ailing household servant, when he was suffering more severely. The young man applied these with devoted faith to his chest, slept most peacefully, with his chest free and without snoring. Upon waking, he felt the bonds of his lungs loosed on all sides, and from then on he found no further discomfort or pressure from heavy breathing.

Brother John Garzia, a layman of the Order of Preachers, [Wound inflicted on the hand,] on the fourth day after Rose's death, by order of his superiors, had entered the Virgin's little garden cell to remove from there a small stool on which Rose, during her life, had been accustomed to sit in solitude. But the narrowness of the doorway seemed to deny the stool an exit; so the layman, with the knife he had brought, began to cut away the obstructing wood in a series of short strokes. But as he was hurrying to get the work done, he carelessly plunged the iron into his left hand so deeply that, along with the torn skin, the flesh hung from the joint. The atrocity of the wound terrified those standing by: for blood gushed out in abundance, and through the gash it was unclear to the onlookers whether some nerves or veins had been severed along with it. John, wounded but not startled, was aware of the present remedy. "And I have," he said, "an effective and certain poultice that will immediately heal my hand." At the same time, she drew from her bosom a piece cut from Rose's linen veil, with which she at once bandaged her left hand, binding it with bandages so faithfully, securely, and steadfastly that she soon returned to her cell to finish the rest of the work. He had scarcely spent an hour there when, once more turning to the waiting ones, he willingly unfastened the bandage, showed his hand whole and sound, and no longer in need of another surgeon's help.

The wife of the Angelino painter Medor, Maria de Mesta, had been worn out for about twenty days by a very dangerous, copious flow of blood. [a dangerous hemorrhage,] Finally remembering that she possessed relics from the white vestments of the Rose, she reverently kissed them and, earnestly commending herself to the Virgin's intercession, she escaped the danger, her former strength being at once restored. Meanwhile, other fluids, flowing from the head, tore at the shoulder with immense agony, and the cephalæa raged no less fiercely in the occiput. But by the benefit of that same particle, after imploring Rose's help, both illnesses were driven away. A severe pain in her swollen leg sometimes afflicted the woman, so that for fifteen days and more she could not stand at all, not even by day, nor get even the briefest sleep at night. resorting to the usual plaster of Rose's virtue: and with the virgin relics applied to the leg, the restlessness and pain subsided, and perfect and stable health followed with the most peaceful sleep.

[118] [& long-standing contraction of the limbs] In the valleys of Chinchae Magdalena Chimasso Inda, from a noble lineage of the Chincha rulers (whom they call Caciquosf), married to a Spanish man, Francisco Morales, for three years and more she had been so weak from the navel down due to her body's contraction that she could only sit on a woolen blanket, and even then only when she was carried back and forth on it and carried by her household servants to the necessities of nature. She was never seen to change her side without them, nor to raise herself of her own accord. Finally, after having spent vast sums in vain on remedies, he had himself transported from Chincha to Lima, to the house of Peter de Vega, because he hoped for more skilled physicians there. Nor did the opinion fail: for she had scarcely lain thus sick in Lima for a month when the scent of sanctity from Rose, then newly buried, and the public fame of her miracles, growing daily greater, penetrated to this sick woman. Confident of this, the supplicant humbly asked that some relics of Rose be brought to him. A very slender sprig of palm was brought, which the Virgin had carried on her deathbed with a lifeless hand. Also two pairs of the scapular and veil particles.

[119] [They are immediately cured by the same remedy.] These, together with a little dust from the sepulchre, were most closely bound to her by Magdalen's faith and devotion, as was fitting. Thus, with the pains suddenly stilled, she herself, in a pleasant slumber, spent the entire night most quietly, contrary to her custom. Upon waking, though he felt strength in the revived parts, he nevertheless refused to move the remnants, as if gladly granting the rest of that day to the fullness of the healing effect. But the next day, no matter how much the servants objected or the household protested, she sprang from her bed in robust health, put on her clothes, and, thanks to Rosa, steadfastly declared herself perfectly well. While horror and a cold stupor fixed the onlookers motionless, Mary Magdalene was already standing on her own feet. Soon, with no one to help him, he was walking about the room with complete freedom. Finally, having obtained clothes (which she had almost grown accustomed to), she left home with an honorable escort and made her way in firm strides straight to the church of Saint Dominic. There, after visiting Rose's tomb, she returned a month later to her own country and to her people by a different route than the one she had taken.

[120] [Other patients, too,] Mary de Morales, widow of Mark Antony, was afflicted not only by a nerve spasm but also by various torments in other limbs for a full five years, and she was most bitterly tormented: nor did the remedies she had procured—the very many of them—at such cost of time and money help her in the least. At last he seized the particle, the last remnant of Rose's garment, with eager faith and hand, and with it suddenly broke the bonds of attraction's nerve. Then, carrying it through the rest of her limbs in the same way, she touched the various places of her pains in the order in which they came, and the weakness and torment immediately fled, until at last she found herself, in every part, completely and miraculously healed. Alfonso Bravo's lawful wife, Andrea de la Massa, had tonsillitis and a severe sore throat, while the rest of her body was dangerously inflamed by a very high fever. A severe itching had set in, by which the nail was always in the ulcer, and sleep never came to the eyes. He had at home a shaggy little blanket that had once wrapped Rose's apoplectic arm. He therefore seized this with reverent confidence. He applied it to an annoying itch, where it burned the most. He successively rubbed this all over his body, and with the same friction simultaneously extinguished the fevers and his sore throat.

The wife of Basil of Bargas, Francisca del Pulgar, [who were suffering from various kinds of diseases,] was suddenly, after childbirth, afflicted most severely by ulcerous tubercles of the sea urchins. Neither was there a remedy for the evil, nor any rest from the evil. In the end, she owed her life to the bone ring she had received from Rosa. For as soon as she touched it with her finger, the disease vanished at once, and in her subsequent births (for she bore young several times afterward) she was not subject to any of the sea-urchin's stinginess. At home, the son of Diego de Requena lay sick, nearly senseless from the heat of the fever and a constant headache. At hand was Rose's belt, with which the Virgin had fastened her tunic in the final years of her life. He gave this to his son, to be applied at the proper times during a raging fit. He complied, and bound the head where it was most acutely throbbing, and with this single application of ointment he banished the fever along with the headache. Andrea Lopez had long been afflicted by a troublesome, though hidden, illness, and the nature of the disease is not specified in the record, except that it had afflicted the patient for a long time. But when she resorted to rose opopanacea and applied its heel-clasp to herself with a preliminary prayer, the disease immediately withdrew.

The governor of the province of Guailas in the Kingdom of Peru was Don Antonio de Contreras, and by that title he and his wife, Blanca de Zuniga, inhabited the village of Jungai. She, eight months pregnant, sensed by clear signs that the fetus in her womb, having become motionless, was no longer alive. While amid those trials and terrors Blanca was miserably adrift until the fifth day, some remnants of Rosa's garments were opportunely brought from Lima to her husband the governor, which Diego de Cantoral, an old friend, had soon after the Virgin's death sent as a gift. Antonius, suddenly bringing these to his wife in peril, easily persuaded her to place them on her womb, having first offered a devout prayer to Rosa. Scarcely had the remains touched the woman's womb, when within the short space of time it takes to recite the Angelic Salutation once, she gave birth to a dead infant, all of it bluish-black with a putrid greenishness. Shortly after, he also let out a second fart, accompanied by a foul odor that nearly suffocated those present. Blanca, relieved of those truly deadly burdens, breathed again, and she, having received the sacrament of penance, had already prepared herself for a most certain death. Within a few days, the fever having departed, she left her bed and for a long time enjoyed the benefit of perfect health.

On another occasion, when she had miscarried a four-month-old fetus after an unfortunate labor, with the afterbirth retained in the womb, she expelled such a quantity of corrupted blood from her abdomen over the course of two months and five days that she nearly despaired of her life. Remembering, then, the favor that Rosa had previously granted, she once again turned to her with humble devotion, promising to perform a novena at her tomb in Lima and to publicly proclaim both miracles by testimony. And behold, the next day, with a great flow of foul, bloody matter, the second womb, already putrefied, was expelled; and Blanca, realizing she had once again been snatched from death, set out for Lima and fulfilled her vow.

[124] [in peril] Bernarda de la Sierra, wife of Juan de Quiñones Zevallos, was more fortunate in having Rosa as her midwife during childbirth. This woman, living with her husband at the port of Gallao, on the very day of the funeral for the late Rosa, in the year 1617, had begun to suffer severe labor pains, by the force of which the child in the womb, turned transversely, while it could neither be retained nor delivered, entangled itself with its mother in the snare of imminent death. The intestine had already protruded (an obvious sign of impending death), and the infant's twisted deformity still impeded its own birth, finding more destruction than deliverance. The terrified midwives had in vain attempted the last of their art to prepare the child for birth; Already, amid the greatest torments, Bernarda, for both herself and her offspring, foresaw that day as the last, when John de la Massa, unexpectedly arriving at Bernarda's husband's home, said he had brought from Lima a few scraps of Rosa's clothing. Immediately the whole household is filled with hope; the sister of the laboring woman, Maria de la Sierra, takes up the relics and flies to the danger. Bernard quickly explains what he has brought.
