A firm reliance on the help of the Virgin Mary was conceived, a brief prayer was offered, the particles brought for the perilous womb were applied, and suddenly, without any obstetric assistance, the child in the womb turned itself for delivery. It was born with the greatest ease, lived, thrived, and grew up, and at its baptism the boy was named Peter de Quixano. Nor should it be left unsaid that on the right side of the newborn's eyelid a rose, lifelike in its depiction, appeared and long remained, as if an indelible seal of a favor.

[125] [They felt a similar power in the sacred relics.] The merchant Basil de Vargas of Seville, while traveling on business from the port of Lima to Potosí, was troubled on the way by constant trickles of discharge from his forehead, the acrimony of which was slowly threatening to produce a cancer in his nostrils, and by the fifth month the corrosive humor had grown so strong that it had broken through the nasal cavity, and began to consume the cartilage not without great agony. Nor was there any remedy for the voracious evil, until devotion anxiously knocked at Rose's apothecary. Basil had, as a gift from his wife, a small quantity of wool from a cushion on which Rosa used to spin or embroider flowers with a needle. He wrapped this in rough papyrus, tied it to his forehead, completely dried up the source of the corrosive discharge, and made a full recovery. This Basil had as his servant Diego de Burguinas, a Cantabrian by birth, who in 1619 in the same street of Potosí felt his throat tighten inside and swell outward from some unknown disorder of the flow. No ointments helped, no gargles broke up the most tenacious phlegm, until one night, with his breath almost cut off, he was suffocated. Therefore he asked Basil for a handful of wool (about which mentioned above), wrapped it around his neck, and, with Rose's help invoked, rested for the rest of the night. In the morning he was found by his roommate with a throat completely free of catarrh; all swelling had subsided, his ability to breathe had returned, and at once Basil's wool was restored to him. A noteworthy thing about it, among other things, is that it cannot be determined whether it is from a fleece or a woolly sheep: in its native color it resembles neither silk nor cotton, but shines through in threads with scattered flashes of gold. Finally, it is believed to be from the pillow on which the little Jesus once familiarly used to sit as a child with the Virgin.

Notes.

Cephalalgia is a headache, as the name itself indicates: for it comes from the Greek root kephale, which means head. However, howκεφαλαία and κεφαλαλγία differ from one another is found in Gorræus, Castellus, and other physicians.

b. The temporal parts are the sides of the head between the temples, the ears, and the crown, and the bones of the skull situated on both sides of the ears. The biographer more often uses this name in the singular number, although it is more customary in the plural number.

Collyria, Greek kollýria, are among the Latins those medicaments which are applied to the eyes when they are afflicted, as Castellus relates in his Medical Lexicon, page 97 and following. This medicine comes in two forms, one liquid and the other dry, and for the use of each and their different names, consult the praised Castellus there.

It is most often called erysipelas, in the genitive erysipelatis, and is of neuter gender, although the biographer in this passage inflects that word differently, yet elsewhere follows the common inflection of this word, as we shall see later. Whatever this matter may be, according to Gorræus in his Medical Definitions, page 157, erysipelas is an unnatural swelling of the skin, arising from blood and yellow bile, which are warmer, or from boiling, thin blood. There, he then assigns the various causes of this disease and their species and appellations.

g John of Laet, book. Chapter 10, West Indies 25 describes this great and pleasant valley of Peru in more detail, so that the curious reader of such matters can read it there.

Cacique or cazique is a name of dignity, which the Spaniards generally attribute to all the rulers or prefects of the Indians, even though it was properly used in Mexico and the islands: for the ancient rulers or chiefs of Peru were formerly called curacas, as the Trivultian Dictionary has carefully observed under the words cacique and curaca.

Castellus, in the medical lexicon on page 315, discusses marshes as follows: Marshes are veins of the seat, which sometimes protrude outward, their mouths gaping, and at a fixed time excrete melancholic blood; otherwise they are called hemorrhoids, and when they flow more than is proper, water follows between the skin. Then Castellus, page. 469 explains the same disease by synonyms in these words: Sycosis, marisca, fig, and fication is a tumor so called from its resemblance to a fig, ulcerous, round, somewhat hard, reddish, and painful, from which sometimes a small and sticky substance, uneven and foul-smelling, issues.

Ceroma, Greek κήρωμα, is an ointment made from oil and wax, which the ancient athletes used to anoint themselves with to become stronger. Others want that oil to have been mixed with a certain kind of earth or sand. However, this name appears to derive from the Greek word κηρὸς, which the Latins call cera.

Opopanax is the juice of the herb called panacea, and it is composed of πᾶν (all) and ἀκέομαι (to be healed). Furthermore, among the Greeks, ὀπὸς is juice, so that the name opopanacea is composed of three Greek words and signifies a juice that heals all.

k Already often cited, Latius, book. Chapter 10, West Indies 26 On the location of the province of Guailas or Guaylas, he mentions it in passing as follows: Those who, however, from Caxamalca journey through the mountains, as they are called, to Lima, their route is through the province of Guaylas, in which few Spaniards live, but very many barbarians, who have immense flocks of sheep, from whose wool they weave various kinds of cloth. The vicarage is (with correction) first and foremost useful and rich.

That same famous port of Peru is Callao, which we mentioned above.

I suspect that Cancrenam is placed here for gangrene: for gangrene among physicians is the mortification or putrefaction of a limb, as everyone knows, in which sense a cancerous wound is read in Cangii's barbarous glossary for a gangrenous wound. It seems that the same imminent destruction or corruption is indicated here.

Cantabrus, that is, born in Cantabria, which is now commonly called Biscay, and whose inhabitants dwell between Asturias, Old Castile, Navarre, and the Cantabrian Sea, and use their own idiom, entirely different from that of the neighboring peoples.

Section VI. Dust taken from the place where the Saint was buried, relieving headaches, fevers, and other illnesses.

[Prodigious powder,] Higher some things must be repeated here. Limensis SS. The convent of the Rosary, though it abounds in religious (usually numbering more than three hundred), would find the space of its single chapter house far too cramped for burying the successive bodies of so many brothers, unless earth had been brought in from elsewhere to hasten the burial of the dead and make room for those who would follow. For this purpose, a clod brought from a distance by ship, far from the fields of Panama, deeply filled the entire floor of that chamber, a soft, fine, loose, and sandy soil, yet subtly voracious by its innate heat. Thus it happens that it soon pulps the introduced bodies and at once dissolves the hardness of the bones. Such was she who there received the virgin body of Rose. But strange to say! In that entire space where that sacred corpse had been placed, the soil changed its nature and hardened into a nearly pumice-like solidity, so that it can only be ground or broken by fingers and a light hand, and must be excavated with iron. Not so the rest of that cemetery's sand, which is very easily ground, tractable, and fluid.

[127] [dug up from the tomb of St. Rose,] Furthermore, in this cenotaph of Rose, the fact that a living spring of the salutary powder lay hidden for the perpetual benefit of mankind was shown by this: that in the year 1632, after the earthen jars had been exported from there and distributed throughout the entire kingdom, (and indeed from that part of the pit only where the head of the Virgin had been placed) scarcely three or four pounds, dug up from a small hollow, were found: and when Father Bernard Marques had a few days before inserted his whole arm into the opening, a little later, with the dust having grown inside, he could scarcely get his whole hand in. Now to the history of treatments.

[128] [She was of great benefit to many sick people,] Rosa had bought the ten-year-old Ethiopian girl for fifty pieces of silver for the domestic services of her parents. The mother loathed the useless slave, since he was subject to various ailments, and especially to frequent dysentery. But Rosa told him to be of good cheer, for the time would come when the girl would make a full recovery by divine grace. He was certainly present, but after Rose's death, when the dust from her cenotaph was given to the little handmaid to drink cold. She drank, and was immediately healed of all her diseases. A severe case of synanchia had inflamed the throat muscles of six-year-old virgin Joseph of Zarate so severely that she could neither swallow food nor open her mouth without agony. Surgeon Ortega was summoned, who, upon inspecting the ulcer, discovered that gangrene had set in, and declared the girl incurable: for the eroded flesh was crumbling away from the ulcer in little pieces. and the evil spread day by day. An anxious mother earnestly recommended the health of her little daughter, Rose, who had been much talked about in the town. Then she dissolved a little of the earth that Joseph's grandmother had brought from the Virgin's tomb for this purpose in the water, and fed it to the sick woman with a spoon. The girl, having taken a single, very easy gulp, immediately opened her mouth in a wide yawn; she then assured them she was fine and asked for food. When the mother, together with her grandmother, more closely examined the little girl's throat, she found a necrotic ulcer, amazed at the wound's size. Meanwhile, Joseph, having been refilled with food (which she eagerly swallowed), recovered without the aid of any other medicine, and faithfully fulfilled the promised Novena.

[129] In the monastery of Saint Clare in Lima, Sister Grimaneca of Valverde, having delivered herself up to the torments of a double tertian fever, [as is proven by her sudden recovery] and a copious hemorrhage after fifteen days and nights of sleepless unrest, was miserably delirious; Only a lucid interval of mind was awaited, so that he might receive the last sacraments of the dying. For the doctors constantly asserted that he had no more than eight hours to live. Therefore, Abbess Isabella de la Fuente, deeming it right to flee to the heavenly defenses, fixed her hope on the favors of a single miraculous Rose. He produced the dust from the Virgin's sepulcher with great faith, signaled to the confessor to dissolve it in pure water, and to give it to the sick woman to drink. It happened that, behold, Grimanus, suddenly regaining consciousness, first felt that the deep, bloody flow had ceased, then that his fever was subsiding; and so, having tried no other remedy, after a peaceful slumber he was, the next day, sound in mind and body.

[130] There, Sister Raphael de Esquivel, scorched by a bilious fever, [and of the women,] and further strangled by the constriction of her throat, while being drained by excessive bloodlettings and evacuations, had lost both her reason and her sanity. Desperate, she was ordered by the doctor to be moved from the common infirmary to another room, so that, freed from more frequent conversation, she might more quietly prepare to receive the last sacraments, if it could be done. There, the anxious guardian offered her a drink of water mixed with dust from the Rose's tomb, and suddenly, the fever broken, Raphaela recovered her senses. She asked for food, ate heartily, and recovered completely without any other remedy.

[131] Father F. Ferdinand de Esquivel, Subprior of the Dominican Order's convent of St. Mary Magdalene in Lima, [then of men,] suffered a dreadful rupture on both sides of his peritoneum, a hernia (which they call an enterocele) that afflicted him so severely that he could neither preach nor travel, no matter how many supports and bandages he tried to constrain the viscera bulging into his scrotum. Half-torn by this painful torment, but no less eager in his faith, he went one evening to Rose's tomb, prayed devoutly, and applied the earth he had taken from there to his loins, and from that moment on, he could not notice a single trace of his former pain: Rather, resuming his preaching duties, he became more vigorous in his work. About to ride, he agilely swung himself from the ground into the saddle, and robustly completed four thousand leagues by land and sea without any impediment or sensation of hernia, free from the bonds with which he had bound himself to his daily prayers as a votive offering.

[132] [who, afflicted with various ailments,] the Virgin Anna Cortes, from a continuous two-month fever accompanied by pleurisy of the rib membrane, and papules (which they call epinyctides) that were fatally puncturing her entire body, had lost all appetite for food and drink to severe nausea; Hope for sleep and quiet was gone; with slack steps, one walked toward death. The mother and her daughter, Laurencia Cortes, having tried in vain human remedies, finally turned to heavenly ones. He drew the powder (which he had once noted smelled most sweetly while he was gathering it from Rose's tomb) from the chest, and, perceiving that same fragrance again, he rose more hopeful of receiving help. Both knelt before the sick woman's bed and, in honor of Rose, recited the Lord's Prayer with the Angelic Salutation. Then the mother gave the languishing man powdered water to drink. She drank, grew hotter than usual, rested, and the next day, with the pleurisy gone, the fever abated, and the papules cleared, Anna rose up unharmed. A chance fall had broken Stephen de Cabrera's rib: his side swelled from the bruised wound, the pain raged with each heartbeat, and a dreadful ache robbed him of sleep. But as soon as he laid the earth of Rose's tomb upon the offended party, he quietly fell asleep and spent the entire night in rest. In the morning, feeling himself free from pain, he inspected and palpated his side and was pleased to find that the swelling had likewise disappeared.

[133] [or oppressed by pains,] Catherine of St. Mary, a tertiary nun of the Order of St. Dominic, had been afflicted for nearly four years with various severe illnesses that had nearly torn her limb from limb. His side pains had increased, his sciatic pains, and his heart, daily tormented seven or eight times by its own pangs, his stomach would twice a day give way into a fainting fit, so that he had to be quickly restored with breakfast at dawn. A slow heat was scorching his remaining viscera, and especially his head, which was kindled by frequent ardors; finally, from a deep melancholy, a stubborn and seared heaviness was gradually turning into consumption. Overwhelmed by these misfortunes, Catherine went to the tomb on the third day after Rose's deposition, poured out prayers with tears, drank the powders ground in a mortar, and at first felt, once the leaden weight of melancholy was cast off, her heart brightening and strengthening; soon the burning ardors of her head, which she had then endured most intensely, were also assuaged. Finally, at that very hour, the whole host of those multiple diseases yielded to the powder of Rosea, leaving the woman strong and perfectly healthy. A very similar relief from these powders was experienced by Isabella de Peralta, a professed nun at the Monastery of the Incarnation in Lima, who, for twenty years besides her constant heart pains, had been daily tormented by apoplexy (especially in winter). But as soon as she applied a little of that earth to her palpitating heart, she found herself overtaken by both diseases.

[134] [Having received him reverently,] There, Beatrix de Montoya, a nun who had suffered from dyspepsia for twenty years, was at last, through the severity of her convulsions, hastening to her end. and had already received the last sacraments, certain to die, unless, by swallowing a mouthful of that earth, he at the same time miraculously broke every twinge of his long-standing pain. The priest Maurice Rodriguez, the confessor of the aforementioned monastery, was vexed by an importunate dizziness and headache, and he omitted no kind of remedy from the pharmacopoeia. But at last he learned that, by his illness, Rose's only tomb was the apothecary. Therefore, he not only inhaled the powder that had been brought, but also rubbed it on his crown, and with one action banished both dizziness and pain. The virgin Lucia de Montoya had a deep wound in her navel, the size of a silver Philippa. But on that spot where Lucia sprinkled a little earth from Rose's tomb, the wound healed within three days, leaving only a scar as a reminder of the favor.

In Cusco, Father Brother The funeral sermon for the happy passing of Rose (who was then known there) had been entrusted to Blasius of Lisbon, the preacher general, to be preached. For him, while he was carelessly pondering the theme of a sermon to be delivered over his lunch, a bolus lodged in his epiglottis had cut off his breath. They rushed to the danger of the Father. John of Aguero was presented, and Father Fr. Thomas Marcos And since neither the infusion of liquids nor a vigorous percussion of the back could dislodge the bolus, they urged the half-conscious man to take the viaticum, so that he might receive sacramental absolution. He fell to his knees, trying to confess. Meanwhile, Aguerus offered a little of this salutary earth in a small vessel of water; having taken it, he immediately spat out the fatal lump and, by expelling death, from whose cold sweat he was already drenched, was saved.

[136] On March 21, 1618, in the presence of several matrons, Catherine of Arriaga's nostrils gushed a prodigious flow of blood; [this is further confirmed] The profuse bleeding seemed to be stopped only by death, for the floor was so widely stained purple by the ceaseless blood. Finally, one of the women bound a handful of that earth to Catherine's forehead, and at that moment, upon the first touch of the sacred powder, the bleeding stopped, the festering wound dried up, and the evil did not return thereafter. A notary and one of the judges appointed by the archbishop to examine Rose's miracles were present at the miracle.

In the year 1631, a twenty-four-year-old Ethiopian girl was serving as a handmaid in the Monastery of the Holy Incarnation. [with many examples of them,] This one's throat was inflamed with angina, her entire body was covered with purplish papules, and moreover, she was swollen all over her body and face, having puffed up like a drumhead rather than a woman. From the mouth, nose, and eyes, a foul pus flowed abundantly, and if any part was even slightly touched, it would be disfigured, the torn skin clinging to the hand. Thus, the decaying one had no hope of life left. Having confessed and been absolved, fortified with the sacred Viaticum and anointed with the Oil, she awaited only the hour when she would breathe her soul through so many wounds. But the confessor, conscious in himself of the benefits of the Rose, encouraged the sick woman to commend herself to the heavenly Rose, promising to send the dust from its sepulchre, by which, with the Virgin's help, she might hope for salvation. When it was sent, the ailing woman, with the liquid poured over it, drank it most devoutly, and at the very threshold of death she recovered.

[138] A virgin girl in Lima had wrongly preferred the pleasure of the flesh to modesty. [who, exposed to the danger of death,] When labor was imminent, she scarcely confessed her crime to her mother, and, lacking midwives, in order to conceal the offense, she faced a life-threatening predicament, being neither able to give birth nor to retain the pregnancy. A fearful mother asked two religious men who happened to be passing by if they had any powder from the Tomb of the Rose with them. The daughter, impatient of pain and delay, greedily licked up the powdered medicine; at that very moment she gave birth. and he believed that the discussion was an absolute danger to his life. But the mother had noticed that the afterbirth had remained in the woman's womb. Therefore, she returned to those same powders, and immediately the girl also gave birth to twins, congratulating herself and the infant on their safety. There, long afterward, an Ethiopian midwife labored for many hours with a precarious outcome and nearly collapsed while assisting with the birth. By chance, the priest Maurice Rodriguez, passing through the olive market, learned of it and urged Eleanor of Villa-Fuerte to quickly bring the land requested from Rose's tomb to her endangered companion. It happened, and as soon as the earth was laid upon the mother's womb, the fetus burst forth with a brief and very easy effort, and freed the mother from both danger and burden.

[139] [or languishing from a long-standing illness,] In the year 1631, Fathers Anthony Montoya and John de Estrada were sent to Guamanga to be ordained to the sacred orders. They had turned aside to the local prefect, Guando, for lodging on their way. And behold, at about the fourth hour after noon, a certain one of the townspeople came running up in a great hurry and, with a loud cry, begged the Brothers (whom he believed to be priests) to hurry to the house of the neighboring Cacique, who was dying, so that they might at least absolve her with the sacrament of penance: for there was no other priest in the town at that time. The religious were grieved that they had not yet been ordained to that order to which the power of absolution is attached. Nevertheless, they followed the Indian with several Spanish laymen, so that at least (when they could do no more) they might attend the agonizing woman, recite litanies, prayers, and the commendation of the soul over the dying woman, and drive away the assaults of demons with holy water. Upon entering the house, they found Inda lying supine, speechless and motionless, stiff, with a horrifying bloody foam on her lips, and more like one dead than one alive. All counsel seemed in vain; the surrounding Indians mourned, and the husband sobbed with his afflicted family. but Fr. Antonius, remembering that he had some of the dust from Rose's cenotaph, addressed the assembled crowd with a few words about the Virgin's worth and grace before God, and about her frequent miracles, having spoken a few words to the bystanders about the Virgin's worth and grace before God, and about her frequent miracles, he finally, seizing a spoon, laboriously poured the diluted earth into the mouth of the dying man (which had to be forced open with a sharp iron), and departed, urging the household to implore help for Rose more fervently in the meantime. After returning with his companions to the sick woman's dwelling two hours later, he found the husband and his entire family in great joy. Entering the room, he saw the Cacique sitting cheerfully on the little bed and eating ravenously. She herself declared most eloquently that, without knowing it, she had been a blessing to Rosa, and that she owed her life to her. Nor was there any other feeling among the strangers who had come with Antony than that of the tenants.

[140] [in treating wounds,] Another, in a Panamense jar, inflamed by the putrefaction of ulcerated viscera with an excretion of blood, had brought her near her end; No other remedy remained. He inhaled the same dust, and in the blink of an eye saw himself perfectly healed. Didacus de Moreno Costilla, a native of Quito, had once received a monstrous wound to the head from a steel club in the Chilean wars, from which spasms, suppuration, and stupor gradually arose due to unskilled care. Thus, upon arriving in Lima to place himself under the care of better surgeons, he labored in vain for six months at the Hospital of St. Andrew under various ointments and plasters. Finally, stirred up by the fame of the miracles that were daily shining forth at the tomb of Rose, he went there, and kneeling down with devout prayers, he loosened the covering with the bandages from her head, removed the malagmah, sprinkled the earth that had been dug up there into the wound, and feeling that he was better from that moment, he hurried home cheerfully, and after two days he found that the wound had solidly closed and had grown over with healthy skin.

The abbess of the monastery of Saint Clare in the city of Trujillo, [of her legs,] had been miserably afflicted for about twenty years by an inflammation of the tibia, in which more than forty ulcers, each the size of a chickpea, gaped here and there, nor was Chironideemed suitable for such a chronic malady, especially since the affected leg, with the remaining blood, was beset by a disorder with the most troublesome alternation of feverish chills and burning heat. Thus, the sick woman, informed of Rose's most frequent miracles, drank with faith the powder brought to her from Lima in water, and instantly extinguished the fever that was raging through her very marrow with a chill. Delighted by this success, she rubbed the tibia in the same powder, and immediately the swelling began to subside, the fissures to close, only the blackish marks remaining as a memorial of the benefit. To wash these away from the outer skin, the surgeon John de Lezana recommended distilled water for this purpose. But when this was applied, the swelling soon returned with pain. The hollow pipes opened again. Having grown weak and sufficiently understanding that the miracle's completion was to be expected not from the surgeon, but from Rosa, she once again sprinkled the same powder for a relapse onto the tibia. Again, it subsided and sealed up the sores from every side by drying them. This triple prodigy brought health to the abbess, astonishment and joy to the entire monastery, and instilled a new devotion in the citizens of Truxilli with heartfelt congratulations.

[142] A certain soldier, by office and rank a standard-bearer, to his confessor, Father Fr. Francisco Nieto confessed that his shin had at one time swollen to the size of a cask, but upon hearing the signs and virtues that were daily growing more frequent at Rose's monument, he went there with others and, briefly praying, took some earth from the tomb and rubbed it on his own immense, swollen calf. and at that very moment the most troublesome swelling had completely subsided. The three-year-old boy Alphonsus Cortes was so weak in his legs that he dragged them along the ground with his hands, as if they were a useless burden. Mother Francisca de Leon, full of faith, stood at the tomb of Rose, had her feet take turns stepping into the pit from which the salutary powders were dug, and then at home she rubbed every joint of her little body with the same powder, and thus, entrusted to the Virgin's intercession, laid her in her little bed. In the morning, the little boy leaped up healthy, walked, and ran swiftly home, as if he had never been lame in his feet. For many years, Isabella de Peralta was tormented by a terrible pain in her gums, caused by an acute head cold. Especially one of her molars, whose middle had split open, and since the surgeons lacked both the skill and the nerve to pull it, tormented the poor woman day and night, so that she could neither sleep nor chew her food. At the advice of his confessor, he applied the said powders to his tooth when the pain was at its worst, and it immediately removed all the torment; and with his gums now free, he chewed solid food.

At the Monastery of the Holy Incarnation, novice Joanna de Ulloa, with her year of probation nearly completed, was distressed and anxious that she could not reach solemn profession, because her strict father (who lived far off in Potosí) was delaying in providing her dowry. Often Joanna would admonish her father in letters, urging and pressing him while weeping. But Marpesias, shrewdly more cautious, either refused to answer or most skillfully evaded the point that had been raised with obscure, empty circumlocutions. Joanna, at a loss for counsel, finally fled to Rosa. Then, having seized a pen, since she had written her last letter to her father, she faithfully sprinkled the dust of Rose's monument onto the page, so that if the arguments she had inscribed there did not soften her father's heart, the dust she had added would do the work. What a wonder! Suddenly, a reply arrived from Potosi, which could not have been more welcome. The father, softened, granted the dowry and everything his daughter had asked for, and, the long delay happily overcome, the nun acknowledged that she owed her vow of solemn profession to a little dust.

[144] [and bodily ailments,] Furthermore, it would be too lengthy to enumerate here, in separate paragraphs, the feverish patients whom the inhalation of those same powders has at times rescued from the very brink of death. It is enough to briefly and incidentally list the names. Joseph de Castro immediately vomited all the harmful crudities as soon as he drank, and recovered. Joanna de Mendoza, the sister-in-law of Peter de Vergas, and her little daughter cured their fevers with this remedy. His same maid drove away the sharpest toothaches. Father Francis Didacus de Palomino, of the Order of Preachers, overcame a double tertian fever. Maria Velasquez, wife of the centurion Didacus Ruiz de Campos, suffered a continuous fever and a deadly hemorrhage; Ursula, a servant of Mariana de Sea from the land of Felutpa, suffered a fever with diarrhea. Francisca, a servant of Angela de Aguirre, was summarily dismissed from the frontier on account of tertian fever and chronic asthma, under an absolute order. John of Palomares was doomed to die of tertian fever with asthma. Shortly after, his wife, who was seven months pregnant and therefore, according to the rules of medicine, unable to take medicine, was also afflicted by the same malady with frequent vomiting. Finally, one month after his birth, the same disease struck little Jerome on the threshold of life. But by successively inhaling each of these same powders, he was suddenly restored to health. The same aid was confirmed by the boy John Ascensius, the virgin Beatrix de Zuniga, and the adult Anthony de Umblea with uniform effect, but in different places. A feverish heat had deprived the fifteen-year-old girl Catherine of India of all sense, speech, and hope of life. Furthermore, with his jaws clenched, he had blocked the passage for food; it was necessary to force his mouth open with iron as a last resort, to pour in powder taken from the mound of Rosæ, without him knowing what was being done. The next day, breathing a sigh of relief, Catherine realized and confessed that she had been rescued from so many evils at once.

[145] [They also tried the same medicine of this powder.] Finally, to make a long story short, Father John Lobo swore an oath before the judges that he had, in various places—Chuquisaca, Potosí, Oruro, and elsewhere—at the request of people of all ages, of every age, sex, and condition, he provided this life-giving earth as a drink, and with this single remedy, openly in full view of all, so many kinds of ailments were cured, so many who were feverish daily returned home in perfect health, that their sheer multitude surpassed all diligence in recording names and rendered the catalog's labor in vain. Let the kitharædus king here exclaim in astonishment: "Will the dust confess to you, or will it announce your truth?" I say and proceed.

Notes.

Enterocele, or rather enterocele, called in Greek ἐντεροκήλη, is, according to Gorræus, a swelling of the scrotum caused by an intestine prolapsing into it. It occurs largely from a loosening, and sometimes even a sudden tearing, of the passage that runs from the peritoneum to the testicles. This is a type of hernia, which we have discussed in general above, and which includes enterocele, epiplocele, and hydrocel, as can be seen in Castellus, page 189.

b The physicians call these papules epinyctidas, in Greek ἐπινύκτιδας, as if you were to say in Latin nocturnal, from ἐπὶ nyktos, that is to say, at nightfall, because they most often arise at night or torment most severely, as Gorræus testifies.

Orexis, Greek ὄρεξις, is called in Latin appetite or the desire for food and drink, from ὀρέγομαὶ, which means I long for. Gorræus and Castellus discussed at greater length the manner and causes of this appetite.

Dyspepsia, which the French call indigestion, could be described in Latin by us as a depraved concoction of food, which Celsus calls crudity. The Greek name comes from the particle δὺς, which implies difficulty or corruption, and the noun πέψις, which signifies digestion. There are various forms of this or a similar disorder, namely apepsia and bradypepsia; consult the physicians who have already been frequently cited on these subjects.

They were formerly called by Philip of Macedon certain gold coins, which are frequently mentioned by the ancients. I suspect that here Hansen may be referring to the silver coin struck by order of Philip, King of Spain, which the biographer elsewhere called a pataco, as I explained above in the annotations.

Caciqua is most likely the wife of some Indian ruler or chief, as can be gathered from the name cazique explained above.

Spasm, Latin: a convulsion, is a contraction of the nerves, rigidity, or distension. This name comes from the Greek word σπάω, which means to pull out. Gorræus and Castellus relate more about this disease.

A malagm is properly a fomentation or plaster, which is applied to soften a hard matter. It comes from the Greek word malatto, which means to soften: for with malagmate the apostemata are softened and become ripe.

Chiron is the proper name of the most expert physician, about whom the ancient poets tell many fables.

chasma, Greek chásma, which is the same as the Latin hiatus, or fissure, and comes from the verb chasmáō that is, to yawn.

l Chuquisaca is the same as the city of Silver, commonly called la Plata, of which Baudrandus in the first volume of his Geography on page. 90 writes as follows: Silver and Argentina, la Plata, a city of South America in the kingdom of Peru and in the province of Charcas, whose capital is situated in the mountains and near the Picolmai River, shines with the dignity of an archbishopric, to which four suffragan bishops are subject. The city, founded by the Spaniards to whom it is subject, was established in the valley or tract called Chuquisaca (from which the city itself is often so called, as Laetius attests), and it is very cultivated, wealthy, and famous for its silver mines.

Since I have long sought in vain the name of this city, Orura, among geographers, and the Louvain edition of this Life is riddled with errors, I am not without reason to fear that the name has been garbled in this passage. However, it could be that the place called Oruro or Horuro is assigned here, about which John Laetius in his book. Chapter 11, West Indies He remembers around the ninth. But we will await this information from the Spaniards more experienced in these parts.

add to the movements, or something like that.

Section Seven. Many miracles have been performed and benefits bestowed on the sick through the painted or sculpted images of this Virgin.

[Through the attached images of St. Rose] Let this be a crown of thorns for the perfidious iconoclasts of our age. It was the common desire of the Peruvian faithful, in memory of a prodigious virtue, to have the image of Rose everywhere in their homes. and the practice became widespread not only in Lima, but throughout the entire kingdom, and not without good effect. For, first of all, the widow of Aloysius Nugnez Maria de Vera, brought to the very brink of death by a profuse discharge, fever, stomach pain, and a fatal vomiting, had prepared herself for her departure with the last sacraments, the physician judging that she would not survive until the next day. Meanwhile, from the tailor's neighbor's wife, Mariana Inda (who had been raised with Rosa from a young age) She asked for the Virgin's painted image on canvas, had it brought, pressed it to her face, kissed it devoutly, and at last, sweetly fallen asleep in its embrace, when she awoke in the morning, marveled to find herself in perfect health, sought refreshment, leapt from bed, and, intending to venerate the image more fittingly, placed it decently on a small altar, lights already lit. He certainly believed that this was owed both to a private benefit and to the common joy of that day. For on that morning, with solemn jubilation throughout the entire city, apostolic letters were being published in the metropolitan church, by which the faithful from everywhere were summoned to testify to the well-known miracles of the Rose. And behold, the image that had always reflected Rosa's pale face at that moment blushed charmingly on its cheeks, as if serenely rejoicing in the public triumph of the city. Díaz de Requena, together with his wife Apollonia, was summoned, and for a long time was stunned by such a conspicuous miracle.

[147] [(of some of these, the color changes in a marvelous way)] A very similar event occurred in the port of Callao on the same day around another image of the Rose: this one had been adorned by the virgin Petronilla de Quisano, in the house of the centurion Alvar de Lugares, with ribbons and flowers, since on that day it was to be placed in the pulpit of the prince's church, while the aforementioned compulsory letters were being read aloud to the assembled multitude. Until that hour, the face in the image was the same one that had been pale in Rosa's coffin. But suddenly, the deathly pallor on his face gave way to a vivid color beneath the eyes of the onlookers, and he lit up everyone's spirits with his charming radiance. Yet more beautifully did the same thing shine everywhere for the benefit of so many sick people.

In December of the year 1631, for nearly a whole year, a nine-year-old girl named María de los Reyes had her entire scalp covered all over with a sickly and foul porridge. After long and fruitless attempts at remedies, he is finally brought to the house of Saint Dominic; There, kneeling before the wooden icon of the Rose, she pressed the line she had drawn on her head against the virgin's image in supplication, and then placed it on her head again. Two days later, Melchiora de los Reyes, at home, uncovered her little daughter's head and found no trace of hardened impetigo on it. Her skin shone healthy and smooth all over, her thick hair gleamed, and the girl seemed not so much to lack a pimple as to have never had one. In November of the same year, another Mary, an orphaned ten-month-old infant, was being supported by Jerome de Soto Alvarado in his almshouses, hideously spotted all over her little body by leprosy. All medical art had despaired of curing it. but the domestic servant Bernarda, in the thaumaturgist Rose of Myrotheca, found an effective ointment for the infant. In the church of St. Dominic, she had gathered the little petals of dried roses scattered over a wooden image of the Rose; bringing these home, she secretly covered each of the little leper's sores with them and, having carefully bandaged her, laid her on a bed to rest. In the morning, Jerome informed Bernarda that the girl had been cleansed of all leprosy. He flew in astonishment, not believing his own eyes, and presented the tender Mary before the apostolic judges to be gazed upon; and, as a perpetual monument of the favor, he ordered her thereafter to be called Mary of the Rose.

When Michael de la Massa had developed a dreadful abscess under her arm, the surgeons inserted cantharides. The woman, though unequal to the most excruciating pain, endured the torture for no fewer than twenty-nine hours: But when the next day she was to be subjected to the same torments, she seized a paper image of Rosa (one of those that had been brought from Rome to Peru), applied it to the painful wound, and from that moment, whatever acrimony was poured upon it, she felt no pain, even though the bitterness of that treatment had to be continued for several more days. Mariana de Requena was afflicted by an intolerable pain in her chest, perpetually fanned by a certain disordered sadness and the heavy burden of melancholic blood, which was followed by a violent and frequent sighing and a painful difficulty in breathing. Whatever medicine had been administered to relieve the discomfort proved ineffective. He pressed the torn image of Rose to his heart, sweetening it with devoted kisses. From that point on he realized he needed no other remedies: for the single image of Rose both wiped away his long-standing sorrow and eased the torment of his heartache. Sister Angela de Haro, from the Monastery of St. Catherine of Siena in Lima, was afflicted for three consecutive months with a heartburn, or stomach convulsion; But when, having despaired of all other remedy, he pressed the same image to his breast, the pain subsided.

[150] A female slave named Francisca, seized by the same disease and further burdened by a fever, was writhing in agony at night, unable to bear the torment. And as she was writhing most severely in the dead of night, her mistress rose, brought the image that had been taken down from the wall to the sick woman, ordered it to be placed over her stomach, and implored Rosa for help. Francisca, after embracing the image, was overcome by sleep; having slept very peacefully for two hours, she awoke in good health, and was not troubled in this way thereafter. Sebastiana de Vega, sometime before setting out with her husband, Doctor Cyprian de Medina, a lawyer for the royal tribunal, on a journey to Succaram, while standing on the stirrup and trying to mount her saddle as her mule backed away, dislocated a bone in her groin and suffered such unbearable pain that she could neither turn over in bed nor apply herself to any strenuous work. One night, the torment of his bone growing more acute, he had the papyrus image of Rosa, printed in Rome with the permission of the Superiors and thence imported into Peru in multiple copies, quickly brought to him. While embracing it with religious devotion, she fell asleep amid her prayers, and upon waking in the morning, she suddenly cried out in testimony to the miracle, telling her husband that she had been completely freed from her pains.

[151] [& three postpartum women] The maid Elizabeth Biafora, pregnant and near her delivery, was being consumed by a severe pleurisy of the ribs, was burning with fever, was vomiting blood and pus from her mouth, and finally, abandoned by the doctors and having received the sacraments, was hastening toward death with the extreme danger of a mature fetus. Finally, having obtained a paper image of the Rose from the monastery of St. Catherine of Siena, she held it pressed against her aching side all night. When the doctors returned in the morning, they found the maidservant healthy and strong, and she was demanding that food be brought to her, as if she were a desperate mother trying in every way to care for her unborn child. Then, once she had recovered, she happily gave birth to a healthy baby three days later.

[152] [in a dangerous childbirth] In the year 1632, Angela de Albildo, the wife of Francis de las Cuentas, was carrying twins in her womb; But when it came time to give birth, on Sunday, May 16, at two hours before noon, one of the membranes ruptured on its own, leaving the second one and the remaining fetal tissue inside the mother. The women standing by whispered that this was fatal to the woman in labor, and now the husband, about to bid his dearest wife a final farewell, was seeking admission. But hindered, he wept outside. Amid this calamitous perplexity, the Roman Rosæ's ectypon is brought in, but on a cloth, and is placed opposite the stricken invalid so that she can see it conveniently. Scarcely had Angela, with her mind full of supplicating prayers, devoutly looked back at the image, when suddenly, seized by new pains, she, in a very rapid delivery, gave birth to the remaining offspring along with the afterbirth onto the bed where she lay, with no time to summon the midwife. The twins were little girls, healthy, beautiful, and a bit plump, and at their baptism they were named Mary and Francisca de Rosa in memory of the benefactor.

[153] [They are prodigiously aided.] Anna Maria, daughter of the widow Maria Morales, carried a womb burdened with a stillborn fetus, and anxiously carried it about until it was buried rather than born. The pains of labor broke upon her, but the lifeless, cold, and heavy child, without movement, blocked the efforts of nature and likewise involved the mother in the final peril of life. Thus the poor woman wrestled with her anxieties for two days, and, slowly sensing her death, had purified her soul by the sacrament of penance. Already pale-faced, she was sinking into deadly fainting spells; every pulse in her arteries had ceased, all hope of life was gone, when her anguished mother brought from her little case a hastily drawn paper bearing Rosa's likeness and handed it to her dying daughter. Then it proceeded to prayers and tears; And behold, at that moment Anna delivered the dead baby and the afterbirth, and immediately, the danger having passed, she breathed. The fetus, large in body, with a soft skull, a flabby chest, and black feet, had already begun to rot at its extremities and exhaled an intolerable stench, which would undoubtedly have killed the mother, had not the image of Rose come to her aid.

[154] [By similar images of apoplexy,] A most violent stroke of apoplexy had pervaded the entire body of an Ethiopian girl, Mary by name, in the house of Diego de Requena, from head to foot; her nerves were stunned, and her arms and legs throbbed violently. For four days now he had been unable to speak, his mind was absent, and, in the doctors' opinion, the final hour of his life was imminent. But the matron likewise produced a paper on which the image of Rosa was inscribed. She covered her weak face with this, and at the same time implored Rose for help. And behold, suddenly the distorted agitation of her feet and hands ceased; the girl, restored to herself, rose, ate, and indicated that the entire force of the disease had vanished. Admiring the virtue of this sacred image, James tried it on himself. Under his right knee a painful swelling had hardened, greatly impeding his ability to walk; so he gently pressed that very image of Rose onto the sore spot, and at the first touch he felt himself instantly free. Apollonia, the wife of Didacus, stopped the most painful headache with nothing other than this very simple poultice. The same remedy was used by the nun Anne Mary of Jesus against long-standing and extremely severe toothaches.

In the autumn of 1630, Francis Gutierrez Magan, a priest from Seville suffering from arthritis with dysentery, was afflicted with severe hip pain. While placing too much trust in hot poultices, he forced the irritated humors to descend into his right foot, where the arthritis from which he suffered intensified his torment to the utmost. His veins swelled, he blushed, and his swollen, terribly inflamed shin burned so fiercely that not even a blanket or a thin sheet could bear it: and because at the same time a bloody dysentery was wasting the poor man, he asked for the last rites with the doctor's consent, for both were convinced that his life was at an end. The sick man requested that, as his last comfort, the painted image of Rose, from the monastery of St. Catherine of Siena (to which he was a confessor), be brought; he ordered it to be hung inside the canopy of his bed, and, drawing the curtains, thus addressed the absent Rose in her present likeness: "Holy and glorious Virgin, who are so powerful with the Most High, in whom you happily rejoice, look upon me, who, if I have never deserved anything else from you, am at least the confessor of your mother; I was a helpful assistant to him in being received into the monastery to take holy orders. I authorized his solemn profession of the black veil with a blessing. Therefore, pray to your Spouse that, forgetting my faults, He may restore me to health, so that I may be able to serve Him. Having said this, with great strength but firmer faith he placed both hands under the swollen part of your shin and, having begged permission, lifted the very heavy weight up to the edge of the painting. Then, after the briefest moment of contraction, he slowly lowered it onto the mattress, and (which he had long been unable to do) rested in peaceful slumber for half an hour. Upon waking, he called his household servants with a cheerful shout and showed them his foot, freed from its bandages, completely free of gout, swelling, or redness, and in no way unlike his left foot. His health had returned completely, and from then on the severe arthritis no longer forced Francis, as before, to use a cane.

The same Francis was the godfather of Mariana de Sea's little son, Louis Cortes. [fever with pleurisy,] This boy, five years old in August of 1631, was being consumed by fevers and was being exanimated by pleurisy. Applied unguents, phlebotomies, leeches, enemas; The potions did the boy more harm: for he spat blood from his mouth, and his ribs, ears, stomach, and liver ached. Doctor Francis Ximenes entered, and at first glance he could not hide his astonishment at how small the child was for being so desperately ill. Francis Gutierrez arrived, and bringing Rose's image, he went ahead of the sick Louis with words by which he might commend himself earnestly to the Virgin. After uttering these words with all his strength, a sweet, faint sleep seized the frail man for about half an hour; when it was broken, he asked for the sacred image to be brought closer, for he wished to honor it with a kiss. Greeting the innocent with a devout kiss, he immediately regained his strength, the pleurisy ceased, the fevers vanished, and in a short time, with no other medicine, he made a full recovery.

[157] [Headaches,] Catherine of Vera endured for twenty consecutive days the most atrocious pains in her brain and temples, so that, unable to take either food or sleep, she was soon thought to be about to pass into the jaws of death. This was the view of the pro-regent's chief physician and another doctor, Rocca. Therefore, after the sacred confession was completed, Catherine earnestly begged Father Francis de Coloma to bring her, for a short time, the image of the Rose that he had in his home. He brought it and placed it inside the bed curtains near the sick woman. Soon she, as best she could, reverently kissed the painting, successively pressing her neck, her temples, and her face against it, drew from the sacred image sleep (of which she had been deprived for nearly three weeks), along with respite from her pains, and finally perfect health. Anna Maria de Torres was afflicted with sharp pains in her head, as if a scorching tower or a fiery spike were pressing down on her skull. The acuity of the eyes was darkened, the blazing times pulsed, the sky seemed to burn; Nor did the woman remain sufficiently composed. Nevertheless, driven by necessity, as if conscious of the danger, she knew that, having abandoned all other remedies, she must seek healing in the image of the Rose alone. She found it, and indeed immediately: for when she embraced the ectypon and pressed it to her head, she was suddenly freed.

[158] [and other diseases are cured.] Anna de Herrera, as much as she had once stubbornly fought against the miracles of the Rose out of incredulity, was afterward, having experienced the benefit in herself, its trumpet and herald. He had long been accustomed either to dismiss with objections or to scorn the thanks and daily miracles that were multiplying at the Virgin's monument. But a persistent nightmare corrected her: she believed that a horrifying earthquake had overturned all of Lima from its very foundations, and amid these terrors, even unwillingly she entrusted herself to Rosa's protection, and with such strain in her voice that she awoke merely from the effort of crying out. Then, with eyes wide open, he saw Rosa (whom he had known from the various images he had seen) kneeling and praying for the safety of her city. From then on, Anna, more at peace in spirit, repented of her former error and more devoutly committed herself to her protection. A hair-raising opportunity did not long fail. For some time, Anna had been afflicted by a violent and painful discharge that gradually so dulled her mind that, deprived of sleep for three days, she realized she was about to lose her sanity, had not Rosa hurried to her aid. Thus he impressed this fleeting image upon his left temple two or three times; soon, sinking back into his pillow, he fell asleep, and when he awoke at midnight, he found that all pain had fled.

[159] [Finally, the great majority of those who were feverish] It has been established concerning fevers that, at the touch of the sacred image—nay, at its mere sight—they abated more quickly than wax melts from the face of a flame. To Doctor N. Rocca had a twelve-year-old daughter named Maria. Fevers and papules announced a premature death sentence. But the same was torn apart by the portrait of Rose that had been brought, as soon as it came into the sick woman's view. In Palama, P. John de Figueroa of the Order of Preachers, struck down by a fatal fever from putrid indigestion of the stomach, and with the city's physicians having despaired, had piously received the last sacraments of the dying. And the dying man, surrounded on his little bed by the religious, awaited the sign of the final agony, so that, according to the Order's rite, the watchers might commend his departing soul to God and the heavenly hosts. At one of them suggesting it, an image of the Rose is brought from the Prior's chamber, which, as the sick man beheld it, he greeted devoutly with a fervent kiss and reverently placed upon his brow, feeling his nerves strengthened and his strength restored. Soon, as he drank the marvelous powders of the sepulchre along with the juice, he simultaneously expelled the entire undigested, corrupt mass of the worm. Thus, the fever, deprived of its fuel, was extinguished of its own accord, when it was believed to be about to kill the patient.

To Alphonso de Yta, administrator of the Monastery of St. Catherine of Siena in Lima, [they are restored to their former health,] his wife was Maria de Aspitia, whom the doctors, seeing her suffer from a double tertian fever, had nearly exanimated by drawing her blood five times and by other very powerful evacuations. Already frail, unable to eat or sleep, she was hastening to the very brink of life. But the image of the Rose, brought from the monastery in haste, checked the raging fever; and when it was placed on the bed for Aspisia to gaze upon, it at that very glance laid low the fever and raised the sick woman. The same paper image of Rosa, most painfully affixed to Philip Aegidius, who was grievously afflicted by a double tertian fever, was placed upon his head during the very paroxysm of the chill. In the port of Callao, the licensed John de Quisado was afflicted by raging fevers for forty consecutive days, and no hope of recovery shone from human remedies. A painted image of the Rose is brought into the sick man's chamber. John, with the devout kisses he had previously offered, placed it on his head and pressed it firmly to his breast, banished the fever, and compelled the place to yield at once to restored health.

Let Mary of Oliva, who gave birth to Rosa, close the history herself. [& the mother herself is delivered from erysipelas,] A few days after she had entered the monastery of St. Catherine of Siena to take her veil, she was hideously disfigured by erysipelas on almost her entire face; A swollen, inflamed tumor burst forth from the nose to the right ear; from there, a swift inflammation of an unsettled ulcer spread across the head and jaws, to which fever, stomach dyspepsia, and other related symptoms readily joined. Thus afflicted, she lay in bed for eight days, trembling greatly at the daily increase of her ailments. The abbess, who loved Rose's mother most tenderly, was likewise deeply distressed at the sick woman's peril; and one night, accompanied by a number of nuns to visit the infirm, after sweet words she gently broached the question of bringing the Virgin's beneficent image. The sick woman did not object, but rather welcomed her to the bed. The nuns who had gone off returned after half an hour and saw Mary bound by a sweet slumber, yet at the same time drenched in profuse sweat. The next day, springing out of bed and hurrying to the odeum to attend the Mass, she ran into the prefect, who was surprised and asked why and where she was going, and above all, how she was. Here Mary confesses that to herself, the sacred image of the Daughter, repeatedly disfigured, had let sleep creep over her, but at the cock's crow in the night she had woken, drenched in sweat. Thus, having felt his pulse, he discovered that the fever was gone; finally, running his hand over his face, he detected no trace of a blister, and now found himself in perfect health and feeling a keen hunger.

[162] Another. To the same Mary of Oliva, already a nun, a narrow cell had been assigned among the other novices, [which she saw, through an icon of her Daughter] and this was made all the more inconvenient by a huge wooden bedstead, built like a wardrobe, which she wished to have raised off the floor and set on four pillars, so that it could hold three larger chests underneath, which otherwise made the cell much more cramped. Indeed, the weight of the machine prevented it from being lifted by feminine strength into the hypostyle, and the broad ledge of the underlying platform kept it from being moved even from the spot where it had once been set. Isabella of Catanno, the novice mistress, had promised that on the very next day she would see to it that, with four strong slaves brought in, the heap would be lifted onto the prepared timbers and the oppressive narrowness of both Mary's cell and the cell itself would be eased. But the pious diligence of the ten-year-old maid prevented it. For seeing the novice Mary constantly afflicted, she, having stepped aside from the choir with the others, boldly set to work on the piece and, with the help of the image of the Rose, completed it. Mary returned from choir and, seeing the machine raised into position and the chests arranged beneath it most conveniently, was astonished, unable to believe that in the entire monastery anyone could have had the strength and spirit to accomplish such a thing in so short a time.

[163] [and other miracles were also performed,] Agnes (for that was the little handmaid's name) freely confessed that she had done it all by herself. The nuns rush to the curious spectacle, and the girl is ordered to explain by what power she could have accomplished such a thing. He replied that he had first knelt before the hanging image of Rosa, had asked for help and strength, and finally had reverently placed the image on the bed, which was to be moved with a cart, and that soon, with little effort, the machine was retracted to one side, he had arranged bricks in those four places, and finally, in the name of Rose, had confidently placed a wooden beam across them, which, as he lifted it, he thought he was carrying not a heavy machine, but light straw. The nuns were amazed and astonished that the ten-year-old girl, under the strong protection of the sacred image, could do what four strong porters were scarcely believed capable of performing without breaking a sweat. But amazement grew when the novice mistress (who had remained in her cell nearby while the others went to the choir) affirmed that she, being awake under the nearby pressure of such a work, had not perceived the slightest noise in the profound silence of the dormitory. The onlookers asked Agnes to try her strength once more in their presence to see if she could lift the machine. She tried, but, as if she had taken up a mountain on her back, soon grew weary of the futile and ridiculous attempt and gave up.

[164] [to which the author adds an epilogue.] Let it be lawful for me, too, to pause here, while I address Rose, daughter of the Olive, mother of favors, with the honeyed words of the honey-making bee (Bernard): "O fruitful stock of the Olive, anointing and shining in the house of God, fostering benefits, radiant with miracles, make us partakers of the light and sweetness of which you enjoy." O fragrant Rose, forever sprouting before the Lord and blooming, spreading everywhere the life-giving scent of sweetness, whose memory is a blessing among us and whose presence is honored in heaven, grant that those who sing of you may not be deprived of such a participation in fullness. Finally, let us rejoice and exult, for we have that heavenly court which cares for us, which protects us by its merits, which it has informed us with its examples and confirmed with its miracles.

Notes.

It was formerly called the Sunday of Palms because of the reasons which Macarius cites in the Hierolexicon, page 120. Here, through monastic usage or a more recent metaphor, it is taken as a reproach, as is clear from the sense.

Dandruff, from the Greek *pityriasis*, literally "flaking," is when small, thin scales like dandels fall from the head. You will find more about the various effects of this disease in Gorræus and Castellus.

Cantharides are small, poisonous animals, from which, however, the most excellent medicines are made, as is commonly known, and can be read more thoroughly by physicians.

Cardialgia is generally heart pain, about which Gorræus writes as follows: "Cardiálgia is the pain and biting at the opening of the ventricle from the acrimony of the humor." He relates more about this disease there, and also shows how cardialgia differs from cardiagma.

Chapter Three
Posthumous glory from the Pontifical diploma of canonization, which briefly contains the Life of the Saint and most of her miracles.

Section I. Preface of the Papal constitution, the deeds of the Saint up to the age of twenty, and her entry into the Third Order of St. Dominic of Penance.

[To the other peoples to whom the Gospel has been proclaimed,] Clement Bishop, servant of the servants of God. For a perpetual memorial of the matter. The Heavenly Head of the household, whose nature is goodness, whose will is power, and whose work is mercy, when he saw the Jews invited to the great feast of divinity excusing themselves as unworthy, sent his faithful servant here and there into the villages and streets of the city to summon other and other guests. Not content with the multitude of the poor, nor with the abundance of the weak, the blind, or the lame, nor with those who were near, nor with those who were far away, he commanded the message destined to fill the royal feast, in which not a few, but all things were prepared, and the heavenly home, to proceed to the distant peoples. From all over Asia, Africa, and Europe, processions of holy men and women of every age, condition, and rank were gathered. America languished, famished and alone, conscious of the secret mystery of the Lord willing the salvation of all, yet unable to hear the voice of the calling minister, until the fullness of time came, and through faithful ministers to those sitting in darkness and in the shadow of death, the voice of the Father thundered*, and the Gospel was carried beyond the Indus, & the Gangesa, & the equatorb, & through all the corners of the crossroads the hungry Gentiles were sought, that in the prepared feast of fat things and marrow with God of Abraham, Isaac, & Jacob they might recline at his table.

[166] Finally, beyond the farthest limits of the entire ancient world, far and wide to the ultimate shores of the Atlantic Ocean, [finally adds the inhabitants of America,] and to the outermost shores of the western Indies, which the sea (called the Pacific) borders with immense coasts, again and again to the faithful servant (through whom the Order of Preachers, the greatest of this S. R. And as is signified by the testimony of our doctor and predecessor Gregoryc), he had to make a detour until he reached Peru, into which, together with those who had a happy entry and success in that region, submitting themselves to God and the holy Roman Church, first penetrated the Orderd, which had long been distinguished by the singular privilege of this holy apostolic See with the title of Preachers, and before others announced the holy Gospel of Jesus Christ to the Peruvian nation, and first there, by sealing the word of salutary preaching with his blood, he effectively invited the entire nation to the providential supper of the Lamb, whose voice, labor, and blood cultivated that golden land to adorn the supper of the whole Church, both militant and triumphant, among the other flowers of Christian piety of that region for God, He brought forth for angels and men a fragrant Rose, the exemplar of all evangelical perfection, and the first ornament to be placed on the roll of the Saints from the new world: for since she had been born and given for this purpose, the Spirit of wisdom and understanding, the dispenser of all good things, filled her, and so inflamed her with the fire of His charity, that she not only refreshed with fragrance, but also shone with splendor in that part of God's house which lay hidden in darkness, so that she might shine forth like a morning star in the midst of a cloud, like the full moon in our days, and like the sun resplendent for all eternity.

[167] [among whom St. Rose flourished,] We therefore judge it just and entirely in keeping with reason, that the one whom our Lord Jesus Christ, whose place we hold on earth, was pleased to show forth in a singular manner, taken up by Himself as His most beloved bride, adorned with His jewels and crowns, we also, for the duty of our office throughout the universal Church, which we preside over, though unworthy, by apostolic authority decree that the venerable one, chosen by God, is to be venerated as holy, so that from the rising of the sun to the setting of the same, every tongue and all peoples may confess that great is the Lord in all things, and very praiseworthy in His wonders; For even in our days His mercies have not failed us. And though He chastens us as a father, our faults demanding it, He does not forget us forever; but in our afflictions He never ceases to protect us with manifold aid through His elect and His friends, by whose merits and intercessions He arms and defends us. And that the faithful of Christ the Lord may understand how exceptional this example of piety is, which in the new world has shone forth for the entire Church amid present calamities and dangers, its more illustrious charisms, virtues, and deeds from the many great gifts which the Lord bestowed upon her, we have deemed it fitting to set forth in these our letters, so that her sanctity, confirmed by the testimony of this apostolic See, may shine forth more brightly throughout the entire world for all time.

[168] In Lima, which is the metropolis of the Kingdom of Peru in South America, [who was born in Lima in the year of Christ 1586] in the year from the reparation of the salutation MDLXXXVI, on the twelfth day before the Kalends of May, this mystical Rose was born to honorable parents, Gaspar de Flores, and Mary of the Olive, in the street of Saint Dominic near S. The Spirit built a church, and on the very solemn day of Pentecost, which we call the Feast of Roses, she was purified in the sacred font of Baptism, so that it seems almost as if all these were signs of this infant's future sanctity. Her original name was Elizabeth from her grandmother, which was later divinely changed to Rose, not only by her mother because of the heavenly miracle of a rose appearing instantly on her face, but also by the most worthy Archbishop of Lima, Thuribiusf (not without a heavenly, as is believed, inspiration) at the anointing with the sacred Chrism, and was augmented by the Virgin Mother of God with the surname a Sancta Maria, so that this too might be more abundantly confirmed by the testimony that the merits of the saints are mighty before God.

The infant was often seen lying on the ground, gazing sweetly at the sky with fixed eyes, as if already a stranger on earth, demanding to be enrolled as a citizen of the heavenly Jerusalem by the eloquent silence with which she had been endowed from that time. And the intelligent little one always appeared a better patient to the strong man, sweet, charming, and serene, and was never heard to disturb the silence of the house with cries or a querulous voice, shunning only to be carried out in public. She displayed such steadfastness in her early years that she endured the dreadful tortures of surgery on her thumb, ear, and nose without complaint, and the punitive corrosion of her head caused by the powders applied as medicine, all with an unconquerable spirit, so that her disposition was already believed to have been born of patience. She was barely five years old when, inwardly instructed by the anointing of the Holy Spirit, she made a vow of virginity. And, warned by her brothers of the vanity of the world, she herself, unknown to her mother, cut her hair down to the living skin, so that no locks might remain with which she could be drawn away from devoted purity. or be drawn to marriage; She emulated that distinguished heroine, Catherine of Siena, whom she had appointed for herself at the very dawn of reason as an exemplar of holiness and a teacher, and whom she never ceased to call her teacher. She added, as a first safeguard, a second vow of perpetual abstinence from meat, by which she would more carefully preserve her virginity consecrated to God, fully understanding that sobriety teaches wisdom and virtue, which are nothing more useful in life to human beings.

Therefore, the little girl still kept the fast by forbidding herself to eat any of the fruits that had been given to her, which she would soon give away to others. She would stretch her fasting out for weeks, and in her childhood years had practiced remarkable abstinence. Later, as a nun, she imposed upon herself a rule of fasting that was plainly beyond nature: for she would spend entire Lents without bread, refueling herself each day with only five grains of a golden apple in memory of Christ's five wounds. For seven months, however, he observed the fast prescribed by his Order from the feast of the Exaltation of the Holy Cross until Easter, so that he would not eat anything until evening, and then would relieve his hunger and thirst with only a small morsel of cheap bread and a drink of cold water. Often, and especially on Fridays, he would torture his palate and bowels with goat's bile, which he kept drinkable for this purpose, so that he might taste the sponge that his Christ thirsted for on the cross. Sometimes, to make her mother think she was buying more expensive provisions, she would add the most bitter granadilla leaves—or other herbs of extreme bitterness from the woods—to the porridge, tossing in a few dried grapes for show. When by force of obedience she was compelled to eat meat, she was seen to be tormented by severe symptoms. She did not restore her first rest until she was allowed to return joyfully to her former abstinence. She had become so accustomed to fasting that for her very frequent infirmities no other medicine was of any use, only bread and water: for thus divine grace heals and makes whole.

[171] [prompt obedience,] In speaking of her victories, she was marvelous in obedience to the Rose, both in following the divine Spouse's ways, by which He drew her through the secret paths of holiness by the flowing scent of His ointments, and in fulfilling the precepts of her harsh and uncouth mother, which urged her from the opposite direction toward the vanities of the world and the vain adornments of the body: For in such a diversity of opposing commands, she excelled with a skill beyond human, neither straying to the right nor to the left. Hence she recognized in the flower-wreathed garland the true daughter of Jerusalem, and, commanded to place it on her head, she concealed the sharp point that pierces the brow—not only beholding but imitating the true King Solomon in the diadem with which his mother had crowned him. She, complying with the fragrant gloves in her hands, accepted the cloak; but having been truly burned by them, as she threw them aside she saw flames shooting forth and the entire chamber glowing. Severely tortured by the flogging her own mother inflicted to restore her health, she endured it in silence and patience, ready rather to lose her life than her obedience. For in all things, and through all things, depending on the guidance of confessors, parents, and elders, she, virtuous and a conqueror of herself even in the smallest matters, obtained the palm of obedience from all. And what is even more astonishing, even after her death, she showed obedience to Lucia, the prefect of the convent of Saint Catherine.

[172] [With singular reverence toward her parents,] Knowing that she could never be equal to God, her parents, and her teachers, she treated her parents with exceptional reverence, piety, and charity. Through their labor, they alleviated their need; prudent and provident in removing whatever might be disagreeable or unsettling to them, and wonderfully discreet in pacifying disputes and calming minds. She was seen to be ever ready and provident in attending to and ministering to their infirmities. To this end, he would extend his labors each night, like that strong woman who rose in the night and gave the prey to her household. For she was weak, languishing, and often ill, and detained by prayers and other exercises, she far surpassed the works of other artists in both speed and perfection. Nor should it be passed over that in this piety toward his parents he happily concluded the course of his life: for foreseeing that his mother would be overcome with the deepest grief at the approach of his death, Having earnestly implored her betrothed, she obtained for her such an abundant outpouring of sensible divine consolations that she afterward testified under oath that, upon experiencing the untimely death of her most beloved daughter, her heart had become too constricted and incapable of partaking in those heavenly joys.

[173] [With sincere self-contempt,] the Humble Virgin seemed to herself to be doing nothing, or suffering anything, in her service to the Spouse. She reproached herself for her supine cowardice, cried out that she was unworthy of the common air, and wondered that she had not yet been swallowed by an earth-gulf while alive. She sobbed, tear-filled, at the confessors' feet, exaggerating the slightest faults as if she were guilty of the most atrocious crimes all at once. He attributed the public calamities of the city or region to his own demerits, accepted the most frequent and most acute illnesses, and loved them as if they were the avengers and just punishments of his perpetual ingratitude toward God. He challenged all the elements of the world to avenge the wrongs he claimed they had done to their Creator. She firmly believed, and strove to have everyone believe, that she was the most abject and pestilent of all creatures, an execrable blemish on an unhappy age, a stain on the world, the final cancer of the human race. Hence, if she perceived that she was being praised even slightly for her virtues, the poor woman would be tormented, turn pale, be drenched in tears, and at once fall apart, plunging herself into the abyss of humility. It happened that, while I was casually eavesdropping from a nearby building, I overheard dignified people speaking of Rosa's remarkable life in absentia with honor and in secret. She gasped and trembled, the color drained from her face; she wasted away with anguish, weeping and sorrow, and, reproaching herself most harshly, struck her fist three or four times against the spiky crown of her head. They flowed beneath a veil of blood, so that the acrimony of pain might blunt the ear to the most unwelcome praise of another. The humble Rosa frankly confessed to her family that the common pyre of hell was not sufficient for her crimes; she deserved a separate hell of her own, one deeper than the old one and far more dreadful.

[174] She was led amid these exercises of virtue by the Bridegroom, both to the outward discipline of the body and to the inner solitude of the spirit, that there she might speak to his heart, while the importunate efforts of her mother strove on the contrary to prepare her for the marriages of the earth, to which she was urged not only by harsher words, but also by beatings. But the unconquerable spirit of Rosa prevailed: for at Cante, where she was staying with her own people, she left home at her mother's command only once in four years; then, following her teacher's example, she strove to lessen the beauty of her face by harsher fasting, to hide the agility of her body in humble and scorned clothing, and to avoid the gaze of men as much as she could. By these holy arts she eluded all hopes of marriage, yet she did not escape every conflict. For in choosing a state of life, there was much to struggle with for a long time.

Finally, at the age of twenty, with her mother's consent, she was publicly received into the daughter of St. Dominic, taking the habit of St. Catherine of Siena, by which the Sisters of the Third Order are commonly known as the Penitents. For they were striving with all their might to have the first virgins of the more austere monastery of St. Clare in Lima as fellow members of the foundation. The nuns of St. Augustine, the Doctor of the Church, were later awaiting her, already gone from home for this purpose, to be clothed in this sacred vestment; and not in vain, had she not been held motionless by divine power before the altar of the Most Blessed Virgin of the Most Holy Rosary for so long, until she retracted the journey she had undertaken. whereby, divinely admonished, she understood that the Order of Preachers was in the holy Church a paradise of divine delight, into which she herself, like a heavenly rose, was to be joined and inserted among the other there budding lilies and roses, which she afterward, with the deepest longing of her heart, desired and sought, and, her vow accomplished, Before the aforementioned altar of the most holy Rosary, most gracious to her, she received the habit of the same Order from Alphonso Velasquez, then confessor to the virgin, in the year of our Lord one thousand six hundred and six, during the celebrated feast of Saint Lawrence.
