[176] [after having first overcome certain external foes] But peace is not yet complete. For Gundisalvus, the treasurer, a most excellent man, who by virtue of his authority and domestic influence held great sway over the virgin Rose, to whom he had been entrusted, later advised that the reformed rule of the barefoot virgins of the holy Carmel be adopted in a cloistered convent, than to live outside the monastery walls as one of the last of Saint Dominic's tertiaries, he promised that he would be a help not only with protection, but also with labor and resources. The scrutiny of theologians in this matter remained inconclusive, until she herself, confirmed from heaven, most steadfastly demonstrated that the choice of the religious state is the work not of human, but of divine inspiration, and that she was to live until her last breath under the guidance of St. Catherine of Siena, and that a time would finally come when a monastery of this name and institution would arise there in Lima, a prediction which subsequent events themselves later confirmed.

[177] [Then, overcome by internal impediments, she embraced them.] Having overcome all these, a still more serious difficulty arose, the more serious because it was more intimate: for an importunate and troublesome thought kept intruding upon her humblest self-knowledge, that she herself, defiled by sins, was altogether unworthy of that snowy and sacred vestment. Nor did the struggle cease until, before the very altar of the Most Holy Rosary, she was rapt in the most peaceful ecstasy, pale and flushed and shining with splendors, and, upon regaining her senses, she broke out into a jubilant expression of gratitude for the victory she had won. It was such a monumental task to peacefully attain the habit and rule of her seraphic mistress, as she later expressed so precisely, that not only was she called by outsiders another Catherine of Siena, but only with the greatest anguish of the most humble Virgin's soul, but even to her confessor, in her features, gestures, and countenance, she appeared so transformed into that marvelous metamorphosis of divine love, that she seemed no longer like her, but the very same.

Notes.

The Indus and the Ganges are the most celebrated rivers of Eastern India, as is well known, and can be seen more distinctly on maps available to geographers.

The Equator is the largest circle of parallels, which divides the globe into two equal parts, leaving an equal distance on both sides toward the world's poles. When the sun reaches this point (which happens twice a year), the days throughout the entire world become equal to the nights, from which the equator gets its name. Nauta commonly call the equator the middle line, or, by antonomasia, the line, in which sense it seems to be taken here.

c At this point, Saint Gregory the Great is designated, the first Pope of that name, who explained the Gospels, book. 2 homily 36, or in the Paris edition of 1705, volume 1 of his Works, Col. It reads: "But who is designated by this servant, who is sent by the head of the household to invite, if not the order of preachers?" Let no one understand these things prophetically or literally of the Order of Preachers, or the Dominicans, to whom those words are here applied by way of figure, since this same holy Pope immediately adds the following: Of which order, indeed, although we are still considered unworthy, although we are burdened by the weight of our sins, we nevertheless live in these days, and when I speak to you about your edification, this is what I do: for I am a servant of the supreme Father of the family. As I admonish you to despise the world, I come to invite you to the supper of God. From this, it is clear that in this text of St. Gregory, he is speaking generally of preachers of the Gospel or sermonizers.

Honorius of Philoponus of the Order of St. Benedict, as I noted above at chapter nineteen of the Vita, contended with many arguments against other religious Orders, a certain Benedictine surnamed Buil, a Catalan by nation and formerly abbot of Montserrat, first with twelve of the Order; He and his companions crossed over to America, and there preached the Gospel to the barbarians. This writer, however, for his opinion, cites Thomas Bosius on the marks of the true Church, and Gilbert Genebrard in his Chronology, and other authors, whom the curious reader can consult in the original source cited on page 25 and following. Perhaps these competitors for the various parts of America could reconcile with one another. But it is not our place to settle or decide petty disputes.

In the annotations to the first chapter of the Vita, we noted that the Italians call Pentecost the Feast of the Roses. But there we also wondered whether the same phrase is in use among the Spaniards. Hence at least one of our opinions is confirmed, and another doubt remains in the same state.

Section II. The wondrous espousal of this virgin to Christ, her more severe corporal afflictions, and her tender charity toward God.

[The Saint, after the admirable vision] that divine Oracle: "I will betroth you to myself in faithfulness" is also recognized to have been fulfilled in this beloved, chosen by God with an exceptional privilege, to which He was pleased to elevate her through marvelous graces. It was unique that the Handsome one, in appearance like the sons of men, appeared in dreams in the guise of a most skilled marble-carver, cheerful and loving, to seek there a virgin bride. When Rosa consented to such a marriage contract, he imposed upon her the arduous task of chiseling marble and set a short deadline for completing the work before his return from abroad. The virgin blushed when her groom returned to find the work still unfinished, a task beyond her strength; to her distress, he opened a vast stonecutter's workshop, where countless chosen virgins were laboring manfully to cut and polish marbles. She perceived that the brides were clad in nuptial garments and gleaming with radiance, their tears softening the stones for carving, and she knew that, together with them, wrapped in similar attire, she was being prepared for the arduous rite of such a marriage.

Therefore, with this and that virginal purity shining forth, [to Christ concerning the betrothed] that she had never been stained even by a venial spot of impurity, all her witnesses sworn by the confessors have attested. The mystery was approached more openly. It was Palm Sunday when, in the very chapel of the Rosary, immersed in the abyss of her own nothingness, He thus addressed her: "Rose of my heart, be thou my bride." The virgin trembled at the sweet words of so divine a Spouse, and at the same time she heard the Mother of God congratulating her and saying, "Behold, O Rose, what honor my Son here bestows upon you." The inestimable treasures of divine love and the greatest nuptial gifts of so great a Spouse were poured out upon the Rose so lavishly that, in order to hide them thereafter, she continually devoted herself to ever humbler exercises, and by thinking the most contemptible thoughts of herself, she came to know that she must acquire self-contempt in all things, and in fact she accomplished it by her deeds.

[180] [cruelly her own body] Enriched with these gifts, and endowed with charisms, the fierce maiden girded herself more fervently, tightening still more tightly her loins with strength, and strengthened her arm. For when tyrants and torturers failed to force her to renounce her faith, she, stronger on her own, carried out the duties of both upon herself. For she exchanged the stones and crosses, with which the little girl, ignorant of the use of the whip, was burdened by the handmaid Mary—almost the sole witness of her own mortifications—whenever she went to prayer or to the garden, for iron chains, which she fitted into scourges, with which, following the example of Saint Dominic, she scourged herself each night until the blood flowed copiously, either for the sufferings of the holy Church, or for the sufferings of the holy Church in peril, or for the peril of the kingdom, or for the needs of the city of Lima, or for making amends for the injuries of sinners, or for the expiation of the souls of the departed, or for obtaining divine assistance for those in their last agony, she offered herself to God as a bloody victim to avert His just wrath, sometimes horrifying her household with the dire blows of the chains, whose use was forbidden, she secretly girded her loins for a long time with one of them, twisted three times, so that she could never appear except under the force of the greatest sciatica pains, which was later loosed only by a miracle; whose rings, after the virgin's death, were found to give off a marvelous and strange fragrance.

[181] [and tormented each of its members,] So that no part of the innocent little body should be free from punishment, he twisted the arms and sinews with penal bonds, and pressed the breast, armpits, and sides with handfuls of nettles and tiny thorns, so that this Rose, placed like a lily among thorns, might be completely conformed to it. She later increased the harshness of the cilice, which she wore from her neck down to her knees, by mixing it with spikes, and she used it for several years until, because of frequent vomiting of blood, she was ordered to take it off. She compensated for the loss of this punishment by another garment, which, though less harmful to her health, was no less troublesome: for under it any movement was torture. Only the soles of his feet were free from these pains, yet he would not allow them to be immune from suffering, either through the collision of rocks or the scorching of a furnace. And what is almost unique in the abundance of divine consolations—by which holiness is wont to abound even on earth, and the flesh of the pious rejoices and exults in the living God—he forced his body to be alien to them and to remain fasting. Further mindful that it is not fitting for a delicate member to be found under a spiky head, it, pierced by the gaze of the pious image, silently proclaims: Behold the man, he fixed the first crown to his head with exceedingly sharp little nails, and for several years wore it not without wounds. In his later years a second one succeeded it, armed with ninety-nine spikes, which he made ever more painful in various ways each day, keeping it hidden from his family by his skill, and he did not lay it aside until he left this life.

For it did not seem to her that she had fully assumed the habit of the seraphic mistress unless she had also taken up her crown of thorns. By the triple act of this diadem, she had become accustomed to instantly banish the demons' suggestions in reverence of the most holy Trinity, sufficiently instructed not to violate such thorns but to hedge about these roses. He wanted its hardness to be such that it would drive sleep away rather than bring it on, so that for the sleeping woman the bed would be the same as a horseblock, a pillow or an unhewn log, or stones hidden for this purpose, for she filled the bed with pre-sharpened fragments of tiles and broken, three-cornered pots so that the pointed end of each piece was turned toward the body. Nor would she lie down to sleep until she had made her throat bitter with a draught of gall. Thus the bed of this Bride was so flourishing, and so pleasing to the divine Spouse, that He would sometimes, on account of this, visibly console her, who appeared almost lifeless, by setting before her His own harsher and more narrow bed of Calvary. With these methods he confined his sleep to the narrow limits of two hours, and sometimes even granted the importunate exactor less time: for he distributed his day and night so that he would set aside twelve full hours from each for prayer: the remaining ten he devoted to manual labor, by which he supported his parents. He devoted the remaining two hours to brief rest and sleep. Against the snares of slumber, he had driven a nail into the wall, on which he most tightly fastened the hair he had reserved for weaving a crown, and by this torment, driving away drowsiness, he had grown accustomed to lengthen his vigils for prayer.

[183] Emulating the retreats of the holy anchorites, who in the solitude of the desert received Christ the Lord, [and a lover of solitude,] she, still an infant, sought lonely corners at home, shunning the frivolities of girls, so that she might hide alone with her God. She built a small oratory with an altar in the shade of the trees' branches, and from the table and from her little bed she would hurry there as if to a sanctuary for prayer. The proverb was: "Go to the garden if you seek a rose." At home, she spent the night alone in her room, praying. He avoided leaving the house by inflicting punishments on himself—either a stone in his shoe or the most biting Indian pepper in his eyes—judging it more tolerable to be blinded than to behold the vanities of the world. For a more secluded retreat, not without great struggle, and a prior miraculous apparition of the Virgin Mother and the child Jesus in the chapel of the most holy Rosary, he finally obtained from his mother a cell in the far corner of the garden, five feet long and four feet wide, where he could devote himself not only to manual labor, but especially to the collection of his spirit and the study of contemplation, rejoicing that there was just enough space there for herself and her heavenly Spouse. In this happy wilderness she arranged her ascensions in her heart, and, lifted up above herself, she seemed rather outside the body than to dwell in it.

In the likeness of a star, the proven perfection of the matron appeared in a vision. [and indulging pious affections,] For the city of kings, as Lima is called, its own star was due, which would lead to Christ the Lord, King of kings. It was divinely ordained that, while residing there, he should hear several distant Masses and see them as if he had been physically present. Countless mosquitoes entered this hermit's cell, yet not one of them bit or even touched him. Even religious persons who approached would be hostilely attacked. He so encouraged these little creatures to sing the praises of God in their own way and guided them so wondrously that Rosa was believed to have nearly attained the realm reserved for innocence and to be dwelling in that solitude as if in paradise. In the seclusion of this little hut, when she was alone or believed herself unseen by anyone, she would burst forth in a more tender and free prose, verse, conversation, and song into every solemn act of seraphic love and praise with such fervor and impetus that she moved the hearts of her listeners to charity and contrition, though they had been unaware. He had gathered from the very fiery quiver of divine love short prayers, which pleased readers and benefited many in their use. She thus addressed the true God and Spouse of her soul, the joy of her heart, and the most benevolent Jesus, protesting that she was consumed with that perfect love with which all the citizens of heaven simultaneously love him.

[185] [offered spiritual gifts to Christ being born.] When her beloved was to be born in Bethlehem, as she contemplated him, a little child and naked with a poor mother, she repeatedly prepared worthy garments for the infant Love, with a singular and precious stitching of pious works: for undergarments, swaddling cloths, a cloth for the time when he lay wrapped, bandages, fringes, and borders, and in place of cradle-beds, she had substituted rosaries, stations to the most holy Eucharist, fasts, crowns for the Lord, Our Father prayers, Hail Marys, recitations of the Apostles' Creed, and many other prayers, scourges, tears, Masses offered, and Communions of the most holy Eucharist, all of which she repeated most frequently. And by these exercises of holy love she had consecrated her soul and heart to Him, so that she was entirely His, like a most beloved bride, and reserved nothing for herself that her Bridegroom did not wholly possess. While working on the wedding ring that he had commissioned to be made as a memento of the great Spouse, with an image of the infant Jesus on it, his brother—though completely ignorant of so great a mystery—by divine inspiration for its motto came upon those same betrothal words of the King of kings: "Rose of my heart, be thou my bride." This ring received its supernatural power from Christ the Lord himself, so that a man of proven virtue, upon touching it, experienced extraordinary infusions of divine love, sublime visions, and unusual ardors of the Holy Spirit, to the point of his soul's ecstasy while his body remained completely motionless. nor could he rise free until the ring was removed, who was so sensibly held in captivity by the joy of so holy a nuptial. For just as much as those divine nuptials availed in the soul of the Bride herself, so too did they avail in the soul of another.

[186] [In contemplation] She therefore, the most loving of the Lord, lived confidently and, as it were, dwelt in his chamber all day long. For she had been taught in her very infancy by him to pray fervently within; even when sleeping she was found to utter daily prayers with her mouth, and her heart was watchful in prayer. In the twelfth year of her life she was raised to that sublime degree of contemplation which mystical theology calls the unitive. While engaged in manual labor, she fixed the keenness of her intellect immovably on God, so that neither in wakefulness nor in sleep did the presence of the heavenly Spouse slip from the eye of her mind, a presence that so sweetly occupied the inner powers of her soul that, speaking within with God Himself, she at the same time also expeditiously attended to all other necessary matters without.

[187] [She stood rooted to the spot for many hours,] For hours she remained motionless before the altars; she neither saw those who passed by nor turned her attention to sudden noises. From the morning of the fifth day of the fast until the Sabbath—and sometimes even until the Lord's Day—she remained thus with her knees bent, so that she could not rise from the floor, and when she was earnestly ordered to, she opened them. He learned that the divine attributes, distributed into one hundred and fifty and recited in decades after the example of the most holy Rosary, had been a terror to demons. His familiar conversations contained as much force as speech could. Woken at three o'clock before breakfast by a singing little bird to the contemplation of God, she was rapt in ecstasy and did not come out of it until evening. He sought to draw his nearest and dearest into the practice of prayer, and especially mental exercise. He urged the confessors to bring penitents to her by every means, and he most frequently exhorted the preachers of the divine word to inflame their hearers with zeal for her, as for a great panacea against all sins, and to popularize the practice of pious meditations. He particularly loved the recitation of the Virgin's rosary with the attached decade-by-decade meditation on the mysteries, as Saint Dominic had instituted, because in it the affections of mental and vocal prayer, petitions, praises, and thanksgivings come together; and it was found in practice that these incentives of the Rosary had benefited many.

[188] Near her death during Lent, with a wonderfully melodious little bird, [and performing certain prodigies,] which would fly to her cell at sunset, Rosa the Canticle and the praises of God were alternated daily for a full hour with such order that, when the bird sang, the Virgin would be silent, and, when the Virgin sang, the bird would be most attentive* nor would it chirp, but even invited the very insensitive plants by a wholly unique example to the praises and prayer of God, by uttering that verse, Bless the Lord, all you that germinate upon the earth, and visibly inclined them so that the tops of the trees touched the ground, as if in a solemn rite of veneration, adoring their Creator. It is so true that he who clings to the Lord is one spirit with him, and that all things also obey him.

[189] To prove whether her spirit was from God, [her spirit or method of living] was subjected to a subtle examination by men, it was repeatedly subjected to examination by men, both learned and pious, and distinguished by spiritual discernment, from which it became clear that its lights and gifts were divine, and that from the very dawn of reason nothing had been sweeter to it than to think of God and to long for His heavenly good pleasure; from the age of twelve, its soul, drawn by the Lord with His spiritual powers, most swiftly clung to the divine beauty, so that it could never be separated from the charity of Christ by any created thing. to have felt, from infancy, a great gift of God: a ready inclination toward virtue, without the turmoil of passions; that from the very first knowledge of God, it was filled with the fear of God, and the horror of sin; that nothing created could be recreated; that his entire comfort was to feel God present in his soul; It would seem to him more bitter than any loss and hell itself to lose him from sight, even for a moment.

[190] Then, out of obedience, he revealed the contest that God had perhaps given him, [he is examined by learned men,] so that he might win and know that wisdom is mightier than all: For he recounted the horrifying visions, terrors, and spirit desertions that he had endured with the utmost constancy for fifteen years, which seemed like the penal postponements of souls in purgatory, and indeed a type of hell itself, since no hope of escape shone for him. Then it became clear, amid these abandonments, that she had suddenly found herself restored in the very midday of her former union, as if in the arms of the Bridegroom; she had perceived an inexplicable, solid, and foundational gift by which she seemed to have been made impeccable, like him who said, "Who will separate us from the love of Christ?" He also explained that, for the most part, even after those dark periods the humanity of Christ the Lord, as well as the most holy Virgin Theotokos, appeared to him, along with intellectual visions in which he experienced the presence of God within himself, through the categories of removal, eminence, and causality, and other circumstances. It was wonderfully evident that she had not received this from books, but had attained it through the Holy Spirit teaching her inwardly. He also explained the exercises of mortification and penance, and in these, not in his own, but established the measure of them by the confessors' judgment, and explained so clearly his own lack of confidence in his own strength, the ardor of his faith, the security of his hope, and the fires of divine love, that the censors, with one mind, concluded by vote and decree that the path on which this chosen Virgin of God walked was sincere and safe.

[191] It was the constant opinion of all who at that time were celebrated in Lima for perfection and holiness that Rosa, as a daughter of God, acted and was acted upon by the Spirit of God, and that they judged her to abound in the gift of wisdom and to be governed by knowledge poured out from heaven: for she explained the most difficult questions concerning the secret of the most holy Trinity, the hypostatic union of the divine Word, the most holy sacrament of the Altar, the profound abyss of predestination, the nature of grace, and to other theological questions proposed by professors of the sacred Scriptures, she explained them with such sublime concepts, profound sentences, and words so proper, that all praised the Almighty Father, the Lord of heaven and earth, with our Savior, because she revealed to the humblest little ones and the uneducated so many things hidden from the wise and prudent.

Notes.

At the prophet Hosea, chapter 2, verse. 20 These words are read, which are commonly explained by the interpreters of the sacred Scriptures concerning the Catholic Church, the bride of Christ, and here are applied to St. Rose, virgin of Lima, on account of the spiritual likeness of that same betrothal.

In the Acts of St. Dominic, on August 4, we recorded that this Saint was accustomed to chastise his body every night.

Lima is commonly called by the Spanish Ciudad de los Reyes, that is, City of the Kings. Joachim Bruliau the Augustinian, in the History of his Order in Peru, book. Chapter 6 1. Concerning the origin of the city of Lima and the deeds of Pizarro the leader, he explains the reason for this name in these words: He called the city of the kings in honor of Charles V, emperor, and his mother Joanna, who at that time were jointly governing Castile; from them, at Pizarro's request, the insignia for the new city were granted: three crowns and the star of the Magi kings. a double eagle and two columns with the inscription PLUS ULTRA, with the two letters I & K added, by which Joanna the mother and Charles the emperor would be signified, and all together would indicate that just as the Magi were the first fruits of faith and of the nascent Church, so Joanna and Charles would give the beginnings to the Peruvian church, taking up its patronage and defense. Then he refutes there the opinion of those who assign another cause for this appellation.

Although this Pope attributes the origin of the Rosary to Saint Dominic, some pious and learned Catholic writers think that this institution is either older or more recent, as in the Preface to the Acts of St. Dominic" § 19 & two following, we have related: for the Roman Pontiffs assert or deny such purely historical opinions with the same faith with which they received them from others.

Section III. Her familiarity with Christ, the Virgin Mary, angels, St. Catherine of Siena; battles against demons, steadfastness in adversity, and a burning love for God.

The conversation of this Virgin was more in heaven than on earth. [Often familiarly to this Virgin] and wherever she came upon the most holy name of Jesus in reading, she would linger for a while: for from it she felt the fires of love in her heart. and Jesus himself often appeared to the loving Rose on the plain of the page, and was sweetly wooed by her, so that the word, in attentive reading, was the most worthy for such a soul, in which are all the treasures of the wisdom and knowledge of God: Likewise, when she was occupied with sewing cloth, He would often sit most quietly on her pillow, and with silent nods speak to the heart of this Beloved, and with every gesture proclaim His divine love. These delights were frequent and almost daily for this holy soul, and sometimes, when the Bridegroom delayed, she would lament in querulous voices until he came. Playing with the one whose throat is the sweetest, the victor obtained relief from throat pain. But when she was lost, she was once again endowed with patience amid her pains, and as she obediently recounted them to her mother, she seemed to radiate an angelic countenance.

[193] To her, already languishing, failing in strength, and destitute of all human aid, the same [Christ] came—He who is a helper in times of need and tribulation—and with the precious ointment from the wounded side of the Myrrhbearer, kindly applied to draw the water of salvation from the fountains of the Savior, He immediately healed the sick woman. She was seen with the innocent virgins standing with Jesus himself, sometimes even walking about, and exchanging the most secret conversations with such majesty that, wherever he set foot, the floor would shine with a brilliant light. that such a rose should adhere to no other plant than to him who is the flower of the field, and the lily of the valleysb, he found basil, or ozyma, which he more diligently cultivated, reserved as a royal plant for the King of ages himself, dry and uprooted, to whom Christ himself, appearing to this grieving one, said that he himself had uprooted and cast it away with his own hands, so that he alone might be its true basilic. By this she showed to be true what she had said in a certain ecstasy, namely, that she carries the Rose in the innermost sanctuary of her divine heart, since she knew that, in turn, she herself dwells most tranquilly in her virgin heart.

[194] The Queen of Heaven also often stood at her right hand, [The Mother of God] so that from the age of eleven until her final breath she never lacked this precious companionship of familiarity: for in the little chapel of the Rosary, through diligent ministry, she had mutual conversation with her, and with this honor she dignified her handmaid, that she might perform for her the duties of a chambermaid by awakening her with those words: "Arise to pray, Daughter, arise, for the hour is at hand": and upon waking, she would present herself to be seen face to face, adorned with majesty and a paradise of heavenly glory, causing the other Elizabeth to exclaim: "How is it that the mother of my Lord comes to me?"

[195] [Angels] The Lord also sent his angel, who would precede Rosa, visibly guard her, and lead her into the place he had prepared for her: for this Virgin had such a domestic confidence with him that she had him not only as a most dear friend, but also as a servant or messenger, whenever at the usual hour the Bridegroom did not appear. One night, growing weak, he sent him to Mary of Usategui to tell her he needed a remedy, which she immediately sent by way of a servant, as the Mother saw and recognized this being done by her daughter through obedience. She was also led unharmed from the garden cell, with the gates' very locks opening of their own accord, to her mother's chamber. God also commanded other angels concerning her: they stood by a certain religious man on a perilous journey because of her prayers, until he was changed for the worse. To him, who was complaining to the virgin herself about this desertion, she revealed a mystery and singular secrets known only to herself and to God—secrets that, from such a distance, could only have been known to the Lord himself or to an angel revealing them to the Virgin.

[196] [& St. Catherine of Siena appeared.] Often also, since the Lord Christ had given her St. Catherine of Siena as a teacher, she would visibly meet her on earth, especially when she was examining her rule; And so frequent and friendly was the conversation between these virgins that the eyes of the blessed Catherine would pass from her own face to Rose's, so that both in life and in death she would completely resemble her by the very features of her mouth, besides the countless graces, favors, services, and virtues with which she had been adorned and enriched by her.

[197] [Even though God tested her] The heavenly Father proved this His Electa among these delights of the spiritual life, like gold in a furnace, by the fire of temptations, afflictions, persecutions, and many grave infirmities: for she often wrestled with demons and was vexed by many of their torments, among which, under the hideous form of a molossus, one of them tried to terrify her while she was praying with a horrid bark, and to tear her apart by dragging her along the ground with a rabid bite, until she cried out to her Spouse; Do not hand over the souls that confess to you to the beasts. He struck her with a slap at other times, and sometimes hurled a rough stone at her, which knocked Rosa to the ground, neither wounding nor frightening her, and tore her spiritual books. Under a gigantic mass, he seized this otherwise impregnable woman, her cell trembling, and twisted her so violently that her bones seemed crushed and her nerves torn apart.

[198] [to be vexed by demons,] But the invincible spirit of the Virgin repressed and mocked the madness of this raging foe, who had contended in vain for many hours, until, despairing of victory, he withdrew. Proceeding to the garden to pray, she sweetly encountered this antagonist, transformed into an angel of light, who, when the contest was over chastity, begged victory with a noble flight, and seizing an iron chain, with the most bitter blows she lacerated her naked shoulders, and while she was lamenting the absence of her Spouse amid streams of blood and tears, he conspicuous one appeared, who, unless he himself had been with her, he would never have won the victory. From the earliest years of his childhood he had obtained the discernment of spirits, and he recognized, taught by his holy mistress who had once drawn from Christ the Lord, that the illuminations are from God, when in the soul humility, a reverent affection, the knowledge of one's own usefulness, order, and unity of heart, and joy in God follow. On the contrary, to oppose these, one smells impressions, fear, the confusion of a divided heart, self-complacency, and restlessness.

She had been in hardship since infancy: for in the ninth month of her life she was deprived of milk, and the various adversities that followed could only be compensated by a very unsuitable diet. He had barely begun to speak when, because of the dispute over his name, he was beaten with rods by his grandmother if he answered "Rosa," and by his mother if he answered "Elizabeth." The virgin's virtues and mortifications, which her mother regarded as mere faults, seemed to her to deserve punishment, and she inflicted not only the harshest words but often also severe beatings, especially when she discovered that her daughter had cut off her red hair. Nor were her own household any more lenient toward her when her virtues and heavenly gifts became known, seized by a vain fear that, to the great disgrace of the family, she would be dragged before the tribunal of the Holy Inquisition as suspected of imposture. Adding to the pile of tribulation for certain confessors was the unbelief or sloth of some, their rudeness or ignorance, as they tried to persuade her that this path of virtue was neither right nor safe, that it was afflicted by dizziness or some other harmful disorder, and that the illusions she herself believed to be divine illuminations were in fact delusions. When the woman urged her, imploring that she be delivered from these torments by her seraphic mistress, she steadfastly refused: for she was ashamed for the Spouse of the Crucified to be found even for a moment outside the crosses, from which she was never absent. For the holiday, the interior was occupied by a more serious affliction than the exterior, and as the external persecution ceased, hordes of illnesses took its place.

In Canta, she lay in bed for three years, suffering intense torment from a dreadful nerve traction, [and would allow herself to be afflicted by diseases,] without a single groan. She was often assailed by the most acute angina, asthma, extremely painful sciatica, pleurisy, pains in the stomach, pericardium, and intestines, and by countless, unceasing convulsions, the doctors being for the most part amazed how a body so dry could have the strength to endure what would have been enough to prostrate even the strongest man. She perceived that these agonizing torments did not arise from disorder, but from the benevolent hand of her Spouse, who, by penetrating her most perfectly, was bringing her these festive torments. She had by now almost turned the occasional fevers, and even the more stubborn ones, into a joke through sheer habit. She was frequently afflicted by diseases of the joints, gout, and arthritis, which she endured with unwavering constancy. He counted all these among the more illustrious favors bestowed upon him by God. For in a sublime and singular elevation of mind he had seen Christ the Lord dividing, by a lance made from the cross, graces and gifts in proportion to one's sufferings; and he heard him say that without the weight of afflictions one cannot reach the storehouses of grace, that the cross is the one true ladder to paradise, and that there is no other by which one can ascend to heaven.

Languishing and burning with the fires of divine love, she was seen in prayer at night by another virgin, who was radiant with great lights; yet she herself kindled in her, as if sparks were shot from her mouth and eyes, proclaiming how great a fire was burning within, which she noted had on other occasions, without her knowledge, appeared before various people. He diligently concealed his other virtues, but he could not hide the great flame of charity: for his conversations, greetings, and responses were continually seasoned with the memory of divine love. He gradually compelled the orders of each creature to love the most sweetest Creator, both with fiery voices and with the strumming of a harp he had not learned, so that with every rite, gesture, and act he might show himself loving to the Beloved. Acting with the same force of charity, as a true bride, she zealously defended the Lord's honor: for she detested the irreverence of the churches, and would gently and kindly rebuke those who spoke there idly. The household servants were careful not to utter foolishness in her presence. For they feared that the censor's brow was still overshadowed by the little rose, and he was filled with immense joy whenever he saw the honor of God promoted by some notable success, as happened in the remarkable conversion of the sinner. With the same zeal, he transferred to himself his confessor's fever, so that he might perform the duties of a preacher at the very celebrated solemnity. As he recovered, a fever that was not his own was tormenting Rosa, yet she did not wish to be absent from the conversation: for she easily overcame the feverish ardor, kindled by a desire for divine glory.

[202] [and others, the fire of divine love,] Christ the Lord himself sealed this concern for God's love for His Beloved with this remarkable miracle. In the year of our Lord 1617, on the seventeenth day before May, Rosa, more vehemently stirred by the divine impulses of love, was loudly and fervently addressing the venerable image of the Savior in the questor's oratory when, at once, droplets of sweat covered the entire face of the image. The summoned witnesses, renowned for their art and learning, after careful consideration, concluded that what was depicted in the icon was supernatural. The pious spectacle lasted for four hours, with all those present stunned with admiration and holy awe. whose traces of sweat remained visible for several centuries. This was merely a sign of how earnestly our Savior desires to be loved by all, as the virgin Rose herself, conscious of the divine secret, testified, for fear that it might be a portent of impending divine vengeance. That sweat, applied to Rose's own arm by the confessor's command—an arm that had previously been so severely injured when struck against the ground that, in the surgeons' judgment, she would have remained maimed on that side—made it sound and supple, to the astonishment of the surgeon, who the day before had deemed all treatment futile, since the arm had appeared utterly unmanageable.

[203] [in which he was especially ardent,] He had not yet passed his boyhood when his confessors, recognizing him as already great in spirit, granted him the frequent permission to eat that Bread of angels, which provides delights for kings. There was in her spirit a clear purity, a shining innocence, and a strong fervor of devotion; yet with such immaculate brightness of conscience, whenever she was to approach the divine banquet, she would each time, in deep anguish and bathed in tears, precede it with sacramental confession. The priests who were to communicate saw the entire flame, and the face of the one himself shining with a heavenly and vehement splendor: indeed, to the priest about to administer the sacred Communion, the furnace seemed to open up blazing as he removed the pallium to receive the divine Host. The terrified priest felt the force of the suddenly bursting flame so keenly that he often drew back his hand from the sudden fear of being burned. The same thing was experienced in the minister offering the sacrifice, as he, according to custom, handed him the chalice of the washing, so that this too might be proven by testimony to be that Sacrament by which God Himself came to cast fire on the earth.

[204] [as she received the Holy Eucharist,] She opened herself to receive from the virgin flesh of God into her soul the wondrous meekness of the heavenly Lamb, to have her spirit quickened with unusual vigor, her strength restored and renewed through a kind of new transformation of her spirit into this divine nourishment: Meanwhile, she perceived in her heart a placid serenity of a higher sphere, peace, temperance, an immensity of joy from the close union with the divine Spouse, the depth of fruition, and a spiritual taste of true sweetness, which were in no way explicable by any comparison. Having been refilled with Holy Communion, she fasted until evening, sometimes until the following day, and on occasion for a full week, content with that alone and abstaining from all other food. His veneration for the venerable mystery was so extraordinary that, once he had girded himself in the sight of others to undergo the martyrdom which rumor had spread was imminent, the martyrdom lacked a victim, not the victim a martyrdom.

[205] She also treated the sign of the holy Cross with the highest veneration, [or offered prayers,] wherever she found it, so that she would form its impression from the garden plants, one of which, given as a gift to the queen-regent, withered shortly afterward; but when it was returned to Rose after four days, it revived, which the Virgin attributed entirely to the virtue of that same holy Cross. He burned with a marvelous devotion to the images of the Blessed Virgin, and especially toward that one which depicts the most august Queen of the Rosary; in whose oratory, while receiving the habit of his Order, he was seen, as a weeping mother, to be lifted up to heaven by the gentle favor of the Mother of God. It was rumored in Lima that Rosa could obtain whatever she asked of that holy image. She obtained, by praying for two days, the peace of a certain community, in which discord had prevailed, not without tears and to the admiration of others, and was there honored with many other favors, revelations, and visions, which she revealed by submitting to an examination.

[206] Chosen as the most fitting and worthy to adorn the statue of Saint Catherine of Siena—whom God had honored more than He had honored her as a mother from His inmost heart—she never ceased in that devotion from tears, kisses, and prayers, and in a marvelous way received in coin the very amount of money she had herself asked for a little earlier for her vestment. He foretold that the predestined virgin, then swollen with no sign of fertility, would in honor of the most holy Trinity produce three carnations, with which she would adorn the sacred image at a time when that kingdom was devoid of such flowers, which had been sought in vain for a long time. And the event corresponds to the oracle, to the amazement of the companions and those seeking forgiveness for their distrust.

[207] Euphemia de Pareja had assisted her in this task, [she showed it by various miracles.] whose servant Francisca, the nurse of her own infant, was gravely ill. While Rosa was praying at Saint Catherine's, once the danger had passed she recovered so well that the next day the doctor permitted her to give the infant the nourishments she had been forbidden. Francisca also obtained from the same Saint that a sulfurous firecracker missile, which sought her face and struck her on the eyebrow, would do her no harm. Let her still be resilient; with a strong blow, another woman's clothes have been torn and set on fire. That same Rosa, in the year of the Lord 1616 in the month of August, to whom the image had been brought for veneration after the procession, was suffering from a dreadful arthritis in her right arm, which had invaded all her joints with excruciating pain, so that she was rendered entirely useless for the aforementioned task. He prostrated himself before the image of Saint Catherine on the day of the blessed. Lawrence, and, after a brief speech, set himself to the task, to the amazement of those present, with the quailer himself watching, his hand, which had been grievously wounded only a short time before, now strong and agile, the physician himself bearing witness to the miracle. To this blessing was added the fact that from that time on arthritis never again afflicted that hand; which was no surprise to Rosa herself, who from it, as from a mother, drew almost daily comfort from their mutual companionship.

Section IV. Care for the well-being of one's neighbor, mercy toward the poor, hope in divine help proven by miracles, and the spirit of prophecy.

[This Virgin, regarding the conversion of the Indian Gentiles,] was also zealous for the house of God and the salvation of souls: for she loved with the same charity the true body of Christ the Lord and the mystical one as well. Whenever he turned his eyes to the interior mountain regions of southern America, he was tormented by grief, weeping over the fate of so many barbarians who populated those inaccessible valleys beyond the snowy ridges. Thus, the constant complaints of the Jews were also a matter against any other nations of unbelievers. He urged religious men, and especially the brothers of his own order whom he knew to be suitable, to convert the Gentiles, and he implored them, by the bowels of Jesus Christ, to devote themselves to this work. She sought to inflame the spirit of one of her confessors, who had been appointed to a mission, to hasten his work, and she agreed with him that half of all the fruits which, in the meantime, she herself hoped to gather from the full practice of good works would be credited to him. And in turn, he would transcribe for the Virgin herself the fruits of the conversion of souls that he was to lead onto the path of salvation through his preaching. He devised this with that intent, to take a poor little boy as a son, to imbue him with the rudiments of the Christian life, and by pious almsgiving to advance him to virtue and a full instruction in the sacred Scriptures, hoping that, when he had become suitable and ordained to the priesthood, he would hasten to the apostolic missions for the conversion of the Gentiles, so that what she could not accomplish herself she might supply through another. which, had it not been prevented by an untimely death, it would have accomplished. For the Christian souls in peril, who by a fatal fault had wretchedly fallen from the state of divine friendship, he spared no expense of tears and daily inflicted bloody wounds on himself, so that these truly miserable souls might be granted by God a serious amendment of their lives. He vehemently urged the preachers themselves to summon the most depraved souls from the very depths of the whirlpool of vice, so that they might not perish, to the safe shore of repentance with all their might.

[209] [Repentance of a Wicked Christian] Divinely instructed about the wicked desire with which a young man named Vincent, of noble birth, had approached her under false pretenses, she rebuked him with such charity that he confessed he recognized Christ the Lord speaking in her, and was completely changed from the man he had been a little while before, Confident and aided by the prayers of the same Virgin, he bid farewell to his youthful desires and faults, and established a holy way of life. It was a miracle if anyone, given over to any vice, approached Rosa and did not come away better. He instructed Mary of Mesta, impatient and given to sudden outbursts of anger—a malady that her long habit had made intolerable to herself—with aphorisms on patience and meekness so that from that day she was seen to be more improved and gentler; and in that brief gymnasium of patience she progressed so much that she now even asked God to send her more adversity. He helped a religious man, who was struggling with the pangs of death in a fearful and dangerous manner, tormented by burning scruples, with the promise of his prayers and an exhortation, and by the pledge of the participation of his satisfactions, so that immediately the fear gave way to joyful hope, who, later, fortified with the salutary sacraments, returned a peaceful and serene spirit to the Creator, of whose soul Rose later announced that she was being treated felicitously and kindly, and was already enrolled in the region of light, enjoying the perpetual refreshment of the Saints' felicity.

[210] The Lord also ordained in her a charity in the corporal works of mercy, [and she was so solicitous for the necessities of the poor,] by which she strove to help her neighbors: For this poor Virgin deprived herself of what was necessary and distributed it to the poor: for, in order to relieve a certain hidden need, she withdrew from herself a supply of food for eight days, living on nothing but bread and water as aid for the needy. Telam, a gift granted by his parents for his own needs, immediately divided it among two virgins, noble in virtue and birth, yet extremely poor. He gave to the very poor virgin, Montoya, one of the cloaks that her mother possessed, predicting that she would be provided with others, new in place of the old. which, wonderfully, came to pass in fact, just as the Virgin had foretold. Divinely admonished, she took into her home Joanna de Bobadilla, a noble but abandoned virgin, whom a voracious cancer under her breast was tormenting, and who, with the remedy delayed, was threatened with destruction; she provided for her monthly room rent and the cost of her medicines, and after several months restored her completely healthy, save for one complaint: that she could not, because of a vow she had made, To publicly acknowledge the virgin's kindness as a token of gratitude while she was still living. Nothing could have been more pleasing to him than if his mother would permit him to bring needy women of any condition, or those afflicted by illness, into his father's house, so that he could there constantly minister to the sick with his own hands. He strove to provide them with everything necessary for sustenance and medicine. Among these beggars there was none so intolerable to look at, or so foul-smelling, that Rosa could scorn her or turn away in disgust. He brought healing, service, and care to all.

[211] To the mother, indignant at the foul stench of moldy decay, [so that she might try to help everyone], she replied from among the clothes she had taken from the sick woman's care: "We are the sweet-smelling fragrance of Christ as we minister to the sick." Charity is not delicate, nor does it despise its neighbor. With an unconquerable spirit, scarcely imitating the example of her holy mistress, she overcame a stomachache in the house of Isabella of Mexico with a heroic act. John of Tineo was aware that Gundisalvus Rosa, the treasurer, had been freed several times from the gravest illnesses by the prayers and merits of this Virgin; he, suffering dangerously from stomach torments, obtained that she, properly adorned after the sacred service, would visit him. Seeing an angelic majesty in his face, he conceived an unwavering hope of safety, and at once all pain vanished: sleep, which had entirely fled, gently seized the infirm. She left as quickly as possible to avoid the applause; he, however, woke up unharmed. Rosa's gentle sweetness extended even to the brute animals, and her tender compassion both showed itself and offered help.

[212] [Moreover, she had conceived such confidence] The entire hope of this holy Virgin was in God, who had anticipated her with blessings of sweetness: hence she most frequently sang that Davidic psalm, familiar to the holy Fathers and hermits: "O God, come to my aid; O Lord, make haste to help me." She often sought the emphasis of these words; but no one ever provided her with a deeper explanation than the one she herself had conceived in her mind, God teaching her inwardly. When asked why he loved this verse above all others, he replied that the entire consolation of his seraphic mother was contained in it, that each of its syllables swelled with a certain secret, familiar sap of confidence in God, and that nothing could taste sweeter to his palate than these very words. He was divinely granted the certainty of faith to attain the three greatest gifts: eternal beatitude, the perpetual and never-to-be-interrupted friendship of God, and unfailing help from on high in whatever needs or sudden dangers might arise.

[213] [On Divine Providence,] This soul was once tormented, pondering with fearful dread the mystery of its predestination, which is awesome to every soul, when the Lord, eager to help, thus spoke to it: "Daughter, I condemn no one, except the one who is willing to be condemned; from now on, be of tranquil mind." At another time, in a trance, the little Jesus appeared to her, resting in the arms of the purest Mother, and, speaking to her gently, commanded her to gather the roses scattered on the ground into the fold of her flowing garment. Then the divine Infant, having been asked once and graciously accepted, added: "You are this Rose; I undertake its prudent care." You may arrange the rest as you wish. By these he knew himself to be held on the right hand of the Savior, like a chosen rose that no one could snatch from his hand. Weaving a sudden garland from the remaining roses, she placed it on the head of the awesome Jesus, who, smiling at the virgin and blessing her, vanished. He knew that the other roses were to be given to the pious virgins of Lima, who, after his death, were to be gathered into the new monastery of St. Catherine of Siena.

[214] [so that those who watched, at the mere sight of the invincible Virgin from afar, suddenly learned to spare the innocent.] With the same confident strength in God, from her earliest years Rose stood fearless against whatever difficulties and hardships of mortal life: whose faith, constancy, and counsels the savage bulls, otherwise goaded into the utmost rage, seemed to revere. for those who watched, amazed at the sight of the invincible Virgin from a distance, suddenly learned to spare the innocent. For when she was twelve years old, in a certain village she encountered a most ferocious bull rushing toward her, and she discouraged those who were with her from fleeing, fixing her eyes on the sky. She had not yet finished speaking when the ferocious beast, with a terrifying roar, charged at the other people far off, Rosa and her party remaining unharmed. She then urged her still trembling companions to place their hope more firmly in the help of the Most High, especially since the closeness of the danger had cut off every means of escape. Thus, on another occasion, a bull once again charged straight at the chariot in which Rosa was riding with her mother and others, throwing it off course; while the others tried to flee, only Rosa, with eyes cast heavenward, predicted that the bull would not rush as far as the carriage. He had spoken, and the event responded, to the astonishment of all, not so much at the bull's return as at the Virgin's spirit, superior to all fear, who in the greatest dangers sang to her heavenly Spouse: "I will not fear any evil, for you are with me."

His confidence in relying on divine providence for the necessary means of life was not small. [having experienced other necessities,] The bread of the numerous family in the paternal house had once failed, and there was nowhere to buy it. With a brief movement of her lips she commended the matter to God; soon, with great faith, she opened the bread chest and found it full of unleavened and flavorful loaves, as evidenced by its strange shape, which did not come from the household baker. An absolutely essential household condiment, honey, had completely run out in those regions: she herself went down in the name of the Lord, secretly inspired by a most secret instinct to hope for God's protection, and found the hive filled to the brim with fresh honey, the whole household amazed—and all the more so because, by a new miracle, the honey, given from heaven, was enough for the entire family's daily use for a full eight months. Gaspar Flores, the Virgin's father, was growing weak, also grieving over a fifty-pound debt which, for lack of funds, he could not pay; the perplexed mother was distressed, her anxieties increased by the creditor's importunity. A daughter goes to the temple, offers prayers, and as she returns home she encounters a complete stranger, who at once hands her a silver ingot wrapped in a band and, telling her to relieve her parents' present need with it, immediately departs. As Rosa unfolded the wrapper, the fifty-pound book, for which her father was a debtor, appeared. What had been told to Gaspar seemed a dream, until the daughter counted the coins as she had received them. These are the gifts that the divine Spouse once promised to provide to his parents, in the guise of a heavenly Lapicida, among the paraphernalia and antidora for this new bride.

[216] He was also illustrious by the testimony of our Lord Jesus Christ, [and had foretold many things to come,] who is the spirit of prophecy. for almost a full decade before the foundation, sometimes through symbols and figures, at other times manifestly and in its own proper form, the monastery was not only divinely shown to him, which was to be under S. under the patronage of St. Catherine of Siena, was to be dedicated to a numerous college of holy virgins, but also recognized in her face the first future abbess and foundress of the convent, Lucia Guerra da Daga, imbued her with his spirit, and in a manner initiated her with the kiss of peace, saying to her: "Well done, mother, God has reserved you for his great work." From the spirit of the Virgin, the blessed matron drew divine senses, and a heavenly desire for the religious state. But the surviving husband and a numerous family stood in the way, whom God shortly after called from this light, and He also removed countless and very powerful other obstacles to the foundation after the virgin's death, and bent the hard hearts that had once been opposed to favoring the pious work. Having laid these aside, Lucia, with her abundant resources, founded and endowed a convent, and finally gave herself to it, taking the sacred veil and the name of Sister Lucia of the Most Holy Trinity, and, as the first superior of her new ascetic community, she wonderfully spread the spirit of religion, and at last, celebrated for her sanctity, she fell asleep in the Lord.

[217] Mary herself, the mother of Olive Rose, who had previously bitterly opposed this prophecy to her daughter, in the year of the Lord 1629, after her husband's death, at the age of sixty, was devoutly received into the new monastery, joining the daughters of St. Father Dominic. [The truth of which events proved.] And, having taken the name Mary of St. Mary with her habit, she made her profession and rested in the Lord with a good death. In which the promise of the Spouse's providence was also fulfilled: for, lacking that necessary dowry, one of the places reserved by the foundress herself for the free reception of more worthy yet needy persons was granted to her, and everything else, down to the smallest detail, happened exactly as the holy virgin had foretold. John Villalobos, under oath, testified that by his own experience he had discerned a prophetic spirit in her, the Virgin having revealed to him a secret matter that he could not have known otherwise than by a heavenly revelation. Something very similar happened to Antonius a Vega, a most upright man of the same Society. Rosa accurately recounted to him the secret thought that the quæstor's daughter had never revealed to anyone, nor ever would, and prescribed the most salutary instructions. He revealed to Mary of the Mass, the wife of Medor Angelini the painter, the plan they had entered into most secretly to set out for Spain: he said that the sum of money they had arranged at their discretion was enough for both of them, to the woman's astonishment and with her attesting that nothing more could have been said by the Virgin, had a third been present in their conversation. As John Miguel of the Order of Saint Dominic was returning to Lima, he recounted in sequence and in detail to him the things that had happened to him in a foreign land, which for human reasons had not been able to come to Rose's knowledge; indeed, he clearly recognized that the most hidden recesses of his own conscience had been laid open to the Virgin. Many other such, and even more marvelous, prophecies of this Spouse of Christ are recorded, all of which were fulfilled by events, confirming that in her soul there dwelt the fullness of grace, not only sanctifying, but also the Holy Spirit given freely.

Notes.

Charybdis is properly the whirlpool in the Sicilian Strait opposite Scylla. It is used here metaphorically, because of its resemblance to the spiritual vortex into which sinners are absorbed, as is quite clear.

A similar heroic deed of St. Catherine of Siena is read in her Acts in our volume for April 3, page 892 and following.

Section V. The last illness of the Saint, a pious death, and honorable funeral.

[A premonition of impending death] A premonition of the day on which she was to hasten to the Bridegroom's chamber from this valley of tears (which she could not hide from her mother), she annually celebrated the feast of St. She celebrated the feast of St. Bartholomew the Apostle, which was the appointed term of her mortal pilgrimagea, with singular devotion, and on its vigil, not content with her own fast, she brought some innocent boys to fast with her, continuing in this even after her death, because they had been so instructed by Rose. Three years before her departure, she was laid low by a serious illness, so that, already near her end and failing, she was weeping with her confessor and household when she ordered the intrepid one to drive away the untimely sorrow, and to be certain that she would not die then. Upon this, it became known that he had learned from divine revelation the appointed time of his passing. Five months before his death, the treasurer Gundisalvus told his wife of the most atrocious pains of his final illness and foretold that an immense and burning thirst would assail her. He had also foretold the place to the same matron a full year earlier in the very same room in which he paid the debt of mortality, requesting that she entrust the task of dressing his little body and placing it in a coffin to no other woman besides herself and his mother.

Three days before her final illness she went to the garden cell, contracted an unknown disease, and, in the manner of a swan, commended her mother, whom she was leaving desolate, to God and to Saint Dominic by singing little songs. The mother, hearing it, believed it was a metrical game, but on August Kalends she realized it had been prophetically foretold: for in the middle of the night she was heard wailing miserably, and her little body was found stretched out on the ground with stiffened limbs. When asked if she wanted a doctor, she replied, "Heavenly." A cold sweat stood upon her, and throughout all her limbs and joints she was wracked with pains and torments which her Spouse had once shown her in a vision would be far more severe than any she had endured in her life. Ordered by her confessor once, by her mother at other times, to recount her pains, though she had endured the force of nearly all illnesses, and though he had learned their names by experience, he could not explain this one except by likening it to the times of a white-hot iron pressing in, and a flaming dagger piercing the body's midriff from the left side, in which the image of the Crucified One was imprinted, and a white-hot helmet enclosing the head, and at the same time each nerve, joint, and bone, and said that the nerves, joints, bones, and marrow were crushed, dislocated, and burned, and that in the end it had no name, nor did he know of any torture to compare it to.

He also revealed to his confessor that the doctors were in vain striving to diagnose this malady, which showed no pulse nor any other external sign, and that therefore he needed nothing other than the incurable patience of Job, so that he might in some way experience the individual pains of his Spouse, the Crucified One, one by one. She asked for, and obtained, for several days a solitary peace and silence, so that she might more intimately partake of the extreme torments of the crucified Love, to which she felt herself nailed limb by limb on the cross. Such torments were increasing daily, so that it was a wonder she did not succumb more quickly to that so powerful, varied, and cumulative host of pains. but it was gradually resolved, because the most glorious kind of martyrdom for him was to die slowly and in stages. For it was certain that these punishments would themselves work the weight of eternal glory. She knew this was the chalice the Bridegroom had drunk, and which he had mixed for her, the bride, and had shown her so long before; from which she drank it all, steadfastly and joyfully, as if it had been offered to her by the hand of Christ the Lord. She held gentle and most devoted conversations with her beloved, and she prayed to the Lord with her household so that the pains of her mind would not hinder her free use of her mind. A thirst far greater than the others, as he had predicted, tormented him inwardly, and no water was given to the thirsty man, the doctors' prescription standing in the way. Thus, the Virgin, who was withering away as if in punishment, still had to endure, even with her dying Bridegroom, the cry: "I thirst."

[221] As the acceptable time drew nearer, when Rose was to depart from this earth to her Spouse's paradise, having laid aside the vestments of mortality, [during which interval she most piously prepared herself] was to be transferred, she repeatedly summoned her confessors, purged her most tender conscience, and once in general of her entire life made her confession, always with sobs and tears of penitence, so that the moved listeners marveled that in her most severe torments she did not weep, yet so deeply lamented the slightest imperfections. Three days before her happy passing, she humbly and with a mournful voice petitioned for the divine Viaticum and the last Anointing. When the Eucharistic food was brought, she blushed graciously like a shining dawn, and, rapt in joy beyond measure, was carried away into the highest ecstasy. However, when the priest presented the most holy Host and, as was the custom, asked, he responded appropriately and promptly. Thus, with the eager cooperation of all her senses, she received the oil of the sick and dying, by which she felt herself animated more for triumph than for agony.

[222] [prepared for death,] She often also, in the presence of those who were standing by, with a clear voice made a profession of the orthodox faith, protesting that she would die, as she had lived, a daughter of the holy Roman and universal Church. He wished to see the sacred scapular of his Order, as the proper standard of his religious militia, which the Virgin Mother had marked on the newborn Order of Preachers, spread out over the swaddling clothes. When the Prior of the Dominican monastery in Lima, to whom she had for five years otherwise revealed the secrets of her conscience, visited her, she obtained from him by humble prayers that he should read it through* the exact formula by which, already dying, she would beg God's pardon for all who had in any way offended her in the course of her life, the words of which the Virgin, clasping the Crucifix, followed with her voice, and could not be satiated with those most tender words: "Father, forgive them." A soul truly worthy of so great a Spouse, which, that it might be wholly cruciform, imitated the divine Lamb on the cross, earnestly entreating for its adversaries.

[223] [and after certain admonitions given to those present] He implored his householders, Gundisalvus's stewards, in a low and mournful voice to forgive him if he had ever wronged anyone in any way, while all wept who had proven his innocence and purity by so many trials. Having been presented with the document in which he requested a burial among his religious order, he signed it with the utmost humility, nothing less than a solicitous care for his own body. She begged her suitor not to cease to refine her with the most exquisite ardors until she ripened for him. into fruit that deserved to be brought to his table. Toward the end of his life, there were more frequent raptures, the sweetest intervals, in which he was known to have tasted sublime and delightful foretastes of the sweetness of God and of the land of eternity. He earnestly sought the final blessing of both parents and received it reverently; in the same way, he wished to be blessed by the quæstor Gundisalvus and his wife, whom he had honored as parents. To his two younger brothers, among salutary admonitions, he earnestly commended the care and reverence of their parents, as if to say to each of them in imitation of the Crucified One: "Behold your mother." She also encouraged both of the questor's daughters, whom she greatly loved for their innocence, to virtue, and especially to love and reverence for their parents. She admonished the other household servants and the Christian household to the duties of their office, so that she might be seen preaching, not as a woman, but as an apostle.

[224] [He peacefully returned his soul to the Creator.] From John of Lorenzana, her last confessor, she earnestly implored a final blessing, preparing herself to attend the morning office on the vigil of St. Bartholomew, knowing she had no more than four hours left. She warned that on that very night, at the beginning of the festival, she would depart for the eternal feast. Which she did with such a serene and joyful countenance, that she appeared truly a prudent and wise Virgin, waiting in the vestibule of paradise with her lamp, for her beloved Bridegroom to come, whose voice, feeling in the inmost depths of her heart, requesting the blessed candle with a nod, she marked her forehead, mouth, and breast with the sign of the cross, and commanded the head-rest to be removed, so that, inclining her head to the bare wood, she might feel herself to die on the cross. Finally, commending his spirit into the hands of the Father, with his eyes fixed on heaven, as he entered with the Bridegroom, he pronounced, "Jesus, Jesus, Jesus be with me." He expired very peacefully. These words, which had been for him in early infancy the first formula of prayer, thus, as a testimony of his own infantile innocence, carried him to the threshold of the heavenly homeland, in the thirty-second year of his age, when the fifth month had scarcely begun.

When the virgin Rose's body had ceased to breathe, she was still believed to be breathing from the vivid color of her face and her red lips, until it was discovered that no breath remained from the mirror applied to her mouth, which was as much its last use as it was its first. By a sudden miracle, a mysterious sense of joy seized everyone, so that even the mother herself, fitting the corpse (just as her living daughter had done), and the entire household seemed, not to be attending the deceased's funeral, but to be sharing in a wedding joy. Thus, even the outsiders, whoever they were, were intimately and secretly seized by a stimulus of pleasure. This funeral was celebrated only with the public wails and tears of the Limesian beggars, the crowd of the needy lamenting that the true nurse and mother of the poor had been cruelly snatched from them by the most bitter fate, far too soon.

A numerous company of angels was seen, before her death dancing festively in turn around the bed of the dying [to the incorrupt body of the Saint], and afterward around the bier of the deceased. The Questor's wife saw the hall in which they had laid the Virgin's body all aglow with a radiant outpouring of heavenly glory. Thus was fulfilled what Christ the Lord had revealed would happen: namely, the death of Rose and her glorious burial. Devout women, in turn, exulted with the sonorous singing of sacred hymns, protesting that there ought to be room there not for laments but for feasts. By a marvelous consensus and a hidden instinct, the great multitude of people, with the day already breaking, filled the questor's palace. The confessors, standing before the bier, broke out into cries of rejoicing and blessings, testifying to all that the baptismal grace had never been interrupted by any lethal stain, but had preserved an immaculate innocence throughout her life and brought the childlike purity of virginal whiteness into heaven to follow the Lamb, wherever he might go.

[227] For the funeral, a rumor having already spread, an innumerable multitude of people from all walks of life gathered, [and after the funeral rites were celebrated, to his tomb,] both pious brotherhoods of various titles of their own accord and mendicant religious Orders processed. soon & the metropolitan college, & the royal senate with all the magistrates. They were carrying the sacred body with unusual honor to the cathedral church, the canons taking turns in this duty, with the superiors of the various religious orders competing to succeed one another. At the church doors the face of the Virgin was seen to glow with a more vivid and beautiful color, so that even of this it might be said by Christ the Lord: "The maiden is not dead, but is asleep." It also seemed to many that the statue of the Most Holy Rosary of the Virgin Mother was shining with light and splendor. When this became public, many others who came saw the prodigy not only from a distance but also up close. People of every age and sex alike, in a frenzy, were devoutly trying to touch the sacred body with crowns, rosaries, and whatever devotional object they could get their hands on.

[228] [but the burial place ought later to have been changed.] The very spacious church resounded on every side with continuous voices acclaiming Rose as BLESSED and HOLY, until the precious deposit was committed to burial. After this, as the people's devout attendance grew because of the exceptional benefits of the cures, which became more frequent by the day, more solemn funeral rites were celebrated, to which the viceroy and a greater multitude attended with festive applause and signs of public joy, as both the inhabitants and the newcomers acclaimed the new Citizen of Heaven. Shortly thereafter, at the urgent request of the city's primates that the sacred body be transferred to a more venerable place, by decree of the Archbishop of Lima it was removed and found not to have succumbed to corruption, and to emit the same heavenly fragrance as when it was first laid to rest. At this celebration there was no one who, in the presence of the virgin's corpse and with the memory of her recent deeds fresh in his mind, was not deeply moved to the marrow; many tears of devotion flowed; and the very name of Rose was received by all with an open sign of reverence. The body was most fittingly deposited to the right of the high altar, but because of the great multitude of votaries there, another place had to be chosen for the sacred relics, namely the chapel of Saint Catherine of Siena itself. For she owed so much to her mother's seraphic daughter, who had brought her to rest.

Thus St. Rose foresaw that she would die on the feast of St. Bartholomew, since she celebrated that day each year with singular devotion as if it were the final term of her life.

Hence it is confirmed that, after midnight, at the very beginning of the feast of St. Bartholomew, the Saint departed for the heavens. But from this we still do not know whether he died on August 24 or the following day, for the reason we cited above in the annotations.
